An exact category approach to Hecke endomorphism algebras ∗
Jie Du, Brian Parshall †, Leonard Scott

Abstract
. Let G G be a finite group of Lie type. In studying the cross-characteristic representation theory of G G , the (specialized) Hecke algebra H = End G ​ ( Ind B G ​ 1 B ) H=\text{\rm{End}}_{G}(\text{Ind}_{B}^{G}1_{B}) has played a important role. In particular, when G = G ​ L n ​ ( 𝔽 q ) G=GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}) is a finite general linear group, this approach led to the Dipper-James theory of q q -Schur algebras A A . These algebras can be constructed over 𝒵 := ℤ ⁡ [ t , t − 1 ] {\mathcal{Z}}:=\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}] as the q q -analog (with q = t 2 q=t^{2} ) of an endomorphism algebra larger than H H , involving parabolic subgroups. The algebra A A is quasi-hereditary over 𝒵 {\mathcal{Z}} . An analogous algebra, still denoted A A , can always be constructed in other types. However, these algebras have so far been less useful than in the G ​ L n GL_{n} case, in part because they are not generally quasi-hereditary. Several years ago, reformulating a 1998 conjecture, the authors proposed (for all types) the existence of a 𝒵 {\mathcal{Z}} -algebra A + A^{+} having a stratified derived module category, with strata constructed via Kazhdan-Lusztig cell theory. The algebra A A is recovered as A = e ​ A + ​ e A=eA^{+}e for an idempotent e ∈ A + e\in A^{+} . A main goal of this monograph is to prove this conjecture completely. The proof involves several new homological techniques using exact categories. Following the proof, we show that A + A^{+} does become quasi-hereditary after the inversion of the bad primes. Some first applications of the result—e.g., to decomposition matrices—are presented, together with several open problems.



This work was supported by grants from the ARC (DP120101436, Jie Du), and the Simons Foundation (#359360, Brian Parshall; #359363, Leonard Scott). The last author would like to thank UNSW for support from the Distinguished Research Visitor Scheme.

August 7, 2026

Introduction

Representations of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
are the first examples of group representations systematically investigated by the pioneers in representation theory such as F.G. Frobenius, A. Young, and W. Burnside. When I. Schur used the symmetric group representations to determine polynomial representations of the complex general linear group
{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, certain finite-dimensional algebras, known as Schur algebras, played a bridging role between the two. The well-known Schur–Weyl duality summarizes the relation between the representations of
{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
.

A Schur algebra is the endomorphism algebra of a direct sum of certain permutation modules associated with Young (or parabolic) subgroups of
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
. These permutation modules are filtered by Specht modules (with multiplicities given by Young’s rule), and such filtrations result in a standard stratification for the Schur algebra so that its representation category is a so-called highest weight category, a notion naturally arising from the study of representations of Lie groups/algebras and algebraic groups. The Schur algebra itself is an example of a quasi-hereditary algebra, a notion introduced by E. Cline together with the last two authors of this monograph (CPS) in the late 1980s.

The endomorphism algebra of a permutation representation on a Borel subgroup
B(q)
, consisting of all upper triangular matrices, of a finite general linear group
{\text{\rm GL}}_{r}(q)
is called an (Iwahori–)Hecke algebra which is a
q
-deformation of the group algebra of
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
. Such Hecke algebras and their generic versions are all defined for finite groups
G
of Lie type with a parameter
q

[
Iwa64
,
C85
]
. Often
q
is a prime power and the associated Hecke algebras are
q
-deformations of the group algebra of the Weyl group
W
of
G
. Now, using
q
-permutation modules associated with parabolic subgroups of
W
, the notion of Schur algebras is generalized not only to
q
-Schur algebras in the type
A
case, but also to Hecke endomorphism algebras (Hecke endo-algebras) in general.

More precisely, let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a generic Hecke algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}:=\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]
associated to a finite group
G
of Lie type with Weyl group
W
. For each subset
J
of the set
S
of fundamental reflections for
W
, form the associated

q
-permutation module
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
. Put
T:=\bigoplus_{J}x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
and call the endomorphism algebra
A:=\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T)
a generic Hecke endo-algebra. If
G
is a finite general linear group and
{\mathscr{K}}
is a field,

A_{\mathscr{K}}={\mathscr{K}}\otimes A
is (Morita equivalent to) a
q
-Schur algebra, as introduced by Dipper and James
[
DJ89
]
, and used effectively in the study of cross-characteristic representation theory of

G
(so that
q
is nonzero in
{\mathscr{K}}
). The definition makes sense for
G
of any type, but
A
itself, without modification, appears less useful (compared to
q
-Schur algebras), partly for structural and homological reasons. For simplicity, we will assume that
G
is a fixed-point group of a Frobenius map on a
simple
algebraic group
{\mathbb{G}}
throughout the monograph.

The structure of
q
-Schur algebras is a natural quantization from the structure of Schur algebras, thanks to the existence of filtrations of
q
-permutation modules by
q
-Specht modules. What is a replacement of Specht or
q
-Specht modules in the general case?

In 1996, CPS developed a theory of stratifying endomorphism algebras. They considered abstract Specht modules and a set of stratification hypotheses with which the associated endomorphism algebra is a standardly stratified algebra, a notion that is slightly weaker than that of a quasi-hereditary algebra. For the aforementioned generic Hecke algebras, the authors used the modules associated with Kazhdan–Lusztig left cells as “Specht modules” to filter these
q
-permutation modules. This results in a standard stratification for
q
-Schur algebras, but a coarser stratification for a generic Hecke endo-algebra
A
of other Lie types; see
[
DPS98b
,
DPS98a
]
. However, in the rank-two case, if we enlarge
T
to
T^{+}=T\oplus X
where
X
is a certain module filtered by certain cell modules, we obtain a finer stratification in terms of two-sided cells.
In other words, if an enlargement
T^{+}
of
T
is used, then
A^{+}=\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T^{+})
could be standardly stratified. This motivated the conjecture
[
DPS98a
, Conj. 2.5.2]
, later modified (slightly) in

[
DPS15
, Conj. 1.2]
.

The conjecture is about the construction of an extended (generic) Hecke endo-algebra
A^{+}
with good homological
properties. It proposes the existence of a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra
A^{+}
with a nicely stratified derived module category, with all strata constructed via Kazhdan-Lusztig cell theory. It is required by the conjecture to extend the
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
T
to
T^{+}=T\oplus X
, for some right

{\mathcal{H}}
–module
X
with a suitable dual left-cell (module) filtration. Note, this implies that
A=eA^{+}e
, where
e
is the idempotent projection of
T^{+}
onto
T
along
X
. The conjecture also specifies “standard modules,” parametrized by left cells, that filter a projective generator for the category
A^{+}
–mod of finitely generated
A^{+}
-modules.

More precisely, the
q
-permutation module
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
, filtered by dual left-cell modules
S_{\omega}
, has a bottom section
S_{\omega^{0,J}}
, where
\omega^{0,J}
is the left cell that contains the longest element
w_{0,J}
of the parabolic subgroup
W_{J}
.
For a left cell
\omega
that satisfies
S_{\omega}\not\cong S_{\omega^{0,J}}
for all
J\subseteq S
, we want to construct a right
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
X_{\omega}
filtered by dual left-cell modules and with bottom section
S_{\omega}
. We require
X_{\omega}
to satisfy a certain homological property, the Ext
1
vanishing property, which forms part of the Stratification Hypothesis
[
DPS98a
, (1.2.9)]
.
Note that, for the symmetric group, every two-sided cell contains some
w_{0,J}
and every
S_{\omega}\cong S_{\omega^{0,J}}

for some
J
. So, we take
X=0
in this case. In general,
we may take
X
to be a direct sum of such
X_{\omega}
. Thus, the proof of the conjecture relies on the existence of such
X_{\omega}
.

In
[
DPS15
]
, we studied a local case of the conjecture, under the equal parameter assumption for
{\mathcal{H}}
in order to use a result from
[
GGOR03
]
. In the DPS work, the module
X_{\omega}
was constructed inductively, using a new order on left cells, to satisfy the required Ext
1
vanishing condition. This order is strictly compatible with the two-sided cell order
\leq_{LR}
, and is defined by a height function on left cells, which is called a sorting function in
[
GGOR03
]
. Note that the height function is motivated by Lusztig’s
a
-function on
W
that takes constant values on two-sided cells. In this way, we proved a special case (
[
DPS15
, Th. 5.6]
) of the conjecture
[
DPS15
, Conj. 1.2]
.

To attack the conjecture in general, the authors developed in
[
DPS17
]
an exact category approach to control the Ext
1
vanishing property when constructing certain modules (such as
X_{\omega}
) with some desired filtrations. Applying
this general construction to
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules yields the exact category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
, where
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
is a full (additive) subcategory of mod-
{\mathcal{H}}
, and
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
is a subcategory of the category of short exact sequences in the category mod-
{\mathcal{H}}
of finite right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules. However, this general construction does not decide if the
q
-permutation modules, though belonging to
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
, satisfy the required Ext
1
vanishing property. To overcome the hurdle, two more exact subcategories are constructed to obtain an exact category
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
in which

X_{\omega}
may be constructed with a required filtration and both
X_{\omega}
and
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
satisfy the Ext
1
vanishing property. Though the process is quite sophisticated, we eventually prove the conjecture
[
DPS15
, Conj. 1.2]
; it is a consequence of Theorem
3.3.1
whose proof is outlined in Section 0.2 below.

0.1.
Further results and applications

After preliminaries in Chapters 1, we begin in Chapter 2 with a construction of a sequence of exact categories, consisting of objects with filtrations of left-cell modules, inside the category
{\mathcal{H}}
-mod of left
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules. By applying a standard duality theory to the construction in Section 2.2, we obtain in Section 2.3 the exact categories
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
, required in the proof of the conjecture, of right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules with filtrations of dual left-cell modules and the homological property satisfied by
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
. Section 2.4 reviews and reformulates an abstract setting developed in
[
DPS17
]
for the construction of these aforementioned extra modules
X_{\omega}
.

The conjecture is
stated and proved in Chapter 3; see Theorem
3.3.1
. The proof is technically quite difficult, so we
provide at the end of this introduction a conceptual framework for the way
A^{+}
and
T^{+}
evolve in these sections.

Theorem
3.3.1
involves
no mention of primes whatsoever
, good, bad, or otherwise. Nevertheless, the theorem proves that the category
A^{+}
-mod has a natural (standard) stratifying system. Section 3.4 is devoted to a “uniqueness theory.”
(See similar uniqueness results for quasi-hereditray covers, discussed by Rouquier in
[
Ro08
, §4]
.) Its original motivation was to sharpen Theorem
3.3.1
: A main step in the proof of that theorem involved the construction of certain
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules
X_{\omega}
associated to
left cells
\omega
; see
Construction
2.4.12
. By Theorem
2.4.10
, the
X_{\omega}
have remarkable homological properties, which are
required in realizing
A^{+}
as an endomorphism algebra. Although no claim is made that the modules
X_{\omega}
are unique, in Theorem
3.4.5
, the
following uniqueness result is proved:

the Morita class of
A^{+}
is independent of the choice of the modules
X_{\omega}
.
The uniqueness theory is formulated in such a way (see Theorem
3.4.2
) that it might be useful for other suitably filtered endomorphism algebras. In Section 3.5, we turn the categorical property — i.e., the stratifying system for
A^{+}
-mod, established in Theorem
3.3.1
— into the structural property for
A^{+}
by explicitly displaying a defining sequence, called a standard stratification, for
A^{+}
with stratifying ideals as sections; see Theoren
3.4.5
.

Remark 0.1.1

(
Good and bad primes).

The “bad primes”
are the only integer primes
that divide the denominators of generic character degree formulas; see
[
DPS98a
, (4.1.12)]
or the discussion of display (
4.1.14
) in the appendix to Section 4.1, where only the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case is considered. They form a subset of
\{2,3,5\}
.
We declare all such prime divisors to be “bad” (for
W
,
G
, or the Lie series to which
G
belongs) and all others to be “good.” Recall the classical definition that a prime is good for
{\mathbb{G}}
if it does not divide any coefficient of the maximal root in the root system of
{\mathbb{G}}
. By
[
DPS98a
, 4.1]
, all primes that are good for
{\mathbb{G}}
are good for
G
.

Suppose that
A^{+\natural}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}A^{+}
is obtained from
A^{+}
by inverting all the bad primes for
W
. Theorem
4.3.4
then proves that
A^{+\natural}
is quasi-hereditary of separable type
in all cases (and split quasi-hereditary except in type
{}^{2}F_{4}
, becoming split upon the adjunction of
\sqrt{2})
).
Sections 4.1 and 4.2 prepare for the above results in Section 4.3. They also provide an interesting study in themselves of the base-changed version
{\mathcal{J}}:={\mathcal{Z}}\otimes{\mathsf{J}}
of the Lusztig asymptotic
\mathbb{Z}
–algebra
{\mathsf{J}}
. There is a natural inclusion of the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
into (a copy of)
{\mathcal{J}}
, and the latter may be regarded as an order in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
containing
{\mathcal{H}}
.
One approach to understanding the representation theory of the generic Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
is to relate it to
the simpler algebra
{\mathcal{J}}
.
Any

{\mathcal{J}}
-module
M
can be restricted to
{\mathcal{H}}
, and our Theorem
4.2.1
says every left-cell module for
{\mathcal{H}}
arises this way. Once again there is no mention of primes. However, if bad primes are inverted in
\mathbb{Z}^{\natural}\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, we show the resulting order
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
is maximal (in the sense of Reiner
[
Reiner03
]
). We then prove that every stratifying ideal arising from the defining sequence for
A^{+}
in Theorem
3.4.5
is, after base change to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, a hereditary ideal of separable type (and of split type, if
{}^{2}F_{4}
is excluded), completing the proof of Theorem
4.3.4
. In the rest of Section 4.3, we focus on a refinement of the defining sequence for
A^{+\natural}
such that its sections are indecomposable split hereditary ideals, a notion used in
[
Ro08
]
. In particular, standard modules and Rouquier’s isomorphism are given in Corollary
4.3.5
. Corollary
4.3.6

brings in the idempotents that define these indecomposable split hereditary ideals. (Note that the approach developed in
[
Ro08
]
is an idempotent-free approach.) Finally, in Corollary
4.3.7
, we prove that
A^{+\natural}
-mod is a highest weight category in the sense of
[
DS94
]
and
[
Ro08
]
.

Chapter 5 is devoted to applications to several related topics.
In Section 5.1, we consider the algebras
A
and
A^{+}
from the point of view of standardly based algebras, as introduced by Du and Rui
[
DR98
]
. For example, Theorem
5.1.8

proves that the algebras
A^{\natural}
,
A^{+\natural}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
are all standardly based. The latter case was previously known (see
[
G07
]
), though our proof is new.

Sections 5.2 focuses on a new study of decomposition numbers of specialized Hecke endo-algebras relevant to those for finite groups of Lie type in cross-characteristic representation theory. It is not a new theme, but our results are quite general and often deeper than those found in the literature. Theorems
5.2.2
and
5.2.8
identify a large collection of decomposition numbers which remain
constant for all groups in a given series, independent of their
q
-parameter, the latter parameter, defined by
[
C85
]
, is a general version of the
q
in
SL_{n}(q)
for the
SL_{n}
-series. The uni-triangularity of the associated decomposition matrix is an easy consequence of the split quasi-hereditary structure.

Section 5.3 links the decomposition matrix for a Hecke endo-algebra
A
with that of the corresponding finite groups of Lie type. We prove in Theorem
5.3.1
that, if
A
is the Hecke endo-algebra associated with the standard finite Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
and
G
is a finite group in the Lie type series associated with
(W,S,L)
, then the decomposition matrix for the specialized
A_{{\mathcal{O}}}
is part of the decomposition matrix of
{\mathcal{O}}G
. In particular, if
r
is good, then this part of the decomposition matrix is unitriangular. This provides evidence for possible improvement of Geck’s conjecture
[
G12
]
proved recently in
[
BDT20
]
. See comments around footnote
13
in Chapter 5.
Part of our proof in the adjoint case is based on a note of Veronica Shalotenko; see Appendix A. We thank her for allowing us to include this work in our monograph. We also point out in a Remark
5.3.2
(1) that her proof continues to work if the hypothesis on the connected center of
{\mathbb{G}}
is removed, due to a result of Dipper and Fleischmann
[
DF92
, Lem. (3.8)]
.

Section 5.4 is motivated by Schur’s original work, the Schur–Weyl duality, as mentioned at the beginning of this Introduction, and its quantum analog. In their study of quantum symmetric pairs of type AIII, H. Bao and W. Wang
[
BW18
]
established a new type of the Schur–Weyl duality between
i
-quantum groups
{\bf U}^{\jmath}
and
{\bf U}^{\imath}
and Hecke algebras of types
B
and
C
, respectively. Wu and the first author have partially lifted Bao–Wang’s duality to the integral level (see
[
DW22
,
DW23
]
). In these works, certain Hecke endomorphism algebras, which are centralizer subalgebras of
A^{\ddagger}
, played the bridging role. In particular, the standard basis theory in Section 5.1 and the discovery about the decomposition matrix for
A^{+}
or
A^{\ddagger}
in Section 5.2 can be applied to representations of
i
-quantum groups and their hyperalgebras. See Theorems
5.4.4
and
5.4.16
.

In the final section of Chapter 5, we pose some problems, suggested by this monograph, on decomposition numbers for finite groups of Lie type.
Each of the problems can be viewed as a step toward understanding the decomposition numbers in reductions
mod
p
of ordinary irreducible representations in the unipotent series. The problems are formulated at a “starter” level, assuming the underlying field characteristic is a good prime. We hope this assumption might be eventually removed in some future reformulation. The framework of Theorem
3.3.1
, which treats all primes alike, makes such a reworking of “good prime” formulations possible, and provides a general starting point. Note that Theorem
3.3.1
itself is a kind of reformulation of the “good prime” result Theorem
4.3.4
.

Finally, the monograph closes with several appendices which cover a number of
preparatory topics used in its main body.

0.2.
Outline and guide for the proof of Theorem
3.3.1

Let
\Omega
be the set of Kazhdan-Lusztig left cells in a finite standard Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
as defined in
[
Lus03
]
, using parameters from an ambient algebraic group
and a fixed-point group of a Frobenius map (see
[
C85
, §1.17]
).
1
1

1

See also footnote
1
in Section 1.1 below.
In
[
DPS17
]
, the authors constructed an early version
A^{\dagger}
of
A^{+}
based on
the existence of suitable modules
X_{\omega}
, for all
\omega\in\Omega
. Each

X_{\omega}
has a filtration with sections isomorphic to dual left-cell modules
S_{\nu}
,
\nu\in\Omega
, and a bottom section
S_{\omega}
(defined
precisely in display (
1.2.10
)), which, in addition, satisfy

(0.2.1)

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}(S_{\tau},X_{\omega})=0,\quad\forall\tau,\omega\in\Omega.

Here
S_{\tau}
and
X_{\omega}
are objects in a suitable exact category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
. The category

{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
is a full subcategory of the category of
\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]
-free, finite right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules. In addition, it is required that
each object in
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
have a certain filtration with sections direct sums of dual left-cell modules.
Also,
A^{\dagger}:=\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})}(T^{\dagger})
, where
T^{\dagger}:=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}X_{\omega}^{\oplus m_{\omega}}
with each
m_{\omega}
a positive integer. This definition of
T^{\dagger}
, together with the

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}
–vanishing (
0.2.1
), implies that
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}(S_{\tau},T^{\dagger})=0
, for all
\tau\in\Omega
. This is a key property for managing filtrations of
A^{\dagger}
that arise from filtrations of
T^{\dagger}
.

Generally speaking, the pair
(A^{\dagger},T^{\dagger})
has many desirable properties, except we want
T^{\dagger}
to contain, as an
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
-summand, each of the
q
-permutation modules
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
, as well as their direct sum (denoted
T
).
This problem
presents a major difficulty in passing from
[
DPS17
]
to the set-up of this monograph. It is solved by introducing a new exact
category
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})=({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{S}}))
inside
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
with
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
and
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S_{\tau},x_{J}{\mathcal{H}})=0
, for all
\tau
.

The construction of
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})
is highly nontrivial.
It begins in Sections 1.3 and 2.1 which study a dual category

({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat})
in the category of
{\mathcal{Z}}
-free left
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules. Of course, it is possible to pass between right and left
{\mathcal{Z}}
-free, finite
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules by simply applying the dual functor
(-)^{*}=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(-,{\mathcal{Z}})
. However, this process interchanges left exact functors with
right exact functors, while we need to use the left-module versions to insure the various functors sending an exact sequence
0\to M\to N\to P\to 0
to the fixed-point sequences
0\to M^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\to N^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\ \to P^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}
,

J\subseteq S
, are left exact. This allows us to build an exact subcategory by focusing on the
short exact sequences
0\to M\to N\to P\to 0
whose associated fixed-point sequences are also all short exact sequences
[
DPS17
, Lem. 3.1]
.
A version of this approach is used in Proposition
2.1.3
to create
{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}={\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
inside an exact category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
dual to
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
.
The category
{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}
is then defined to consist of objects with certain “internal” candidate exact sequences belonging to
{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}
. Remarkably, the objects
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
belong to
{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}
; see Corollary
2.1.8
. This implies that each dual object

x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
belongs to
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
. We want more than that, however. We want
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
to be
a direct summand of a new version of
T^{\dagger}
with the key vanishing (
0.2.1
) preserved. This forces us to prove that
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S_{\omega},x_{J}{\mathcal{H}})=0
, for each
\omega\in\Omega
and
J\subseteq S
. This vanishing is proved in its dual
incarnation, Theorem
2.1.6
. The short exact sequence (
2.1.8
) displayed in the proof of Theorem
2.1.6
is the key ingredient. It is available from the exactness of
(-)^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}
there.

Section 2.3 can be viewed as dualizing the constructions of Section 2.1. Of course, Section 2.1 was formulated with these duals in mind.

A main step in completing the proof of the conjecture, at this point, is carried out in Section 2.4, namely, in Theorem
2.4.10
, which imitates the construction of
X_{\omega}
in
[
DPS17
, Th. 4.5]
.

Section 3.1 is also preparatory, providing more detail on dual left-cell module filtrations of
q
-permutation
modules. It refines the analysis of
q
-permutation module filtrations begun above Corollary
2.1.5
. The discussion
draws from earlier work in
[
DPS98a
]
.

The main goal of Section 3.3 is to prove Theorem
3.3.1
. This implies Conjecture
3.2.1
, equivalent to
[
DPS15
, Conj. 1.2]
, a modification of
[
DPS98a
, Conj. 2.5.2]
.
A further consequence of the proof is the
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}
-vanishing result Corollary
3.3.2
. It is used later in Section 3.4 in constructing a uniqueness Morita theory complementary to Theorem
3.3.1
, and discussed further below.

For further discussions in outline of this monograph, see the previous two sections.

Sydney and Charlottesville, Jie Du

September 2022 Brian Parshall
†

Leonard Scott

A special note

Our lifelong collaborator and friend, Brian Parshall, passed away on January 17, 2022. This was a few days after a Zoom meeting we had held, generally weekly, since mid 2020. This last meeting was very successful and lasted 2 hours. The last question, one Jie had for Brian, was about using “Hecke endo-algebra” as a short form for “Hecke endomorphism algebra”. Brian answered “it sounds good. I will think about it”. Unfortunately, these words from Brian became the last words we heard from him. He passed away within a week of this last meeting. It was a shock and surprise for everyone, certainly for Brian’s collaborators. Most of the work on this monograph, however, had been done with Brian’s participation. Its remaining co-authors, Jie Du and Leonard Scott, wish to take this opportunity to acknowledge Brian Parshall as being a co-author of this monograph. In a variation on the words above that Brian used in our last meeting,“we didn’t have to think about it.”

Jie Du and Leonard Scott

Chapter 1
Preliminaries

In this chapter, we briefly review some notions that will be used throughout. Topics include finite groups of Lie type, generic Hecke algebras, exact categories and stratified algebras.

If
B
is a ring, then
B
-mod (respectively, mod-
B
) will denote the category of all finite (i.e., finitely generated) left
(respectively, right)
B
-modules. Usually,
B
will be a finitely generated algebra over a commutative Noetherian ring

{\mathscr{K}}
. In this case, and unless otherwise noted, objects in
B
-mod will be assumed to be finite
{\mathscr{K}}
-modules
(with a similar convention for mod-
B
).

1.1.
Finite groups of Lie type

We review some basic notions concerning finite groups of Lie type. More material on this topic will be introduced in Chapter 5. We closely follow the treatment given in Carter’s book
[
C85
]
. In particular, we will assume the reader is familiar with the notion
[
C85
, pp.16–17]
of a connected, reductive algebraic group over an algebraically closed field of positive characteristic. Such a group
{\mathbb{G}}
may be characterized as the internal product
\mathbf{Z}^{\circ}{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}
, where
\mathbf{Z}^{\circ}
is a torus, the connected component of the identity in the center
\mathbf{Z}=Z({\mathbb{G}})
of
{\mathbb{G}}
, and
{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}
is a semisimple algebraic group, defined by Carter to be nontrivial and an “almost direct product” (a “central product” in traditional terminology) of simple algebraic groups. The latter are defined as having a maximal normal closed subgroup which is finite. Note that

{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}
is necessarily the commutator subgroup of
{\mathbb{G}}
.

Now let
{\mathbb{G}}
be a (connected) reductive algebraic group over an algebraically closed field
of positive characteristic
p
. Recall that a Frobenius map
F:{\mathbb{G}}\to{\mathbb{G}}
in Carter’s sense
[
C85
, §1.17]
is a morphism,
some power of which equals a standard Frobenius map. Fix a Frobenius map
F
, and let
G:={\mathbb{G}}^{F}

be the group of fixed points of
F
. Necessarily,
G
is finite. It is called a
finite group of Lie type
, and all finite groups of Lie type arise this way.
1
1

1

Our notation differs slightly from that in
[
C85
]
, where
{\mathbb{G}}
is sometimes written
G
. Also, we sometimes call a Frobenius map a Frobenius endomorphism or a Frobenius morphism. See
[
C85
, p. 31]
for a detailed definition. A Frobenius endomorphism is sometimes called a Steinberg endomorphism in other sources (see, e.g.,
[
MT11
]
).
We remark from
[
MT11
, p.184, top]
that Carter’s assertion near the bottom of
[
C85
, p. 31]
(just above the definition)
regarding surjective endomorphisms is not correct. However, this does not affect the validity of his definition.

The finite groups of Lie type most studied here arise in the context of the previous paragraph when

{\mathbb{G}}
is taken to be simple.
2
2

2

This assumption is convenient for the description of the Lie type series and weight function
L
discussed below. Note also that sometimes it is useful to study the more general case where
{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}
is simple, either to place
{\mathbb{G}}
in a familiar context (e.g.,
{\mathbb{G}}
is a general linear group), or, after beginning a study using a simple group
{\mathbb{G}}
, viewing it as a commutator group of a larger reductive group
\widetilde{{\mathbb{G}}}
with favorable properties, such as a connected center. See Lemma
5.2.4
in Chapter 5.

In addition to
the defining (algebraically closed) field, the (simple) algebraic group
{\mathbb{G}}
is determined by its root datum and its isogeny class (see
[
C85
, §§1.9, 1.11]
), and
{\mathbb{G}}^{F}
is discussed in
[
C85
, §1.19]
.

It is known that
{\mathbb{G}}
has an
F
-stable Borel subgroup
{\mathbb{B}}
. In addition,
{\mathbb{B}}
contains a maximal torus
\mathbb{T}
of
{\mathbb{G}}
which is
F
-stable. The Weyl group
\mathbb{W}:=N_{{\mathbb{G}}}(\mathbb{T})/{\mathbb{T}}
is then part of a finite Coxeter system
(\mathbb{W},\mathbb{S})
for a (finite) subset
\mathbb{S}
of elements of order 2 and
\bf W
is irreducible (i.e., the Coxeter diagram is connected) under the assumption that
{\mathbb{G}}
is simple. In addition, the pair
(\mathbb{B},\mathbb{N})
, where
\mathbb{N}=\mathbb{N}_{\mathbb{G}}(\mathbb{T})
,
give
\mathbb{G}
the structure of a BN-pair. The group
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
also has a natural BN-pair structure
B,N
, where
B=\mathbb{B}^{F}
and
N=\mathbb{N}^{F}
. Its Weyl group is
W=\mathbb{W}^{F}
. In fact,
W
is generated by the set
S
of all the long words in the various
\mathbb{W}_{J}
, as stated in
[
C85
, p. 34]
and proved in
[
MT11
, Lem. C.1]
.
Moreover, the proof in
[
MT11
]
shows that every element
w\in W
can be written as a product of elements of
S
with lengths additive. One can use these facts to complete Carter’s sketch
[
C85
, p.34]
that
W
is the Weyl group in a (split) BN-pair structure on
G
, so, in particular, it is a Coxeter group (with
S
as its set of fundamental generators).

Let
\Phi
be the root system of
\mathbb{G}
, and let
\Delta
be the set of simple roots associated to
{\mathbb{B}}
.
The Frobenius map
F
on
{\mathbb{G}}
is closely related to a certain permutation
\rho
of
\Delta
. In fact,
\rho
is a graph automorphism of the undirected
Dynkin diagram associated to
\Delta
.
(See
[
C85
, §1.19]
.) Two finite groups
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are said to be in the same
Lie type series
if there exist a connected reductive group
{\mathbb{G}}
and Frobenius morphisms
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, inducing the same graph automorphism
\rho
, and such that
G_{i}={\mathbb{G}}^{F_{i}}
, for
i=1,2
. By inspection, this amounts to saying that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are in one of the groupings
on
[
C85
, pp. 39–41]
; see also
[
MT11
, p.193]
.
Further, if
\rho
is the trivial (or identity) graph automorphism, then
G
is called a
split
(or untwisted) group of Lie type; if
\rho
is non-trivial, then
G
is called a
quasi-split
(or twisted) group of Lie type. Here, following Carter’s classification in
[
C85
, p.41]
, we exclude the Ree and Suzuki groups in the quasi-split case.

There is a natural action of
F
on the character group
\mathbb{X}
(respectively, cocharacter group
\mathbb{Y}
) of
{\mathbb{T}}
.
The maximum of the absolute values of the eigenvalues of
F
as an operator on
\mathbb{X}
is denoted
q
.
It is a (possibly fractional) power
q=p^{d}
, where
p
is the characteristic of the defining field of
{\mathbb{G}}
. For example, if
G
is a classical finite (split) Chevalley group over a finite field
k
of order
r
(an integral power of the defining characteristic
p
),
then
q=r
. In this case,
\rho=1
, the identity map.

Returning to the general case, a useful construction of
W
as a subgroup of
\mathbb{W}
using only the Dynkin diagram permutation
\rho
associated to
F

(rather than
F
itself) can be based on
[
C85
, p. 34]
: First,
\mathbb{W}
may be viewed as constructed from the Dynkin diagram
\Gamma
, with the fundamental reflections associated to the nodes of
\Gamma
. Thus, we can associate to each orbit
J
of
\rho
on
\Gamma
a parabolic subgroup
\mathbb{W}_{J}
of
\mathbb{W}
. Now, the fundamental reflections
s
in
W
may be defined as the longest words of the various subgroups
\mathbb{W}_{J}
. Next, define the “weight function”
L
on
W
by taking it to be the restriction of the length function
\boldsymbol{\ell}
on
\mathbb{W}
to
W
. That is,
L(w):=\boldsymbol{\ell}(w)
, for

w\in W\subseteq\mathbb{W}
. Then,

(W,S,L)
is a finite (irreducible) Coxeter system with weight function
L:W\to\mathbb{Z}
in the sense of
[
Lus03
, §3.1]
. Thus,
if
w=xy
with
\ell(w)=\ell(x)+\ell(y)
, then
L(xy)=L(x)+L(y)
, for
x,y,w\in W
.

Within its Lie type series, the group
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
is determined by
(and determines) a parameter
q

[
C85
, p. 35]
which is described as follows.
If
\delta
is the smallest positive integer such that
F^{\delta}
is a standard Frobenius endomorphism (as per
[
C85
, p. 31]
), then
q^{\delta}
(rather than
q
itself) is defined to be the power of
p
associated to the latter endomorphism. The number
\delta
is also the order of the Dynkin diagram graph automorphism
\rho
. If
G
is a (split) Chevalley group,
\delta=1
and
q
can be any positive power of the defining characteristic
p
. In the quasi-split cases which are not split,
\delta
is the order of a non-trivial automorphism of the Dynkin diagram, and
q
is still any positive power of
p
. However, in the Ree and Suzuki cases,
\delta=2
and
q^{2}=p^{2n+1}
with
p=2
for the
{}^{2}B_{2}
and
{}^{2}F_{4}
cases and
p=3
for the
{}^{2}G_{2}
case. In particular,
q
is a positive power of
\sqrt{p}
. See

[
C85
, p. 41]
.
Remarkably, in all cases,
q
together with the weight function
L
gives

(1.1.1)

q^{L(s)}=[B:B\cap B^{s}],

where
B
is a Borel subgroup of
G
and
s
is a fundamental reflection in its Weyl group
W
. Here
B=\mathbb{B}^{F}
, where
\mathbb{B}
is an
F
-stable Borel subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
; see
[
C85
, pp. 34-35, and p. 74]

for further details.
This formula holds in the more general case where
{\mathbb{G}}
is connected and reductive.
(See the partial discussion in
[
C85
, p. 74]
and also
[
St68
, 11.8]
.)

Throughout this work,
W
will be, unless otherwise noted, the Weyl group of a finite group
G

of Lie type (i.e., the Weyl group of the BN-pair associated to
G
). We will use the natural
positive weight function
L
, constructed above and illustrated in
[
Lus03
, Lem.16.2]
. The resulting weighted Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
is irreducible and will be called
standard finite
.
3
3

3

The Hecke algebras associated to these triples are especially important
for the modular representation theory of unipotent blocks for finite groups of Lie type. According to a theory put forward by Bonafé and Rouquier

[
BR03
]
,
[
BR17
]
, the case of general blocks (for finite groups of Lie type) could, eventually, reduce to the unipotent case. In any event, all irreducible or indecomposable modules with nontrivial cohomology belong to the unipotent principal block.

We also ignore the rank one case, where
W
has order 2, unless otherwise noted.
4
4

4

This connectedness assumption is a matter of convenience and is unnecessary in most cases, e.g., if no component is dihedral of order 16, the Weyl group of
{}^{2}F_{4}
.

Note that standard finite Coxeter systems
(W,S,L)
can also be divided into the
split case
, where the weight function
L
equals the length function
\ell
on
W
, and the
quasi-split case
, where
L\neq\ell
. (The only non-trivial case for the Ree and Suzuki groups is the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case, where
W
is the dihedral group of order 16.) See
[
Lus03
, Chs. 15&16]
.

Example 1.1.1
.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{q}
be the finite field of
q
elements, and let
n
be a positive integer. The finite general linear group
{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is the fixed-point group of the following (standard) Frobenius map

(1.1.2)

F_{q}:{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)\longrightarrow{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k),\;(a_{i,j})\longmapsto(a_{i,j}^{q}),

where
k
is the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Note that
{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)
is a connected reductive group and the subgroup

{\text{\rm SL}}_{n}(k)
of determinant 1 matrices is simple with
F_{q}
-fixed-point group
{\text{\rm SL}}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

For any field
k
,
{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)
has the following split BN-pair:

\displaystyle B_{n}(k)

\displaystyle:=\{\text{subgroup of all upper triangular matrices in }{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)\}

\displaystyle N_{n}(k)

\displaystyle:=\{\text{subgroup of all monomial matrices in }{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)\}.

Let
T_{n}(k)=B_{n}(k)\cap N_{n}(k)
be the subgroup of diagonal matrices.
If
n_{i}
denotes the permutation matrix obtained by interchanging the
i
-th and
(i+1)
-th rows of
I_{n}
, then the Weyl group

\widetilde{W}:=N_{n}(\mathbb{F})/T_{n}(\mathbb{F})\cong{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}\text{ under the correspondence }\;n_{i}\mapsto\tilde{s}_{i}=(i,i+1).

See, e.g.,
[
G03
, 1.6.9]
for more details.

The other finite classical groups can be constructed by fixed-point groups of certain group automorphisms on
{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. In Example
1.1.2
, we consider the symplectic and orthogonal groups. They are relevant to our studies of
i
-quantum groups in Section 5.4. Unitary groups are taken up in Example
1.1.3
. These both help to complete the classical group picture and to provide examples of “unequal parameter” weight functions.

Example 1.1.2
.

For any field
k
and
J\in{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)
, consider the group isomorphism

(1.1.3)

\vartheta=\vartheta_{J}:{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)\longrightarrow{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k),\;x\longmapsto J^{-1}(x^{t})^{-1}J,

and define orthogonal and symplectic groups as the fixed-point groups of some
\vartheta
:

\displaystyle{\text{\rm O}}_{n}(k)

\displaystyle:=\{x\in{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)\mid J_{n}=x^{t}J_{n}x\},

\displaystyle{\text{\rm Sp}}_{2m}(k)

\displaystyle:=\{x\in{\text{\rm GL}}_{2m}(k)\mid J_{2m}^{-}=x^{t}J_{2m}^{-}x\},

where

(1.1.4)

J_{n}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\cdots&0&1\\
0&0&\cdots&1&0\\
&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\\
0&1&\cdots&0&0\\
1&0&\cdots&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad J^{-}_{2m}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&J_{m}\\
-J_{m}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Let
{\text{\rm SO}}_{n}(k)={\text{\rm O}}_{n}(k)\cap{\text{\rm SL}}_{n}(k)
and
let
{\textbf{G}}\subseteq{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)
be one of the classical groups:

{\textbf{G}}=\begin{cases}{\text{\rm SO}}_{2m+1}(k),&n=2m+1,\text{char}(k)\neq 2;\\
{\text{\rm Sp}}_{2m}(k),&n=2m,\text{any characteristic};\\
{\text{\rm SO}}_{2m}(k),&n=2m,\text{char}(k)\neq 2.\end{cases}

These are known as split twisted groups
in
[
St67
, Ch. 11]
.
5
5

5

See
[
MT11
, p.8]
for the definition for char
(k)=2
.

If
k
is the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, then
G
is a connected simple group and has a split BN-pair
{\textbf{B}}={\textbf{G}}\cap B_{n}(k)
,
{\textbf{N}}={\textbf{G}}\cap N_{n}(k)
.

For the Frobenius map
F_{q}
as defined in (
1.1.2
) and
J=J_{n}
or
J_{2m}^{-}
, since
J=x^{t}Jx
implies
J=(F_{q}(x))^{t}JF_{q}(x)
, it follows that
F_{q}
induces the (standard) Frobenius maps

F:{\textbf{G}}\longrightarrow{\textbf{G}},

and
{\textbf{G}}^{F}={\text{\rm SO}}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\;(n=2m+1,2m)
or
{\text{\rm Sp}}_{2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Note also that
{\textbf{G}}^{F}={\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vartheta}
has BN-pair
{\textbf{B}}^{F}=B_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vartheta}
and
{\textbf{N}}^{F}=N_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vartheta}
since
\vartheta
stabilizes the split BN-pair
(B_{n}(k),N_{n}(k))
.
In particular,
\vartheta
induced a group automorphism

\sigma:{\widetilde{W}}\longrightarrow{\widetilde{W}}
, and there is a group isomorphism

{\textbf{W}}:={\textbf{N}}/{\textbf{B}}\cap{\textbf{N}}\cong{\widetilde{W}}^{\sigma}=N_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vartheta}/T_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vartheta}.

Note that the map induced by
F
on
W
is the identity map. Note also that, for
{\textbf{G}}={\text{\rm SO}}_{2m+1}(k)
or
{\text{\rm Sp}}_{2m}(k)
,
W:={\textbf{W}}^{F}={\textbf{W}}
is the Weyl group of type
B/C
with generators
S=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{m-1},t\}
, where

\displaystyle s_{i}

\displaystyle=\tilde{s}_{m-i}\tilde{s}_{m+1+i},\text{ and }t=\tilde{s}_{m}\tilde{s}_{m+1}\tilde{s}_{m},\text{ for }n=2m+1;

\displaystyle s_{i}

\displaystyle=\tilde{s}_{m-i}\tilde{s}_{m+i},\quad\text{ and }t=\tilde{s}_{m},\text{ for }n=2m.

Finally,
t
is the homomorphic image of the elements in
N_{n}(k)^{\vartheta}
:

\begin{pmatrix}I_{m-1}&0&0\\
0&J&0\\
0&0&I_{m-1}\end{pmatrix},\text{ where }J=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}\text{ or }\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

For more details, see, e.g.,
[
G03
, 1.7.3]
.

Example 1.1.3
.

Now, following
[
DVV19
, §4.1]
, we combine
F_{q}
in (
1.1.2
) with
\vartheta
in (
1.1.3
), using the choice
J=J_{n}
, to obtain a new Frobenius morphism

F:=F_{q}\vartheta=\vartheta F_{q}:{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)\longrightarrow{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k).

Since
\vartheta^{2}
is the identity map, it follows that
F^{2}
is the standard Frobenius map
F_{q^{2}}
and

{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)^{F}\subseteq{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})
. We have

{\text{\rm GU}}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}):={\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(k)^{F}=\{x\in{\text{\rm GL}}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})\mid(F_{q}(x))^{t}J_{n}x=J_{n}\}.

This is called the
general unitary group
associated with the hermitian form defined by
J_{n}
. Replacing G by S defines the special unitary group. (See also
[
MT11
, Ex. 21.48(2)]
or
[
G03
, 4.1.10(c)]
.)

For
{\text{\rm SU}}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})
, the
F
-orbits of the simple roots
\alpha_{1,2},\alpha_{2,3},\alpha_{3,4}
for
{\text{\rm SL}}_{4}(k)
, with associated root subgroups
U_{i,j}=\{I_{4}+aE_{i,j}\mid a\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}\}
, are
\{\alpha_{1,2},\alpha_{3,4}\}
,
\alpha_{2,3}
.
The root subgroups
{\text{\rm SU}}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})={\text{\rm SL}}_{4}(k)^{F}
are

(U_{1,2}U_{3,4})^{F}=\{I_{4}+aE_{1,2}-a^{q}E_{3,4}\mid a\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}\},\quad U_{2,3}^{F}=\{I_{4}+aE_{2,3}\mid a+a^{q}=0\}.

The values
q^{L(s)}
(
s\in S
) associated to the weight function
L
on
{\textbf{W}}^{F}=W(B_{2})
in this case are
q^{2},q
. In general,
the values
q^{L(s)}
for
{\text{\rm SU}}_{2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})
are listed in the Coxeter diagram:

q^{2}
q^{2}
q^{2}
q^{2}
q
1
2
3
m-1
m

For
{\text{\rm SU}}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})
, the
F
-orbits of the simple roots
\alpha_{1,2},\alpha_{2,3}
for
{\text{\rm SL}}_{4}(k)
are
\{\alpha_{1,2},\alpha_{2,3}\}
, and we have the root subgroup (cf.
[
G03
, 4.5.12(b)]
)

(U_{1,2}U_{2,3}U_{1,3})^{F}=\bigg\{\begin{pmatrix}1&a&b\\
0&1&-a^{q}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\;\bigg|\;a,b\in\mathbb{F}_{q},b^{q}+b+a^{q+1}=0\bigg\}.

So,
q^{L(s)}=q^{3}
. In general, the values
q^{L(s)}
(
s\in S
) for
{\text{\rm SU}}_{2m+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})
take the form:
q^{2}
q^{2}
q^{2}

1.2.
(Iwahori-)Hecke algebras

We review the theory of Hecke algebras in
[
Lus03
]
. Each weighted Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
defines an algebra

{\mathcal{H}}
, called an
Iwahori-Hecke algebra
in
[
Lus03
]
and called a
generic Hecke algebra
in this monograph. See (
1.2.1
) and
(
1.2.2
) for more details.

Many results in
[
Lus03
]
are contingent on a series P1—P15 of
conjectures (involving
(W,S,L)
and
{\mathcal{H}}
), all of which have been proved in the standard finite case. See
[
Lus03
, Chs. 15, 16]
,
revisited in
[
GJ11
, Ch. 2]
(especially
[
GJ11
, 2.4.1(b)]
for the case
{}^{2}F_{4}
). Thus, we can assume Lusztig’s conjectures
P1–P15 hold for the triples
(W,S,L)
considered in this monograph.

Henceforth, we assume that
(W,S,L)
is standard finite unless otherwise noted. In particular,
L
is positive and Lusztig’s conjectures
P1-P15 hold, as does
condition (
1.2.11
) below.
In fact, we mention that in
Chapters 1–3 which include the main
theorem, we need only assume conjectures P4 and P9, which, together with the rank 2 discussion
in
[
DPS98a
, p. 204]
,
6
6

6

See footnote
3
in Chapter 3 which corrects a typo.
imply that property (
1.2.11
) holds.

Let
{\mathcal{Z}}={\mathbb{Z}}[t,t^{-1}]
be the ring of integer Laurent polynomials in a variable

t
.
Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be the generic Hecke algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}
associated with
(W,S,L)
. Recall
that
{\mathcal{H}}
has basis
\{T_{w}\}_{w\in W}
and defining
relations (
s\in S,\,w\in W
):

(1.2.1)

T_{s}T_{w}=\begin{cases}T_{sw},\quad sw>w;\\
t_{s}^{2}T_{sw}+(t_{s}^{2}-1)T_{w},\quad sw<w,\end{cases}

where
t_{s}:=t^{L(s)}
(and, more generally,
t_{w}=t^{L(w)}
). Also, for
x,y\in W
,
x<y
denotes the Chevalley-Bruhat partial order.
The algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
is defined just using
t^{2}
, but, in practice, it is convenient to have its
square root
t
available.
7
7

7

It is well known that the algebra
\mathbb{Q}(t)\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}}{\mathcal{H}}
is a semisimple

\mathbb{Q}(t)
-algebra. See
[
CR81
, Cor. 68.12]
as well as Remark
3.3.4
(b) below.

We often use a different basis
\{\widetilde{T}_{w}\}
also indexed by
W
defined by
putting

(1.2.2)

\widetilde{T}_{w}:=t^{-L(w)}T_{w},\quad w\in W.

(Our notation here differs slightly from that in
[
Lus03
]
, where our
\widetilde{T}_{w}
is denoted
T_{w}
.)

The
bar operator

\bar{\ }:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
sending
t
to
t^{-1}
and
T_{w}
to
(T_{w^{-1}})^{-1}
is a ring involution on

{\mathcal{H}}
(sending
t
to
t^{-1})
, and

{\mathcal{H}}
admits a Kazhdan-Lusztig (or “canonical” in the
sense of
[
Lus90
]
; see also
[
DDPW08
, §0.5]
) basis
\{{\textsc{c}}_{w}\}_{w\in W}
. This basis is defined in
[
Lus03
, Chap.5]
using the formula

{\textsc{c}}_{w}=\sum_{y\leq w}p_{y,w}{\widetilde{T}}_{y}.

In addition to the condition
\overline{{\textsc{c}}}_{w}={\textsc{c}}_{w}
, for all
w
, the Laurent polynomials
p_{y,w}\in{\mathcal{Z}}
satisfy (1)
p_{y,w}=0
unless
y\leq w
, (2)
p_{w,w}=1
, and (3)
p_{y,w}\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{<0}:=t^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[t^{-1}]
, if
y<w
. The polynomials
p_{y,w}
are determined by these properties.
For example,

(1.2.3)

{\textsc{c}}_{s}=t_{s}^{-1}+\widetilde{T}_{s}=t_{s}^{-1}(1+T_{s}).

(Note that the element
{\textsc{c}}_{x}
is denoted
c_{x}

in
[
Lus03
]
.)

If
J\subseteq S
is non-empty, let
W_{J}:=\langle s\rangle_{s\in J}
, a parabolic subgroup of
W
; if
J=\emptyset
, set
W_{J}:=\{1\}
. Then,
W_{J}
has a unique
longest (reduced) word, denoted
w_{0,J}
, and

(1.2.4)

{\textsc{c}}_{w_{0,J}}=t^{-L(w_{0,J})}\sum_{y\in W_{J}}T_{y}=:t^{-L(w_{0,J})}x_{J}

(which defines
x_{J}
).
See
[
Lus03
, Cor. 12.2]
(or
[
DDPW08
, (7.2.5)]
in the split case). In the literature, the endomorphism algebra

(1.2.5)

A:=\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\left(\bigoplus_{J\subseteq S}x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}\right)

(or any algebra Morita equivalent to it) is called “the” generalized generic
q
-Schur algebra or a
generic Hecke endomorphism algebra
(or
generic Hecke endo-algebra
for short) by analogy with our naming of
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Various specializations of
A
and its extensions
will also be considered in later chapters.

Let
h_{x,y,z}\in{\mathcal{Z}}
denote the structure constants for
{\mathcal{H}}
, defined by the equations

(1.2.6)

{\textsc{c}}_{x}{\textsc{c}}_{y}=\sum_{z\in W}h_{x,y,z}{\textsc{c}}_{z}.

In particular, we have
[
Lus03
, Th. 6.6]

(1.2.7)

{\textsc{c}}_{s}{\textsc{c}}_{w}=\begin{cases}{\textsc{c}}_{sw}+\sum_{x:sx<x<w}\mu^{s}_{x,w}{\textsc{c}}_{x},&\text{ if }w<sw;\\
(t_{s}+t_{s}^{-1}){\textsc{c}}_{w},&\text{ if }w>sw.\end{cases}

Here
\mu^{s}_{x,w}\in{\mathcal{Z}}
is fixed under the bar operator. In the case of split groups,
\mu^{s}_{x,w}
is constant (in
\mathbb{Z}
). Also,
(
1.2.7
) implies that the
{\textsc{c}}_{s}
,
s\in S
, generate the
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
.

If
{\textsc{c}}_{y}
appears in
{\textsc{c}}_{s}{\textsc{c}}_{w}
with a nonzero coefficient, we set
y\leftarrow_{L}w
, following
[
Lus03
, 8.1]
. Define a
preorder
(that is, a relation which is reflexive and transitive) on
W
by setting,
for
w,w^{\prime}\in W
,
w\leq_{L}w^{\prime}
if there exists
w=w_{0},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}=w^{\prime}
in
W
such that
w_{i-1}\leftarrow_{R}w_{i}
, for all
i=1,2,\ldots,n
, and define
w\leq_{R}w^{\prime}
if
w^{-1}\leq_{L}(w^{\prime})^{-1}
.

We now consider the preorders
\leq_{L}
and
\leq_{R}
defined on

W
, and the preorder
\leq_{LR}
generated by
\leq_{L}
and
\leq_{R}
and their associated
quasi-poset

(W,\leq_{L})
etc. See, e.g.,
[
Lus03
, Ch. 8]
. Let
\sim_{L},\sim_{R}
and

\sim_{LR}
be the equivalence relations associated with
\leq_{L},\leq_{R}
, and

\leq_{LR}
, respectively. The equivalence classes for
\sim_{L},\sim_{R}
, and

\sim_{LR}
are called the
left, right
, and
two-sided cells
, respectively.

For a quasi-poset, there is an associated poset (see Section B2 in Appendix B).
The poset associated with the quasi-poset
(W,\leq_{L})
is the set
\Omega
of all left cells, while the poset associated with the quasi-poset
(\Omega,\leq_{LR})
is the set
\overline{\Omega}
of all two-sided cells. Note that if a left cell
\omega\in\Omega
contains an element
w_{0,J}
, then
J
is a uniquely determined
subset of
S
. See
[
Lus03
, Lem. 8.6]
and the argument above Lemma
3.1.2
in this monograph.

We record that

(1.2.8)

h_{x,y,z}\neq 0\implies z\leq_{L}y,z\leq_{R}x.

See
[
Lus03
, 13.1(d)]
. In particular, for any
w\in W
,

(1.2.9)

{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{L}w}:=\sum_{y\in W\atop y\leq_{L}w}{\mathcal{Z}}{\textsc{c}}_{y},\;\;{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{R}w}:=\sum_{y\in W\atop y\leq_{R}w}{\mathcal{Z}}{\textsc{c}}_{y},\;\;{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{LR}w}:=\sum_{y\in W\atop y\leq_{LR}w}{\mathcal{Z}}{\textsc{c}}_{y}

is a left, right, or two-sided ideal of
{\mathcal{H}}
, respectively (see
[
Lus03
, Lem. 8.2]
.). We may also define similar ideals by using
<_{L},<_{R},<_{LR}
.

Each left cell
\omega\in\Omega
defines a
left-cell module

S(\omega):={\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{L}\omega}/{\mathcal{H}}_{<_{L}\omega}
—it is explicitly defined in
[
Lus03
, §8.3]
(but with a different notation). Here
{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{L}\omega}={\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{L}y}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{<_{L}\omega}={\mathcal{H}}_{<_{L}y}
, for any
y\in\omega
. Similarly, we may define right- or two-sided-cell modules. Also, there are base-change versions associated with the Hecke alegbra
{\mathscr{K}}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}{\mathcal{H}}
and the basis
\{1\otimes{\textsc{c}}_{w}\}_{w\in W}
over any commutative ring
{\mathscr{K}}
which is a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module.

In addition, there is a
corresponding

(1.2.10)

dual left-cell module
S_{\omega}:=S(\omega)^{*}=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(S(\omega),{\mathcal{Z}})
.

It is a
right

{\mathcal{H}}
-module. Both
S(\omega)
and
S_{\omega}
are free
{\mathcal{Z}}
-modules; see
[
Lus03
, §8.3]
.

Remark 1.2.1
.

If
{\mathcal{H}}
is the generic Hecke algebra for a symmetric group
W=\mathfrak{S}_{r}
,
then it can be proved that the dual left-cell module
S_{\omega}
is a “classical” Specht module for
{\mathcal{H}}
, as defined more generally by Dipper-James; see
[
DJ86
, p 34]
, taking
R
in the latter reference to be the ring
\mathbb{Z}
of integers. On the other hand, the right cell
\gamma:=\omega^{-1}
, defines a right-cell module

K_{\gamma}
, which is related to
S_{\omega}
by the isomorphism
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{H}}S_{\omega}\cong K_{\gamma}
,

\gamma=\omega^{-1}
. (Here,
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{H}}
is the contravariant duality functor on mod-
{\mathcal{H}}
defined in

[
DPS98c
, (1.2)]
.) We will not make use of any of the theory of Specht modules in this monograph.

The preorders
\leq_{L},\leq_{LR}
on
W
induce partial orders
\leq_{L},\leq_{R}
on
\Omega
. Thus, for
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
and
X\in\{L,LR\}
,
\omega\leq_{X}\omega^{\prime}\iff x\leq_{X}x^{\prime}
, for some
x\in\omega,x^{\prime}\in\omega^{\prime}.

Also,
\omega\in\Omega
defines a unique two-sided cell
\bar{\omega}
with the property that
\omega\subseteq\bar{\omega}
. It is extremely important in our context here (see the second paragraph of Section 1.2 or Proposition
B.5
in Appendix B) that, given
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega
,

(1.2.11)

\omega_{1}<_{L}\omega_{2}\implies\overline{\omega}_{1}<_{LR}\overline{\omega}_{2}.

Thus,
\leq_{L}
strictly dominates
\leq_{LR}
in the sense of Definition
B.3
in Appendix B.
As discussed at the beginning of the section, this property holds when
(W,S,L)
is standard finite, our standing assumption.

For each two-sided cell
\mathbf{c}
of
W
, there is an
{\mathcal{H}}
-bimodule
M(\mathbf{c})
.
The two-sided cell decomposition of
W
induces a decomposition of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
into two-sided ideals
M(\mathbf{c})_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
and, hence, a decomposition of
\Lambda=\text{Irr}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)})
.
Further, the partial order
\leq_{LR}
on the set
\bar{\Omega}
of two-sided cells induces a preorder on
\Lambda
by setting, for
E,E^{\prime}\in\Lambda
,

(1.2.12)

E\leq_{LR}E^{\prime}\text{ if }\mathbf{c}_{E}\leq_{LR}\mathbf{c}_{E^{\prime}}.

Here,
\mathbf{c}_{E}
denotes the two-sided cell
\mathbf{c}
such that
E|M(\mathbf{c})_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}

in the sense that
E
is a direct summand of the left
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
-module defined by
M(\mathbf{c})_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
.

We end this section with a brief discussion about the origin of the generic Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
associated with a standard finite Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
. Here
W=\mathbf{W}^{F}
is the Weyl group of a finite group
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
of Lie type that has
a Borel subgroup
B={\mathbb{B}}^{F}
; see Section 1.1.
The following result, which will not be used until Section 5.2, was first discovered by Iwahori
[
Iwa64
]
for (split) Chevalley groups.

Lemma 1.2.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}^{o}
be the subalgebra over
\mathbb{Z}[t^{2}]
generated by all
T_{s}\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
s\in S
.
8
8

8

Thus,
{\mathcal{H}}^{o}
is a form for
{\mathcal{H}}

over
\mathbb{Z}[t^{2}]
.
Suppose
{\mathcal{O}}
is a commutative ring and
q\in{\mathcal{O}}
, and view
{\mathcal{O}}
as a
\mathbb{Z}[t^{2}]
-algebra by the homomorphism

\mathbb{Z}[t^{2}]\to{\mathcal{O}}
sending
t^{2}\mapsto q
.
Then,

{\mathcal{H}}^{o}_{{\mathcal{O}}}\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{O}}G}({\mathcal{O}}G/B),

where
{\mathcal{O}}G/B
is the permutation module for the action of
G
on the set of left cosets of
B
.

Proof.

A proof of display (
1.1.1
) is outlined in
[
C85
, p.74]
, beginning with a general root group cardinality formula

|X_{\Psi}^{F}|=q^{|\Psi|},

where
\Psi
is a certain subset of the positive roots
\Phi^{+}
of
{\mathbb{G}}
. For each fundamental reflection
s
in the Weyl group
W
of
G
, one of the root groups
X^{F}_{\Psi}
can be seen, using
[
C85
, Prop. 2.5.11]
,
to satisfy
|B|=|X^{F}_{\Psi}(B\cap B^{s})|=|X_{\Psi}^{F}|\cdot|B\cap B^{s}|
. The set
\Psi
here is the set of positive roots in
\Phi
made negative by the action of
s
. Thus,
|\Psi|=L(s)
for this
\Psi
, where
L(s)
is the length of
s
in the Weyl group
\mathbf{W}
of
{\mathbb{G}}
; see Section 1.1. Thus, we obtain the expression (
1.1.1
) above from the expression

q^{L(s)}=q^{|\Psi|}=|B/B\cap B^{s}|=[B:B\cap B^{s}].

(Carter actually gives in
[
C85
, p. 74]
an analogous formula for
q^{L(w)}
, for any
w
in the Weyl group
of
{\mathbb{G}}
.)

As in
[
CPS73
, p. 188]
, the elements

(1.2.13)

\frac{1}{|B|}{\underline{BwB}},\quad w\in W

span a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
H
, well known to be the endomorphism algebra of the permutation module
\mathbb{Z}G/B
for the action of
G
on coset space
G/B
. This algebra base changes well, so that we can replace
\mathbb{Z}
by
{\mathcal{O}}
, obtaining a basis for
\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{O}}G}({\mathcal{O}}G/B)
. See, e.g.,
[
DDPW08
, Exercise 9.3]
. In
[
CPS73
, fn. 31]
, a multiplication relation is showed to be analogous to the right-hand version of the relation
T_{s}T_{w}=T_{sw}
in (
1.2.1
), when
\ell(sw)=1+\ell(w)
. Here, we think of
\frac{1}{|B|}{\underline{BwB}}
as analogous to

T_{w}
in (
1.2.1
). There is also the “quadratic relation”

\left(\frac{1}{|B|}{\underline{BsB}}\right)^{2}=a\frac{1}{|B|}{\underline{BsB}}+b1

with
b=[B:B\cap B^{s}]
and
a=b-1
. Such a relation holds in any finite doubly transitive group, such as
\langle B,s\rangle

acting on the coset space
\langle B,s\rangle/B
.

Hence
b=q^{L(s)}
(and
a=b-1
). The two types of relations are analogous to (
1.2.1
) with
w=s
, replacing the
t_{s}^{2}
there with
q^{L(s)}
. The two types of relations we have considered clearly form special cases of (
1.2.1
), sufficient to provide generators and relations for
{\mathcal{H}}^{o}_{\mathcal{O}}
. Similarly, we have obtained from
\text{\rm{End}}_{G}({\mathcal{O}}G/B)
analogs with enough information to do all
multiplications of the basis elements
\frac{1}{|B|}{\underline{BwB}}
.
∎

1.3.
Exact categories and stratified algebras

Beginning in this section, considerable use is made of exact categories
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
as formulated in Keller
[
K90
, Appendix A]
and revisited in Bühler’s exposition
[
Bu10
]
. The axioms given in these papers are equivalent to (but shorter than) the
original axioms of Quillen
[
Q73
]
. A version
9
9

9

There are several different versions of exact categories in the literature. In particular, following Quillen, we do not assume any kind of idempotent splitting.
is stated in
[
DPS17
, Appendix]
which follows
[
K90
, Appendix A]
quite closely (as quoted in
[
DRSS99
, Appendix A, Set 2]
written by Keller). Briefly,
{\mathscr{A}}
is an additive category, while
{\mathscr{E}}
consists of certain pairs
(i,d)
of composable morphisms
i:X\to Y

and
d:Y\to Z
in
{\mathscr{A}}
, such that
i
is the kernel of
d
and
d
is the cokernel of
i
. The pairs

(i,d)
which belong to
{\mathscr{E}}
are required to satisfy certain axioms; see explicitly

[
K90
, Appendix A]
,
[
DRSS99
, Appendix A, Set 2]
,
[
Bu10
, Def. 2.1]
, or
[
DPS17
, Appendix A]
. As in
[
K90
]
, we sometimes refer to
i
as an

inflation
,
d
as a
deflation
, and the pair
(i,d)
as a
conflation
. Conflations
(i,d)
are usually denoted
by
(X\to Y\to Z)
.
10
10

10

Such a sequence is denoted by
X\rightarrowtail Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
in
[
Bu10
]
. There are also other notations in the literature used with the same meaning.
As in
[
Bu10
]
, we sometimes call conflations “short exact sequences” in
{\mathscr{E}}
. When
it is convenient and clear from context,
(X\to Y\to Z)
is denoted
0\to X\to Y\to Z\to 0
and called a short
exact sequence (in
{\mathscr{E}}
).

Notice that
{\mathscr{E}}
is itself an additive category, using the usual mappings between
the categorical diagrams formed by composable pairs of morphisms in
{\mathscr{A}}
; see
[
Bu10
, Cor. 2.10]
. As such,
{\mathscr{E}}
is required to be closed
under isomorphisms as part of the exactness axioms.

One can consider a pair
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
consisting of an additive category
{\mathscr{A}}
together with a
collection
{\mathscr{E}}
of composable morphisms
(i,d)
in
{\mathscr{A}}
and ask if it defines an exact category. Of course, one needs
any
(i,d)
in
{\mathscr{E}}
to be a kernel-cokernel pair (in the terminology of
[
Bu10
]
). Expanding on
[
DPS17
]
,
Chapters 2 and 3 present some important examples of exact categories
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
inspired by Hecke algebra
representation theory, especially involving the construction and control of endomorphism rings, as in Theorem 3.3.1 below.
Often
{\mathscr{A}}
is a full subcategory of an additive category
{\mathscr{B}}
, associated to
an already introduced exact category
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
. The theme of constructing new exact categories from old ones is continued in Sections 2.2 and 2.3 using duality.

Finally, for an exact category
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
, we can consider the abelian group
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}}(X,Y)
for any
pair of objects
X,Y\in{\mathscr{A}}
. In case
{\mathscr{A}}
is an additive
{\mathscr{K}}
-category for a commutative ring
{\mathscr{K}}
,
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{\mathscr{E}}(X,Y)

carries a natural structure as a
{\mathscr{K}}
-module; see
[
DPS17
, Prop. A.2]
. The action of
a\in{\mathscr{K}}
on
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}}(X,Y)
is given by either pull-back of multiplication by
a
on
X
or (equally, as it turns out) by push-out of multiplication of
a
on
Y
.

1.3A. Exact subcategories: some terminology and a new strategy.

A note on exact category terminology: Let

({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
and
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
be exact categories. Suppose
{\mathscr{A}}
is a subcategory of
{\mathscr{B}}
, and
{\mathscr{E}}\subseteq{\mathscr{F}}
, viewing
{\mathscr{E}}
and
{\mathscr{F}}
as classes of composable pairs of morphisms in
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
, respectively.
We say that
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
is an
exact subcategory
11
11

11

Some authors note that the set
{\mathscr{E}}
has a natural
category structure, using “morphisms” constructed
from one short exact sequence to another via
commutative diagrams. This does not, however,
affect our definition of an exact subcategory.
of
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
. It is a
full exact subcategory
if
{\mathscr{A}}

is also a full subcategory of
{\mathscr{B}}
—that is,
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{A}}}(A_{1},A_{2})=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(A_{1},A_{2})
whenever
A_{1},A_{2}
are objects in
{\mathscr{A}}
.

With this terminology in hand, we can now describe our new approach and some of its motivation.

Strategy 1.3.1
.

If
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
is a full exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
, then the fullness implies that the inclusion
{\mathscr{E}}\subseteq{\mathscr{F}}
induces an inclusion, i.e., an injective map,
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}}(A,A^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{F}}}(A,A^{\prime})
for each
pair
(A,A^{\prime})
of objects in
{\mathscr{A}}
.
12
12

12

The members of exact category
\text{Ext}^{1}
groups are defined as equivalence classes of short exact sequences under a well-known equivalence relation. See
[
DPS17
, p.249]
. In the presence of
fullness, each equivalence class in
{\mathscr{F}}
intersects at most one of the
{\mathscr{E}}
equivalence classes, resulting in the inclusions of this sentence.
We take this opportunity to note that our notion of “full exact subcategory” is quite different from the notion “fully exact subcategory”
in
[
Bu10
, Def. 10.21]
, apparently aimed in a different direction.
The inclusion is even
{\mathscr{K}}
-linear if
{\mathscr{K}}
is a commutative Noetherian ring and the inclusion
{\mathscr{A}}\subseteq{\mathscr{B}}
is also an inclusion of
{\mathscr{K}}
-categories (with
{\mathscr{A}}
full in
{\mathscr{B}}
as before.) See
[
DPS17
, Prop. A.2]
.

As in traditional homological algebra, the vanishing of a group
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{F}}}(A_{1},A_{2})
leads to the exactness
of
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{F}}}(A_{1},-)
when applied to any given short exact sequence
\gamma
in
{\mathscr{F}}
that begins with
A_{2}
. Assume this exactness is desirable for some set
\Gamma
of such
\gamma
’s, but the proposed
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}
vanishing is too much to ask for. In that case, we can try to build a full exact subcategory
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
as above, but with
\Gamma\subseteq{\mathscr{E}}
and
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{\operatorname{{\mathscr{E}}}}(A_{1},A_{2})=0
. This gives the desired exactness.

Though the discussion above is only an outline, we are able to use it when constructing stratifying systems, as we now show.

1.3B. Stratifying Systems.
Let
{\mathscr{K}}
be a Noetherian commutative ring. Let
B
be an algebra which
is finite (= finitely generated) and projective as a
{\mathscr{K}}
-module.
Let
\Lambda
be a quasi-poset, i.e., a finite set
\Lambda
with a preorder, denoted

\leq
here. The associated poset is
\bar{\Lambda},
and
\bar{\lambda}
is the element of
\bar{\Lambda}
associated to any given
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda
, assume that there is given both a
B
-module
\Delta(\lambda)
, which is finite and projective over
{\mathscr{K}}
, and a finite and projective
B
-module
P(\lambda)
, together with an epimorphism
P(\lambda)\twoheadrightarrow\Delta(\lambda)
. The following
conditions are assumed to hold:

(SS1)

For
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
,

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B}(P(\lambda),\Delta(\mu))\not=0\implies\lambda\leq\mu.

(SS2)

Every irreducible
B
-module is a homomorphic image of some
\Delta(\lambda)
.

(SS3)

For
\lambda\in\Lambda
, the
B
-module
P(\lambda)
has a finite filtration by
B
-submodules
with top section
\Delta(\lambda)
and other sections of the form
\Delta(\mu)
with
\bar{\mu}>\bar{\lambda}
.

When these conditions hold, the data

(1.3.1)

\{\Delta(\lambda),P(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}

form (by definition) a
stratifying system
13
13

13

Following
[
DPS17
]
, we drop the word “strict” in “strict stratifying system” as used in

[
DPS98a
]
. I.e., all stratifying systems in this monograph are strict in the sense of
[
DPS98a
]
, thus, we insist on
the strict comparison
\bar{\mu}>\bar{\lambda}
in
(SS3).

for the category
B\mbox{--mod}
of finite
B
-modules.
Note that if (
1.3.1
) is a stratifying system for
B\mbox{--mod}
, then
\{\Delta(\lambda)_{\mathscr{K}^{\prime}},P(\lambda)_{\mathscr{K}^{\prime}}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
is a
stratifying system for
B_{\mathscr{K}^{\prime}}
-mod
for any base change
\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
, provided
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
is a Noetherian commutative
ring. (See
[
DPS15
, p. 231]
. For more details and further results, see
[
DPS98a
, Lem. 1.2.5]
.)

Remark 1.3.2
.

If
B
has a stratifying system as explained above, it has a “standard stratification” by idempotent ideals

0\subseteq J_{0}\subseteq J_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{n}=B
much like a defining sequence in the
theory of quasi-hereditary algebras. Thus,
B
is a kind of generalization of a quasi-hereditary algebra, or, equivalently,
if
B
-mod has a stratifying system, then
B
-mod is a generalization of a highest weight category. We call
B
a
standardly stratified algebra
; see Section 3.5 for more details.
We do not pursue this further here, but refer to

[
DPS98a
, §1.2]
and
[
DPS18
]
for a more complete discussion.

1.3C. Exact categories and the stratification hypothesis.
Stratifying
systems can be constructed in an endomorphism-ring setting. Originally, the construction was based on assuming a “stratification hypothesis” in
[
DPS98a
, Hyp. 1.2.9, Th. 1.2.10]
, which
required a difficult
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}
-vanishing condition (see
[
DPS98a
, Th. 2.3.9, Th. 2.4.4]
). This problem was
partly circumvented in
[
DPS17
, Hyp. 2.5 & Th. 2.6]
by introducing “designer” exact categories which had, a priori, smaller
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}
-groups, and thus were more likely to vanish. However, the new version was somewhat awkward to use in the Quillen axiom system. See
[
DPS17
, Rem. 2.7]
. The updated version below, given in the Stratification Hypothesis
1.3.3
, removes this issue,
by replacing all
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}
-vanishings
with existence assertions for needed short exact sequences. As noted in Remark
1.3.4
, these assertions are at least implied by a natural exact category
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}
-vanishing condition. This is the way they are verified in this monograph.

Let
R
be a finite and projective
{\mathscr{K}}
-algebra. Our aim will be to provide ingredients in mod-
R
, including a
right
R
-module
T
with suitable filtrations, which enable the construction of a stratifying system for
B\mbox{--mod}
,
where
B:=\text{\rm{End}}_{R}(T)
. Clearly,
T
is naturally a left
B
-module.

Construction.
We will construct
T
as an object in
a full subcategory
{\mathscr{A}}
of mod-
R
, with the following assumptions. First,
the subcategory
{\mathscr{A}}
is part of an exact category
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
. Second, the exact sequences
(X\to Y\to Z)\in\mathscr{E}
are among the short exact sequences
0\to X\to Y\to Z\to 0
in mod-
R
. Thus,

({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
is a full exact subcategory of mod-
R
. (In our terminology, “full exact” is just an abbreviation for
“full and exact.”)
Third, we have a collection of objects
S_{\lambda},T_{\lambda}\in{\mathscr{A}}
indexed by the elements
\lambda
of a finite quasi-poset

\Lambda
, where
T_{\lambda}
is a finite and projective
{\mathscr{K}}
-module.
14
14

14

This assumption is not often used
directly, but can be a step toward the required projectivity over
{\mathscr{K}}
of the modules
\Delta(\lambda)
; see
Remark
1.3.6
(b). The assumption for
T

often comes for free, since the
S_{\nu}
are typically projective (or even free)
{\mathscr{K}}
-modules and each
T_{\lambda}
is constructed to have a filtration with each section a direct sum of various
S_{\nu}
.
For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
S_{\lambda}
is a subobject of
T_{\lambda}
, with
the inclusion
S_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow T_{\lambda}
an inflation in
{\mathscr{E}}
.
For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, fix a positive integer
m_{\lambda}
. Define

(1.3.2)

T:=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}T_{\lambda}^{\oplus m_{\lambda}}.

With this notation, we can now state the following:

Stratification Hypothesis 1.3.3
.

Assume the set-up introduced in the previous paragraph. In particular,
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
is an exact category, a full exact subcategory of mod-
R
, and
T\in{\mathscr{A}}
with
B=\text{\rm{End}}_{R}(T)=\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathscr{A}}(T)
. There are also indexed objects
S_{\lambda},T_{\lambda}\,(\lambda\in\Lambda)
, and the following statements are required as part of the hypothesis:

(1)

For
\lambda\in\Lambda
, there is an increasing filtration of
T_{\lambda}
:

0=T^{-1}_{\lambda}\subseteq T^{0}_{\lambda}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq T^{\ell(\lambda)}_{\lambda}=T_{\lambda}

in which each inclusion
T^{i-1}_{\lambda}\subseteq T_{\lambda}^{i}
defines a conflation

(1.3.3)

(T^{i-1}_{\lambda}\longrightarrow T_{\lambda}^{i}\longrightarrow T_{\lambda}^{i}/T_{\lambda}^{i-1})\in{\mathscr{E}}.

In addition,
T^{0}_{\lambda}\cong S_{\lambda}
, and, for
i>0
,
the ‘‘section”
15
15

15

Observe that
T^{i}_{\lambda}\to T^{i}_{\lambda}/T^{i-1}_{\lambda}
is the unique (up to isomorphism) cokernel in
{\mathscr{A}}
of the inflation

T^{i-1}_{\lambda}\to T^{i}_{\lambda}
and, thus, is a deflation. We generally leave such exact category exercises to the reader.

T^{i}_{\lambda}/T^{i-1}_{\lambda}
is a direct sum of various
S_{\mu}
,
\mu\in\Lambda
and
\bar{\mu}>\bar{\lambda}
(repetitions allowed).

(2)

For
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
,
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(S_{\mu},T_{\lambda})\not=0\implies\lambda\leq\mu.

(3)

For all
\lambda\in\Lambda
and integers
i\geq 0
, applying the contravariant ‘‘diamond’’ functor
16
16

16

This functor will also be used later in the book.

(-)^{\diamond}:=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{R}(-,T)
to (
1.3.3
) gives the exact sequence

0\to(T_{\lambda}^{i}/T_{\lambda}^{i-1})^{\diamond}\longrightarrow(T^{i}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}\longrightarrow(T^{i-1}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}\to 0

of
B
-modules.

Remark 1.3.4
.

Notice that condition (3)
is implied by the vanishing of

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{\mathscr{E}}(S_{\lambda},T)
, for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
; see
[
DPS17
, Prop. A.2]
. This is the way that we will use the (updated) Stratification Hypothesis
1.3.3
in this monograph.
We mention, without proof, that it is implied by the less flexible earlier
[
DPS17
, Hyp. 2.5]
, which was modeled
closely on
[
DPS98a
, Hyp. 1.2.9]
and which is harder to check, as written, in the Quillen exact category setting.

The following theorem updates
[
DPS98a
, Th. 1.3.3]
and
[
DPS17
, Th. 2.6]
.

Theorem 1.3.5
.

Assume that the Stratification Hypothesis
1.3.3
holds.
For
\lambda\in\Lambda
, put

(1.3.4)

\begin{cases}\Delta(\lambda):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(S_{\lambda},T)=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{R}(S_{\lambda},T)\in B\mbox{--mod},\\
P(\lambda):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{A}}(T_{\lambda},T)=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{R}(T_{\lambda},T)\in B\mbox{--mod}.\end{cases}

If each
\Delta(\lambda)
is projective over
{\mathscr{K}}
, then
\{\Delta(\lambda),P(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
is a
stratifying system for
B\mbox{--mod}
.

Proof.

First, we check (SS1). Observe that the contravariant functor
(-)^{\diamond}
provides maps

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{R}(S_{\mu},T_{\lambda})\longrightarrow\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B}(P(\lambda),\Delta(\mu)).

Passing to
T=\bigoplus_{{\lambda}\in\Lambda}T_{\lambda}^{\oplus m_{\lambda}}
on the left and
{}_{B}B=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}P(\lambda)^{\oplus m_{\lambda}}
on the right, we obtain an isomorphism

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{R}(S_{\mu},T)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B}({{}_{B}B},\Delta(\mu))\cong\Delta(\mu).

Consequently, the original maps must also be isomorphisms. Thus, (SS1) follows from Condition (2) (of Hypothesis
1.3.3
).

Next, we will prove (SS2) assuming (SS3) has been proved. By the construction of
B=\text{\rm{End}}_{R}(T)
, we have that
{}_{B}B\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}P(\lambda)^{\oplus m_{\lambda}}
. Using (SS3),
{}_{B}B
has a filtration with sections various

\Delta(\nu)
,
\nu\in\Lambda
. Any irreducible
B
-module is, of course, a homomorphic image of
{}_{B}B
, and so
(using irreducibility) a homomorphic image of some
\Delta(\nu)
. This proves (SS2) in the presence of (SS3).

Now consider (SS3) itself for a given
\lambda
. Let
n
be an integer such that
T^{n}_{\lambda}=T_{\lambda}
. Let
j\geq i\geq-1
be integers. Thus,
T^{i}_{\lambda}
is a submodule of
T^{j}_{\lambda}
.

Claim.
The natural map
(T^{j}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}\to(T^{i}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}
is a surjection.

To see this, observe that the inclusion
T^{i}_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow T^{j}_{\lambda}
factors into a composition of inclusions
T^{i}_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow T^{i+1}_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow T^{i+2}_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow T^{j}_{\lambda}
. By Condition (3), for
i\leq a<j
, the inclusion
T^{a}_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow T^{a+1}_{\lambda}
becomes
a surjection

(T^{a+1}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}\twoheadrightarrow(T^{a}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}
upon applying
(-)^{\diamond}
. The Claim follows by taking compositions.

We return to the proof of (SS3) for a given
\lambda
.
Let
n\geq j\geq 0
. The maps

(T^{n}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}\to(T^{j}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}
and
(T^{n}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}\to(T^{j-1}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}
are both surjective by the Claim.
This gives the two exact sequences forming the rows of the natural commutative diagram

(1.3.5)

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>(T^{n}_{\lambda}/T^{j}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}@>{}>{}>(T^{n}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}@>{}>{}>(T^{j}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}@>{}>{}>0\\
@V{\alpha}V{}V\Big\|@V{\beta}V{}V\\
0@>{}>{}>(T^{n}_{\lambda}/T^{j-1}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}@>{}>{}>(T^{n}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}@>{}>{}>(T^{j-1}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}@>{}>{}>0\\
\end{CD}

Take a sufficiently large integer
n
, so that
T^{n}_{\lambda}=T_{\lambda}
. Then,
P(\lambda)=T^{\diamond}_{\lambda}
has a filtration by submodules of the form
(T_{\lambda}/T^{j}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}
, and the map
\alpha
above is an inclusion between adjacent filtration terms. The Snake Lemma shows that the section
{\text{\rm Coker}}(\alpha)
of these two filtration terms is isomorphic to

{\text{\rm Ker}}(\beta)=(T^{j}_{\lambda}/T^{j-1}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}
. Thus,
P(\lambda)
has a filtration with sections isomorphic to various

(T^{j}_{\lambda}/T^{j-1}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}
. The module
(T^{j}_{\lambda}/T^{j-1}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}
is of the form
S_{\nu}^{\diamond}=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{R}(S_{\nu},T)=\Delta(\nu)
, where
\nu>\lambda
if
j>0
, and
\nu=\lambda
if
j=0
. In the
j=0
case,
{\text{\rm Coker}}(\alpha)

is the “top” section of the given filtration on
P(\lambda)
, and it is isomorphic to
{\text{\rm Ker}}(\beta)=(T^{0}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}=S_{\lambda}^{\diamond}=\Delta({\lambda})
. This completes the proof of (SS3).

Finally, because it is assumed that each
\Delta(\nu)
,
\nu\in\Lambda
, is a projective
{\mathscr{K}}
-module, it follows that each
P(\lambda)
,
\lambda\in\Lambda
, is also a projective
{\mathscr{K}}
-module. Since
B
is a direct sum of
the
P(\lambda)
(repetitions allowed),
B
is a projective
{\mathscr{K}}
-module. This completes the proof.
∎

Remarks 1.3.6
.

(a) Correspondingly,
{\mathscr{A}}
can be one of the categories
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
in Section 2.3 below (or a full
subcategory,
such as
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
there.) A similar remark holds for the corresponding exact
category in Section 2.2; however, the notation above is chosen to be compatible with the applications in Section 2.4 as developed in
Section 2.3.

(b) If
{\mathscr{K}}
is a regular ring of Krull dimension at most 2, then, in the statement of Theorem
1.3.5
,
the sentence “Assume that each
\Delta(\lambda)
is projective over
{\mathscr{K}}
” can be omitted, since
\Delta(\lambda)
is automatically projective over
{\mathcal{Z}}
. This is shown in the proof of
[
DPS98a
, Cor. 1.2.12)]
,
using earlier work of Auslander-Goldman together with the projectivity of
T
as a
{\mathscr{K}}
-module (assumed in the preamble to the Stratification Hypothesis
1.3.3
). For a detailed account, see
[
DDPW08
, §C.3]
. A similar result holds
if the stratifying system is “defined over
Z
” where
Z
is a regular ring of Krull dimension
\leq 2
. This means
that the stratifying system over
{\mathscr{K}}
is obtained by base change from the regular ring
Z
.

(c) As an example in (b), we will be mainly interested in the special case in which
R={\mathcal{H}}
is the Hecke algebra defined over
{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]
(and its localizations
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
at multiplicative subsets, not containing 0, of
\mathbb{Z}
). Since
{\mathcal{Z}}
is a regular ring of dimension 2, (b) above applies to show that each
\Delta(\lambda)
is projective over
{\mathcal{Z}}
. In fact, by a result of Swan
[
Sw78
, p.111, first paragraph]
,
\Delta(\lambda)
is then

{\mathcal{Z}}
-free. (Swan’s paragraph also implies that any finite, projective
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-module is free. In addition, if

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\oplus m}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\oplus n}
for integers
m,n
, then
m=n
.)

Chapter 2
Some exact categories

Section 1.3 in Chapter 1 provides a general theory of stratifying endomorphism algebras using exact categories, reformulating that developed in
[
DPS17
]
. To apply the theory in the context of the conjecture
[
DPS98a
, Conj. 2.5.2]
by the authors, we need to construct a nested sequence of exact categories involving Hecke algebra modules with cell module filtrations. We eventually prove the Ext
1
vanishing condition (see Remark
1.3.4
) in one of the exact categories and, then, prove the (slightly modified) conjecture in the next chapter.

2.1.
Three exact categories

In this section, we work exclusively with left modules for an algebra.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be the generic Hecke algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}
, associated to a standard finite Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
(as defined in Section 1.1).
