We begin with the following variation on
[
DPS15
, Lem. 4.3]
. If
V
is a left
{\mathcal{H}}
-module, the “fixed-point module” for the action of
{\mathcal{H}}
on
V
is defined to be

V^{\mathcal{H}}:=\{v\in V\,|\,{\textsc{c}}_{s}v=(t_{s}+t_{s}^{-1})v,\quad\forall s\in S\}\in{\mathcal{Z}}{\text{\rm--mod}},

where
t_{s},{\textsc{c}}_{s}
are defined in (
1.2.1
) and (
1.2.3
), respectively.
Equivalently,

v\in V^{\mathcal{H}}\iff T_{s}v=t_{s}^{2}v,\text{ for all }s\in S.

The assignment
V\mapsto V^{\mathcal{H}}
is part of an evident left-exact endo-functor.
Similarly,
V^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}
is defined for any parabolic subalgebra
{\mathcal{H}}_{J}
of
{\mathcal{H}}
(
J\subseteq S
).
The next result follows from
[
DPS15
, Lem.4.3]
and the above definition
of fixed points, using (
1.2.1
) and (
1.2.3
).

Lemma 2.1.1
.

Let
{\mathfrak{I}\subseteq\mathfrak{K}}
be left ideals in the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
over
{\mathcal{Z}}
. Assume that
both
\mathfrak{I}
and
\mathfrak{K}
are spanned by the Kazhdan-Lusztig basis elements
{\textsc{c}}_{x}
,
x\in W
, that they contain.
Then, for any
J\subseteq S
, the natural map

(2.1.1)

{\mathfrak{K}}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\longrightarrow({\mathfrak{K}}/{\mathfrak{J}})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}

is surjective.

Notation 2.1.2
.

At this point, the reader should review the notion of height functions
\mathfrak{h}

discussed in Appendix B below.
In this section, we fix

\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{l}:\Omega\to\mathbb{Z},

a function defined on the set
\Omega
of
left cells in
W,
and compatible with the preorder
\leq_{LR}
; see display (
B.2
) in Appendix B for the meaning of “compatible.”
For another example, see the order defined in display (
3.1.1
). The subscript
l
is there to remind the reader of our left-module context.
Such a height function

\mathfrak{h}
is also compatible with
\leq_{L}
, as formalized
in
Corollary
B.8
(which uses property (
1.2.11
) via Proposition
B.5
). In particular,
\mathfrak{h}
is constant on left cells which belong to the same two-sided cell.
It is also observed in Remark
B.9
that
\mathfrak{h}
may be viewed as assigning heights to left-cell modules
S(\omega)
. Thus, if

\omega\in\Omega
,
\mathfrak{h}
assigns the left-cell module
S(\omega)
height
\mathfrak{h}(\omega)
.
Also, the value
\mathfrak{h}(\omega)=\mathfrak{h}(\bar{\omega})
, where
\bar{\omega}
is the two-sided cell containing
\omega
, may be assigned
without ambiguity as a “height” to the irreducible constituents of the base-changed module
S(\omega)_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
. In particular, this means that
\mathfrak{h}
uniquely determines a height function on the set
\Lambda
of irreducible
modules for the semisimple algebra
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
. Since this height function also determines the original

\mathfrak{h}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
(see Remark
B.9
), we continue to denote it by
\mathfrak{h}
. As explained in Example
B.1
,
\mathfrak{h}

induces a canonical decreasing filtration

(2.1.2)

M=M_{0}\supseteq M_{1}\supseteq M_{2}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq M_{n-1}\supseteq M_{n}=0

on each
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
M
. We call this filtration the
\mathfrak{h}
-
filtration
or the
height filtration,
when
\mathfrak{h}
is understood on
M
. Similar terminology can be used for right modules in the next three sections, although the height filtrations are increasing and the subscripts here become superscripts there.

Finally, as a convenience, we
assume in this section (unless noted otherwise) that
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{l}:\Omega\to\mathbb{Z}
only takes negative values. See Appendix B. Later, we make use of
\mathfrak{h}_{r}:=-\mathfrak{h}_{l}:\Omega\to\mathbb{Z}
, which is, in particular, used to assign positive height values to dual left-cell modules

S_{\omega}=S(\omega)^{*}=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(S(\omega),{\mathcal{Z}}),\;\;\omega\in\Omega
(see (
1.2.10
)) which are naturally
right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules. See Remark B.8.

Relative to such a height function
\mathfrak{h}:\Omega\to\mathbb{Z}
that induces
\mathfrak{h}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
as above, there is an exact category
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
, as constructed in Example
B.1
. Thus,
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
consists of the full subcategory
{\mathscr{A}}={\mathscr{A}}_{l}

of
{\mathcal{H}}
-mod whose objects are left
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules, finite and torsion-free over
{\mathcal{Z}}
, and the (full) “subcategory”
{\mathscr{E}}={\mathscr{E}}_{l}
(in the sense of footnote
11
in Chapter 1) of short exact sequences

0\to M^{\prime}\to M\to M^{\prime\prime}\to 0
in
{\mathscr{A}}
satisfying the condition that
0\to M^{\prime}_{h}\to M_{h}\to M^{\prime\prime}_{h}\to 0
is exact, for all heights
h
.
(Compare the right-module version given in
[
DPS17
, Constr. 3.5]
or Theorem
2.2.1
below.)

Below, building on
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})=({\mathscr{A}}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}_{l})
, we present three more examples of exact categories:

{\mathcal{H}}\text{-mod}\geq({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})\geq({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}))\geq({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))\geq({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})),

which will be important in the sequel. Here “
\geq
” indicates “a full subcategory”. In particular,
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{C}}(M,N)=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(M,N)
for objects
M,N
in
\mathscr{C}
and every
\mathscr{C}={\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
, etc.

2.1A. The exact category
\boldsymbol{({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}))}
.

Its construction involves the category
{\mathcal{H}}
-mod
of (finite) left
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules, but it closely follows the more abstract discussion in
[
DPS17
, Assump. 3.2 and Constr. 3.8]
which worked with the category mod-
H
of right modules for a suitable algebra
H
more general than the generic Hecke
algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
.

For any integer
h
, let
{\mathscr{L}}_{h}
be the full subcategory
of
{\mathscr{A}}
consisting of modules
N
which are a finite direct sum of modules isomorphic to left-cell modules
S(\omega)
of height
h
.
Let
{\mathscr{L}}
be the set-theoretic union of the
{\mathscr{L}}_{h}
.
Next, let

{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
be the full additive subcategory of
{\mathcal{H}}
-mod with objects
M
(necessarily in
{\mathscr{A}}_{l}
) whose
\mathfrak{h}
-filtration
(
2.1.2
)
has the property that, for any integer
h
,
M_{h-1}/M_{h}
is an object in

{\mathscr{L}}_{-h}
.
1
1

1

The minus sign is chosen to be compatible with the duality theory developed later in Sections 2.2 and 2.3. In particular, we will consider dual left-cell modules
S_{\omega}
, for each
\omega\in\Omega
, and assign to each
S_{\omega}
a positive height, negative to that of
S(\omega)
. See the beginning paragraphs of Section 2.3 and also (
2.2.2
). Note that, with Notation
2.1.2
, this height filtration coincides with the one defined in Example
B.1
.

We will also informally call such an
\mathfrak{h}
-filtration
a left-cell filtration
.
2
2

2

Clearly, every
\mathfrak{h}
-filtration here may be refined to a filtration with sections isomorphic to left-cell modules. This refined filtration is a left-cell filtration in the sense of
[
DPS98a
, §2.2]
.

By analogy with

[
DPS17
, §3]
, let
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
consist of those sequences
(A\to B\to C)
of objects
in
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
such that, for every
h
,

0\longrightarrow A_{h-1}/A_{h}\longrightarrow B_{h-1}/B_{h}\longrightarrow C_{h-1}/C_{h}\longrightarrow 0

is a
split

short exact sequence in the additive category
{\mathscr{L}}_{-h}
(or, equivalently, in the category
{\mathcal{H}}
–mod). Then,

(2.1.3)

({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}))
is an
exact category.

This was proved in the more abstract setting of right
H
-modules in
[
DPS17
, Th. 3.9]
for an analogous
category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
associated to
H
. The proof in the present situation
is entirely similar. The result can also be deduced from the special case
H={\mathcal{H}}^{\text{op}}
of the cited theorem.

Note that left-cell modules are, by definition, finite and free over
{\mathcal{Z}}
. This implies that any object in

{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
is also finite and free over
{\mathcal{Z}}
. Looking ahead to Section 2.2, this
implies that
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})\subseteq{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l}
, the
left-hand version of the category
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
introduced in Theorem
2.2.2
. Thus,
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l}
denotes the full additive subcategory of objects
M\in{\mathscr{A}}_{l}
for which
M_{h}/M_{h+1}
is projective as a
{\mathscr{K}}
-module, for each
h\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l}={\mathscr{E}}_{l}|_{{\mathscr{A}}_{l}^{\prime}}
.
Also,
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})\subseteq{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l}
, so
that
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}))
is a (full) exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l})
; see the definition at the beginning of Subsection 1.3A.

Note also that, for each object
M
in
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
with the
\mathfrak{h}
-filtration given in display (
2.1.2
), each inclusion
M_{h}\subseteq M_{h-1}
gives a conflation
(M_{h}\to M_{h-1}\to M_{h-1}/M_{h})\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
, for all
1\leq h\leq n
, so that its
{\mathcal{Z}}
-dual
M^{*}
satisfies the condition (1) in the Stratification Hypothesis
1.3.3
.

2.1B. The exact category
\boldsymbol{({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))}
.

With the notation above, let
{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
denote the full subcategory of
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
with objects

(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
such that, for any integer
h
and any subset
J
of
S
,
the sequence

(2.1.4)

0\longrightarrow(M_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\longrightarrow(N_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\longrightarrow(P_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\longrightarrow 0

of fixed points is exact. Note that, for any
h
, each
0\to M_{h}/M_{h-1}\to N_{h}/N_{h-1}\to P_{h}/P_{h-1}\to 0
is split in

{\mathcal{H}}
-mod, since
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
, but this does not mean that the short exact sequence
0\to M_{h}\to N_{h}\to P_{h}\to 0
is split. In particular, the exactness of (
2.1.4
) is a nontrivial property.

We have:

Proposition 2.1.3
.

({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))

is an exact category.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be the abelian category of all finite
{\mathcal{Z}}
-modules. Put

F(-):=\bigoplus_{J,h}((-)_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}:{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})\to{\mathscr{C}}.

Then,
F
is an
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
-left exact functor. Now we apply
[
DPS17
, Lem. 3.1]
, with
{\mathscr{E}}
there
taken to be
{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
as defined above.
∎

For
J{\subseteq}S
, if
W_{J}
is the parabolic subgroup of
W
defined by
J
, then
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
is the usual (left)
q
-permutation module determined by any
J\subseteq S
, where

(2.1.5)

x_{J}:=\sum_{w\in W_{J}}T_{w}

was defined in display (
1.2.4
).
For future use in Section 5.2, we also define

(2.1.6)

y_{J}:=\sum_{w\in W_{J}}\text{{\rm sgn}}(w)(t^{2})^{-L(w)}T_{w},

where
\text{{\rm sgn}}(w):=(-1)^{\ell(w)}
is the sign of
w
. See
[
DPS98c
, pp. 324–326]
, taking
q=t^{2}
in our current notation.

Proposition 2.1.4
.

The
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
belongs to
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
.

Proof.

Let
M
denote
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
. Using display (
1.2.8
), we find

M
has a basis of elements
{\textsc{c}}_{v}
, with
v\in W
, in
a certain quasi-poset ideal
V
for
\leq_{L}
, necessarily closed under
\sim_{L}
. In fact, we have

V=\{w\in W\mid ws<w,\forall s\in J\}=\{w\in W\mid w\leq_{L}w_{0,J}\}
; see
[
DPS98a
, (2.3.3)]
or Lemma
3.1.2
in this monograph.
The quasi-poset ideal
V
is, thus, a union of
left cells. For any integer
h
, let
V_{h-1}
be the set of all
v\in V
belonging to a left cell
\omega
with

\mathfrak{h}(\omega)\leq-h
. Then, each
V_{h-1}
is, by construction, a union of left cells, and is a quasi-poset ideal, since

\mathfrak{h}
is compatible with
\leq_{L}
on
\Omega
. (This was observed in Notation
2.1.2
as a consequence of (
1.2.11
).) The
{\mathcal{Z}}
-span
M_{h-1}
of
V_{h-1}
is, thus, a left

{\mathcal{H}}
-module, as follows from the definition of
\leq_{L}

[
Lus03
, p. 43]
. Similar considerations identify

M_{h-1}/M_{h}
as a direct sum of left-cell modules of height
-h
, one summand for each left cell in
V
of height

-h
. Thus,
M\in{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
, proving the assertion.
∎

We remark that improvements of Proposition
2.1.4
occur in several places in this monograph. For example, Corollary
2.1.8
gives the sharper result that, for
J\subseteq S
,
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
. See
Theorem
3.1.3
for a right-module version involving the exact category
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
.

Corollary 2.1.5
.

Let
M={\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
, and consider the decreasing filtration

M=M_{0}\supseteq M_{1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq M_{l_{J}}=0

defined by the height function
\mathfrak{h}
. For any
h
, the short exact sequence
0\to M_{h}\to M_{h-1}\to M_{h-1}/M_{h}\to 0

belongs of
{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
.

Proof.

By the discussion at the end of the proof of Proposition
2.1.4
, each inclusion
M_{h}\subseteq M_{h-1}
is an inflation. Thus, each

(M_{h}\to M_{h-1}\to M_{h-1}/M_{h})\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
. By
Lemma
2.1.1
, the sequence

0\to(M_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\to(M_{h-1})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\to(M_{h-1}/M_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\to 0
is exact for all
J\subseteq S
.
Hence, by definition (
2.1.4
), the short exact sequence
0\to M_{h}\to M_{h-1}\to M_{h-1}/M_{h}\to 0

belongs of
{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
.
∎

In the following theorem, the
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})}
-group is calculated in the exact category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
. Subsection 2.1C below introduces a second exact category
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
, possibly setting up a conflict of notation. However, as pointed out in Proposition
2.1.7
(or the proof of its corollary), the
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})}(M,N)
-groups can
be calculated in either exact category, as long as
M,N
both belong to
{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
. We also remark that
the condition (
2.1.1
) plays an essential role in the proof of the theorem.

Theorem 2.1.6
.

For any
J\subseteq S
,
\text{\rm{Ext}}_{{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})}^{1}({\mathcal{H}}x_{J},S(\omega))=0
, for all
\omega\in\Omega
.

Proof.

Consider an exact sequence

(2.1.7)

0\longrightarrow S(\omega)\longrightarrow E\ \overset{g}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}\longrightarrow 0

in
{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
. We want to prove that the sequence is split. By definition, we obtain an exact sequence

(2.1.8)

0\longrightarrow S(\omega)^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\longrightarrow E^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\overset{g_{J}}{\longrightarrow}({\mathcal{H}}x_{J})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\longrightarrow 0.

Consider the
{\mathcal{H}}_{J}
-submodule
{\mathcal{Z}}x_{J}
of
({\mathcal{H}}x_{J})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}
. The exactness of (
2.1.8
) implies that there exists
e\in E^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}
such that
g_{J}(e)=x_{J}
. Thus,
{\mathcal{Z}}x_{J}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}e
as
{\mathcal{H}}_{J}
-modules. Since
{\mathcal{Z}}e\subseteq E
, this induces an
{\mathcal{H}}_{J}
-module homomorphism
f_{J}:{\mathcal{Z}}x_{J}\to E
. By Frobenius reciprocity, there is an
{\mathcal{H}}
-module homomorphism

f:{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}\to E
such that
f(x_{J})=e
. Hence,
gf(x_{J})=x_{J}
, and, consequently,
gf
is the identity map on
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
. In other words, the sequence (
2.1.7
) is split.
∎

Again, we note that
{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})\subseteq{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})\subseteq{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l}
, the latter defined in Section 2.2 (see
Theorem
2.2.2
). Thus,

({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
is a full exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l})
.

Note that, in the exact category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
, not every object satisfies the property in Corollary
2.1.5
. This motivates the definition of the following full subcategory.

2.1C. The exact category
\boldsymbol{({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))}
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
be the full subcategory of
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
with objects
M

having the property that, for any integer
h
and any
J\subseteq S
, the map

(2.1.9)

(M_{h-1})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\longrightarrow(M_{h-1}/M_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}

is surjective. In other words, each conflation

(M_{h}\to M_{h-1}\to M_{h-1}/M_{h})
in
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
gives rise, for each
J\subseteq S
, to a short exact seqeunce

0\to(M_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\to(M_{h-1})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\to(M_{h-1}/M_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\to 0
. Hence,
(M_{h}\to M_{h-1}\to M_{h-1}/M_{h})\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
.

Proposition 2.1.7
.

Let
M,P\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
, and assume a given
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
.
Then,
N\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
if and only if
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
.
In particular, the full subcategory
{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
of
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
is closed under extensions in
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
.

Proof.

First, assume that
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
. For an integer
h
and a subset
J\subseteq S
, form the commutative diagram

(2.1.10)

\begin{CD}(M_{h-1}/M_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}@>{}>{}>(N_{h-1}/N_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}@>{}>{}>(P_{h-1}/P_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
(M_{h-1})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}@>{}>{}>(N_{h-1})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}@>{}>{}>(P_{h-1})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
(M_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}@>{}>{}>(N_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}@>{}>{}>(P_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}.}\end{CD}

Since
M,P\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
, columns 1 and 3 are short exact sequences. Since
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
, the three rows are all short exact sequences as well. Row 1 is already, before taking fixed points, a split short
exact sequence of
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules, because
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}})
. Thus, it remains exact upon taking
{\mathcal{H}}_{J}
-fixed points. Hence, column 2 is also exact, using
[
Mac94
, Ex. 2, p. 51]
, so
that
N\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
, as required.

Conversely, if
N\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
, then all columns in (
2.1.10
) are short exact sequences.
Row 1 is exact as in the previous paragraph. By induction on
h
, the bottom row is also a short exact sequence. By
[
Mac94
, Ex. 2, p. 51]
again,
row 2 is exact, proving that
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
, as required.
∎

If
\mathscr{F}
is a category of composible morphism pairs from an additive category
\mathscr{B}
, and
\mathscr{C}
is an additive
subcategory of
\mathscr{B}
, let
\mathscr{F}|_{\mathscr{C}}
denote the category of pairs from
\mathscr{F}

whose objects belong to
\mathscr{C}
.
In the corollary below and elsewhere, we abuse notation by writing

({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
for
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})|_{\mathscr{A}^{\flat}(\mathscr{L})})
.

Corollary 2.1.8
.

With the above notation,
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
is an exact category, and is a full exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
. Moreover, for every
J\subseteq S
,
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
.
(In particular, Theorem
2.1.6
holds in the full exact subcategory
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
.)

Proof.

Observe that the category
{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
is closed under extensions in the exact category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
by Proposition
2.1.7
. So
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
is itself exact
by
[
Bu10
, Lem. 10.20]
. (The proof in
[
Bu10
]
is largely left as an exercise, in part using the
diagram of
[
Bu10
, Lem. 3.5]
.)
The assertion of the corollary regarding
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
follows from Lemma
2.1.1
. Alternately, it follows from a downward induction on
h
by applying Proposition
2.1.7
to Corollary
2.1.5
.
∎

Lemma 2.1.9
.

Let
M\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
,
h<a
be integers, and
J\subseteq S
. Then,
M_{h}
and
M_{h}/M_{a}
belong to
{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
, and the natural map

(2.1.11)

(M_{h})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\longrightarrow(M_{h}/M_{a})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}

is surjective. The same result is true if
M_{h}
is replaced by
M
.

Proof.

Obviously,
M_{h}\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
by definition. Once the surjectivity of (
2.1.11
) is proven, it follows that
M_{h}/M_{a}\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
, by writing the map
M_{h}\longrightarrow M_{h}/M_{h+1}
as the composite

M_{h}\to M_{h}/M_{a}\to M_{h}/M_{h+1}
and taking
{\mathcal{H}}_{J}
-fixed points. It remains to prove the surjectivity
of (
2.1.11
). Of course, it holds for
a=h+1
by the definition
of
{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
. By induction on
a-h
, it holds if
h
is replaced by
h+1
. The required surjectivity follows from, say,
a diagram chase in
{\mathcal{Z}}
-mod, using the commutative diagram

\begin{CD}M^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}_{h+1}@>{}>{}>M_{h}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}@>{}>{}>(M_{h}/M_{h+1})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}\\
@V{f}V{}V@V{g}V{}V\Big\|\\
(M_{h+1}/M_{a})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}@>{}>{}>(M_{h}/M_{a})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}}@>{}>{}>(M_{h}/M_{h+1})^{{\mathcal{H}}_{J}},\end{CD}

whose rows are short exact sequences.
∎

Corollary 2.1.10
.

(a)

If
M\in{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
has the
\mathfrak{h}
-filtration (
2.1.2
), and
a\in\mathbb{Z}
with
0\leq a\leq n
, then

(M_{a}\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow M/M_{a})\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}).

In particular,
(M_{h}\to M_{h-1}\to M_{h-1}/M_{h})\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
, for all
1\leq h\leq n
.

(b)

Suppose
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
. If
h\in\mathbb{Z}
, then both
(M_{h}\to N_{h}\to P_{h})
and

(M/M_{h}\to N/N_{h}\to P/P_{h})
belong to
{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
.

Proof.

(a) follows from Lemma
2.1.9
, using
M
for
M_{h}
.

(b) Clearly,
(M_{h}\to N_{h}\to P_{h})\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
. The corresponding statement for the quotients follows from the

3\times 3
-lemma for exact categories
[
Bu10
, Cor. 3.6]
, using (a) to obtain the columns.∎

Note that
{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
is, by definition, a full subcategory of
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
and, thus, of
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l}
. The latter is defined, along
with
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l}
, in Section 2.2.
Also,
{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})\subseteq{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l}
so that
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
is a full
exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l})
.
3
3

3

See the discussion above (
1.3.2
) for the terminology.
By the discussion in Section 2.2 below Proposition
2.2.3
(including Remark
2.2.4
), the contravariant
dual
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))^{{\text{\rm op}}}
is realized as
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})^{*},{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})^{*})

inside
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime*}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime*}_{l})=({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{r},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{r})=({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
.

Remarkably, it is this dual of the exact category in the corollary that interests us most. We pursue this in
Section 2.3.

2.2.
Some generalities on duality

This section uses the (more abstract) right-module setup of
[
DPS17
, Assump. 3.2]
; see Example
B.1
in Appendix B for the left-module setup.
Thus,
{\mathscr{K}}
is a Noetherian integral domain,
K
is its fraction field, and
H
is a finite and torsion-free algebra
over
{\mathscr{K}}
such that
H_{K}
is semisimple. The (isomorphism classes of) irreducible
right

H_{K}
-modules are indexed by a given set
\Lambda
, and
\mathfrak{h}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
is a height function.

It was also assumed “for convenience” in
[
DPS17
, Assump. 3.2]
that
\mathfrak{h}
takes non-negative values. We do not make that assumption here. The “convenience” was in being able to use 0 as a lower bound for indexing elements of
\Lambda
via
\mathfrak{h}
. In no case is the validity of a stated result in
[
DPS17
]
affected by dropping the requirement that
\mathfrak{h}
takes non-negative values. One such general result is the following.

Theorem 2.2.1

(
[
DPS17
, Th. 3.6]
)
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
denote the category of right
H
-modules which are finite and torsion-free over
{\mathscr{K}}
. For each
M
in
{\mathscr{A}}
, and
h\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
M^{h}
denote
M\cap(M_{K})^{\leq h}
, where
(M_{K})^{\leq h}
is the sum in
M_{K}
of all irreducible right
H_{K}
-submodules indexed by
\lambda\in\Lambda
with
\mathfrak{h}(\lambda)\leq h
. Let
{\mathscr{E}}
denote the set of short exact sequences
0\to X\overset{i}{\to}Y\overset{d}{\to}Z\to 0
in mod-
H
of objects
X,Y,Z\in{\mathscr{A}}
, such that, for each
h\in\mathbb{Z}
, the induced sequence

(2.2.1)

0\longrightarrow X^{h}/X^{h-1}\longrightarrow Y^{h}/Y^{h-1}\longrightarrow Z^{h}/Z^{h-1}\longrightarrow 0

is also exact in mod-
H
. Then,
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
is an exact category.

We also have

Theorem 2.2.2
.

Keep the notation above, but let
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
denote the full additive subcategory of objects
M\in{\mathscr{A}}
for which
M^{h}/M^{h-1}
is projective as a
{\mathscr{K}}
-module, for each
h\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}
denotes
{\mathscr{E}}|_{{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}}
, the short exact sequences in
{\mathscr{E}}
whose objects all belong to
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
, then
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
is an exact category.

Proof.

The category
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
is precisely the category
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
in
[
DPS17
, Constr. 3.8]
with
{\mathscr{S}}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},{\mathscr{S}}_{i}
in the special case that each
{\mathscr{S}}_{i}
is the full additive category of
{\mathscr{A}}
consisting of objects
S
projective over
{\mathscr{K}}
with
S_{K}
a sum of irreducible right
H_{K}
-modules of height
i
, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Note that
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
is closed under extensions in
{\mathscr{A}}
, since the middle term of the exact sequence (
2.2.1
) is projective over
{\mathscr{K}}

when the other terms are projective over
{\mathscr{K}}
. Consequently,
[
Bu10
, Lem. 10.20]
implies that
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})

is an exact category.
∎

Observe that the category
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})
, as defined in
[
DPS17
, Constr. 3.8]
, is not used in the proof of the above result. Nevertheless,
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
is a (full) exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
, while
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
provides us with a general place
to park duals, arising from the construction below. Those duals of most interest to us will actually belong to

({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
(with
{\mathscr{S}}
restricted to suitable direct sums of dual left-cell modules).

2.2A. Duality.
In Section 2.1, we emphasized the differences between left
and right modules, because of the historical use of left-cell modules
and their duals. Here, we want to do the opposite and try to view left and right modules
on a more equal footing.

The discussion and results above in this section are formulated in terms of right
H
-modules. They all have analogs for left
H
-modules as may be seen from the right
H
-module case by substituting the opposite algebra
H^{\text{op}}
for
H
. In a context where both left and right
H
-modules are used, it is useful to use a subscript
l
or
r
to distinguish the two cases. Thus,
{\mathscr{A}}_{l}
,
({\mathscr{A}}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}_{l})
,
({\mathscr{A}}_{l}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}_{l}^{\prime})
are
{\mathscr{A}}
,
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
,
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
analogs in the left
H
-module case, and a subscript
r
could be used similarly to give a balanced treatment of the (original) right
H
-module case.

Next, let
(-)^{*}=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{K}}}(-,{\mathscr{K}})
be the usual duality functor—actually, there are two functors,
{\mathscr{A}}_{l}\rightarrow{\mathscr{A}}_{r}
and
{\mathscr{A}}_{r}\rightarrow{\mathscr{A}}_{l}
. Both are contravariant and are denoted by the same
symbol. The functors apply also to
{\mathscr{A}}_{l}^{\prime}
and
{\mathscr{A}}_{r}^{\prime}
and induce equivalences of additive categories

{\mathscr{A}}_{l}^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathscr{A}}_{r}^{\prime\text{op}}\quad{\text{\rm and}}\quad{\mathscr{A}}_{r}^{\prime}{\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathscr{A}}_{l}^{\prime\text{{\text{\rm op}}}}}.

Similarly, we can apply
(-)^{*}
to
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l}
and
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{r}
. Finally,
(-)^{*}
can be applied to the pairs
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l})

and
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{r},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{r})
as well as the opposite categories.

Our notation hides the height function
\mathfrak{h}
, which affects the definition of
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime},\cdots
. If we wish, we can always add a constant integer to any given
\mathfrak{h}
function without affecting
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}\ldots
. We assume in the proposition below, and unless otherwise noted, that the dual of any irreducible right
H
-module indexed by
{\lambda}\in\Lambda
is also indexed by
{\lambda}
and that

(2.2.2)

\mathfrak{h}_{l}({\lambda})=-\mathfrak{h}({\lambda})=-\mathfrak{h}_{r}(\lambda),

for all
{\lambda}\in\Lambda
.

Proposition 2.2.3
.

The duality functor
(-)^{*}
induces a contravariant equivalence between
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l})
and
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{r},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{r})
.

We leave the proof to the reader, except for noting that the projectivity (over
{\mathscr{K}}
) property of objects in
{\mathscr{A}}_{l}^{\prime}
is preserved by
(-)^{*}
and also guarantees that
(-)^{*}
sends
{\mathscr{E}}_{l}^{\prime}
to
{\mathscr{E}}_{r}^{\prime}
. Similar properties hold
with
r
and
l
interchanged. Also,
(-)^{**}=((-)^{*})^{*}
is naturally isomorphic to the identity functor.

Remark 2.2.4
.

The functor
(-)^{*}
above is more than a contravariant equivalence. It defines an isomorphism of one category with a
full representative subcategory of another. (Recall that a representative subcategory contains a member of every isomorphism class of objects in the larger category. If it is also full, its inclusion is an equivalence.)

In view of the proposition, we may think of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{r},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{r})
as a dual of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l})
, via the contravariant functor
(-)^{*}
. More interesting to note is the possibility of using
(-)^{*}
to construct new exact categories inside
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{r},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{r})
as duals (i.e., strict images under
(-)^{*}
) of exact subcategories of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l})
. More precisely, if
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
is a full exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}_{l}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}_{l}^{\prime})
, let
{\mathscr{B}}^{*}
denote the (strict) image of
{\mathscr{B}}
under
(-)^{*}
in
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{r}
, and define

{\mathscr{F}}^{*}\subseteq{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{r}
similarly. Then,
(-)^{*}
induces a contravariant equivalence
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})\overset{\sim}{\to}({\mathscr{B}}^{*},{\mathscr{F}}^{*})
. That is, the subcategory
({\mathscr{B}}^{*},{\mathscr{F}}^{*})\subseteq({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
is equivalent to the contravariant dual

({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})^{\text{\rm op}}
of
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
.

In general, the notation
M^{h}
applies to both the left- and right-module case.
If it is really needed to distinguish the left case from the right, subscripts
may be used, with the convention
M_{h}=M^{-h}
. This notation is implicit in Section 2.1, and we use it below.

2.2B. Balanced subcategories.
A property we are looking for in exact subcategories of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})

(=({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{r},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{r}))
is
balance.
More generally, if
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
is a full
4
4

4

We could
use a weaker condition than“full” in this definition. All we really need is that, for any
X,Y\in{\mathscr{B}}
,
if
f\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}}(X,Y)
is an isomorphism in
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
, then

f\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(X,Y)
. One could call the embedding
{\mathscr{B}}\to{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}

isomorphism-full
under such a condition.
Note that the strict closure
\overline{{\mathscr{B}}}
of
{\mathscr{B}}
in
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
(throwing in all objects in
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
isomorphic to an object in
{\mathscr{B}}
) makes sense as a category in the presence of this condition, using for
morphisms
f:X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
between its objects those morphisms
f
in
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
for which there are in
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
isomorphisms
e:X\to X^{\prime}
and
g:Y^{\prime}\to Y
with objects
X,Y\in{\mathscr{B}}
and composite
efg
a morphism in
{\mathscr{B}}
.
As such, the inclusion of
{\mathscr{B}}
into

\overline{{\mathscr{B}}}
is full and an equivalence of categories, and
{\mathscr{B}}
is a subcategory of
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
closed under object
isomorphisms.
exact
subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
, we say
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
is
balanced
provided, for each
M\in{\mathscr{B}}
and
h\in\mathbb{Z}
, the objects
M^{h}
and
M/M^{h}
are both isomorphic to objects in
{\mathscr{B}}
, and the sequence
(M^{h}\to M\to M/M^{h})
(which is obviously in
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}
) is isomorphic to a member of
{\mathscr{F}}
. Here, we are using the defining properties of
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}
introduced in Theorems
2.2.1
and
2.2.2
above. Of course,
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
itself is balanced.

We have not assumed in the definition above that
{\mathscr{B}}
is closed under isomorphism in
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
. However,
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
is balanced if and only if its strict closure in
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
is balanced. See footnote
4
above
for further discussion.

A similar discussion applies for
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l})
.

Proposition 2.2.5
.

Suppose
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
is a balanced exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{l})
. If
({\mathscr{B}}^{*},{\mathscr{F}}^{*})
denotes its strict image in
({\mathscr{A}}_{r}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}_{r}^{\prime})
under
(-)^{*}
, then

({\mathscr{B}}^{*},{\mathscr{F}}^{*})
is balanced.

We leave the proof of this result to the reader.

We have already observed in Corollary
2.1.10
and Proposition
2.3.3

below
that the exact categories
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
and
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
are balanced. As a consequence, the following proposition applies to both. Note that
{\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
and
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
are, respectively, closed under isomorphism of objects in
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{l}
and
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{r}
.

Proposition 2.2.6
.

Let
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
denote either
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{r},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{r})
or its left-hand counterpart. Suppose
({\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{F}})
is a balanced exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})
.
If
M\in{\mathscr{B}}
,
h\in\mathbb{Z}
, then the sequence

(2.2.3)

M^{h-1}\longrightarrow M^{h}\longrightarrow M^{h}/M^{h-1}

belongs to
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}
and is
isomorphic to a sequence in
{\mathscr{F}}
. In fact, the sequence belongs to
{\mathscr{F}}
, if
{\mathscr{B}}
is closed under isomorphisms in
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We treat the right-hand case
({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime})=({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{r},{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{r})
, which
also implies the left-hand version of the proposition.

Fix
M
in
{\mathscr{B}}
and let
h
be an integer. Applying the definition of balanced twice, we obtain that
M^{h},(M^{h})^{h-1},
and
M^{h}/(M^{h})^{h-1}
all belong to
{\mathscr{B}}
. Also,
((M^{h})^{h-1}\to M^{h}\to M^{h}/M^{h-1})\in{\mathscr{F}}
. Using the definition of

{\mathscr{A}}
in Theorem
2.2.1
we obtain the following commutative diagram in
{\mathscr{A}}
with all vertical arrows isomorphisms.

\begin{CD}(M^{h})^{h-1}@>{}>{}>M^{h}@>{}>{}>M^{h}/(M^{h})^{h-1}\\
@V{\wr}V{}V\Big\|@V{\wr}V{}V\\
M^{h-1}@>{}>{}>M^{h}@>{}>{}>M^{h}/M^{h-1}.\end{CD}

Since
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
is closed under isomorphism
(and full) in
{\mathscr{A}}
, this diagram and its isomorphic images are all in
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
. The top row is in
{\mathscr{B}}
and, in fact, belongs to
{\mathscr{F}}
. The bottom rows belongs to
{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}
and are isomorphic to the top row (as a diagram). This proves
the first assertion of the proposition.

To prove the second, assume
{\mathscr{B}}
is closed under isomorphism.
Then, the objects and morphisms in the bottom row all belong to
{\mathscr{B}}
, so the bottom row belongs to
{\mathscr{F}}
.
(Recall that the conflations in any exact category are required to be closed under isomorphism as part of their definition.)
This completes the proof of the proposition.∎

The last part of the proof above implicitly used the fact that
{\mathscr{B}}
is full, assumed in the definition of balanced. It would
have been enough to assume the weaker property in the footnote
4
of this chapter.

2.3.
The dual category of
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))

We now turn back to the generic Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
associated with a standard finite Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
.
We first consider the right
{\mathcal{H}}
-module analog
({\mathscr{A}}_{r}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{r}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
of
({\mathscr{A}}_{l}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}_{l}({\mathscr{L}}))
defined in
display (
2.1.3
) (see (
2.3.2
) below).

Thus,
{\mathscr{A}}_{r}
is the full
subcategory of right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules which are finite and torsion-free over
{\mathscr{K}}={\mathcal{Z}}
. We often
denote the category
{\mathscr{A}}_{r}
simply as
{\mathscr{A}}
when no confusion can arise.

Notation 2.3.1
.

It will be convenient in this section to use the height function
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{r}
, which is the negative of the height function
\mathfrak{h}_{l}

introduced in Notation
2.1.2
. Thus, the symbol
\mathfrak{h}
in this section, without further comment, denotes

\mathfrak{h}_{r}=-\mathfrak{h}_{l}
; it takes positive values. It is also compatible with the preorder
\leq_{LR}^{{\text{\rm op}}}
and partial order
\leq_{L}^{{\text{\rm op}}}
on
\Omega
.

For an integer
h
, let
{\mathscr{L}}^{*}_{h}
be the full subcategory of
{\mathscr{A}}
consisting of right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules
N
such that
N
is a finite direct sum of modules which are isomorphic to dual left-cell modules
S_{\omega}:=S(\omega)^{*}
of height
h
. Then let
{\mathscr{L}}^{*}:=\bigcup_{h}{\mathscr{L}}^{*}_{h}
. We remind the reader that, by a slight abuse of notation, a left-cell module
S(\omega)
has a negative height

h=\mathfrak{h}_{l}(\omega)<0
, and the dual left-cell module
S_{\omega}
has height
-h=\mathfrak{h}_{r}(\omega)>0
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
be the full additive subcategory of mod-
{\mathcal{H}}
with objects
M
whose
\mathfrak{h}_{r}
-filtration

(2.3.1)

0=M^{0}\subseteq M^{1}\subseteq M^{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq M^{n-1}\subseteq M^{n}=M

has the property that, for any integer
h>0
,
M^{h}/M^{h-1}
is an object in
{\mathscr{L}}^{*}_{h}
. See
[
DPS17
, §3]
, which
also allowed
h=0
(but did not use this case). Like the refinement in footnote
2
, such a filtration will be informally called
a dual left-cell filtration
.

By analogy with

[
DPS17
, §3]
, let
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
consist of those sequences
(M\to N\to P)

in
{\mathscr{A}}
such that, for every
h
,

0\to M^{h}/M^{h-1}\to N^{h}/N^{h-1}\to P^{h}/P^{h-1}\to 0

is a split
short exact sequence in the additive category
{\mathscr{L}}^{*}_{h}
. Then, as a special case of

[
DPS17
, Th. 3.9]
, we have

(2.3.2)

({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
is an
exact category.

Recall from Section 2.2 the standard
{\mathcal{Z}}
-duality functor
(-)^{*}
discussed in the context of the exact categories used
here. Thus, if
N\in{\mathscr{A}}_{l}({\mathscr{L}})
, then
N^{*}\in{\mathscr{A}}_{r}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
and
(N_{h-1}/N_{h})^{*}\cong(N^{*})^{h}/(N^{*})^{h-1}
,
for each
h\in\mathbb{Z}
.
More precisely, if
N
has a (left-cell)
\mathfrak{h}_{l}
-filtration as in (
2.1.2
), then

N^{*}
has a (dual left-cell)
\mathfrak{h}_{r}
-filtration

0=(N^{*})^{0}\subseteq(N^{*})^{1}\subseteq(N^{*})^{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq(N^{n-1})^{*}\subseteq(N^{*})^{n}=N

as given in (
2.3.1
), where
(N^{*})^{i}=(N/N_{i})^{*}
so that
(N^{*})^{h}/(N^{*})^{h-1}\cong(N_{h-1}/N_{h})^{*}\in({\mathscr{L}}_{-h})^{*}=({\mathscr{L}}^{*})_{h}
, for all
1\leq h\leq n
. Hence,
N^{*}
is an object of
{\mathscr{A}}_{r}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
. Similarly, for a conflation
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}_{l}({\mathscr{L}})
, we have
(P^{*}\to N^{*}\to M^{*})\in{\mathscr{E}}_{r}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
,
since
\big((P^{*})^{h}/(P^{*})^{h-1}\to(N^{*})^{h}/(N^{*})^{h-1}\to(M^{*})^{h}/(M^{*})^{h-1}\big)
, which is isomorphic to

\big((P_{h-1}/P_{h})^{*}\to(N_{h-1}/N_{h})^{*}\to(M_{h-1}/M_{h})^{*}\big)
, is split exact, for all
1\leq h\leq n
.

Hence, the exact category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
is naturally isomorphic to the dual category

({\mathscr{A}}_{l}({\mathscr{L}})^{*},{\mathscr{E}}_{l}({\mathscr{L}})^{*})
of
({\mathscr{A}}_{l}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}_{l}({\mathscr{L}}))
.
As in Section 2.2,
\mathscr{A}_{l}(\mathscr{L})^{*}
and
\mathscr{E}_{l}(\mathscr{L})^{*}
both denote strict images (closed under isomorphism).

Let
{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})={\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})^{*}
and let
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})={\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})^{*}
.
We easily prove the following key proposition, motivated by the weaker
[
DPS15
, Cor. 4.5]
and

[
DPS98a
, Th. 2.3.9]
. For
J\subseteq S
,

(2.3.3)

x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}\cong({\mathcal{H}}x_{J})^{*}

by
[
DPS98a
, Lem. 2.3.9]
. In particular,

(2.3.4)

x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}\,\,\,{\text{\rm belongs to ${\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})$ }}

by Corollary

2.1.8
.

Proposition 2.3.2
.

With the notation above,
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
is an exact category.
Also,
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
is a full exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
which is closed under extensions.
Moreover, for any left cell
\omega\in\Omega
and any
J\subseteq S
, there
is a vanishing

(2.3.5)

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})}(S_{\omega},x_{J}{\mathcal{H}})=0

in both of these categories.

Proof.

The category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
is the (contravariant) dual

({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
. Hence, it is exact, since
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
is exact by Proposition

2.1.3
. Similarly,
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
is exact, by Corollary
2.1.8
. Clearly,
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
is a full exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
and is closed under extensions by Proposition
2.1.7
. The displayed vanishing, in the case of
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
, follows from Theorem
2.1.6

and duality. However, we have already noted that
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
is an exact subcategory of

({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
, and
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
is full in
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
, so the displayed vanishing also holds for
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
. This completes the proof of the proposition. ∎

We conclude this section with the following proposition, a version of Corollary
2.1.10
in our present dual setting. The
proposition is essentially equivalent to that corollary, and can be deduced from it using the duality functor
(-)^{*}
.
We omit the details. An alternative argument can be given using the duality functors for part (a); then using
the
3\times 3
-Lemma. See the proof of Proposition
2.1.10
(b).

Proposition 2.3.3
.

(a) If
M\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
has the
\mathfrak{h}
-filtration (
2.3.1
), and
a\in\mathbb{Z}
with
0\leq a\leq n
. Then,

(M^{a}\to M\to M/M^{a})\in{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}).

In particular, each inclusion
M^{h-1}\subseteq M^{h}
gives a conflation
(M^{h-1}\to M^{h}\to M^{h}/M^{h-1})\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}})
.

(b) Suppose
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
. If
h\in\mathbb{Z}
, then both
(M^{h}\to N^{h}\to P^{h})
and

(M/M^{h}\to N/N^{h}\to P/P^{h})
belong to
{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
.

2.4.
Another abstract setting

Our main goal in this section is to prove Theorem
2.4.10
, which builds a kind of injective envelope for certain exact categories, improving upon
[
DPS17
, Th. 4.7]
. This goal is achieved by
refining the abstract framework used in
the earlier paper. At least some abstraction is desirable. The category
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
used in the main Theorem
3.3.1
is not easy to investigate directly, but it inherits many properties
from its “dual”
({\mathscr{A}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}),{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}))
. Those dual properties are often most easily checked
in the abstract setting of an exact category satisfying Conditions
2.4.2
below.

We follow the notation of
[
DPS17
, Assump. 3.2 and Constr. 3.8]
.
5
5

5

In
[
DPS17
, Const.3.8]
at the end of the first paragraph, it should be assumed that
M^{j}/M^{j-1}
is isomorphic to, not necessarily equal to, an object in
{\mathscr{S}}

for all
j
. This is not a problem usually, since one often requires that
{\mathscr{S}}
be closed under isomorphism.

Thus,
{\mathscr{K}}
is a Noetherian integral
domain with fraction field
K
. Also,
H
is a finite and torsion-free
{\mathscr{K}}
-algebra such that
H_{K}
is semisimple.
Thus,
H_{K}
has a finite set
\Lambda
of isomorphism classes of irreducible modules. In this section, “modules” for

H
and
H_{K}
are taken as right modules, We are given a “height function”

\mathfrak{h}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
, as defined in Appendix B. We will assume
\mathfrak{h}
takes positive values only on

\Lambda
, unless
noted otherwise.

Often
{\mathscr{K}}
is the algebra
{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]
of Laurent polynomials, and
H=\mathcal{H}
is a generic Hecke
algebra (as defined in Section 1.2 above and used in Sections 2.1 and 2.3). To pass from the height function domain
\Omega
used in Sections 2.1 and 2.3 to the set
\Lambda
above, see Notation
2.1.2
and Remark
B.9
. We will later apply the abstract results of this section to Section 2.3, where the height function
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{r}
also takes positive values.

Let
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
be the exact category given in Theorem
2.2.1
. In particular,
{\mathscr{A}}
is the full subcategory of mod-
H
consisting of right
H
-modules which are finite and torsion-free
over
{\mathscr{K}}
. For each integer
i
, let
{\mathscr{S}}_{i}
be a set of objects in a full additive
subcategory of
{\mathscr{A}}
. For each
S\in{\mathscr{S}}_{i}
, it is required that
S_{K}
be a direct sum of finitely many irreducible modules of the same height
i
. If
i
is not in the image of
\mathfrak{h}
, set
{\mathscr{S}}_{i}=[0]
, a full subcategory of zero objects in
{\mathscr{A}}
. Let
{\mathscr{S}}:=\bigcup_{i}{\mathscr{S}}_{i}
.
Finally, there is an exact category

({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
which is constructed in
[
DPS17
, Constr. 3.8]
.
(This construction with some details has already been
referenced in
a left-module setting in (
2.1.8
). See also (
2.3.2
) for a right-module setting.) The construction shows that

{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
is a full subcategory of
{\mathscr{A}}
(and thus of mod-
H
), and it shows that
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})
is contained in
{\mathscr{E}}
.

Remark 2.4.1
.

For convenience, we will assume that each category
{\mathscr{S}}_{i}
, and thus
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
, is closed under isomorphism in mod-
H
.

In particular,
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
, in the Section 2.3 setting, is an example of an exact category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
.

We will be interested in exact categories
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})
, which satisfy the following

Conditions 2.4.2
.

({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})
is a full exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
, with

{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
an inclusion of
{\mathscr{K}}
-categories, such
that the following hold:

(1)

{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
is object-closed under isomorphisms in
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
(and thus, by Remark
2.4.1
above,
in mod-
H
).

(2)

All objects of
{\mathscr{S}}
are contained in
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
.

(3)

If
M\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
and
h
is an integer, then
M^{h}
,
M^{h-1}
and
M^{h}/M^{h-1}
belong to
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
, and
(M^{h-1}\to M^{h}\to M^{h}/M^{h-1})
belongs to
{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
.

(4)

For
M,N,P\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
, if
(M\to N\to P)\in{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
, then
(M^{h}\to N^{h}\to P^{h})\in{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
for
all integers
h
.

Example 2.4.3
.

(a)
The exact category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
, a full exact subcategory of itself, satisfies
the conditions of Conditions
2.4.2
. In particular, in the setting of Section 2.3,
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))

provides an example.

(b) Another example is
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
, constructed in
Section 2.3 and viewed as a full exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
. Conditions (1) and (2) are clear,
while conditions (3) and (4) (for
H={\mathcal{H}}
) are explicit in Proposition
2.3.3
.

(c) The exact category
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
discussed in Proposition
2.3.2

does
not
satisfy the Conditions
2.4.2
. In fact, condition (3) fails to hold in general.

We now state several lemmas on
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}
-vanishing which hold for an exact category
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})
satisfying the above conditions. Lemma
2.4.5
is difficult, while the remaining ones are more routine generalizations of known results for the exact categories
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
studied and proved in

[
DPS17
, §4]
.
Thus, we give a detailed proof of Lemma
2.4.5
, and just brief comments on the remaining ones. Everything
is pulled together in Theorem
2.4.10
.

Throughout the rest of this section,

({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})
denotes a fixed exact category which satisfies Conditions
2.4.2
.

Lemma 2.4.4
.

If
M,N\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
,
then the natural maps

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(M,N)\rightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}(M,N)
and
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(M,N)\rightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{H}(M,N)
, as well as
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}(M,N)\rightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{H}(M,N)
, are inclusions of
{\mathscr{K}}
-modules.

The above lemma follows from the discussion of Strategy
1.3.1
.

Lemma 2.4.5
.

Let

h
be an integer. Let
S\in{\mathscr{S}}_{h}
and
M\in{{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}}
, and suppose the map

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}}}(S,M^{h}/M^{h-1})\longrightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}}(S,M^{h-1})

defined by pull-back is surjective. Then,
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}}(S,M)=0
.

Proof.

First, we claim it is sufficient to treat the case
M=M^{h}
: Given
(M\to E\to S)\in{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
,

(M^{h}\to E^{h}\to S)\in{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
by Condition
2.4.2
(4). The commutative diagram

(2.4.1)

\begin{CD}M@>{}>{}>E@>{}>{}>S\\
@A{\cup}A{}A@A{}A{}A\Big\|\\
M^{h}@>{}>{}>E^{h}@>{}>{}>S\end{CD}

identifies the original sequence
(M\to E\to S)
as a push-out of
(M^{h}\to E^{h}\to S)
in mod-
H
. Since we have full subcategory
inclusions

{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}\subseteq{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})\subseteq{\mathscr{A}}\subseteq{\text{mod-}}H
, each map in the diagram (
2.4.1
) above belongs to
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
. Also, each row is a
conflation in
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})
. Thus, the diagram (
2.4.1
) defines the original sequence
(M\to E\to S)
as a push-out in
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
.
That is,
the top row
(M\to E\to S)
is isomorphic to the push-out of the bottom row, regarded as a conflation in

({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})
. Obviously, this push-out is 0 in
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}^{\flat}}(S,M)
if it is a push-out of 0
in
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M^{h})
.
Thus, from now on,
we can assume that
M=M^{h}
. (A somewhat shorter argument can be made using
[
Bu10
, Prop. 2.12]
, which relates push-outs to rectangles like (
2.4.1
) in general, in an exact category.)

Let
\chi\in\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}}(S,M)
and let
M\overset{\iota}{\to}E\overset{\delta}{\to}S
be a representative conflation sequence for
\chi
. Without loss of generality, we can assume
M
is a (right)
H
-submodule of
E
, with
i
its inclusion. Also,
M^{h-1}
identifies with
E^{h-1}
. Form the push-out diagram of the deflation
M=M^{h}\to M^{h}/M^{h-1}
and the sequence
M\overset{\iota}{\to}E\overset{\delta}{\to}S
, to get a commutative diagram in the abelian category mod-
H
:

(2.4.2)

\begin{CD}M^{h}/M^{h-1}@>{}>{}>E/E^{h-1}@>{}>{}>S\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\Big\|\\
M@>{\subseteq}>{}>E@>{}>{}>S\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
M^{h-1}@>{=}>{}>E^{h-1}\end{CD}

with exact rows and columns (after adding 0 maps at the beginning and end of each row). Also, all objects in the diagram are in
{{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}}
, and each-three term row or column is in
{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}
. (The only non-obvious case, given our hypothesis on
({{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}},{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}})
, is the top row. However, this row is also in
{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}
, since the push-out of an inflation is an inflation; see
[
DPS17
, A.1(a)]
which is based on
[
K90
]
.)

In particular, the top row is defined by an element of

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}}(S,M^{h}/M^{h-1})\subseteq\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}}(S,M^{h}/M^{h-1})=0.

It is, thus, split by an
H
-module map
\alpha:S\to E/E^{h-1}
. Let
j
denote the map
M^{h}/M^{h-1}\to E/E^{h-1}
in the top row, and let
\beta:S\to M^{h}/M^{h-1}
be any (right)
H
-module homomorphism. Fullness of
{{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}}
in
{{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})}
, and of the latter in mod-
H
, guarantees that
\beta
is a map in the additive category defined by
{{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}}
. In particular, the pull-back through
\beta
of the element of
\text{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(M^{h}/M^{h-1},M^{h-1})
defining the left-hand column is an element of
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}}(S,M^{h-1})
. Indeed, this process defines the natural homomorphism

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}}}(S,M^{h}/M^{h-1})\longrightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}}(S,M^{h-1}),

which our hypothesis in the lemma guarantees to be surjective. Notice that the image of
\beta
may also be described as the image of
j\circ\beta
under a similarly defined map

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}}}(S,E/E^{h-1})\longrightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}}(S,E^{h-1})=\text{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M^{h-1}).

(The top left square in diagram (
2.4.2
) defines
M
as a pull-back with top row
j
. Extend it to the left by a
pull-back square with top row
\beta
, and consider the composite
j\circ\beta
.)

Observe that the map
\alpha+j\circ\beta:S\to E/E^{h-1}
is still a splitting map of the top row in (
2.4.2
). Now choose
\beta
so that its image in
{\text{\rm{Ext}}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M^{h-1})
is the negative of the image of
\alpha
under the display above, using its identification of
E^{h-1}
with
M^{h-1}
. With this choice, the image of
\alpha+j\circ\beta
is 0. This implies that the map
\alpha+j\circ\beta:S\to E/E^{h-1}
can be lifted to a map
\alpha^{\prime}:S\to E
of right
H
-modules. That is, the composition of
\alpha^{\prime}
with
E\to E/E^{h-1}
is equal to
\alpha+j\circ\beta
. Composing further with
E/E^{h-1}\to S
gives the identity map of
S
to itself.

We have thus constructed a splitting map of the map
E\to S
in mod-
H
. That is, the short exact sequence defined by
\chi
is 0 in
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{H}(S,M)
. It is, therefore, also 0 in
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M)
.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The following result is a generalization of
[
DPS17
, Lem. 4.1]
. See
[
DPS17
, Prop. A.2]
for the definition of Ext
{}^{1}_{\mathscr{E}}
-groups.

Lemma 2.4.6
.

Let
M,N\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
and
h\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(a)

There is a
natural isomorphism

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(N^{h},M)\cong\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(N^{h},M^{h})
.

(b)

In particular, if
S\in{\mathscr{S}}_{h},
we have

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M)\cong\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M^{h}).

(c)

Assume that
S\in{\mathscr{S}}_{h}
. If
M=M^{h}
and
M^{h-1}=0
, then
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M)=0
.

Proof.

Note that part (b) follows from part (a). Part (a) follows from
[
DPS17
, Prop. 4.1]
and its description of the isomorphism

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}(N^{h},M)\cong\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}}(N^{h},M^{h})
, together with
Condition
2.4.2
(4) and Lemma
2.4.4
. Since the proof will be quoted later, we provide some of the details.

First, we give a map

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}(N^{h},M)\overset{\phi}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}(N^{h},M^{h})

and show that it is an isomorphism by explicitly giving an inverse. The map
\phi
is obtained by applying the
functor
(-)^{h}
to a conflation. The inverse of
\phi
is defined by push-out
through
M^{h}\rightarrow M
. The restriction of
\phi
to
{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
makes sense using Condition
2.4.2
(4) and Lemma
2.4.4
.

This push-out also make sense in the exact category

({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})

and agrees with the push-out in
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
. (This is an elementary argument. The agreement of push-outs
also follows from
[
Bu10
, Prop. 2.12]
.) Thus, the isomorphism
\phi
induces a corresponding
isomorphism when
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})
is replaced by
{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
.

Finally,
part (c) (which is independent of (a) and (b)) follows from
[
DPS17
, Lem. 4.1(c)]
and Lemma
2.4.4
above. ∎

Lemma 2.4.7
.

Let
M\in\mathscr{A}_{\flat}
. If
S\in{\mathscr{S}}_{h}
, then

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M^{h-1})\hookrightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}(S,M^{h-1})\cong\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{H}(S,M^{h-1}).

Proof.

The containment
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}\hookrightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}
in the display
is obtained from Lemma
2.4.4
. The isomorphism in the display is from
[
DPS17
, Lem. 4.2]
.
∎

Lemma 2.4.8
.

Let
S\in\mathscr{S}_{h}
, and let
M\in\mathscr{A}_{\flat}
.
Suppose that
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M^{h-1})
is generated as a
\mathscr{K}
-module by
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}
(regarded as elements
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{H}(S,M^{h-1})
using the inclusion above). Let
M^{h-1}\to N\to S^{\oplus n}
represent the element of

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{H}(S^{\oplus n},M^{h-1})

corresponding to
\chi:=\epsilon_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\epsilon_{n}
above. Suppose there is
a commutative diagram in
mod-
H

\begin{CD}M^{h-1}@>{}>{}>N@>{}>{}>S^{\oplus n}\\
\Big\|@V{}V{}V@V{f}V{}V\\
M^{h-1}@>{}>{}>M^{h}@>{}>{}>M^{h}/M^{h-1}.\end{CD}

Then,
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M)=0
.

Proof.

By the construction of addition in
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{H}(S,M^{h-1})
, for
1\leq j\leq n
,
there is an inclusion
\iota_{j}:S\to S^{\oplus n}
, inducing by pull-back a map
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{H}(S,M)\longrightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{H}(S^{\oplus n},M^{h-1})
taking
\chi
to
\epsilon_{j}
. This gives the top rectangle in the commutative
diagram below, with top row representing
\epsilon_{j}
.

\begin{CD}M^{h-1}@>{}>{}>N_{j}@>{}>{}>S\\
\Big\|@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
M^{h-1}@>{}>{}>N@>{}>{}>S^{\oplus n}\\
\Big\|@V{}V{}V@V{f}V{}V\\
M^{h-1}@>{}>{}>M^{h}@>{}>{}>M^{h}/M^{h-1}.\end{CD}

Thus, the conflation corresponding to
\epsilon_{j}

may be obtained by a pull-back of
M^{h-1}\to M^{h}\to M^{h}/M^{h-1}
using the evident composite

g_{j}:S\to S^{\oplus n}\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}M^{h}/M^{h-1}.

Consequently, the image of
g_{j}\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}}(S,M^{h}/M^{h-1})\subseteq\text{\rm{Hom}}_{H}(S,M^{h}/M^{h+1})
under the connecting homomorphism to
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M^{h-1})\subseteq\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{H}(S,M^{h-1})
is
\epsilon_{j}
. Since
j
was arbitrary, the connecting homomorphism is surjective, so
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M)=0
by Lemma
2.4.5
.
∎

The next result will assume the following hypothesis for
{\mathscr{S}}
:

Finiteness Hypothesis 2.4.9
.

For any integer
h
,
{\mathscr{S}}_{h}
is strictly generated as an additive category by a finite set
F(h)
, i.e., every object in
{\mathscr{S}}_{h}
is isomorphic to a finite direct sum of objects chosen from
F(h)
.

Theorem 2.4.10
.

Suppose
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})
is a full
exact subcategory of
({\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}}),{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}}))
satisfying conditions (1)–(4) in Conditions
2.4.2
. Assume
{\mathscr{S}}

satisfies
Hypothesis
2.4.9
.
Then, given
M\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
, there exists an object
X=X_{M}
in
\mathscr{A}_{\flat}
and an inflation
M\overset{i}{\to}X

in
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})

such that
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,X)=0
, for all

S\in\mathscr{S}
.
In addition,
if
h
is chosen minimal such that
M^{h-1}\neq 0
, it may be assumed that the inflation
M\overset{i}{\longrightarrow}X
induces an isomorphism
M^{h-1}\cong X^{h-1}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
M\not=0
and that
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,M)\neq 0
, for some
S\in\mathscr{S}
. Choose an integer
h
minimal with
such a non-vanishing occurring for some
S\in\mathscr{S}_{h}
. Note that

M^{h-1}\neq 0
by Lemma
2.4.6
(c). We will enlarge

M
to an object
X
, closer to the
X
required in the theorem.

Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}
be generators for
\mathscr{S}_{h}
. For each index
i
, let
\epsilon_{i,1},\cdots,\epsilon_{i,n_{i}}
be a finite
set of generators for
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S_{i},M^{h-1})
. Form conflations

0\to M^{h-1}\to Y_{i}\to S_{i}^{\oplus n_{i}}\to 0
corresponding to

\chi_{i}:=\epsilon_{i,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\epsilon_{i,n_{i}}\in\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S_{i}^{\oplus n_{i}},M^{h-1})
. Put
\chi:=\chi_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{m}
, and let

\chi^{\prime}\in\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(M^{h}/M^{h-1},M^{h-1})
correspond to the
conflation
M^{h-1}\to M^{h}\to M^{h}/M^{h-1}
. Put
Z:=\oplus_{i}S_{i}^{\oplus n_{i}}\oplus M^{h}/M^{h-1}
, and let
M^{h-1}\to X^{h}\to Z
correspond to

\chi\oplus\chi^{\prime}
. Observe that there is a
commutative diagram

\begin{CD}M^{h-1}@ >>>Y_{i}@>{}>{}>S_{i}^{\oplus n_{i}}\\
\Big\|@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
M^{h-1}@>{}>{}>X^{h}@>{}>{}>Z,\end{CD}

in which the top row corresponds to
\chi_{i}
and the bottom row to

\chi\oplus\chi^{\prime}
. Note that the bottom row belongs to
{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
.
Comparison with
Lemma
2.4.8
, allowing for the differences in notation, shows

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S_{i},X^{h})=0
, for all
i
. Thus,
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,X^{h})=0
, for all
S
in
{\mathscr{S}}_{h}
. Note that we have
the same vanishing for
S\in{\mathscr{S}}_{j}
with
j<h
, by our
choice of
h
. In all cases, we can replace
X^{h}
with any
X^{\prime}

containing it with
(X^{\prime})^{h}=X^{h}
by Lemma
2.4.6
.

So far, we have not constructed an object
X
, only
X^{h}
. However,
the latter may be viewed as the middle term of an exact sequence of right

H
-modules
0\to M^{h}\to X^{h}\to S^{\prime}\to 0
, where
S^{\prime}:=\bigoplus S_{i}^{\oplus n_{i}}\in\mathscr{S}_{h}
. This sequence clearly corresponds to a conflation in
{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
.
(Notice that
X^{h}\to S^{\prime}
is the composition of the deflation
X^{h}\to Z
and the split deflation
Z\to S^{\prime}
, both for

{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
. It is therefore a deflation for
{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
. The morphism
M^{h}\to X^{h}
belongs to
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
and is a kernel of
X^{h}\to S^{\prime}
in mod-
H
, hence is a kernel in
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}.
) Applying a push-out construction using
M^{h}\to M
(see
Lemma
2.4.6
(a,b) and their proof), we obtain an object
X
in

\mathscr{A}_{\flat}
which contains a copy of
M
under an
inflation and has our constructed
X^{h}
as its image under the
functor
(-)^{h}
. In addition,
X^{j}=M^{j}
for
j\leq h-1
.

As noted above,
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(-,X)

vanishes on all objects in
\mathscr{S}_{j}
with
j\leq h
. Now repeat
the argument with
X
in the role of
M
. This requires a bigger

h
, unless
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S,X)
already vanishes, for all
S\in\mathscr{S}
. Eventually the process stops, at which point
X

satisfies all requirements of the theorem.
∎

Remark 2.4.11
.

Note that
[
DPS17
, Th. 4.7]
is the special case of the theorem with
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}={\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
and
{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}={\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})
.
The result was applied in the main theorem
[
DPS17
, Th. 4.9]

there to build, for each dual left-cell module
S_{\omega}
, an object
X_{\omega}
in
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
such that

S_{\omega}\subseteq X_{\omega}
(part of the filtration structure required for
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{S}})
) and such that

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})}(S,X_{\omega})=0
, for all
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
. We can apply the same construction here.

In preparation for the next section, we now assume that
H={\mathcal{H}}
is the generic Hecke algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]
as discussed in Section 1.2. Thus, the Noetherian domain
{\mathscr{K}}
will be specialized to
{\mathcal{Z}}
.

Construction 2.4.12
.

Let
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})=({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
, using a height
function
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{r}
as in Notation
2.3.1
. (Thus,
\mathfrak{h}
is compatible with the preorder
\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}
.) Let
\Omega
be the set of (Kazhdan-Lusztig) left cells in
W
.

(a)

Let
\Omega^{\prime}
be the set of left cells that do not contain the longest element
w_{0,J}
in a
parabolic subgroup
W_{J}
,
J\subseteq S
. For
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
, define (as given in Theorem
2.4.10
)

X_{\omega}=X_{M}
, for
M=S_{\omega}
, and let

X:=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}}X_{\omega}
.

(b)

Define right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules

(2.4.3)

\begin{cases}T:=\bigoplus_{J\subseteq S}\text{\bf\it x}_{J}{\mathcal{H}}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega\backslash\Omega^{\prime}}T_{\omega},\\
T^{+}:=T\bigoplus X,\end{cases}

and form the generic Hecke endo-algebra and its extended version

(2.4.4)

\begin{cases}A:=\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T),\\
A^{+}:=\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T^{+}).\end{cases}

(c)

Finally, for
\omega\in\Omega\backslash\Omega^{\prime}
, let
T^{+}_{\omega}:=x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
, where
J
is the unique subset of
S
with
w_{0,J}\in\omega
, and
T^{+}_{\omega}=X_{\omega}
, for
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
.
Define the following left
A^{+}
-modules

(2.4.5)

\begin{cases}\Delta(\omega):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(S_{\omega},T^{+}),\\
P(\omega):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T^{+}_{\omega},T^{+}),\,\,{\text{\rm for any}}\,\,\omega\in\Omega.\end{cases}

As considered in (
1.3.2
) and also for later application in Section 5.4, we define, for any
poset ideal
\Theta
of
(\Omega,\leq_{L})
,

(2.4.6)

T^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}:=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Theta}(T^{+}_{\omega})^{m_{\omega}}\;\text{ and }\;A^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}=\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}),

and

(2.4.7)

A^{\ddagger}=\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T^{\ddagger}_{\Omega}),

where
\{m_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in\Theta}
is any fixed set of positive integers.
6
6

6

We view
T^{\ddagger}
as a modification of
T^{+}
, rather than
T
.

Chapter 3
The conjecture and its proof

The conjecture in the title of this chapter is Conjecture
3.2.1
, which is

[
DPS15
, Conj.1.2]
and also captures essentially the earlier conjecture
[
DPS98a
, Conj. 2.5.2]
, as
discussed in Remarks
3.3.4
. A special local case
[
DPS15
, Th. 3.6]
of the
[
DPS15
]
conjecture was proved in a Cherednik algebra context, using an Ext
1
vanishing result

[
DPS15
, Cor. 5.3]
obtained in part from
[
GGOR03
]
. The latter results seemed to be hard to generalize. Nevertheless, in Chapter 2 here, we have used
exact categories to formulate by analogy a tractable global version of the Ext
1
vanishing condition, not
mentioning localization or Cherednik algebras. Using it, we are now ready to prove this new version of the conjecture.

For this chapter, we continue the notation in the previous chapter, but, as in Construction
2.4.12
, return to the special case where
H
is the generic Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
(so that
{\mathscr{K}}={\mathcal{Z}}
)
associated to a standard finite Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
.

3.1.
q
-permutation modules and their filtrations

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a generic Hecke algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]
associated to a standard finite Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
. Let
\Omega
be the set of left cells of
W
. The preorders
\leq_{L}
,
\leq_{LR}
on
W

induce partial order
\leq_{L}
and preorder
\leq_{LR}
on
\Omega
; see (
1.2.11
).
We take this opportunity to repeat and highlight the following result, mentioned after Proposition
B.5
.

Proposition 3.1.1
.

Each two-sided cell module
is a direct sum of left-cell modules.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{c}
be a two-sided cell of
W
, and set
M(\mathfrak{c}):={\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{LR}\mathfrak{c}}/{\mathcal{H}}_{<_{LR}\mathfrak{c}}
, the associated two-sided cell module, where
{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{LR}\mathfrak{c}}={\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{LR}w}

for
w\in\mathfrak{c}
is defined in (
1.2.9
).
Write
\mathfrak{c}=\cup_{i=1}^{m}\omega_{i}
(a disjoint union) with
\omega_{i}\in\Omega
. Then, the free
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module
M(\mathfrak{c})
is the direct sum
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}S^{\prime}(\omega_{j})
, where each submodule
S^{\prime}(\omega_{j}):=\sum_{y\in\omega_{j}}({\mathcal{Z}}{\textsc{c}}_{y}+{\mathcal{H}}_{<_{LR}\mathfrak{c}})/{\mathcal{H}}_{<_{LR}\mathfrak{c}}
is a free
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module. It remains to prove that each
S^{\prime}(\omega_{j})
is isomorphic to the left-cell module
S(\omega_{j})={\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{L}\omega_{j}}/{\mathcal{H}}_{<_{L}\omega_{j}}
. This is clear, since the inclusion
{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{L}\omega_{j}}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{LR}\mathfrak{c}}
induces by (
1.2.11
) an
{\mathcal{H}}
-module homomorphism
{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{L}\omega_{j}}\to M(\mathfrak{c})
with image
S^{\prime}(\omega_{j})
and with kernel
{\mathcal{H}}_{<_{L}\omega_{j}}={\mathcal{H}}_{\leq_{L}\omega_{j}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{<_{LR}\mathfrak{c}}
. (Observe that, for some
z\in\omega_{j}
, by (
1.2.11
), if
y<_{L}z
, then
y<_{LR}z(\in\mathfrak{c})
, and if
y\leq_{L}z
and
y<_{LR}w
for some
w\in\mathfrak{c}
, then
y<_{L}z
.)
∎

Observe that the argument for this result uses display (
1.2.11
). A similar fact holds for right-cell modules or dual left-cell modules.
Also, the
q
-permutation modules
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
in Proposition
2.1.4
have natural sections arising from
\mathfrak{h}_{l}
which are direct sums of left-cell modules. Once more, this goes back to (
1.2.11
), as discussed at the end of the proof of Proposition
2.1.4
. The (right)
q
-permutation modules
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
have, similarly, filtrations by the direct sum of dual left-cell modules
S_{\omega}:=S(\omega)^{*}
defined as the
{\mathcal{Z}}
-linear dual of the left-cell module
S(\omega)
; see Notation
2.1.2
.

We now define a new preorder
\preceq=\preceq_{\Omega}
on
\Omega
by setting, for,
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
,

(3.1.1)

\omega\preceq\omega^{\prime}\iff\mathfrak{h}(\omega)<\mathfrak{h}(\omega^{\prime}),\text{ or }\mathfrak{h}(\omega)=\mathfrak{h}(\omega^{\prime})\text{ and }\omega\sim_{LR}\omega^{\prime}.

Here, the height function
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{r}=-\mathfrak{h}_{l}
is chosen so that
it is compatible with
\leq^{\text{\rm op}}_{LR}
on
\Omega
. See Notation
2.3.1
and display (
B.2
) in Appendix B.
In particular,

\mathfrak{h}
is constant on two-sided cells.
Thus,
\preceq
above is well-defined on
\Omega
and even induces a partial order on the set
\overline{\Omega}

of two-sided cells.

It also follows from the definition in display (
3.1.1
) that the equivalence classes of the preorder
\preceq
are precisely the two-sided cells,
satisfying a requirement in
[
DPS15
, Conj. 1.2]
. More is true along these lines: the preorders
\leq^{\text{\rm op}}_{LR}
and
\preceq
share a compatible height function
\mathfrak{h}
. In addition,
\leq^{\text{\rm op}}_{LR}
strictly dominates
\preceq
(see Definition
B.3
in Appendix B)
1
1

1

To check this, suppose that
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
and

\omega<^{\text{\rm op}}_{LR}\omega^{\prime}
. Since
\mathfrak{h}
is compatible with
\leq^{\text{\rm op}}_{LR}
, we have
\mathfrak{h}(\omega)<\mathfrak{h}(\omega^{\prime})
.
Thus,
\omega\preceq\omega^{\prime}
. However, it is not possible for
\omega\sim_{LR}\omega^{\prime}
, since
\mathfrak{h}
is constant on two-sided cells. So
\omega\prec\omega^{\prime}
. Since we already know for
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
, that
\omega\sim_{LR}\omega^{\prime}
implies
\omega\sim_{\prec}\omega^{\prime}
, we have proved that
\leq^{\text{\rm op}}_{LR}
strictly
dominates
\preceq
.
so that any height function compatible with
\preceq
is also compatible
with
\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}
(as well as with
\leq_{L}^{\text{\rm op}}
).
See Proposition
B.6
(and Proposition
B.5
.) Again, the discussion traces back to (
1.2.11
).

The
right set
of an element
w\in W
is defined as the set
{\mathscr{R}}(x)=\{s\in S\,|\,xs<x\}.
Then,
if
x\leq_{L}y
, it is known (and elementary) that
{\mathscr{R}}(x)\supseteq{\mathscr{R}}(y)

[
Lus03
, Lem. 8.6]
. For example, if
J\subseteq S
, let
w_{0,J}
be the longest
word in
W_{J}
. Clearly,
{\mathscr{R}}(w_{0,J})=J
. Thus, if
\omega\in\Omega
contains
w_{0,I}
and
w_{0,J}
, for subsets
I,J
of
S
,
then, if
w_{0,I}\leq_{L}w_{0,J}\leq_{L}w_{0,I}
, so
I=J
.

We have the following elementary result.

Lemma 3.1.2
.

Let
J\subseteq S
, and let
w_{0,J}
be the longest word in the parabolic subgroup
W_{J}
.
The following sets
A_{J}
,
B_{J}
,
C_{J}
are equal:

(1)

A_{J}=\{w\in W\,|\,{\mathscr{R}}(w)\supseteq J\}.

(2)

B_{J}=\{w=uw_{0,J}\,|\,\ell(w)=\ell(u)+\ell(w_{0,J})\}
.

(3)

C_{J}=\{w\in W\,|\,w\leq_{L}w_{0,J}\}
.

Proof.

Let
w\in A_{J}
, and write
w=uv
, where
v\in W_{J}
and
u
is a distinguished left
W_{J}
-coset
representative. For
s\in J
, it follows that
ws<w
, so
vs<v
. Thus,
v=w_{0,J}
and
w\in B_{J}
. Conversely,
if
w=uw_{0,J}\in B_{J}
, then it follows easily that
u
is a distinguished left
W_{J}
-coset representative. So

J\subseteq{\mathscr{R}}(uw_{0,J})
, and
B_{J}\subseteq A_{J}
. Thus,
A_{J}=B_{J}
. Next,
w\leq_{L}w_{0,J}
implies
{\mathscr{R}}(w)\supseteq{\mathscr{R}}(w_{0,J})
, so
C_{J}\subseteq A_{J}
. Given
w=uw_{0,J}\in B_{J}
, let
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{r}}
be a reduced expression for

u
. Because
\ell(w)=\ell(u)+\ell(w_{0,J})
, it follows that, if
1<m\leq r
and
u_{m}:=s_{i_{m}}\cdots s_{i_{r}}
, then

s_{-_{m-1}}u_{m}w_{0,J}>u_{m}w_{0,J}
, so that
s_{i_{m-1}}u_{m}w_{0,J}\leq_{L}u_{m}w_{0,J}
. Thus,
w\leq_{L}w_{0,J}

and
w\in C_{J}
.∎

Theorem 3.1.3
.

Let
\omega\in\Omega
and assume that, for some
J\subseteq S
,
w_{0,J}\in\omega
,
where
w_{0,J}
is the longest
word in
W_{J}
. Then
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
has an increasing filtration in the exact category
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
which satisfies Condition
2.4.2
. The sections of the filtration are direct sums of dual left-cell modules

S_{\omega^{\prime}}
, starting with
S_{\omega}\subseteq x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
. The other sections have summands
S_{\omega^{\prime}}

satisfying
\omega<_{L}\omega^{\prime}
(equivalently,
\omega^{\prime}>^{\text{\rm op}}_{L}\omega
).

Proof.

We consider the left module
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
and then apply the contravariant
{\mathcal{Z}}
-duality
(-)^{*}
in Section 2.2. We use
the construction of the height filtration for
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
in the proof of Proposition
2.1.4
.
The quasi-poset ideal
V
used in the construction from
[
DPS98a
, Lem. 2.3.5]
is, by inspection of the latter lemma, the
set
A_{J}
in Lemma
3.1.2
, which is also the set
C_{J}
. In particular,
\omega
generates
V
as
a quasi-poset ideal with preorder
\leq_{L}
. The construction uses a height function which we will take to be

\mathfrak{h}_{l}=-\mathfrak{h}
for a given height function
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{r}
compatible with
\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}
. Thus,
\mathfrak{h}_{l}=-\mathfrak{h}
is compatible with
\leq_{LR}
and, therefore, with
\leq_{L}
. Hence,
\omega
has a unique maximal height among all
left cells in the construction. The left-cell module
S(\omega)
is the top section in this height filtration of
{\mathcal{H}}x_{J}
, and the
left-cell modules
S(\omega^{\prime})
, which must satisfy
\omega^{\prime}<_{L}\omega
, must appear in lower sections. The theorem
now follows by duality.
∎

3.2.
The conjecture

A main theorem in this monograph is Theorem
3.3.1
, which, we will see, implies Conjecture
3.2.1
below. As noted in the introduction to this chapter, this latter conjecture was stated in
[
DPS15
