, Conj. 1.2]
, which revised slightly
2
2

2

See Remarks
3.3.4
.
an earlier formulation
[
DPS98a
, Conj. 2.5.2]
. In assertion (2), the “strict” stratifying system in
[
DPS15
]
is simply called a stratifying system here (with the same meaning).

Recall that the set
\Omega
of left cells (unequal parameter in the sense of Lusztig
[
Lus03
]
) in the Weyl group
W
has a natural preorder
\leq_{LR}^{{\text{\rm op}}}
which partitions

\Omega
into its “two-sided Kazhdan-Lusztig cells.” That is,
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
belong to the same two-sided cell provided

\omega\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}\omega^{\prime}
and
\omega^{\prime}\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}\omega
. Similarly, any preorder
\leq
on
\Omega

also partitions
\Omega
into “cells.” Let
\widetilde{\Omega}
be the corresponding set of cells. We say that
\leq
is
strictly compatible
with

\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}
provided
\overline{\Omega}=\widetilde{\Omega}
. The preorder
\preceq
in display (
3.1.1
) is clearly
an example of such a preorder.

Recall also that given a left cell
\omega\in\Omega
,
S_{\omega}
denotes the corresponding dual left-cell module
S(\omega)^{*}=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(S(\omega),{\mathcal{Z}})
; see Notation
2.1.2
. For the definition of the element
x_{J}
, see (
2.1.5
).

Conjecture 3.2.1
.

There exist a preorder
\leq
on the set
\Omega
of left cells in
W
, strictly compatible with its partition
\overline{\Omega}
into two-sided cells, and a right
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
X
such that the following statements hold, where
T=\bigoplus_{J\subseteq S}x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
:

(1)

X
has a finite filtration with sections of the form
S_{\omega}
,
\omega\in\Omega
.

(2)

Let
T^{+}:=\!T\oplus X
,
A^{+}:=\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T^{+})
and, for any
\omega\in\Omega
, put

\begin{cases}\Delta(\omega):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(S_{\omega},T^{+}),\\
P(\omega):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T^{+}_{\omega},T^{+}),\;\text{as in \eqref{delta}.}\\
\end{cases}

Then, for any commutative, Noetherian​
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra​
R
, the set
\{\Delta(\omega)_{R},P(\omega)_{R}\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
is a stratifying system for
A^{+}_{R}
-mod relative to the quasi-poset​

(\Omega,\leq)
.

In
[
DPS15
, p. 231]
, in the notion of a stratifying system for
A^{+}
-mod, it is assumed, for each
\Delta(\omega)
, that there
is a certain projective
A^{+}
-module
P(\omega)
satisfying certain properties. In the notation of the present monograph, reviewed in Section 1.3, the projective modules
P(\omega)
are explicitly mentioned as part of the data for a stratifying system.
The
P(\omega)
’s are required to satisfy the same properties. Finally, as already noted above Remark
1.3.2
, the notion of a stratifying system behaves well with respect to base change, so it suffices to prove the conjecture for
R={\mathcal{Z}}
and, thus, for

A^{+}_{R}=A^{+}
.

3.3.
Proof of the conjecture

Before proceeding, we state and prove our main result, Theorem
3.3.1
. The preorder
\preceq
in the statement of the theorem is defined explicitly in display (
3.1.1
). It depends upon a suitable height function
\mathfrak{h}
.
Also, the exact category
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
depends on the same height function, as discussed, as does Construction
2.4.12
.

Theorem 3.3.1
.

Let
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})=({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
,
T,X,T^{+}
, and
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{r}
be as
in Construction
2.4.12
. For
\omega\in\Omega
, let

T^{+}_{\omega}=\begin{cases}X_{\omega},&\text{if }\omega\in\Omega^{\prime},\\
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}},&\text{if }w_{0,J}\in\omega.\end{cases}

(Recall that
X_{\omega}

is constructed from
S_{\omega}
, possibly non-uniquely, as in Theorem
2.4.10
. Here
\Omega^{\prime}
is defined in Construction
2.4.12
(a).)

Form the endomorphism algebra

(3.3.1)

A^{+}:=\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T^{+}).

Similarly, for
\omega\in\Omega
,
\Delta(\omega):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(S_{\omega},T^{+})
and
P(\omega):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T_{\omega}^{+},T^{+})
.

Then
\{\Delta(\omega),P(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
is a stratifying system for the category
A^{+}
-
mod
with respect to the quasi-poset
(\Omega,\preceq)
.

Proof.

By Remarks
1.3.6
(b)&(c), the left
A^{+}
-modules
\Delta(\omega)
,
\omega\in\Omega
, are automatically
{\mathcal{Z}}
-projective, even
{\mathcal{Z}}
-free. If
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
,
X_{\omega}
is, by construction, a free
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module. Also, for
\omega\in\Omega\backslash\Omega^{\prime}
,

T^{+}_{\omega}\cong x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
, for some
J\subseteq S
. By Theorem
3.1.3
,
T^{+}_{\omega}
has a filtration by
dual left-cell modules
S_{\tau}
,
\tau\in\Omega
. (This follows already from (
2.3.4
).)
Each
S_{\tau}
is, by construction,
{\mathcal{Z}}
-free, so
T^{+}_{\omega}
is
also
{\mathcal{Z}}
-free.

Now we must verify conditions (1), (2), and (3) in the Stratification Hypothesis
1.3.3
.
Given
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
, Condition (1) follows from Theorem
2.4.10
. If
\omega\in\Omega\backslash\Omega^{\prime}
, then Condition (1) follows from Theorem
3.1.3
.

Suppose that
\omega,\tau\in\Omega
, and
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})}(S_{\omega},T^{+}_{\tau})\not=0
.
Then, setting
K=\mathbb{Q}(t)
,
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{K}}(S_{\omega,K},S_{\nu,K})\not=0
, so for some
\tau\in\Omega
,
\tau\preceq\nu
. Since
S_{\omega,K}
and
S_{\nu,K}
share a composition factor, they belong to the same (dual) two-sided cell
module. Thus,
\omega
and
\nu
belong to the same two-sided cell for the preorder
\leq_{LR}
, or, equivalently
for
\leq_{LR}^{{\text{\rm op}}}
. Since
\preceq
is strictly compatible with
\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}
,
\omega
and
\nu
belong to the same two-sided cell for
\preceq
. It follows that
\tau\preceq\omega
.
This proves (2).

Next, we prove condition (3). In case
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
, we can apply the 4-term exact sequence
in
[
DPS17
, Prop. A.2]
and the vanishing of
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})}(S,X)
in Theorem
2.4.10

to conclude that condition (3) holds in this case. If
\omega\in\Omega\backslash\Omega^{\prime}
, we can argue in the same
way, but using Theorem
3.1.3
and Proposition
2.3.2
.∎

The following is a corollary of the proof of the theorem, and Construction
2.4.12
, using Theorem
2.4.10
, Theorem
3.1.3
, Proposition

2.3.2
, displays (
2.3.4
), and (
3.1.1
). The last assertion of the corollary follows from Theorem
2.4.10
(
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
) and the last assertion of Theorem

3.1.3
(when
\omega\not\in\Omega^{\prime}
).

Corollary 3.3.2
.

Let
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})=({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}),{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*}))
and keep the notation of the theorem above. Then,
S_{\omega}
and

T^{+}_{\tau}
belong to
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
, for all
\omega,\tau\in\Omega
. Also,

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}}(S_{\omega},T^{+}_{\tau})=0.

In addition,
S_{\omega}
is a submodule (via an inflation in
{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat}
) of
T^{+}_{\omega}
and is the lowest nonzero term in its height filtration.

If
\Theta
is a poset ideal of
(\Omega,\leq_{L})
, then
(\Theta,\preceq)
is a quasi-poset co-ideal of
(\Omega,\preceq)
. Thus,

T^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}:=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Theta}(T^{+}_{\omega})^{m_{\omega}}\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}
with all
m_{\omega}>0
, as defined in (
2.4.6
), satisfies Stratification Hypothesis
1.3.3
relative to the exact category
({\mathscr{A}}_{\flat},{\mathscr{E}}_{\flat})
.
Theorems
1.3.5
and
3.3.1
immediately imply the following.

Corollary 3.3.3
.

Maintain the notation introduced above and form the endomorphism algebra
A^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}=\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T^{\ddagger}_{\Theta})
and
A^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}
-modules
\Delta^{+}(\omega)=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(S_{\omega},T^{\ddagger}_{\Theta})
and

P^{+}(\omega)=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T^{+}_{\omega},T^{\ddagger}_{\Theta})
. Then,
\{\Delta^{+}(\omega),P^{+}(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Theta}
is a stratifying system for the category
A^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}
-
mod
with respect to the quasi-poset
(\Theta,\preceq)
.

We now complete the proof of Conjecture
3.2.1
.

The above theorem proves
part (1) of Conjecture
3.2.1
. Part (1) of the conjecture deals with the filtration of
X
by dual left-cell modules and follows easily from the construction given above. Our construction here is, in fact, much more informative than that envisioned by
part (1) of the conjecture. For example,
X_{\omega}
is built as an object in
{\mathscr{A}}_{\flat}({\mathscr{L}}^{*})
.
As such, it has a height filtration with sections that are direct sums of dual left-cell modules. Also, left cells associated to distinct sections always lie in distinct two-sided cells.

Part (2) of the conjecture
is phrased in the language of base ring extensions of
{\mathcal{Z}}
, but it is sufficient to check it over
{\mathcal{Z}}
, by
[
DPS98a
, Lem. 1.2.5(4)]
. (Stratifying systems behave well under base change.) Now taking
R={\mathcal{Z}}
, part (2) of the conjecture follows from Theorem
3.3.1
, provided we know the preorder
\preceq
has the same equivalence classes
as
\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}
. The actual preorder
\preceq
in Theorem
3.3.1
has this property (and is constructed from

\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}
, sharing the height function
\mathfrak{h}
with it; see display (
3.1.1
)).

This completes the proof. ∎

The following remarks discuss further the difference between the Conjecture proved in Theorem
3.3.1
and its
original version given in
[
DPS98a
]
. It can be skipped by readers interested only in the more recent version
as stated in Theorem
3.3.1
or in
[
DPS15
, Conj. 1.2]
.

Remarks 3.3.4
.

(a) When
[
DPS98a
, Conj. 2.5.2]
was written and checked for all rank 2 cases,
3
3

3

We correct some typos around the third display on
[
DPS98a
, p. 203]
: …Each module
{\widetilde{S}}_{\{u\}}^{\mathscr{R}}=S_{0^{\prime}}\oplus S_{0^{\prime\prime}}
and
{\widetilde{S}}_{\{v\}}^{\mathscr{R}}=S_{0}\oplus S_{0^{\prime\prime}}
has a length two filtration with two dual left-cell modules
{\widetilde{S}}_{\omega k}
as sections, corresponding to left cells
\omega=B,C_{1},C_{2}
, and
D
(in the notation of (3.5) below). The filtrations are of the form:

{\widetilde{S}}_{\{u\}}^{\mathscr{R}}=\boxed{\begin{matrix}C_{1}\\
D\end{matrix}}\;\text{ and }\;{\widetilde{S}}_{\{v\}}^{\mathscr{R}}=\boxed{\begin{matrix}B\\
C_{2}\end{matrix}},

and …

the best choice of a preorder
\leq
seemed to be

\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}
. In the revised conjecture
[
DPS15
, Conj.1.2]
(identical to Conjecture
3.2.1
above), we realized that this choice of
a preorder was too strong, and so it was weakened to require only a preorder with the same equivalence classes as those of
\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}
. As
discussed in (a), the preorder
\preceq
here in Theorem
3.3.1
satisfies this condition and more, sharing also a height function
\mathfrak{h}
with
\leq_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}
. Moreover,
\preceq
is a preorder completely determined by its equivalence classes and the height function
\mathfrak{h}
, a specificity
giving it some advantage over
\leq^{\text{\rm op}}_{LR}
. For example, suppose
\mathfrak{h}
is given by Lusztig’s
a
-function (see (
4.1.2
) below), a choice of height function we can certainly make, provided conjectures P4 and P11 hold; see
[
Lus03
, 13.6, 14.1]
. If
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
belong to distinct 2-sided cells, to decide if
\omega\preceq\omega^{\prime}
, (equivalent here to
\omega\prec\omega^{\prime}
),
we need only check that
a(\omega)<a(\omega^{\prime})
—which is a much more computable condition
than
\omega\leq^{\text{\rm op}}_{LR}\omega^{\prime}
or
\omega<_{LR}^{\text{\rm op}}\omega^{\prime}
.

(b) The other main difference between the conjecture in
[
DPS98a
]
and its version in
[
DPS15
]
is that
we generally worked over
\mathbb{Z}[t^{2},t^{-2}]
in
[
DPS98a
]
rather than
{\mathcal{Z}}
. A case for using
{\mathcal{Z}}
rather than
\mathbb{Z}[t^{2},t^{-2}]
is that Lusztig’s explicit isomorphism
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}\cong\mathbb{Q}(t)W
holds without exception (for the standard finite Coxeter system, even when
G
has type
{}^{2}F_{4}
). These Hecke algebras are all semisimple and, in fact, split semisimple except for the case
{}^{2}F_{4}
. We mention that the explicit isomorphisms can be obtained by applying
[
Lus03
, 18.12]
to
the cases
R=\mathbb{Q}
and
R=\mathbb{Q}(t)
. This works for any finite
W
satisfying P1–P15, and semisimplicity follows. The splitting, except for the
{}^{2}F_{4}
-case, follows from the isomorphisms
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}\cong\mathbb{Q}(t)W
above, and the fact that
\mathbb{Q}(t)W
(in fact,
\mathbb{Q}W
) is split semisimple; see
[
B71
]
,
[
BC71
]
. The latter reference discusses the Weyl groups arising in the “split” (Chevalley group) cases, also including those
W
for the “Steinberg twisted” cases. The remaining groups, associated to the Ree and Suzuki groups, are finite dihedral groups, and the only new group
W
arising, that has not already been considered, is
the dihedral group
I_{2}(8)
of order 16 associated to
{}^{2}F_{4}
. It has
\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{2}]
as its unique minimal splitting field.

(c)
[
DPS98a
, Conj. 2.5.2]
left an ambiguity, removed in
[
DPS15
, Conj. 1.2]
. In detail, the second sentence of part (2)
of
[
DPS98a
, Conj. 2.5.2]
contains an expression “
\widetilde{A}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}}=\text{\rm{End}}_{\widetilde{H}}({T}^{+}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}})
” which
is a misprint, and should have been written “
{\widetilde{A}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}}=\text{\rm{End}}_{\widetilde{H}}(T^{+})_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}}
.” However, it is shown in

[
DPS98a
, Th. 2.4.4(b)]
that “
\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T)_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}}\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}})
,” not using the “
+
” as a superscript on
T
.
Possibly the same isomorphism holds using
+
, but we only know this to be true if
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
is flat over
{\mathcal{Z}}
.

(d) Finally, we observe that while the methods of
[
DPS98a
]
are useful in studying low rank examples of the
Conjecture, they do not provide much help in the ultimate proof of the Conjecture!

3.4.
A uniqueness theory

The main aim of this section is to provide a companion uniqueness theorem for (the Morita classes of) the algebras
A^{+}
resulting from Construction
2.4.12
.
If
A^{+}
is quasi-hereditary, at least one such uniqueness result is available in Rouquier’s theory of quasi-hereditary covers; see
[
Ro08
, §4]
.

However,
A^{+}
may not be quasi-hereditary.
The method we use instead applies in a quite general, but natural, context of endomorphism algebras of suitably filtered injective–like objects in an exact category (e.g., the object
T^{+}
in Theorem
3.3.1
). We begin with a description of this general context (with a notation that is independent of previous sections, though chosen to be suggestive).

Let
{\mathscr{K}}
be a commutative Noetherian ring, and let
H
be a finite and projective

{\mathscr{K}}
-algebra. We view
H
and its modules as described by
{\mathscr{K}}
-module structure.
Let
({\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{E}})
be a strict and full exact subcategory of mod–
H
. Recall this means that
{\mathscr{A}}
is strict (closed under
isomorphisms) and full in mod–
H
and that all conflations in
{\mathscr{E}}
are also short exact sequences in mod
-H
. Given
M\in{\mathscr{A}}
, a filtration

0=M^{-1}\subseteq M^{0}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq M^{\ell}=M
in mod–
H
is called an
{\mathscr{E}}
-filtration provided
each short exact sequence
0\to M^{i-1}\to M^{i}\to M^{i}/M^{i-1}\to 0
(
0\leq i\leq\ell
) in mod-
H
is a conflation in
{\mathscr{E}}
. (In particular, each
M^{i}
belongs to
{\mathscr{A}}
.)

Lemma 3.4.1
.

Suppose
M\in{\mathscr{A}}
has an
{\mathscr{E}}
-filtration, indexed as above, and that
T
is an object in
{\mathscr{A}}
such that
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{\mathscr{E}}(N,T)=0
for each section
N=M^{i}/M^{i-1}
,
0\leq i\leq\ell
. Then,

0\to\text{\rm{Hom}}_{H}(M^{j}/M^{i},T)\to\text{\rm{Hom}}_{H}(M^{j},T)\to\text{\rm{Hom}}_{H}(M^{i},T)\to 0

is a short exact sequence, for all
0\leq i<j\leq\ell
. In particular, the map

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{H}(M,T)\to\text{\rm{Hom}}_{H}(M^{0},T)

is surjective.

For the proof, see the Claim in the proof of Theorem
1.3.5
.

Now let
{\mathscr{S}}
be a (strict) full additive subcategory of
{\mathscr{A}}
generated by a finite set
\{S_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
of
nonzero objects indexed by a finite set
\Omega
. That is, every object in
{\mathscr{S}}
is a finite direct sum of objects

S_{\omega}
,
\omega\in\Omega
. Define
{\mathscr{A}}_{{\mathscr{E}}}({\mathscr{S}})
to be the subcategory of objects
M
in
{\mathscr{A}}
which have
an
{\mathscr{E}}
-filtration with every section
M^{i}/M^{i-1}
in
{\mathscr{S}}
.

Note that, by Corollary
3.3.2
, the theorem below applies in particular to the algebra
A^{+}
in the conclusion of the main Theorem
3.3.1
, using
A^{+}
for

A^{\dagger}
.

Theorem 3.4.2
.

Assume that, for each
\omega\in\Omega
, there exists an object
X_{\omega}\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})

such that

(1)

X_{\omega}\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})
has an
{\mathscr{E}}
-filtration with lowest section
S_{\omega}
(and all other sections in
{\mathscr{S}}
), and

(2)

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}}(S_{\omega^{\prime}},X_{\omega})=0
, for all
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
.

If
A^{\dagger}=\text{\rm{End}}_{H}(T^{\dagger})
where
T^{\dagger}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}X_{\omega}
,
then the Morita class of
A^{\dagger}
is independent of the choices of the
X_{\omega}
satisfying (1) and (2).

As a corollary, we have the following result, whose statement will also be used in the proof of the theorem.

Corollary 3.4.3
.

Suppose that, for each
\omega\in\Omega
, there are given objects
X_{\omega},Y_{\omega}\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{S}})
such that conditions (1) and (2) in Theorem
3.4.2
hold for each

X_{\omega}
and maintain the
notations
T^{\dagger}

and
A^{\dagger}
. Assume also that each
Y_{\omega}
satisfies condition (2) in the sense that
\text{\rm{Ext}}^{1}_{{\mathscr{E}}}(S_{\omega^{\prime}},Y_{\omega})=0
, for all
\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
.

Put

T^{\dagger\prime}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}(X_{\omega}\oplus Y_{\omega})
and
A^{\dagger\prime}:=\text{\rm{End}}_{H}(T^{\dagger\prime})
.
Then,
A^{\dagger}
and
A^{\dagger\prime}
are Morita equivalent.

Note that, as a special case of this corollary,
A^{\ddagger}:=A^{\ddagger}_{\Omega}
in (
2.4.7
) is Morita equivalent to
A^{+}
.

Proof.

It is clear that the theorem implies the corollary by using
X_{\omega}\oplus Y_{\omega}
for
X_{\omega}

satisfying (1) and (2). The filtration of the direct sum starts with that of
X_{\omega}
, then uses that of

Y_{\omega}
.
However, it is also true that the corollary implies the theorem. In fact, if
Y_{\omega}
also
satisfies condition (1), then the roles of
X_{\omega}
and
Y_{\omega}
can be interchanged in the corollary, thus
giving Morita equivalences (designated here by
\sim
)

(3.4.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{\rm{End}}_{H}(\bigoplus_{\omega}Y_{\omega})\sim\text{\rm{End}}_{H}(\bigoplus_{\omega}(Y_{\omega}\oplus X_{\omega}))\\
\cong&\text{\rm{End}}_{H}(\bigoplus_{\omega}(X_{\omega}\oplus Y_{\omega}))\sim\text{\rm{End}}_{H}(\bigoplus_{\omega}X_{\omega}).\end{aligned}\right.

Thus, the corollary and the theorem are equivalent, so it remains to prove the corollary directly. We follow the
strategy in
[
DPS18
, proof of Th. 4.1]
.

Let

e\in A^{\dagger\prime}=\text{\rm{End}}_{H}(T^{\dagger\prime})
be the idempotent projection of
T^{\dagger\prime}
onto
T^{\dagger}\subseteq T^{\dagger\prime}
along
\bigoplus_{\omega}Y_{\omega}
. Since
A^{\dagger}\cong eA^{\dagger\prime}e
, it suffices to show that the projective
module
A^{\dagger\prime}e
is a progenerator, or, equivalently, that every irreducible
A^{{+\prime}}
-module
L
is a homomorphic image of
A^{\dagger\prime}e
. (A better-known criterion is that
A^{\dagger\prime}eA^{\dagger\prime}=A^{\dagger\prime}
. However, if this fails, then the two-sided ideal generated by
e
is contained in
a maximal left ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
A^{\dagger\prime}
. Take

L=A^{\dagger\prime}/\mathfrak{m}
, and note that
eL=0
, so that
L
cannot be a homomorphic image of
A^{\dagger\prime}e
.)

However,
A^{\dagger\prime}
is filtered by modules

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{H}(S_{\omega},T^{\dagger\prime})
by Lemma
3.4.1
, and each of these is a homomorphic image of
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{H}(T^{\dagger},T^{\dagger\prime})
again by Lemma
3.4.1
. The corollary now follows from the identification

A^{\dagger\prime}e\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{H}(T^{\dagger},T^{\dagger\prime})
of
A^{\dagger\prime}
-modules. This completes the proof of the corollary and, as noted, the theorem.
∎

Remark 3.4.4
.

The proof of Corollary
3.4.3
can be used to give an explicit description of a Morita (
{\mathscr{K}}
-category) equivalence

A^{\dagger}\text{-mod}\overset{\sim}{\to}A^{\dagger\prime}\text{-mod}
, where
A^{\dagger}
and
A^{\dagger\prime}
are as stated in the corollary. First, identify
A^{\dagger}
-mod with
eA^{\dagger\prime}e
-mod as above. Then, use the well-known equivalence

A^{\dagger\prime}\otimes_{eA^{\dagger\prime}e}(-):eA^{\dagger\prime}e\text{-mod}\longrightarrow A^{\dagger\prime}\text{-mod}.

Theorem 3.4.5
.

Let
A^{+}
be a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra as constructed in the hypothesis of
Theorem
3.3.1
and let
A^{+\prime}
be a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra also constructed to satisfy this hypothesis. (Here we use the same
height function for
A^{+}
and
A^{+\prime},
but allow the
X_{\omega}
to vary.) Then
A^{+}
is Morita equivalent to
A^{+\prime}
.

The proof is immediate from Corollary
3.3.2
using Theorem
3.4.2
. Further details on the Morita
equivalence can be obtained from the proof of the latter theorem and its corollary.

Remarks 3.4.6
.

We now mention several useful applications of Theorem
3.4.2
and its corollary:

(a) The identification
A^{\dagger\prime}e\cong\text{Hom}_{H}(T^{\dagger},T^{\dagger\prime})
in the proof of the corollary, together with the corresponding identification for
eA^{\dagger\prime}
and the equality
A^{\dagger\prime}eA^{\dagger\prime}=A^{\dagger\prime}=\text{End}_{H}(T^{\dagger\prime})
, shows:

(i)

the trace of
T^{\dagger}
is all of
T^{\dagger\prime}
. In particular,

(ii)

any
H
-projective direct summand of
T^{\dagger\prime}
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
(T^{\dagger})^{\oplus m}
, for a sufficiently large
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(b) Part (ii) of (a) may be used to prove the claim made in
[
DPS17
, Remark 4.10(a)]
that
H=\mathcal{H}
is a direct summand of
T^{\dagger}
there in the conclusion of
[
DPS17
, Th. 4.9]
. (In the context of
[
DPS17
, Remark 4.10(a)]
, we are allowed to replace
T^{\dagger}
here by
(T^{\dagger})^{\oplus m}
.) In fact, it can also be proved that
T^{\dagger}
on
[
DPS17
, p. 245]
, taking the multiplicities
m_{\omega}
all equal to 1, always has the regular representation
H=\mathcal{H}
as a direct summand.

(c) Other Morita equivalences in the spirit of Corollary

3.4.3
are developed in
[
DPS18
, §4]
. Conversely, Theorem
3.4.2
can be used to prove
[
DPS18
, Th. 4.3]
. The theorem gives a Morita equivalence between localizations of stratified algebras in
[
DPS17
]
with algebras in
[
DPS15
]
which base change to algebras arising in a Cherednik category
{\mathcal{O}}
context (with equal parameters).

3.5.
The standard stratification of
A^{+}

We now use the stratifying system
\{\Delta(\omega),P(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
in the category
A^{+}
-mod of finite
A^{+}
-modules given in Theorem
3.3.1
to construct a sequence of stratifying ideals in
A^{+}
and to show that
A^{+}
is a standardly stratified algebra.

Let
{\mathscr{K}}
be a commutative Noetherian ring and let
B
be a
{\mathscr{K}}
-algebra which is finite and projective as a
{\mathscr{K}}
-module.

Definition 3.5.1

(
[
DPS18
, Defs. 3.1&3.2]
)
.

An ideal
J
in
B
is called a
standard stratifying ideal
if the following conditions hold:

(HI1)

B/J
is projective over
{\mathscr{K}}
;

(HI2)

{}_{B}J
is
B
-projective as a left
B
-module;

(HI3)

J^{2}=J
.

The algebra
B
over
{\mathscr{K}}
is called a
standardly stratified algebra
(SSA) provided there exists
a finite “defining sequence” of ideals in
B
:

(3.5.1)

0=J_{0}\subseteq J_{1}\subseteq J_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}=B

such that, for
0<i\leq m
,
J_{i}/J_{i-1}
is a standard stratifying ideal in
B/J_{i-1}
. We also call (
3.5.1
) a
standard stratification
.

We will see in Section 4.3 that stratifying ideals and standardly stratified algebras are weakened (more general) versions of heredity ideals and quasi-hereditary algebras.

We first observe the following nice properties.

Proposition 3.5.2
.

(1) Every ideal in the standard stratification (
3.5.1
) is an idempotent ideal.

(2) Suppose that
J
is a stratifying ideal in a
{\mathscr{K}}
-algebra
B
. If
M,N
are
B/J
-modules which are also regarded as
B
-modules by inflation, then, for any integer
n\geq 0
, there is
a
{\mathscr{K}}
-module isomorphism

\text{\rm{Ext}}^{n}_{B/J}(M,N)\longrightarrow\text{\rm{Ext}}^{n}_{B}(M,N).

Proof.

A proof for (2) can be found in
[
DPS17
, Appendix B]
. We now prove (1).
Since
J_{1}^{2}=J_{1}
and
(J_{2}/J_{1})^{2}=J_{2}/J_{1}
, it follows that

J_{2}^{2}=J_{2}^{2}+J_{1}^{2}=J_{2}^{2}+J_{1}=J_{2}+J_{1}=J_{2}.

Hence,
J_{2}
is an idempotent ideal. An induction argument proves the first assertion.
∎

We now prove that the Hecke endomorphism
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra
A^{+}
is a standardly stratified algebra. There is a general proof of such a fact for any
{\mathscr{K}}
-algebra
B
which is finite projective over
{\mathscr{K}}
, provided that the category
B
-mod has a stratifying system; see
[
DPS18
, §3]
. The proof for
A^{+}
below is independent and more direct, laying down a foundation for a further proof of the quasi-heredity of a base-changed
A^{+\natural}
in the next chapter.

Theorem 3.5.3
.

If
A^{+}
is the (extended) generic Hecke endo-algebra associated with a standard finite Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
as defined in (
2.4.4
),
then the
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra
A^{+}
is a standardly stratified algebra.

Proof.

We maintain the notation of Theorem
3.3.1
, so, in particular,

A^{+}=\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T^{+})
is a certain endomorphism algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}
, where
T^{+}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}T^{+}_{\omega}
, and the category
A^{+}
-mod has a stratifying system
\{\Delta(\omega),P(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
.
Recall also from Theorem
3.3.1
that, for each
\omega\in\Omega
, the left
A^{+}
-module
s
\Delta(\omega)
and

P(\omega)
are defined as
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\omega},T^{+})
and

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T_{\omega}^{+},T^{+})
, respectively,
with the action of
A^{+}:=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T^{+},T^{+})
through compositions of morphisms (between right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules).

Now, put
P:=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}P(\omega)
and observe that
P\cong A^{+}
as left
A^{+}
-modules. (Use the equality
\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}T^{+}_{\omega}=T^{+}
of right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules.)

Let
\mathfrak{h}:\Omega\to\mathbb{Z}
and
\preceq
be as discussed above Proposition
3.1.1
, and used in Theorem
3.3.1
. Here
\Omega
is the set of (Kazhdan–Lusztig) left cells of
W
, and
(\Omega,\preceq)
is the quasi-poset used in Theorem
3.3.1
. For more details on our set-up here, see the paragraph above the statement of Theorem
3.3.1
and the internal references it provides.

Our immediate strategy is to construct a tractable “defining sequence” of ideals in
A^{+}
(see Def.
3.5.1
)

(3.5.2)

0=J_{0}\subseteq J_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}=A^{+}

such that all the conditions (HI1)–(HI3) in Definition
3.5.1
, for every
J=J_{i}/J_{i-1}
, hold as an ideal of
B=A^{+}/J_{i-1}
.

We construct the defining sequence for
A^{+}
as follows. First, for any
\omega\in\Omega
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define a submodule
P(\omega)_{j}\subseteq P(\omega)
as the sum of all images in
P(\omega)
of all maps in
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(P(\mu),P(\omega))
with
\mathfrak{h}(\mu)\geq j
. Using the ring-theoretic language of traces, we may write this as

P(\omega)_{j}=\sum_{\mathfrak{h}(\mu)\geq j}\text{trace}_{P(\omega)}(P(\mu))=\text{trace}_{P(\omega)}\Big(\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{h}(\mu)\geq j}P(\mu)\Big):=\sum_{f\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(X,P(\omega))}\text{Im}(f),

where
X=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{h}(\mu)\geq j}P(\mu)
.
Observe that
P(\omega)_{j+1}\subseteq P(\omega)_{j}
. It is proved
4
4

4

The method of proof reorders the sections
\Delta(\nu)
in any given filtration of
P(\omega)
that has sections given by various
\Delta(\nu)
’s with
\nu\in\Omega
. The multiplicities of any given
\Delta(\nu)
does change in this process.
in
[
DPS17
, Prop. 2.2]
and
[
DPS18
, Prop. 3.9]
that
P(\omega)_{j}/P(\omega)_{j+1}
is a direct sum of modules
\Delta(\nu)
with
\nu\in\Omega
and
\mathfrak{h}(\nu)=j
. Multiplicities are allowed. That is, isomorphic copies of the same
\Delta(\nu)
may appear in the direct sum.

Next, we may repeat the above discussion of
P(\omega)_{j}
with
P
replacing
P(\omega)
. That is, we define

(3.5.3)

P_{j}=\text{trace}_{P}\Big(\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{h}(\mu)\geq j}P(\mu)\Big).

Clearly,
P_{j}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}P(\omega)_{j}
, since
P=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}P(\omega)
as a left
A^{+}
-module. From this we obtain
P_{j+1}\subseteq P_{j}
and deduce also that
P_{j}/P_{j+1}
is a direct sum of
A^{+}
-modules isomorphic to
\Delta(\nu)
, for some
\nu\in\Omega
, with
\mathfrak{h}(\nu)=j
. If the equality holds for a given
\nu
, then some
\Delta(\nu)
does occur.

We are now ready to return to
A^{+}
and its ideals. We have observed near the beginning of this proof that
P\cong A^{+}
as left
A^{+}
-modules. For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
A_{j}^{+}
denote the left ideal in
A^{+}
identifying with
P_{j}\subseteq P
.

Claim.

Each
A_{j}^{+}
is a two-sided ideal of
A^{+}
. It is idempotent (allowing zero), and
A^{+}/A^{+}_{j}
is free as a
\mathcal{Z}
-module.

To prove the claim, we first show that each
A^{+}_{j}
is a two-sided ideal in
A^{+}
. By definition, it is the left ideal of
A^{+}
corresponding to the left
A^{+}
-submodule
P_{j}
of
P
under the natural identification
P\cong A^{+}
of left
A^{+}
-modules discussed above. Similarly,
A^{+}/A^{+}_{j}
is isomorphic to
P/P_{j}
as left
A^{+}
-modules. As such, it is filtered by copies of various modules
\Delta(\nu)
with
\mathfrak{h}(\nu)<j
, and
A^{+}_{j}
is similarly filtered by modules
\Delta(\tau)
with
\mathfrak{h}(\tau)\geq j
. (In both cases, “filtered by” refers informally to a filtration by left
A^{+}
-modules, with the given
\Delta
’s as the only nonzero sections.)

If
A^{+}_{j}
were not a two-sided ideal in
A^{+}
, it would not be a right ideal, and there would be an element
a\in A^{+}
with
A^{+}_{j}a\not\subseteq A^{+}_{j}
. This implies that the composition

A^{+}_{j}\longrightarrow A^{+}_{j}a\longrightarrow(A^{+}_{j}a+A^{+}_{j})/A^{+}_{j}\subseteq A^{+}/A^{+}_{j}

of left
A^{+}
-module homomorphisms would be non-zero. Combining this with the above paragraph, we find from this that there is a nonzero map
\Delta(\tau)\to\Delta(\nu)
with
\mathfrak{h}(\tau)>\mathfrak{h}(\nu)
. However, this is impossible. It leads to a nonzero composite
P(\tau)\twoheadrightarrow\Delta(\tau)\to\Delta(\nu)
, implying
\tau\preceq\nu
, by the axiom (SS2) for stratifying systems. But
\tau\preceq\nu
and
\mathfrak{h}(\tau)>\mathfrak{h}(\nu)
cannot both hold, by the compatibility of
\mathfrak{h}
with
\preceq
; see (
3.1.1
). This contradiction shows that each
A^{+}_{j}
is a two-sided ideal.

Next, we show each ideal
A_{j}^{+}
is idempotent. In fact, we will show it is generated by an idempotent element
e_{j}\in A^{+}_{j}
(if
A^{+}_{j}\neq 0
).

First, we need some refinements of our notation. Define

P_{j,0}=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{h}(\omega)\geq j}P(\omega)_{j}\;\;\text{ and }\;\;P_{j,1}=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{h}(\omega)<j}P(\omega)_{j}.

Note that the trace definition
P(\omega)_{j}=\text{trace}_{P(\omega)}\Big(\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{h}(\mu)\geq j}P(\mu)\Big)
implies
P(\omega)_{j}\subseteq P(\omega)
, for any
\omega\in\Omega
, and
P(\omega)_{j}=P(\omega)
if
\mathfrak{h}(\omega)\geq j
. (In particular,
P_{j,0}=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{h}(\omega)\geq j}P(\omega)
.) Recalling the identity
P_{j}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}P(\omega)_{j}
, we have

P_{j}=P_{j,0}\oplus P_{j,1}
. It is also useful to set
\widehat{P}_{j,1}=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{h}(\omega)<j}P(\omega)
; so that
P_{j,1}\subseteq\widehat{P}_{j,1}
and
P=P_{j,0}\oplus\widehat{P}_{j,1}
.

We are almost ready to define
e_{j}
. First, let
A^{+},A^{+}_{j},A^{+}_{j,0},A^{+}_{j,1},\widehat{A}^{+}_{j,1},\ldots
correspond to
P,P_{j},P_{j,0},P_{j,1},\widehat{P}_{j,1},\ldots
under our standard isomorphism
A^{+}\cong P
of left
A^{+}
-modules. Next, let
f_{j}:P\twoheadrightarrow P_{j,0}\subseteq P_{j}\subseteq P
be the obvious idempotent endomorphism of
P
passing through
P_{j}
. Then, there is a unique idempotent
e_{j}\in A^{+}
such that right multiplication by
e_{j}
gives the map
A^{+}\twoheadrightarrow A^{+}_{j,0}\subseteq A^{+}_{j}\subseteq A^{+}
corresponding to
f_{j}
. Thus,
e_{j}
is idempotent (since
e_{j}^{2}=1\cdot e_{j}^{2}=f_{j}^{2}(1)=f_{j}(1)=1\cdot e_{j}=e_{j}
), and
e_{j}\in A^{+}_{j}
(since
e_{j}=1\cdot e_{j}\in A^{+}_{j,0}\subseteq A_{j}^{+}
).

To complete the proof that
A^{+}_{j}
is an idempotent ideal in
A^{+}
, it is enough now (and even necessary and sufficient) to show
A^{+}e_{j}A^{+}=A^{+}_{j}
. This is actually fairly easy. The submodule
A^{+}e_{j}
of
A^{+}_{j}
corresponds, by construction, to the submodule
P_{j,0}
of
P
. Also, we have the equality
P_{j}=\text{trace}_{P}(P_{j,0})
by definition. It follows, passing to
A^{+}
, that

(3.5.4)

A^{+}_{j}=\text{trace}_{A^{+}}(A^{+}_{j,0})=\text{trace}_{A^{+}}(A^{+}e_{j})=\sum_{f\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(A^{+}e_{j},A^{+})}\text{Im}(f)=A^{+}e_{j}A^{+}.

Here the last equality follows from a well-known identification of
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(A^{+}e_{j},A^{+})
with
e_{j}A^{+}
, which leads to an identification of trace
{}_{A^{+}}(A^{+}e_{j})
with
A^{+}e_{j}A^{+}
.

To prove the last remaining assertion of the claim that
A^{+}/A^{+}_{j}
is free as a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module, it is enough to show
A^{+}_{i}/A^{+}_{i+1}
is free for every
i<j
. However, we have already observed that the isomorphic module
P_{i}/P_{i+1}
is a direct sum of
A^{+}
-modules isomorphic to
\Delta(\nu)
for some
\nu\in\Omega
. Thus, it is projective (of finite rank) over
{\mathcal{Z}}
, hence free, by Swan’s theorem. See Remark
1.3.6
(c). The claim is now proved.

We are now able to construct a candidate for the defining sequence (
3.5.2
) previewed earlier in this proof.

Observe that
A^{+}_{j}=0
, for all sufficiently large integers
j
. In fact, it suffices to take
j>\mathfrak{h}(\omega)
, for all
\omega\in\Omega
, using the trace formula for
P_{j}
. Put
J_{0}=0
, and define
J_{1}=A^{+}_{j(1)}
, where
j(1)
denotes the largest value of
\mathfrak{h}
on
\Omega
. Continuing, define
J_{2}=A^{+}_{j(2)}
, where
j(2)
denotes the largest value of
\mathfrak{h}
on
\Omega\backslash\Omega_{j(1)}
with
\Omega_{j(1)}=\{\omega\in\Omega\mid\mathfrak{h}(\omega)=j(1)\}
. This process continues until
j(1),j(2),\ldots,j(m)
have been defined as all the elements of
\mathfrak{h}(\Omega)
, listed in decreasing order. For each integer
i
with
1\leq i\leq m
, we set
J_{i}=A^{+}_{j(i)}
, generalizing our definitions for
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
. We note that
A^{+}_{j(m)}=A^{+}
, since
P_{j(m)}=P
by the trace formula. Thus,
J_{m}=A^{+}_{j(m)}=A^{+}
, the terminal member of our ascending sequence of
J_{i}
’s:

(3.5.5)

0=J_{0}\subseteq J_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}=A^{+}\;\text{ with }\;j(1)>j(2)>\cdots>j(m).

(The general inclusion
P_{j}\subseteq P_{j-1}
, which holds for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, also implies that
P_{j}\subseteq P_{j-a}
, for any positive
a\in\mathbb{Z}
. Consequently,

A^{+}_{j}\subseteq A^{+}_{j-a}
. So
A^{+}_{j(i)}\subseteq A^{+}_{j(i+1)}
, using
j=j(i)
and
a=j(i)-j(i+1)
. Here,
i
is an integer with
1\leq i\leq m-1
. Since we have defined
J_{i}=A^{+}_{j(i)}
for each
i
, as well as
i=m
, we have the desired sequence
0=J_{0}\subseteq J_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}=A^{+}
.)

Continuing the discussion above, we now wish to analyze more closely the left
A^{+}
-modules
J_{1}=J_{1}/J_{0}
and
J_{i}/J_{i-1}
for
2\leq i\leq m
. We start with
J_{1}=A^{+}_{j(1)}
. Recall that
j(1)
is the largest value of the height function so that
A^{+}_{j}=0
, for
j>j(1)
. (Use the trace formula for
P_{j}
.) Thus,
A^{+}_{j(1)}=A^{+}_{j(1)}/A^{+}_{j(1)+1}
is a direct sum of various left
A^{+}
-modules
\Delta(\omega)
with
\mathfrak{h}(\omega)=j(1)
.
These modules are projective for
A^{+}
, by the maximality of
\mathfrak{h}(\omega)
. (Just observe that
P(\omega)=\Delta(\omega)
by axiom (SS3).)

We now consider
J_{i}/J_{i-1}
for
2\leq i\leq m
(the case
i=1
having been just discussed). We note

(3.5.6)

J_{i}/J_{i-1}=A^{+}_{j(i)}/A^{+}_{j(i-1)}=A^{+}_{j(i)}/A^{+}_{j(i)+1}.

The right-hand equality holds because
j(i)<j(i-1)
are adjacent values of the height function. Consequently, for any integer
j
with
j(i)+1\leq j<j(i-1)
, the
A^{+}
-module
A^{+}_{j}/A^{+}_{j+1}
must be 0. (Use our description, earlier in this proof, of
P_{j}/P_{j+1}
as a direct sum of modules

\Delta(\omega)
with
j=\mathfrak{h}(\omega)
.) But this gives
A^{+}_{j}=A^{+}_{j+1}
, with repeated application giving
A^{+}_{j(i)+1}=A^{+}_{j(i-1)}
, as desired.

We are now in a position to prove condition (HI2), the projectivity of
J:=J_{i}/J_{i-1}
as a left
B
-module, for
B:=A^{+}/J_{i-1}
,
1\leq i\leq m
. (The case
i=1
has already been treated.) We have
J=A^{+}_{j(i)}/A^{+}_{j(i)+1}
by (
3.5.6
). Since
j(i)
is a height function value, we know that
J
in this latter quotient form is the direct sum of various
A^{+}/A^{+}_{j(i)+1}
-modules isomorphic to
\Delta(\omega)
with
\mathfrak{h}(\omega)=j(i)
. (Such a module may be regarded as a left
A^{+}
- or
A^{+}/A^{+}_{j(i)+1}
-module.) Recall that we have direct sum formulas
P=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}P(\omega)
and
P_{j}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}P(\omega)_{j}
for each integer
j
, and variations. In particular, note that
P/P_{j+1}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}P(\omega)/P(\omega)_{j+1}
.
When interpreted with
A^{+}
replacing
P
and
j=j(i)
, we find that the left-hand side is
B=A^{+}/A^{+}_{j(i)+1}
, and the right-hand side, viewed a left
A^{+}
-module, has
\Delta(\omega)
as a direct summand whenever
\mathfrak{h}(\omega)=j(i)
, as is the case for
\Delta(\omega)
above. Thus,
\Delta(\omega)
, as a left
B
-module, is projective. Since the
\Delta(\omega)
also represents the general direct summand of
J
, we have proved
J
is projective as a left
B
-module, as desired. (Note also the
A^{+}
analog of the formula
P_{j}/P_{j+1}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}P(\omega)_{j}/P(\omega)_{j+1}
. For
j=j(i)
, the left-hand side becomes
A^{+}_{j(i)}/A^{+}_{j(i)+1}=A^{+}_{j(i)}/A^{+}_{j(i-1)}=J_{i}/J_{i-1}
, which is
J
in the current context. On the right, we obtain a direct sum of modules
\Delta(\omega)
with
\mathfrak{h}(\omega)=j(i)
, all now known to be projective as left
B
-modules.)

This completes the proof of (HI2) for
B
and
J
and all choices of
i
with
1\leq i\leq m
. Properties (HI1) and (HI3) hold as immediate consequence of our claim studied several paragraphs above.
This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

In Chapter 4, we will consider the localization
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
of
{\mathcal{Z}}
at the multiplicative set generated by all bad primes for
W
; see Remark
0.1.1
. By base change from
{\mathcal{Z}}
to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, (
3.5.2
) gives rise to a sequence of ideals of
A^{+\natural}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes A^{+}
:

(3.5.7)

0=J_{0}^{\natural}\subseteq J_{1}^{\natural}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}^{\natural}=A^{+\natural}.

We will prove in Section 4.3 that every ideal
J^{\natural}:=J_{i}^{\natural}/J_{i-1}^{\natural}
(
1\leq i\leq m
) is a “heredity ideal of split type” in
B^{\natural}:=A^{+\natural}/J_{i-1}^{\natural}
. More precisely, every
\text{\rm{End}}_{B^{\natural}}(J^{\natural})
is a direct product of matrix algebras
M_{n}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
. Consequently, if
W
is not of type
{}^{2}F_{4}
,
A^{+\natural}
is a (split) quasi-hereditary algebra.

Remark 3.5.4
.

Replacing
\Omega
by a poset ideal
\Theta
of
(\Omega,\leq_{L})
,
T^{+}
by the
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
T^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}
,
A^{+}
by
A^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}
as defined in (
2.4.6
), and using the stratifying system in Corollary
3.3.3
(thus, replacing
\Delta(\omega),P(\omega),P
, etc. by
\Delta^{+}(\omega),P^{+}(\omega),P^{+}
, etc.) throughout the proof of Theorem
3.5.3
, we may construct a standard stratification

(3.5.8)

0=J_{0}^{+}\subseteq J_{1}^{+}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}^{+}=A^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}.

for the standardly stratified algebra
A^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}
.

Chapter 4
The quasi-heredity of
A^{+\natural}

We proved in the last chapter that the enlarged Hecke endomorphism algebra
A^{+}
is standardly stratified, a property reminiscent of quasi-hereditary algebras
[
CPS88
]
. In this chapter, we will prove that its base change
A^{+\natural}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}}A^{+}
over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
(in which all bad primes are invertible) is
integral
quasi-hereditary; see, e.g.,
[
CPS90
]
(also
[
DPS18
]
) for integral quasi-hereditary algebras.

4.1.
Maximal orders
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathsf{J}}
arising from Lusztig’s asymptotic form
{\mathsf{J}}

Let
(W,S,L)
be a standard finite Coxeter system associated to a finite group
G
of Lie type, and let
{\mathcal{H}}
be its generic Hecke algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}:=\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]
. As noted in Section 1.2, Lusztig’s conjectures P1–P15 all hold for this situation. Also,
W
is, of course, finite, so
the standing hypotheses of Lusztig used in
[
Lus03
, §18]
hold.
In this section, we temporarily exclude the case that
G
is of
type
{}^{2}F_{4}
, i.e., we assume
W
is not the dihedral group
I_{2}(8)
of order 16.
We will treat this
case in a short appendix at the end of this section. (However, everything up through (
4.1.6
) holds for

{}^{2}F_{4}
. Recall that the smallest splitting field of
I_{2}(8)
is
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})
, the field generated by its irreducible character values.
The other Weyl groups have
splitting field
\mathbb{Q}
.)

Let
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}:={\mathcal{S}}^{-1}{\mathcal{Z}}
, the localization of
{\mathcal{Z}}
at the multiplicative set
{\mathcal{S}}
generated by the
bad primes of
W
.
1
1

1

As noted with more detail in Remark
0.1.1
, one could use “bad prime” in the traditional sense here, or, for our purposes, simply define it to be a prime divisor of the generic degree denominator
m
for a principal series character.
Set
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}{\mathcal{H}}
. Of course,
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
is a unique factorization domain of Krull dimension 2 (which is regular). In particular, its height one
prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}
are all principal and are given (in this case) as follows:
(i)
\mathfrak{p}=p{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, where
p\in\mathbb{Z}
is a good prime for
W
; and (ii)
\mathfrak{p}=f(t){\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
,
where
f(t)
is an irreducible polynomial in
\mathbb{Z}[t]
, of positive degree and not equal to
\pm t
.
This description follows easily from
[
Mum88
, Example H, p. 74]
, noting that the prime ideals of
{\mathcal{Z}}
correspond
naturally to those of
\mathbb{Z}[t]
which do not contain
t
.

Before going further we introduce some general terminology on lattices and orders over a Noetherian integral
domain
{\mathscr{K}}
. We have
{\mathscr{K}}={\mathcal{Z}}
or
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
in mind. Often it will be convenient to let
K
be the fraction
field of
{\mathscr{K}}
, e.g.,
K=\mathbb{Q}(t)
, if
{\mathscr{K}}={\mathcal{Z}}
or
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
.

Definitions 4.1.1
.

(a) For any finite-dimensional
K
-space
V
, a
(full)
{\mathscr{K}}
-lattice
in
V
is a finite

{\mathscr{K}}
-submodule
M
in
V
such that
KM=V
, where

KM:=\Big\{\sum r_{i}m_{i}(\text{finite sum})\mid r_{i}\in K,m_{i}\in M\Big\}.

Following
[
Reiner03
, p. 108]
, a
{\mathscr{K}}
-order
is a
{\mathscr{K}}
-subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}
of a finite-dimensional
K
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, having the same identity as
\mathfrak{A}
, such that
{\mathcal{A}}
is a full
{\mathscr{K}}
-lattice in
\mathfrak{A}
.
Unless otherwise noted, from now on, the term “lattice” refers to a “full lattice.”

(b) Also, by some abuse of notation, we shall refer to a module
M
for a
{\mathscr{K}}
-order

{\mathcal{A}}
as an
{\mathcal{A}}
-lattice if it inherits a
{\mathscr{K}}
-lattice structure from the action of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
A
{\mathscr{K}}
-order
{\mathcal{A}}
in
\mathfrak{A}
is a
maximal
{\mathscr{K}}
-order
if it is not properly contained in a larger
{\mathscr{K}}
-order in
\mathfrak{A}
; see
[
Reiner03
, p. 110]
.

To give a common example of a maximal order in a finite-dimensional
K
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, assume
that
{\mathscr{K}}
is integrally closed and
\mathfrak{A}
is a direct product
\prod_{i}M_{n_{i}}(K)
of a finite number of matrix algebras
M_{n_{i}}(K)
. Then
{\mathcal{A}}:=\prod_{i}M_{n_{i}}({\mathscr{K}})
is a maximal
{\mathscr{K}}
-order in
\mathfrak{A}
. (See
[
Reiner03
, Th. 8.7]
.)

We need some further notation, mostly taken from
[
Lus03
]
. For
w\in W
, let
\text{\text{{\rm sgn}}}(w)=(-1)^{\ell(w)}
. Recall the notation
\widetilde{T}_{w}
from display (
1.2.2
). The map

(4.1.1)

\widetilde{T}_{w}\longmapsto\widetilde{T}_{w}^{\dagger}:=\text{sgn}(w)\widetilde{T}_{w^{-1}}^{-1}

defines a

{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra involution
h\mapsto h^{\dagger}
of
{\mathcal{H}}
. See
[
Lus03
, 3.5]
.
Next,
for
z\in W
, let
a(z)
be the smallest non-negative integer such that

(4.1.2)

t^{a(z)}h_{x,y,z}\in\mathbb{Z}[t],\;\;\text{for all }x,y\in W.

Here
h_{x,y,z}
is the structure constant defined in
(
1.2.6
). Then let
\gamma_{x,y,z}\in\mathbb{Z}
be the coefficient of
t^{a(z)}
in
h_{x,y,z}
.

Obviously,
{\mathcal{H}}
is a
{\mathscr{K}}
-order in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)},
using
{\mathscr{K}}={\mathcal{Z}}
. Next, we define two further orders in (isomorphic copies of)
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
in the sense of Definition
4.1.1
. The first one
{\mathcal{J}}:={\mathcal{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathsf{J}}

is the base change of a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
{\mathsf{J}}
; the latter, defined below, is called
Lusztig’s asymptotic form
. As we shall see, the order

{\mathcal{J}}
contains a copy
of
{\mathcal{H}}
, as discussed below. The second order
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathsf{J}}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}{\mathcal{J}}
, a
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-order, is obtained from the first by inverting all bad primes for
W
.

The algebra
{\mathsf{J}}
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
with
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
\{{\mathsf{j}}_{x}\,|\,x\in W\}
and multiplication given by

(4.1.3)

{\mathsf{j}}_{x}{\mathsf{j}}_{y}=\sum_{z\in W}\gamma_{x,y,z^{-1}}{\mathsf{j}}_{z},\quad x,y\in W.

Then
{\mathsf{J}}
is associative and unital
[
Lus03
, 18.3]
. (Note that rank
({\mathsf{J}})=|W|
.)

Recall that
W
has a subset
\mathcal{D}
of distinguished involutions
[
Lus03
, P6, 14.1]
. Each left cell

\omega
contains a unique element
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
. Also, for
d\in\mathcal{D}
, an integer
n_{d}=\pm 1
is defined in

[
Lus03
, 14.1, P5 & P13]
. Given
z\in W
, let
d\in\mathcal{D}
be the unique distinguished involution
satisfying
z^{-1}\sim_{L}d
, and set
\hat{n}_{z}:=n_{d}
.
By
[
Lus03
, Th. 18.9]
, there is a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra
homomorphism

\phi:{\mathcal{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{J}}
defined by

(4.1.4)

\phi({\textsc{c}}_{w}^{\dagger})=\sum h_{w,d,z}\hat{n}_{z}{\mathsf{j}}_{z}.

In this expression,
w\in W
and the sum is over all pairs
(d,z)\in\mathcal{D}\times W
such that
a(z)=a(d)
.
Then, for any commutative
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra
R
,
\phi
induces an

R
-algebra homomorphism
\phi_{R}:{\mathcal{H}}_{R}\to{\mathcal{J}}_{R}
. By
[
Lus03
, Prop. 18.12a]
, the kernel of
\phi_{R}
is a nilpotent ideal. Thus, when
R
is a field and
{\mathcal{H}}_{R}
is a semisimple algebra,
\phi_{R}

is injective and, thus, an isomorphism by
dimension considerations. In particular,
\phi_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
is an isomorphism. Of course, this means that
\phi
is an injection, so that

{\mathcal{J}}
and
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
both contain subalgebras isomorphic to
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The case
R=\mathbb{Q}
leads to a similar isomorphism
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, regarding
\mathbb{Q}
as a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra by means of the specialization
t\mapsto 1
. Note
{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
by construction. For later use in the proof of the theorem below, note also that

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong{\mathbb{Q}}W
, and so,
{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong\mathbb{Q}W
.

Remark 4.1.2
.

All the assertions of the above paragraph (below display (
4.1.4
)) hold as written if the homomorphism
\phi
is replaced
by the composition

(4.1.5)

\varpi:=\phi\circ\dagger:{\mathcal{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{J}},

where
\dagger
is defined in display (
4.1.1
).
This is not needed right away, but will be useful in showing in the next section that all left-cell modules
for
{\mathcal{H}}
extend to
{\mathcal{J}}
-modules. For the convenience of the reader, we write down explicitly the expression for the composite
\varpi=\phi\circ\dagger
:

(4.1.6)

\varpi({\textsc{c}}_{w})=\sum_{z\in W}\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}\atop a(d)=a(z)}h_{w,d,z}\hat{n}_{z}{\mathsf{j}}_{z}\quad(w\in W).

We will also use
\varpi
in the context of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
and
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
, in which case we define
\varpi^{\natural}

to be the base-changed map

(4.1.7)

\varpi^{\natural}:\,{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}\overset{\varpi_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}={\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}.

Moreover, the isomorphisms
{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong\mathbb{Q}W
and

{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}={\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}\cong{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
induce an isomorphism

\mathbb{Q}(t)W\cong{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
and, hence, a bijection

(4.1.8)

\text{Irr}(\mathbb{Q}W)\longrightarrow\text{Irr}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}),\;E\longmapsto E_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}.

If
G
is of type
{}^{2}F_{4}
,
\mathbb{Q}
should be replaced by
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})
.

Next, we pursue some of the remarkable properties of the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-order
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
. Here,

{\mathscr{K}}={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
and
K=\mathbb{Q}(t)
.

Note that
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{{\mathbb{Q}}(t)}
identifies with
{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
which is isomorphic (via the inverse of
\phi_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
) to

{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}(t)}
. The latter is semisimple by Remark
3.3.4
(b) and even split, since
\mathbb{Q}W
is split semisimple.
(Recall that we have postponed the case
{}^{2}F_{4}
to an appendix at the end of this section.)
Hence, there is a “natural” isomorphism

(4.1.9)

{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}_{{\mathbb{Q}}(t)}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\prod_{i}\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}(t)}(E_{i}),

where
E_{i}
ranges over the distinct irreducible
{\mathcal{J}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}(t)}
-modules.
The map is obtained from the action of
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}={\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
on the various
E_{i}
.
We can assume that
E_{i}=KL_{i}

where
L_{i}
is a
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
-stable lattice in
E_{i}
. In fact, using Auslander-Goldman theory (see
[
DDPW08
, Cor. C.18]
), it can also be assumed (after replacing

L_{i}
by its double dual
L_{i}^{**}
) that all the
L_{i}

are projective over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. Then, by the result of Swan mentioned in
Remark
1.3.6
(c)), the
L_{i}
are free over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
.

Thus, (
4.1.9
) may be obtained by base change from
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
to
\mathbb{Q}(t)

of an injection

(4.1.10)

\psi:{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}\to\prod_{i}\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}(L_{i}),

where each
L_{i}
is
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-free. Note that each
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
-module
L_{i}
is an
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
-module by restriction
through the map
\varpi^{\natural}
in (
4.1.7
).

We can now prove:

Theorem 4.1.3
.

Maintain the assumptions on
W
,
{\mathcal{H}}
, etc. above. The map
\psi
in (
4.1.10
) is an isomorphism
of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebras.

Proof.

We have already noted that the map
\psi
is an injection (since its base change to
{\mathbb{Q}}(t)
is obviously
an injection). Clearly,
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
is a
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-order (and, in particular, a full lattice) in
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
. To prove the theorem, it suffices to prove that
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
is a maximal
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-order in
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
. This will show that
\psi
is an isomorphism, since it is an inclusion of orders. To do this, it is necessary and sufficient, by the Auslander-Goldman theorem (see
[
Reiner03
, Th. 11.4]
), to check two conditions:

(a)

the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-order
\Xi:={{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
is reflexive in the sense that
\Xi^{**}\cong\Xi
as
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-modules;

(b)

for any height one prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
,
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a maximal
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}
-order in
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
.

But
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
is
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-free, so it is certainly reflexive. Thus, (a) holds.

For part (b), it suffices to show, for any height one prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, that
there is an abstract algebra isomorphism

(4.1.11)

{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong\prod_{i}M_{n_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}})

for some finite list of positive integers
n_{i}
. See
[
Reiner03
, Ths. 8.7, 10.5]
.

Consider first the case in which
{\mathfrak{p}}=(p)
, where
p
is a good prime
for
W
. Then
[
DPS98a
, Ths. 4.2.1, 4.2.2]
and their proofs show that

(4.1.12)

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)}\cong\prod_{i}\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)}(L_{i{\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)}})

with
L_{i}
as above, and
L_{i{\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)}}=(L_{i})_{\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)}
denoting the
base-changed module
\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}L_{i}
. Note that
\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)
is the residue field of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. (Essentially, the argument in
[
DPS98a
]
uses Schur matrix relations and the fact that good
primes avoid generic degree denominators
[
DPS98a
, (4.1.2)]
to show that all matrix units on the right have preimages on the left.)
Since the action of
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}
on
L_{i}
is by restriction
through the algebra

{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
, we have a surjective composite map

({\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}})_{\mathfrak{p}}\hookrightarrow{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}\longrightarrow M_{n_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}})\longrightarrow M_{n_{i}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p}(t)),

where the two matrix algebras are the full endomorphism rings of
(L_{i})_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}}

and
(L_{i})_{\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)}
, respectively.
Consequently, the map

{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}\longrightarrow\prod_{i}M_{n_{i}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p}(t))

is surjective, as is the map

{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}\longrightarrow\prod_{i}M_{n_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}})\qquad(\text{by Nakayama's Lemma}).

Since the latter map is an injection, it is also an isomorphism.

Finally, it is enough to show that an isomorphism (
4.1.11
) holds whenever
{\mathfrak{p}}=(f(t))
with
f(t)\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]

an irreducible polynomial of positive degree and
\not=\pm t
. (These are the remaining height one primes in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
.) In this case, no nonzero integer
z\not=0
is divisible
by
f(t)
in the unique factorization domain
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. Thus,
\mathbb{Q}\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Since

{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong{\mathbb{Q}}W\cong\prod_{j}M_{n_{i}}({\mathbb{Q}})

for some positive integers
n_{i}
,
2
2

2

The first isomorphism is discussed above Remark
4.1.2
. It may be regarded as one of the many well-known results of Lusztig generalized in
[
Lus03
]
to an unequal parameter setting.
we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}}{\mathcal{J}}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}}{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathsf{J}}

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathsf{J}}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathsf{J}}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathsf{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong\prod_{i}M_{n_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}),

completing part (b) of the proof. ∎

This has several consequences. The first corollary, which we call a theorem, follows either from the proof of Theorem
4.1.3
or from its statement. (Remember that
K=\mathbb{Q}(t)
and
G
is not of type
{}^{2}F_{4}
.)

Theorem 4.1.4
.

(a)
The
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra

{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}={\mathcal{J}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}
is isomorphic to a maximal order in
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
.

(b)
Furthermore,

(4.1.13)

{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}={\mathcal{J}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}\cong\prod_{i}M_{n_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}),

for a finite sequence
n_{1},n_{2},\cdots
of positive integers.

Remarks 4.1.5
.

(1) The above theorem has an analogous version in which the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
is replaced by
the
\mathbb{Z}^{\natural}
-algebra
{\mathsf{J}}^{\natural}
. In this context, another consequence of Theorem
4.1.4

is that
{\mathsf{J}}^{\natural}
is a maximal order in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathbb{Q}W.
Explicitly,
{\mathsf{J}}^{\natural}
is isomorphic to a
direct product of full matrix algebras over
\mathbb{Z}^{\natural}
. This follows by a base-change argument using the
specialization
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{\natural}
given by
t\mapsto 1
. Finally, all corollaries below
have an analogous version for
{\mathsf{J}}^{\natural}
. They are easily derived through a similar proof, or through a base-change
argument together with the statements for
{\mathcal{Z}}
.

(2) By the theorem, we have
{\mathcal{J}}_{K}={\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}_{K}\cong\prod_{i=1}^{s}M_{n_{i}}(K)
, for some
s
, when
K=\mathbb{Q}(t)
. Thus, analyzing projections, there are a total of
s
non-isomorphic irreducible
{\mathcal{J}}_{K}
-modules
L_{1},\ldots,L_{s}
such that, for each
i
with
1\leq i\leq s
,
L_{i}\cong K^{n_{i}}
as
{\mathcal{J}}_{K}
-modules, where
K^{n_{i}}
is the irreducible left
M_{n_{i}}(K)
-module via matrix multiplication. The discussion of the map
\psi
, in and above Theorem
4.1.3
, implies that
L_{i}
has a
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-lattice
L_{i,{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}
isomorphic to
({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})^{n_{i}}
. In particular,
L_{i,{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}
is
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-free.

(3) In the equal parameter case, Lusztig first proved in
[
Lus87d
, (3.1j),(1.2a)]
that, for a large enough field
F
,
{\mathsf{J}}_{F}
is split semisimple. Further, in
[
G98
, Remark 2.5]
, Geck observed that this result applies to the case where the characteristic of
F
is a good prime. See also
[
GJ11
, Props. 1.5.7&2.3.16]
for a similar result in general over a sufficiently large field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic 0.

We note that a maximal order copy of
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
is
\phi_{K}^{-1}({{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural})
, in the notation of (
4.1.4
). Another useful copy is obtained by applying to this copy the involution on
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
induced
by
\dagger
. The resulting copy may also be written
\varpi_{K}^{-1}({{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural})
, with
\varpi
as in Remark
4.1.2
.

Recall that if
R
is a commutative ring, an algebra
B
over
R
is separable if
B
is a
projective
B\otimes_{R}B^{{\text{\rm op}}}
-module; see Auslander-Goldman
[
AG60b
, p. 1]
.

Corollary 4.1.6
.

(a)

The algebra
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
is separable over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
.

(b)
Every finite module for
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
, which is projective over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, is projective over
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
.

Proof.

The reader may directly check that the
direct product of a finite set of separable algebras is a separable algebra. Then, in view of Theorem
4.1.3
, part
(a) follows from
[
AG60b
, Prop. 5.1]
. Finally, part (b) follows from
[
Hat63
, Th. 2.11]
.
(See also
[
Reiner03
, Ex. 9, p. 106]
.)
∎

Corollary 4.1.7
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be finite
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
-modules, projective over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. If
M_{K}\cong M^{\prime}_{K}
as

{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{K}
-modules, then
M\cong M^{\prime}
as
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
-modules.

Proof.

Note that
M
and
M^{\prime}
are projective over
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
by Corollary
4.1.6
(b). By Theorem

4.1.4
, we can reduce to the case where
M
and
M^{\prime}
are finite projective modules for a matrix algebra
M_{n}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})

which become isomorphic over the fraction field
K
. By Morita theory, we can assume
n=1
. (Let

F:\text{mod-}M_{m}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})\rightarrow{\text{ mod-}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
be an equivalence of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-categories, e.g., a suitable idempotent
projection. Note that
F
induces by base change an equivalence
F_{K}:{\text{mod-}}M_{m}(K)\rightarrow\text{mod-}K
. For
X,Y\in{\text{mod-}}M_{m}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
, we clearly have
X\cong Y
if and only if
F(X)\cong F(Y)
, and
X_{K}\cong Y_{K}
in mod-
M_{m}(K)
if and only if
F(X)_{K}\cong F(Y)_{K}
in mod-
K
.)
By Swan’s theorem (see Remark
1.3.6
(c)), we can assume that
M
and
M^{\prime}
are free
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-modules which are isomorphic over
K
. Obviously, this means
M\cong M^{\prime}
.
∎

Finally, we have:

Corollary 4.1.8
.

If
M
is a
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
-module which is finite and projective over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
,
then

(a)

M
is a direct sum of
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
-modules which are projective over
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
and which have irreducible base change to
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{K}
-modules.

(b)

Moreover,
M
is uniquely determined (up to isomorphism) by the multiplicity of each isomorphism type of the irreducible summands of the
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{K}
-module
M_{K}
.

Proof.

We first prove part (a). By Theorem
4.1.4
, we may replace
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
by one of its direct
factors
M_{m}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
. Using Morita theory, we can even replace
M_{m}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
by
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. Once again, applying Swan’s theorem, the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-module
M
must be free, and so clearly is a direct sum of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-modules with irreducible base change to
K
. This proves (a).

For the proof of (b), the reduction is simply reversed. Further details are left to the reader.∎

Appendix to Section 4.1: the case of the Ree groups of type
{}^{2}\!F_{4}

The Weyl group
W
is the dihedral group
I_{2}(8)
of order 16, generated by
reflections
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
with
s_{1}s_{2}
having order 8. The weights are determined from the longest words in each
individual orbit of a graph “symmetry”; see
[
C85
, pp. 36-38]
or by analogy with the construction
[
Lus03
, 16.1–16.2]
formulated in the quasi-split case. We can assume
L(s_{1})=2
and
L(s_{2})=4
for the values of the weight function
L
on fundamental reflections
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
. See
[
C85
, pp. 489-490]
for the character degrees of the unipotent characters in the principal series. (Note that the degree associated to
\rho_{2}
should be corrected to

(1/2)q^{4}\Phi_{8}^{2}\Phi_{24}
.)
3
3

3

The character degree given in
[
C85
]
does not appear in Lusztig’s list of unipotent character degrees. The value we have given does appear in
[
Lus84
, p. 374]
but just as a unipotent character
degree. It can be seen to be a generic/principal series degree by direct calculation using
[
GP00
, pp.
270-272]
.
There are 7 degrees in all, labeled
1,\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime\prime},\epsilon
(for the
degree 1 representations of
{\mathbb{C}}W
) and
\rho^{\prime}_{2},\rho_{2}^{\prime\prime},\rho_{2}
(for the degree 2 representations of
{\mathbb{C}}W
). The degrees of all the unipotent characters are in terms of polynomial functions, elements of

\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})[q]
, evaluated with
q
an odd power of
\sqrt{2}
. The generic degrees are obtained by replacing
q
by the variable

t
. From the corrected list, we see that the generic degrees have the form

(4.1.14)

{\frac{1}{m}}t^{a}\Phi^{\prime}_{b_{1}}(t)\cdots\Phi^{\prime}_{b_{r}}(t),

where
a
is a non-negative integer. Also,
\Phi^{\prime}_{b_{i}}(t)
is a factor in
\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{2}][t]
of the corresponding cyclotomic polynomial
\Phi_{b_{i}}(t)
in
\mathbb{Z}[t]
. The integer
m
is equal to 1, 2, or 4.

The expression (
4.1.14
) is sufficiently similar to
[
DPS98a
, (4.1.2)]
that analogs of

[
DPS98a
, Th. 4.2.1]
and
[
DPS98a
, Th. 4.2.2]
may be proved with parallel arguments. It is only
necessary to replace
\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{2}]
,
\mathbb{Q}
by
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{2}]
,
{\mathcal{H}}
by
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}[t,t^{-1}]}
,
K
by
K^{\prime}=\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}(t)=K[\sqrt{2}]
, etc.

It will also be
useful to define
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}[t,t^{-1}]=\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{2},t,t^{-1}]
,
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}={\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}[1/\sqrt{2},t,t^{-1}]
,

\mathbb{Z}^{\natural}=\mathbb{Z}[1/2],
and
\mathbb{Z}^{\natural\prime}=\mathbb{Z}[1/2][1/\sqrt{2}]=\mathbb{Z}[1/\sqrt{2}]
. Finally, defining
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}=\mathbb{Z}^{\natural\prime}[t,t^{-1}]
, we have

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}={\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}.

The only “bad prime” is
p=2
in
\mathbb{Z}
(or
p=\sqrt{2}
in
\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}
) in the sense of dividing
m

in (
4.1.14
). Fortunately,

\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{2}]
is a principal ideal domain. Using these facts, the argument in the proof of
Theorem
4.1.3
establishing the intermediate isomorphism (
4.1.12
) determines an analogous
isomorphism. In the analogy,
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
replaces
{\mathcal{H}}
, and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}
-free
\mathcal{J}^{\prime\natural}
-modules

L_{i}^{\prime}
replaces
L_{i}
, for each
i
. Also,
\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)
is replaced by
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}_{p}(t)
, the residue field
of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
(which equals the residue field of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}_{\mathfrak{p}}.
) Here,

\mathfrak{p}
is the ideal generated by
p\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
, where
p
is a prime not dividing
\sqrt{2}
. With this analog
of the isomorphism (
4.1.12
) in hand, an evident version of the isomorphism (
4.1.11
) follows for each
\mathfrak{p}
, using the argument in the proof of Theorem
4.1.3
.
Consequently, each
\mathcal{J}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime\natural}
is a maximal order. Thus,
\mathcal{J}^{\prime\natural}
, which is clearly reflexive, is also a maximal order, and the map

(4.1.15)

\psi:\mathcal{J}^{\prime\natural}\to\prod_{i}\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}}(L^{\prime}_{i}),

analogous to (
4.1.10
),
is an isomorphism of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}
-algebras. This proves the following theorem, an analog of Theorem

4.1.3
above, in the Ree group setting
of this appendix.

Theorem 4.1.9
.

The map
\psi
in (
4.1.15
) is an isomorphism of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}
-algebras.

Each of the endomorphism (“matrix”) components of (
4.1.15
) is a maximal order in some
M_{n}(K^{\prime})
by
[
Reiner03
, Th. 8.7]
, since
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}
is integrally closed in
K^{\prime}=\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}(t)
. The result below is a corollary of the above theorem, but given its importance, we upgrade it to a theorem.

Theorem 4.1.10
.

The
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}
-algebra
\mathcal{J}^{\prime\natural}=\mathcal{J}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}}

is a maximal order in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}(t)}
. Moreover,

(4.1.16)

{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime\natural}={\mathcal{J}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}}\cong\prod_{i}M_{m_{1}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural})

for a finite sequence
m_{1},m_{2},\cdots
of positive integers.

Remarks 4.1.11
.

(a) Similarly, Corollaries
4.1.6
–
4.1.8
above have analogs for
{}^{2}F_{4}
in the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}={\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}
-setting. We leave the details and proofs, which follow the original arguments, to the reader. Theorem
4.1.10
above is similarly analogous to Theorem
4.1.4
.

(b) Also, Corollaries
4.1.6
to
4.1.8
and Theorem
4.1.4
(a)
remain true in the

{}^{2}F_{4}
-case, as stated, that is, using
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}:=\mathbb{Z}[{{1}\over{2}},t,t^{-1}]
, and
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}:={\mathcal{J}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}
. (However, Theorem
4.1.4
(b) is not true without further modification; see display (
4.1.17
) below.)
The proofs below, in this new setting for the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case, are somewhat more involved.

To prove Theorem
4.1.4
(a) in this new setting, note that if

{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
were properly contained in a
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-order—call it
{\mathcal{J}}^{\clubsuit}
—then the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
-order

{\mathcal{J}}^{\clubsuit}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}}
would properly contain
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural\prime}
, contradicting Theorem
4.1.10
above.

Next, to prove Corollary
4.1.6
in the new setting, observe first that part (b) follows from part (a), using the argument already
given in the original context. Part (a) follows from the following faithfully flat descent theorem:

Theorem 4.1.12

(
[
F17
, Th. 8.1.20(1)]
)
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,

B
a finite
R
-algebra. Suppose
S
is a commutative faithfully flat
R
-algebra such that
B\otimes_{R}S
is separable over
S
. Then,

B
is separable over
R
.

For a more elementary proof of Corollary
4.1.6
(a), first show, using the maximality of
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
(Theorem
4.1.4
(a)),
that

{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
is a direct product of
1\times 1
and
2\times 2
full matrix algebras over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, and a

2\times 2
full matrix algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
:

(4.1.17)

{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\oplus{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\oplus{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\oplus{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\oplus M_{2}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})\oplus M_{2}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}).

Here, the factor
M_{2}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime})
arises, using the field automorphism
\sqrt{2}\mapsto-\sqrt{2}
, as a
“diagonal”
{\mathcal{Z}}
-subalgebra of
M_{2}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime})\oplus M_{2}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime})
.
It suffices to prove the separability of this factor over

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, which reduces to the separability of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, and then to the
separability of
\mathbb{Z}^{\natural\prime}
over
\mathbb{Z}^{\natural}
. A splitting of the (bimodule) multiplication surjection
\mathbb{Z}^{\natural\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}^{\natural}}\mathbb{Z}^{\natural^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{\natural\prime}
is given by sending
1\in\mathbb{Z}^{\natural\prime}
to
(1/4)(\sqrt{2}\otimes\sqrt{2})+(1/2)(1\otimes 1)
. This proves the needed separability and completes the elementary proof.

To prove Corollary
4.1.7
in the new setting, note that proving a similar statement
for each component algebra of
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
in (
4.1.17
) is equivalent. The original proof obviously works for all
components except for the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra
M_{n}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime})
. Here,
it is necessary and sufficient to show (call it a claim) that projective modules
M,M^{\prime}
for this component, which are isomorphic
after base change to
K
, are themselves isomorphic.
If we view
M_{n}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime})
as a
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
-algebra in this claim, and replace
K
by
K^{\prime}
, its validity follows from the proof
of the original Corollary
4.1.7
, in the spirit of (a) above. However, the module categories
for this matrix component, whether viewed as a
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
- or
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
-algebra,
are the same. Moreover, the respective base-change functors,

K\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}(-)
and
K^{\prime}\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}}(-)
are naturally isomorphic,
even in the larger category of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
-modules. In view of these identifications,
the cited argument proves the claim, and the version of Corollary
4.1.6
in new setting follows.

Corollary
4.1.8
follows in the new setting from its current proof together with the decomposition of
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
given in (
4.1.17
) above. The above identification of
K-
and
K^{\prime}-
base changes for

M_{n}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime})
-modules is also useful here.

Note, too, that specializing
t\mapsto 1
in the expression (
4.1.17
) gives an analogous and very precise version of Remark
4.1.5
in the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case.

Finally, we alert the reader that Theorem
4.1.12
will be needed in Section 4.3.

4.2.
Left cells and the order
{\mathcal{J}}:={\mathcal{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathsf{J}}

Recall from (
4.1.5
) that
\varpi:{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{J}}
is the
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra homomorphism
\varpi=\phi\circ\dagger
, where
\phi:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{J}}
is defined in (
4.1.4
) and
\dagger:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
is defined in (
4.1.1
). Given
a
{\mathcal{J}}
-module
M
, there is a unique
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
M^{\varpi}=\{m^{\varpi}\,|\,m\in M\}
obtained by letting

{\mathcal{H}}
-act on
M
through
\varpi
, i.e.,
hm^{\varpi}=(\varpi(h)m)^{\varpi}
, for each
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
m\in M
. In other words,
M^{\varpi}
is the restriction of the
{\mathcal{J}}
-module
M
to

{\mathcal{H}}
through
\varpi
. (Throughout this section, modules are taken to be left modules, unless otherwise indicated.)

One approach to understanding the representation theory
of a generic Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
is to relate it to the representation theory of the “simpler”
asymptotic algebra
{\mathcal{J}}
, by means of the inclusion
\varpi:{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{J}}
. For
example, as just explained, any

{\mathcal{J}}
-module
M
can be restricted to
{\mathcal{H}}
, giving rise to a functor
M\mapsto M^{\varpi}
.
Conversely, Theorem
4.2.1
below shows that (under the standing hypotheses on
{\mathcal{H}}
of Section 1.2) for certain
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules
S
, there exists a unique (up to isomorphism)

{\mathcal{J}}
-module
V
such that
V^{\varpi}\cong S
. In fact, we prove that this is true whenever
S
is a finite
direct sum of left-cell modules for
{\mathcal{H}}
.

If bad primes are inverted as in Section 4.1, then
{\mathcal{Z}}
becomes
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, and
{\mathcal{J}}
becomes a maximal (and separable) order
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. See Theorem
4.1.4
, and Remark

4.1.11
(b) in the case
{}^{2}F_{4}
. This implies that every finite and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-projective
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-module is a direct sum of finite projective
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-modules, each of which becomes irreducible upon base change to
{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
. Such a decomposition is unique up to
isomorphism. Also, because
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
is regular of Krull dimension
\leq 2
(it is dimension 2, in fact), every irreducible
{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
-module is the base change of a
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-module which
is projective over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. In fact, applying Swan’s theorem, any such module is free
over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. In effect, this provides a new refinement of the set of left-cell modules.

Theorem 4.2.1
.

Let
S
be a left-cell module (or even a finite direct sum of left-cell modules) for
{\mathcal{H}}
. Then, there exists a
{\mathcal{J}}
-module
M
, unique up to isomorphism, such that
S\cong M^{\varpi}
.

Proof.

We use several results from
[
Lus03
, Ch. 18]
. Let
*
be as defined
in
[
Lus03
, 18.10]
; it is a left-module action of
{\mathcal{J}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
. Now define a new action
\boxdot
of
{\mathcal{J}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
by putting
j\boxdot h:=(j*h^{\dagger})^{\dagger}
, for
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
and
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
. For each integer
a
, let

(4.2.1)

{\mathcal{H}}_{a}=\oplus_{w;a(w)=a}{\mathcal{Z}}{\textsc{c}}^{\dagger}_{w}\quad{\text{and}}\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{\geq a}=\oplus_{w;a(w)\geq a}{\mathcal{Z}}{\textsc{c}}_{w}^{\dagger}

as in
[
Lus03
, 18.10]
, where
a(-)
is the Lusztig
a
-function defined in (
4.1.2
); see
[
Lus03
, 13.6]
. Lusztig observes that
{\mathsf{j}}_{x}*c^{\dagger}_{w}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{a(w)}
, for all
x,w\in W
.
In particular,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\geq a}
is stable under the
*
action of
{\mathcal{J}}
.
He proves

[
Lus03
, 18.10(a)]
that

h{\textsc{c}}_{w}^{\dagger}\equiv\phi(h)*{\textsc{c}}_{w}^{\dagger}\,\,{\rm mod}\,\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\geq a(w)+1},\quad\forall w\in W.

Replacing
h
by
h^{\dagger}
, and then applying
\dagger
to both sides, we get

(4.2.2)

h{\textsc{c}}_{w}=(\phi(h^{\dagger})*{\textsc{c}}^{\dagger}_{w})^{\dagger}=\varpi(h)\boxdot{\textsc{c}}_{w}\quad{\rm mod}({\mathcal{H}}_{\geq a(w)+1})^{\dagger}.

It follows from Lusztig’s observation above that
({\mathcal{H}}_{\geq a})^{\dagger}
is stable under the
\boxdot
-action of
{\mathcal{J}}
,
as is
({\mathcal{H}}_{\geq q})^{\dagger}/({\mathcal{H}}_{\geq a+1})^{\dagger}
. Observe that
-a(-)
is a height function in the sense of
Appendix B compatible with
\leq_{LR}
by
[
Lus03
, Ch. 14, P4]
. By Corollary
2.1.8
above, the left
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
{\mathcal{H}}={{}_{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}}
has a height filtration with respect to the (compatible and negatively-valued) height function
-a(-)-1
. Note that
({\mathcal{H}}_{\geq a})^{\dagger}/({\mathcal{H}}_{\geq a+1})^{\dagger}
is a section of
such a filtration. The equation (
4.2.2
) shows that this natural
{\mathcal{H}}
-module is also the restriction through
\varpi
of a module for

{\mathcal{J}}
, the latter acting via
\boxdot
on the same
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module.
Since the
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
{}_{{\mathcal{H}}}{\mathcal{H}}

belongs to
{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{L}})
using Corollary
2.1.8
, its filtration sections are direct sums of left-cell modules. Also, it is clear that every left-cell module
for
{\mathcal{H}}
appears as a direct summand of some such section. The theorem now follows from the elementary
Lemma
4.2.2

below, which proves uniqueness (see part (c)) as well as completing the proof of existence (see part (d)—the notation differs slightly).∎

Temporarily, let
{\mathscr{K}}
be any Noetherian integral domain, and let
K
be its fraction field. The following lemma is inspired by the arguments in
[
Reiner03
, below (8.2), p. 109]
. Note the definition of
\mathfrak{B}
-mod
f
is given in part (a). A similar definition applies for
\mathfrak{A}
-mod
f
. Note also that part (b) will be used in the proof of Theorem
4.3.4
.

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a finite and torsion-free
{\mathscr{K}}
-algebra and let
\mathfrak{B}
be an order in the
K
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{K}
containing
\mathfrak{A}
. Then,

(a)

The restriction functor
\mathfrak{B}
-mod
\,{}^{f}\rightarrow\mathfrak{A}
-mod defines a fully faithful
