{\mathscr{K}}
-linear functor from the
{\mathscr{K}}
-category

\mathfrak{B}
–mod
\,{}^{f}
of
{\mathscr{K}}
-finite and
{\mathscr{K}}
-torsion-free
\mathfrak{B}
-modules
to the
{\mathscr{K}}
-category of
\mathfrak{A}
-modules.

(b)

In particular, for any
M\in\mathfrak{B}
–mod
\,{}^{f}
, we have

\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(M)\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathfrak{A}}(M|_{\mathfrak{A}})

as
{\mathscr{K}}
-algebras. Also,

M|_{\mathfrak{A}}\in\mathfrak{A}
–mod
\,{}^{f}
.

(c)

Suppose that
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}
-mod
\,{}^{f}
, and let
N,N^{\prime}
denote the
\mathfrak{A}
-modules which are their respective restrictions to
\mathfrak{A}
. Then,
M\cong M^{\prime}
as
\mathfrak{B}
-modules if and only if
N\cong N^{\prime}
as
\mathfrak{A}
-modules.

(d)

Suppose
M\in\mathfrak{B}
-mod
\,{}^{f}
. Assume that
N:=M|_{\mathfrak{A}}
can be written as
a direct sum
N=N_{1}\oplus N_{2}
in
\mathfrak{A}
-mod. Then,
M=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
in
\mathfrak{B}
-mod
f
, where

M_{i}|_{\mathfrak{A}}\cong N_{i}
,
i=1,2
. (Also,
N_{1},N_{2}
are in
\mathfrak{A}
–mod
f
.)

Proof.

We first prove part (a). Obviously, restriction is a
{\mathscr{K}}
-linear functor. We have to prove it induces an isomorphism
on Hom-spaces between objects.
For
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}
–mod
\,{}^{f}
, note that
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(M,M^{\prime})

identifies with the
{\mathscr{K}}
-submodule of
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathfrak{B}_{K}}(M_{K},M^{\prime}_{K})
consisting of the elements of
the latter that send

M\subseteq M_{K}
to
M^{\prime}\subseteq M^{\prime}_{K}
. A similar identification holds for
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathfrak{A}}(M|_{\mathfrak{A}},M^{\prime}|_{\mathfrak{A}})
in
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{K}}(M_{K},M^{\prime}_{K})
. Since
\mathfrak{B}_{K}=K\mathfrak{B}=K\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}_{K}
, the isomorphism required in part (a) holds.

Part (b) follows from the argument above with
M=M^{\prime}
(using the fact that restriction is a functor, hence it induces an algebra homomorphism). The fact that
M|_{\mathfrak{A}}
belongs to
\mathfrak{A}
–mod
f
is obvious. Part (c) also follows from a functorial argument using (a). Finally, (d) follows from the functorial properties of (a) and (b).
It is also necessary to observe for part (d) that idempotents split in
\mathfrak{B}
–mod
f
. More precisely, in terms of the
argument for (a), if
e\in\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(M)
is an idempotent, with
M\in\mathfrak{B}
–mod
\,{}^{f}
, then
eM

also belongs to
\mathfrak{B}
–mod
f
.
∎

Now return to the setting of Theorem
4.2.1
, but consider modules over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, rather than
{\mathcal{Z}}
. For each
E\in\text{Irr}(\mathbb{Q}W)
, let
E_{{\mathbb{Q}}(t)}
be the corresponding irreducible

\mathbb{Q}(t)W
-module, regarded as a
{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
-module through the isomorphism

\mathbb{Q}(t)W\cong{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
.
(In the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case,
E
and
E_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
may not be absolutely irreducible.) Thus, by Remark
4.1.5
(2),
E_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}\cong L_{i}
, for some
i
.
Choose a
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-lattice
E_{\natural}
in

E_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
that is a projective as a
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-module
(thus free by Swan’s theorem
[
Sw78
, first paragraph, p. 111]
). For example, for a type other than
{}^{2}F_{4}
, we may choose
E_{\natural}\cong L_{i,{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}
, for some
i
, which is
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-free in the notation of Remark
4.1.5
(2).
Since
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
is projective over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, the
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-module
E_{\natural}
is projective over
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
; see Corollary
4.1.6
(b) and its analog
in Remark
4.1.11
(b).

We remark that
E_{\natural}
is uniquely determined (up to isomorphism) by Corollary
4.1.7
and
its twisted
{}^{2}F_{4}
analog in Remark
4.1.11
(b).
Define

(4.2.3)

S^{\natural}(E):=(E_{\natural})^{\varpi^{\natural}},\quad S^{\natural}_{E}:=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}(S^{\natural}(E),{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}),

which belong to
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
-mod and mod-
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
, respectively.
Note that
S^{\natural}(E)
and
S^{\natural}_{E}
are natural
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
-forms for the
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}[t,t^{-1}]}
-modules
S(E)
and
S_{E}
defined in
[
DPS15
, §3]
. Note also that
S^{\natural}(E)_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}\cong E_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}\cong L_{i}
.

Remark 4.2.3
.

Since
\{S^{\natural}(E)_{K}\mid E\in\text{Irr}(\mathbb{Q}W)\}
, where
K=\mathbb{Q}(t)
, forms a complete set of irreducible left
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
-modules (see Remark
4.1.5
(2)), it follows that
\{(S^{\natural}_{E})_{K}\mid E\in\text{Irr}(\mathbb{Q}W)\}
is a complete set of irreducible right
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
-modules. Thus, since every
(S_{E}^{\natural})_{K}
is an irreducible constituent of
T^{+}_{K}
, the set

\{\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}_{K}}((S_{E}^{\natural})_{K},T^{+\natural}_{K})\mid E\in\text{Irr}(\mathbb{Q}W)\}

forms a complete set of irreducible left
A^{+}_{K}
-modules. (Note that
A^{+}_{K}=\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(T^{+\natural})_{K}
. So,
A^{+}_{K}
is split semisimple if
{}^{2}F_{4}
is excluded.)
Now, if
\Delta(E):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(S_{E}^{\natural},T^{+\natural})
, then
\Delta(E)\subseteq\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}_{K}}((S_{E}^{\natural})_{K},T^{+\natural}_{K})
is an
A^{+\natural}
-submodule, which is a torsion-free
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-module. Hence,
\Delta(E)_{K}=K\Delta(E)
is a (nonzero)
A^{+}_{K}
-submodule of
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}_{K}}((S_{E}^{\natural})_{K},T^{+\natural}_{K})
. Irreducibility (treated above) forces

\Delta(E)_{K}=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}_{K}}((S_{E}^{\natural})_{K},T^{+\natural}_{K})
.

Therefore,
\{\Delta(E)_{K}\mid E\in\text{Irr}(\mathbb{Q}W)\}
forms a complete set of irreducible left
A^{+}_{K}
-modules. To include the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case, we may simply replace
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
by
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}[\sqrt{2}]
,
K
by
K^{\prime}
, and
\Delta(E)
by
\Delta^{\prime}(E):=\Delta(E)_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}}
to get a complete set

\{\Delta^{\prime}(E)_{K^{\prime}}\mid E\in\text{Irr}(\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}W)\}
of irreducible left
A^{+}_{K^{\prime}}
-modules. Here, the fields
K^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
are obtained by adjoining
\sqrt{2}
to
K
,
\mathbb{Q}
, respectively.

Theorem 4.2.4
.

(a) Let
N
be a finite direct sum of left-cell modules for
{\mathcal{H}}
, and let
N^{\natural}:=N_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}
be the corresponding (base-changed) module for
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
. If
M
is a direct summand of
the
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
-module
N^{\natural}
, then
M
admits a direct sum decomposition

M=\oplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{i}
in
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
-mod such that each
M_{i}\cong S^{\natural}(E_{i})
, for some

E_{i}\in\text{\rm Irr}({\mathbb{Q}}W)
.
The modules
M_{i}
and their multiplicities in
M
are determined up to isomorphism by
M_{K}
, where

K=\mathbb{Q}(t)
.

(b) Assume that
(W,S,L)
is of type
{}^{2}F_{4}
(so the base-change notations
(-)^{\natural\prime}
and
(-)^{\prime}
are defined). Then, (a) also holds after
substituting the top row for the bottom row in the display (see the appendix to Section 4.1):

(4.2.4)

\begin{matrix}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\,&{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}\,&{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural\prime}\,&\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}\,&K^{\prime}\,&N^{\natural\prime}\\
{\mathcal{H}}\,&{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\,&{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}\,&\mathbb{Q}\,&K\,&N^{\natural}\end{matrix}

Proof.

(a) First, we establish some projectivity properties. Note that all left-cell modules for
{\mathcal{H}}
are projective (even free) over
{\mathcal{Z}}
. Thus,
N
is projective over
{\mathcal{Z}}
, and both
N^{\natural}
and its direct summand
M
are projective over

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. Consequently, if
M\cong X^{\varpi^{\natural}}
(the restriction of
X
to
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
through
\varpi_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}
) for a given
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-module
X
, then
X
is projective over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. Consequently,
X
is also projective over

{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
, by Corollary
4.1.6
(b).

By Theorem
4.2.1
,
N
is (isomorphic to) the restriction (through
\varpi
) to
{\mathcal{H}}
of a module for
{\mathcal{J}}
.
Thus,
N^{\natural}
is the restriction to
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
of a module
Y
for the order
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}
, and the analogous
statement holds for
M
by Lemma
4.2.2
(d). (Use
\mathfrak{A}={\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
and let
\mathfrak{B}=\varpi_{K}^{-1}({\mathcal{J}}^{\natural})
.) Accordingly, let
X
be a
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-module such that
M
is the restriction
X^{\varpi^{\natural}}
, and note that
X
is projective by the paragraph above. (Alternately, Lemma
4.2.2
(d) gives an
X
which is a direct summand of
Y
, and
Y
can also be shown to be projective over
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
.)
Write

X_{K}\cong L_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus L_{r}
, where the
L_{i}
are irreducible
{{\mathcal{J}}}^{\natural}_{K}
-modules. Then,
L_{i}=(L^{\prime}_{i})_{K}
, for
some
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-lattice
L_{i}^{\prime}
in
L_{i}
. In fact, we may take
L_{i}\cong(E_{i})_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
,
E_{i}\in\text{Irr}(\mathbb{Q}W)
, and
L^{\prime}_{i}\cong E_{i\natural}
as discussed above the theorem. Note also from that discussion that

E_{i\natural}
is projective over
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
and restricts though
\varpi^{\natural}
to
S^{\natural}(E_{i})
. The direct sum of all of the
E_{i\natural}
remains projective and has the same base change to
{\mathcal{J}}_{K}^{\natural}
as
X_{K}
. Using the projectivity of
X
and Corollary
4.1.7
, we deduce that
X
is the direct sum of all the

E_{i\natural}
. Applying the functor
(-)^{\varpi^{\natural}}
to
X
and its summands gives the desired description of
M
in part (a)—all but the last sentence, which
is again a consequence of Corollary
4.1.7
.
The
L^{\prime}_{i}
and their multiplicities are uniquely determined by Corollary
4.1.7
as well.

This completes the proof of (a), while the proof of (b) is similar,
mutatis mutandis
. Remark
4.1.11
(a)
can be used as a guide.
∎

Corollary 4.2.5
.

The following statements hold for
{\mathcal{H}}
-mod:

(a)

Let
N_{1},N_{2}
be left-cell modules for
{\mathcal{H}}
, and suppose
M_{1},M_{2}
are direct summands of
N_{1}^{\natural},N_{2}^{\natural}
, respectively. Then,
M_{1}\cong M_{2}
as
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
-modules if and only if

(M_{1})_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}\cong(M_{2})_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
as
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
-modules.

(b)

In the type
{}^{2}F_{4}
case, (a) also holds if changes in notation analogous to those in Theorem
4.2.4
(b) are made.

Part (a) is clear from the above theorem and its preamble, using the split semsimplicity of
\mathbb{Q}(t)W
and
algebras isomorphic to it. Part (b) is similar, replacing
\mathbb{Q}(t)W
by
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}(t)W
.

4.3.
The quasi-heredity of
A^{+\natural}

Quasi-hereditary algebras (over a field) were first introduced by CPS (E. Cline and the second and third authors)
[
CPS88
]
in order to deal with highest weight categories arising naturally in the representation theory of semisimple Lie algebras and algebraic groups.
Examples of quasi-hereditary algebras include
Schur (or, more generally,
q
-Schur) algebras and Auslander algebras which arise rather naturally. With the maximal order theory developed in the previous sections, we are now ready to prove that the base change from
{\mathcal{Z}}
to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
of the standardly stratified algebra
A^{+}
is, in fact, (integrally) quasi-hereditary.

We begin with some terminology about (integral) quasi-hereditary algebras.
A good source for much of this material is
[
DPS18
, §2]
.

Definition 4.3.1
.

(1) Let
B
be an algebra over a commutative Noetherian ring
{\mathscr{K}}
. Assume that
B
is projective and finite
over
{\mathscr{K}}
. An ideal
J
of
B
is called a
heredity
ideal
in
B
if the following four conditions hold:

(HI1)

B/J
is projective over
{\mathscr{K}}
;

(HI2)

{}_{B}J
is
B
-projective;

(HI3)

J^{2}=J
; and

(HI4)

The endomorphism algebra
E:=\text{\rm{End}}_{B}({{}_{B}J)}
is
{\mathscr{K}}
-semisimple.
4
4

4

This means that, for all
\mathfrak{p}\in{\text{\rm Spec}}({\mathscr{K}})
,
E({\mathfrak{p}})
is a semisimple algebra over the residue field
{\mathscr{K}}({\mathfrak{p}}):={\mathscr{K}}_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathfrak{p}{\mathscr{K}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Equivalently,
any short exact sequence of left
E
-modules which is split over
{\mathscr{K}}
, is split over
E
. (See
[
CPS90
, §2]
.)

(2) The algebra
B
, still assumed to be projective over
{\mathscr{K}}
, is called a
quasi-hereditary algebra
(QHA)
provided
there exists a finite “defining sequence”

0=J_{0}\subseteq J_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}=B

of ideals in
B

such that, for
0<i\leq m
,
J_{i}/J_{i-1}
is a heredity ideal in
B/J_{i-1}
.

(3) If, in addition, every
J_{i}/J_{i-1}
is a heredity ideal of
split
type, in the sense that
the
{\mathscr{K}}
-semisimple algebra
\text{\rm{End}}_{B/J_{i-1}}(J_{i}/J_{i-1})
is isomorphic to a direct product of
\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathscr{K}}(P)
, where
P
is a finite (faithful)
{\mathscr{K}}
-projective module, we call
B
a
split
quasi-hereditary algebra (or a quasi-hereditary algebra of
split
type).

Notice that, since
P
is necessarily a projective generator for
{\mathscr{K}}
-mod,
\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathscr{K}}(P)
is Morita equivalent to
{\mathscr{K}}
.

Clearly, a heredity ideal is a stratifying ideal and every quasi-hereditary algebra is a standardly stratified algebra. The “split” quasi-hereditary algebras defined above are quite popular and useful. Some mild variations have been collected in Remark
4.3.2

below.

Remark 4.3.2
.

(1)
Recall that an algebra
E
is
separable
over
{\mathscr{K}}
, provided the multiplication map
E\otimes_{\mathscr{K}}E^{\text{\rm op}}\rightarrow E
splits as an
(E,E^{\text{\rm op}})
-bimodule map. By definition,
E
is
semisplit
if it is a direct product of algebras, each of which is separable with center
{\mathscr{K}}
(i.e., an Azumaya algebra). If the
{\mathscr{K}}
-semisimple algebra
E:=\text{\rm{End}}_{B}({{}_{B}J})
in (HI4) is separable (respectively, semisplit) over
{\mathscr{K}}
, we say that
J
is of
separable
(respectively,
semisplit) type
over
{\mathscr{K}}
.

Then, if each
J_{i}/J_{i-1}
is of separable (respectively, semisplit) type over
{\mathscr{K}}
, we say that the quasi-hereditary algebra
B
is of
separable
(respectively,
semisplit
)
type
over
{\mathscr{K}}
. See
[
CPS90
]
for more details.

(2) Following
[
Ro08
, Def. 4.1.4]
, a heredity ideal
J
of split type is said to be
indecomposable
, or
J
is an
indecomposable split heredity ideal
,
if (HI4) is replaced by (HI4)
′
:

(HI4)
′

The algebra
E:=\text{\rm{End}}_{B}({{}_{B}J)}
is Morita equivalent to
{\mathscr{K}}
.

Rouquier established a very nice relation between indecomposable split heredity ideals
J
and “standard (projective)
B
-modules”
L
that are
{\mathscr{K}}
-faithful and satisfy one of the equivalent conditions in
[
Ro08
, Lem. 4.5]
:

Given
L
, the coresponding
J
satisfies the following
B
-
B
-bimodule isomorphism

(4.3.1)

J\cong L\otimes_{\text{\rm{End}}_{B}(L)}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B}(L,A),

while, given
J
, the corresponding
L
satisfies the left
B
-module isomorphism

(4.3.2)

L\cong J\otimes_{\text{\rm{End}}_{B}(J)}P,

where
P
is a progenerator for
\text{\rm{End}}_{B}(J)
with
\text{\rm{End}}_{\text{\rm{End}}_{B}(J)}(P)\cong{\mathscr{K}}
.

The construction of
L
from
J
in (
4.3.2
) is also given in
[
CPS90
, p.157]
. This, in particular, implies that
\text{\rm{End}}_{B}(L)\cong{\mathscr{K}}
.
We will call the isomorphism (
4.3.1
)
Rouquier’s isomorphism
. This isomorphism is useful in the construction of a standard basis for the split quasi-hereditary algebra; see Section 5.1.

(3) Note also that the definition of split quasi-hereditary algebras in
[
Ro08
, 4.1.4]
, using indecomposable split heredity ideals (
[
Ro08
, Def. 4.1]
) and a poset, is stronger (at least in formulation) than Definition
4.3.1
(3).

The following result is needed in the proof of the theorem below and is obtained by adapting an argument of Hochster
[
Ho07
]
(compare also
[
Bo72
, Prop. 11, p.23]
).

Lemma 4.3.3
.

Let
{\mathtt{H}}
be a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra which is finite and projective as a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module, and let
M,N
be finite left
{\mathtt{H}}
-modules. If
M
is a finitely presented
{\mathtt{H}}
-module, then there exists a
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-module isomorphism

(4.3.3)

\phi^{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},{\mathtt{H}}}_{M,N}:{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural}),

defined, for
u\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
and
f\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N)
, by

\phi^{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},{\mathtt{H}}}_{M,N}(u\otimes f)(v\otimes m)=uvf(m)\;\;(v\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},m\in M).

Moreover, when
M=N
,
\phi^{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},{\mathtt{H}}}_{N,N}:{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathtt{H}}(N)\to\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural})
is a natural isomorphism of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebras.

Proof.

Let
\phi=\phi^{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},{\mathtt{H}}}_{M,N}
.
It is helpful to think of
\phi
as a composition
\nu\circ\psi
of two simpler maps
\nu
and
\psi
. The map

\psi:{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N)\longrightarrow\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N^{\natural})

is given by
\psi(u\otimes f)(m)=uf(m)
(
u\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
,
f\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N)
,
m\in M
). We will discuss
\nu
in a moment, but, for the next two paragraphs, we continue to focus on
\psi
.

We will, however, let
M
vary. First, take
M={}_{\mathtt{H}}{\mathtt{H}}
, the left
{\mathtt{H}}
-module which is
{\mathtt{H}}
itself. We abbreviate
{}_{\mathtt{H}}{\mathtt{H}}
to
{\mathtt{H}}
when usage is clear from context. There are well-known isomorphisms
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}({\mathtt{H}},N)\cong N
(here
N
could be any left
{\mathtt{H}}
-module) in which
f\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}({\mathtt{H}},N)
corresponds to
f(1)\in N
. Equivalently,
n\in N
corresponds to
f_{n}\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}({\mathtt{H}},N)
, where
f_{n}(h)=hn
(
h\in{\mathtt{H}}
). Using these isomorphisms and correspondences, we obtain a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes N@>{=}>{}>N^{\natural}\\
@V{}V{}V@A{}A{}A\\
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}({\mathtt{H}},N)@>{\psi}>{}>\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}({\mathtt{H}},N^{\natural}).\end{CD}

with the top arrow equality and each vertical map an isomorphism. As a consequence, the map
\psi
is an isomorphism, under the
M={}_{\mathtt{H}}{\mathtt{H}}
hypothesis. This carries over to the weaker assumption that
M
is a direct sum of finitely many copies of the
{\mathtt{H}}
-modules
{}_{\mathtt{H}}{\mathtt{H}}
. Finally,
\psi
is an isomorphism whenever
M
is a finitely presented over
{\mathtt{H}}
.

We sketch the argument for this last assertion, which follows the spirit of
[
Ho07
]
: Let
E\to G\to M\to 0
be a presentation of
M
in terms of finite direct sums
E
and
G
of the
{\mathtt{H}}
-modules
{}_{\mathtt{H}}{\mathtt{H}}
. Use it to form the columns of the diagram below

\begin{CD}{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(E,N)@>{}>{}>\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(E,N^{\natural})\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(G,N)@>{}>{}>\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(G,N^{\natural})\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N)@>{}>{}>\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N^{\natural})\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
00\end{CD}

The columns are exact, by virtue of partial exactness properties of
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}
, together with flatness of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
over
{\mathcal{Z}}
. The bottom row is
\psi
and the other two rows are the versions of
\psi
obtained by using
G
and
E
, respectively, in the role of
M
. The squares are
both commutative, as can be checked with the formulas for
\psi
which apply in each row. Finally, since the top two rows are isomorphisms, we conclude that the bottom row is an isomorphism (by the 5-Lemma), as asserted.

We now return to the map
\nu:\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N^{\natural})\to\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})
and define it as follows. First, define

\tilde{\nu}:\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(M,N^{\natural})\longrightarrow\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})

on
f\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(M,N^{\natural})
by
\tilde{\nu}(f)(v\otimes f)=vf(m)
(
m\in M,v\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
). From this formula, it is easily seen that
\tilde{\nu}(f)\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})
. Moreover, if
f
belongs to the Hom space
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N^{\natural})
, then
\tilde{\nu}(f)
belongs to
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})
. Starting from
f\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}}(M,N^{\natural})
, we have
hf(m)=f(hm)
, for
h\in{\mathtt{H}},m\in M
. If
v\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, we have

h\tilde{\nu}(f)(v\otimes m)=hvf(m)=vhf(m)=vf(hm)=\tilde{\nu}(f)(v\otimes hm)=\tilde{\nu}(f)h(v\otimes m).

Thus,
\tilde{\nu}(f)\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})
. We already know that
\tilde{\nu}(f)\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})
, so now we have
\tilde{\nu}(f)\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}{\mathtt{H}}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})
. At this point, we can (and do) define
\nu
to be the restriction of
\tilde{\nu}
to
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N^{\natural})
:

\nu:\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N^{\natural})\longrightarrow\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural}).

Our next aim is to show that
\nu
is an isomorphism, assuming
M
is finitely presented (as we assumed when dealing with
\psi
). As before, the case
M={}_{\mathtt{H}}{\mathtt{H}}
(which we abbreviate to
{\mathtt{H}}
) leads to a standard identification
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}({\mathtt{H}},N^{\natural})\cong N^{\natural}
, and there is a similar identification
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}({\mathtt{H}}^{\natural},N^{\natural})\cong N^{\natural}
. A calculation, much as we carried out for
\psi
, leads to a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}N^{\natural}@>{=}>{}>N^{\natural}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}({\mathtt{H}},N^{\natural})@>{\nu}>{}>\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}({\mathtt{H}}^{\natural},N^{\natural}).\end{CD}

We provide a few more details about the diagram and its commutativity. Here, the two vertical maps are “standard”, sending
n^{\natural}\in N^{\natural}
to the unique function in the Hom space drawn below it, and taking the value
n^{\natural}
at 1. We let
f_{n^{\natural}}\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}({\mathtt{H}},N^{\natural})
be the unique function on the left, and
f_{n^{\natural}}^{\natural}\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}({\mathtt{H}}^{\natural},N^{\natural})
on the right (keeping
n^{\natural}
the same). Then,
\nu(f_{n^{\natural}})(1\otimes 1)=f_{n^{\natural}}(1)=n^{\natural}
. Consequently,
\nu(f_{n^{\natural}})=f_{n^{\natural}}^{\natural}
, and the diagram (which has equality as its top row, and the map
\nu
as its bottom row) is now shown to be commutative.
Since the columns are isomorphisms, as is the top row, it follows that the bottom row
\nu
is also an isomorphism.

We can also use a similar argument to show that
\nu
is an isomorphism when
M
is a direct sum
{\mathtt{H}}^{\oplus r}
of finitely many copies of
{\mathtt{H}}
(say
r
of them). In the diagram used for
M={\mathtt{H}}
, replace the variable
{\mathtt{H}}
inside the left “Hom” term with
{\mathtt{H}}^{\oplus r}
and replace
{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}
inside the right “Hom” term with
({\mathtt{H}}^{\natural})^{\oplus r}
. Both terms
N^{\natural}
in the top row are replaced by
(N^{\natural})^{\oplus r}
. Remaining details, showing
\nu
is an isomorphism in this
M={\mathtt{H}}^{\oplus r}
case, are left to the reader.

We can now show that
\nu
is an isomorphism whenever the associated
{\mathtt{H}}
-module
M
has a presentation
E\to G\to M\to 0
by finite direct sums
G
and
E
of copies of
{\mathtt{H}}
. The relevant diagram is displayed below.

\begin{CD}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(E,N^{\natural})@>{}>{}>\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(E^{\natural},N^{\natural})\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(G,N^{\natural})@>{}>{}>\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(G^{\natural},N^{\natural})\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N^{\natural})@>{}>{}>\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
00\end{CD}

The right column is obtained by applying
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes(-)
and, then,
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(-,N^{\natural})
to
E\to G\to M\to 0
. The left column is obtained similarly, omitting the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes(-)
step. Both columns are exact. The rows are given by the maps
\nu
associated with
M,G,E
on their respective rows. By our previous discussion, this implies that the top two rows are isomorphisms. Both squares are commutative, as can be checked with the formulas for
\nu
that apply. By the 5-Lemma, the bottom row
\nu
is also an isomorphism.

It now follows that the composite

\phi=\nu\circ\psi:{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(M,N)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})

is an isomorphism, proving the first assertion of the lemma.

We now assume
M=N
and prove the last assertion for

\phi=\phi_{N,N}^{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},{\mathtt{H}}}
. We begin by showing that
\phi
, as a
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-linear map, induces a natural homomorphism of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-modules between its domain
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(N,N)
and its image
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural},N^{\natural})
. More precisely, for any
u\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
, we show that
\phi(u\otimes 1_{N})
is an element of

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}(M^{\natural},N^{\natural})=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathtt{H}}^{\natural}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes N,{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes N)
that acts on
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes N
via multiplication by
u\otimes 1_{N}
: If
v\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
and
n\in N
, then

\phi(u\otimes 1_{N})(v\otimes n)=uvn=(u\otimes 1_{N})(v\otimes n),

proving the naturality assertion.
Since
v
and
n
were arbitrary, this equation proves the assertion regarding
\phi(u\otimes 1_{N})
.

To establish the isomorphism of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebras, we need only show that
\phi
preserves multiplication. Let
u_{1},u_{2}\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
and
f_{1},f_{2}\in\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathtt{H}}(N,N)
. Then, for
v\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
and
n\in N
,
\phi((u_{1}\otimes f_{1})(u_{2}\otimes f_{2}))(v\otimes n)=\phi(u_{1}u_{2}\otimes f_{1}f_{2})(v\otimes n)=u_{1}u_{2}vf_{1}f_{2}(n)
, and

\phi(u_{1}\otimes f_{1})\cdot\phi(u_{2}\otimes f_{2})(v\otimes n)=\phi(u_{1}\otimes f_{1})(u_{2}v\otimes f_{2}(n))=u_{1}u_{2}vf_{1}f_{2}(n)
, as desired. This completes our arguments for
\phi=\phi_{N,N}^{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},{\mathtt{H}}}
and, hence, the proof of the lemma.
∎

We now prove
a result which strengthens Theorem
3.5.3
upon localizing
{\mathcal{Z}}
away from bad primes.
In the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case,
we take
2
as the only bad prime (thus,
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}:={\mathcal{Z}}[1/2]
in this case). We maintain the notation of Theorem
3.3.1
, so, in particular,

A^{+}=\text{\rm{End}}_{H}(T^{+})
is a certain endomorphism algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}
which has a stratifying system
\{\Delta(\omega),P(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
. Now, base change to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
or
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}[\sqrt{2}]
, and set
A^{+\natural}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes A^{+}
,
A^{+\natural\prime}={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}\otimes A^{+}
.

Theorem 4.3.4
.

(a)
The algebra
A^{+\natural}
over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
is quasi-hereditary of separable type in all cases.

(b)
Except in the case of

{}^{2}F_{4}
, the algebra
A^{+\natural}
over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
is quasi-hereditary of split type.

(c)
In the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case, the algebra
A^{+\natural\prime}
over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
is quasi-hereditary of split type.

Proof.

First, we prove (b). Thus, we are not in the case of
{}^{2}F_{4}
. It has already been shown in Theorem
3.3.1
that
A^{+}
has a stratifying system
\{\Delta(\omega),P(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
, where

\Delta(\omega)=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\omega},T^{+})
and
P(\omega)=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T_{\omega}^{+},T^{+})
. Likewise,
Theorem
3.5.3
gives us that
A^{+}
is standardly stratified with the standard stratification

0=J_{0}\subseteq J_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}=A^{+},

where
J_{i}=A^{+}_{j(i)}
is an idempotent ideal, which is the image of the submodule
P_{j(i)}:=\text{trace}_{P}\big(\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{h}(\mu)\geq j(i)}P(\mu)\big)
of
P=\oplus_{\omega\in\Omega}P(\omega)
(see (
3.5.3
)) under the left
A^{+}
-module isomorphism
A^{+}\cong P
. Thus, each
J_{i}/J_{i-1}
is an ideal of
A^{+}/J_{i-1}
satisfying (HI1)–(HI3).

It remains to study (HI4) under the base change to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
from
{\mathcal{Z}}
, but we will not do that yet. Instead, we study some further interesting properties that do not require base change.

The first is the isomorphism

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(S_{\mu},T^{+}_{\lambda})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(P({\lambda}),\Delta(\mu)),

obtained (in greater generality) at the beginning of the proof of Theorem
1.3.5
. Restricting
{\lambda}
and
\mu
to having the same height, we quickly find that
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(P({\lambda}),\Delta(\mu))\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(\Delta({\lambda}),\Delta(\mu))
. (Use (SS3) and (SS1).) Next, we observe an isomorphism
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(S_{\mu},S_{\lambda})\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(S_{\mu},T^{+}_{\lambda})
from the exact sequence
0\to S_{\lambda}\to T^{+}_{\lambda}\to T^{+}_{\lambda}/S_{\lambda}\to 0
in mod-
{\mathcal{H}}
and the vanishing of
{\mathcal{Z}}
-torsion-free module
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\mu},T^{+}_{\lambda}/S_{\lambda})
. (Base change from
{\mathcal{Z}}
to
\mathbb{Q}(t)
to get the vanishing. Then, observe that the kernel of the restricting map
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\mu},T^{+}_{\lambda})\to\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\mu},T^{+}_{\lambda}/S_{\lambda})=0
is the image of the injective map
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\mu},S_{\lambda})\to\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\mu},T^{+}_{\lambda})
, which is, therefore, both injective and surjective.) Putting together isomorphisms and inverses of isomorphisms (which are also isomorphisms),
we obtain the (
{\mathcal{Z}}
-linear) composite isomorphism, for
{\lambda},\mu
, of the same height:

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\mu},S_{\lambda})\overset{\sim}{\to}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\mu},T^{+}_{\lambda})\overset{\sim}{\to}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(P({\lambda}),\Delta(\mu))\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(\Delta({\lambda}),\Delta(\mu)).

Reading
from left to right, these isomorphisms fit in nicely with the commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\mu},T^{+}_{\lambda})@>{}>{}>\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(P({\lambda}),\Delta(\mu))\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\mu},S_{\lambda})@>{}>{}>\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(\Delta({\lambda}),\Delta(\mu)).\end{CD}

Here, the top row and vertical columns are isomorphisms we have seen before, in which the map in the bottom row arises from the (
{\mathcal{Z}}
-linear) contravariant functor
(-)^{\diamond}=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(-,T^{+})
from mod-
{\mathcal{H}}
to
A^{+}
-mod. We leave it to the reader to check that the diagram is commutative.

The remarkable consequence is that the bottom row map is an isomorphism. Preceding it with the isomorphism

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S({\lambda}),S(\mu))=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}((S_{\lambda})^{*},(S_{\mu})^{*})\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S_{\mu},S_{\lambda}),
it remains an isomorphism. In other words, the composite is a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-linear isomorphism

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(S({\lambda}),S(\mu))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(\Delta({\lambda}),\Delta_{(}\mu)),

now the specialization of a covariant
{\mathcal{Z}}
-linear functor from
{\mathcal{H}}
-mod to
A^{+}
-mod. (The functor in question is the composite
(-)^{\diamond}\circ(-)^{*}
, which is being “specialized” in its domain category, to the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{H}}
-mod formed by the objects
S(\tau)
with
\tau
of the same height as
{\lambda},\nu
.)

Now, return to the
A^{+}
-module
J_{i}/J_{i-1}
, which we have shown in (
3.5.6
) to be isomorphic to
A^{+}_{j(i)}/A^{+}_{j(i)+1}
. In turn, the latter
A^{+}
-module is a direct sum, with various multiplicities, of modules
\Delta(\omega)
, with
\omega\in\Omega
having height
\mathfrak{h}(\omega)=j(i)
. Ignoring uniqueness issues, fix a decomposition

J_{i}/J_{i-1}\cong\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega,\,\mathfrak{h}(\omega)=j(i)}\Delta(\omega)^{\oplus n_{\omega}},

where
j(1)>j(2)>\cdots>j(m)
by (
3.5.5
).
Using the same multiplicities
n_{\omega}
of
\Delta(\omega)
as a direct summand, as in the formula above, define a module
N
for
{\mathcal{H}}
by the formula

(4.3.4)

N:=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega,\,\mathfrak{h}(\omega)=j(i)}S(\omega)^{\oplus n_{\omega}}.

The reader will immediately notice that we have an isomorphism

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{H}}(N,N)\overset{\sim}{\to}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{A^{+}}(J_{i}/J_{i-1},J_{i}/J_{i-1})
, via the functor
(-)^{\diamond}\circ(-)^{*}
, as discussed above. More is true, however. Because the isomorphism is induced by a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-linear functor, it induces an isomorphism of
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebras

(4.3.5)

\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathcal{H}}(N)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{End}}_{A^{+}}(J_{i}/J_{i-1})\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{B}(J),

where, for notational simplicity in the sequel, we set
J=J_{i}/J_{i-1}
and
B=A^{+}/J_{i-1}
when
i
is fixed.

We now make a base change from
{\mathcal{Z}}
to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
to find a verifiable version of (HI4). Note that the base-changed version of (HI1), (HI2), and (HI3) all hold, since base change takes projectives over
{\mathcal{Z}}
(or
B
) to projectives over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
(or
B^{\natural}
). Also, base change takes idempotent ideals to idempotent ideals.

It remains to consider the base-changed version of (HI4). Here, we have to use the flatness of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
over
{\mathcal{Z}}
(which we have) and the existence of finite presentations of
N
(and
J=J_{i}/J_{i-1}
) over
{\mathcal{H}}
(and
B=A^{+}/J_{i-1}
). We may apply Lemma
4.3.3

to the
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebras
{\mathtt{H}}={\mathcal{H}}
and
B
, and the
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
N
and
B
-module
J
, respectively. We thus obtain algebra isomorphisms:

(4.3.6)

\displaystyle\phi_{N,N}^{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},{\mathcal{H}}}

\displaystyle:{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathcal{H}}(N)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural})

\displaystyle\phi_{J,J}^{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},B}

\displaystyle:{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes\text{\rm{End}}_{B}(J)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{End}}_{B^{\natural}}(J^{\natural}).

Combining the two isomorphisms with the one in (
4.3.5
) gives a string of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra isomorphisms:

(4.3.7)

\text{\rm{End}}_{B^{\natural}}(J^{\natural})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes\text{\rm{End}}_{B}(J)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathcal{H}}(N)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural}).

Reading from left to right, the first isomorphism is the inverse of
\phi_{J,J}^{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},B}
as given in (
4.3.6
),
the second is the inverse of the base change from
{\mathcal{Z}}
to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
of (
4.3.5
), the latter map arising from our construction of
N
using left-cell modules (see (
4.3.5
)). The third and last isomorphism in the string is
\phi_{N,N}^{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural},{\mathcal{H}}}
, the map in (
4.3.3
), for
M=N
. To complete the proof in progress of Theorem
4.3.4
(b), we must show that the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra
\text{\rm{End}}_{B^{\natural}}(J^{\natural})
on the left is a direct sum (categorical direct product) of (a finite number of) full matrix algebras over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. By the string of isomorphisms above, it is enough to prove that property for the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra
\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural})
on the right.

Applying Theorem
4.2.1
, we find that
N^{\natural}
is the restriction through
\varpi^{\natural}
(see (
4.1.7
)) of a module
N^{\natural}_{\text{lift}}
for
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
. Of course
\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural})
is isomorphic to
\text{\rm{End}}_{\varpi^{\natural}{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural}_{\text{lift}})
, where the latter endomorphism algebra is isomorphic to
\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural}_{\text{lift}})
by the Reiner observation Lemma
4.2.2
(b).

Next, note from Theorem
4.1.4
(b) that
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
is isomorphic to a direct product of full matrix algebras
M_{m_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
, for a finite sequence
m_{1},m_{2},\ldots,m_{s}
of positive integers; see (
4.1.13
). There is a corresponding decomposition of the identity
1\in{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
into a sum
1=e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{s}
of pairwise orthogonal central idempotents
e_{i}
of
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
with
e_{i}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}={\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}e_{i}\cong M_{m_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
,
i=1,2,\ldots,s
. There is also a decomposition of the
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-module
N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
into
\oplus_{i}e_{i}N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
, each
e_{i}N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
is a left
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-module, the latter acting through
e_{i}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
(and all
e_{j}\neq e_{i}
acting as 0). Clearly,
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}}(e_{i}M,e_{j}M)=0
, for all left
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-modules
M
, whenever
i\neq j
. Thus,

\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}}(M)\cong\bigoplus_{i}\text{\rm{End}}_{e_{i}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}}(e_{i}M)
. (Here and below, we use
\bigoplus
instead of
\Pi
, with some abuse of notation.) Taking
M=N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
and using the previous paragraph,
we have the reduction (all through
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra isomorphisms)

(4.3.8)

\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural})\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}}(N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural})\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\text{\rm{End}}_{e_{i}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}}(e_{i}N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}).

It is useful to note at this point that
N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
is a projective
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-module, since it is projective (even free) as
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-module. See Corollary
4.1.6
(b). It follows easily that each
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-module
e_{j}N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
is projective. Recall that the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra
e_{i}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
is isomorphic to the full matrix algebra
M_{m_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
, well-known to be Morita equivalent to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
.
A
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-linear Morita equivalence which achieves this is a multiplication by an idempotent
f_{i}\in e_{i}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
, corresponding to an idempotent matrix unit
e_{1,1}
in
M_{m_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
, on
e_{i}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-modules. Such an equivalence takes projectives to projectives and preserves endomorphism algebras up to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra isomorphism.

Since (finitely generated) projective
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-modules are free (see Remark
1.3.6
(c)), the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-module
f_{i}e_{i}N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
is free and isomorphic to
{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}^{\oplus n_{i}}
, for some integer
n_{i}\geq 0
, depending on
e_{i}N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
.
We mention here that the ring
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
used as the base ring here is
e_{1,1}M_{m_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})e_{1,1}\cong f_{i}e_{i}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}f_{i}
, the target of the Morita equivalence given by left multiplication by
f_{i}
. We find now that the Morita equivalence induces an isomorphism

(4.3.9)

\text{\rm{End}}_{e_{i}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}}(e_{i}N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural})\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}({{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}^{\oplus n_{i}})\cong M_{n_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}).

Finally, combining these isomorphisms with many others demonstrated above, tracing our way from the left to the right of (
4.3.7
) and (
4.3.8
) and to the direct sum over
i
of (
4.3.9
), we obtain (again with some abuse of notation, using
\bigoplus
instead of
\Pi
)

(4.3.10)

\text{\rm{End}}_{B^{\natural}}(J^{\natural})\cong\bigoplus_{i}M_{n_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}).

This establishes (HI4) and completes the proof of part (b).

Note that (a) follows from (b) except in the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case. Assume we are in the latter case. After making the same changes in the appendix to
Section 4.1 (
{\mathcal{Z}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
, etc.), the above argument shows,
mutadis mutandis,

that
A^{+\natural\prime}
is quasi-hereditary of split type. In particular, it is of separable type. Therefore,
as a faithfully flat descent, Theorem
4.1.12
implies that
A^{+\natural}
itself is of separable type. (Note that each

J_{i}^{\natural\prime}/J_{i-1}^{\natural\prime}
is the base change of the projective module
J_{i}^{\natural}/J^{\natural}_{i-1}
, hence its endomorphism algebra is a corresponding base change of the latter module. Also, note that

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
is faithfully flat over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
.) This not only completes the proof of (a) but also establishes (c).
∎

We remark that the heredity ideals
J_{i}^{\natural}/J_{i-1}^{\natural}
involved in the defining sequence above are not indecomposable in Rouquier’s sense (see Remark
4.3.2
(2)).
The reader may ask if the defining sequence in the theorem can be refined so that only indecomposable split heredity ideals will occur. This is possible due to the fact that each
\Delta^{\natural}(\omega)
is a direct sum of various
\Delta(E)
’s (Theorem
4.2.4
), with the
E
’s all of the same height as
\omega
. Recall
\Lambda:=\text{\rm Irr}(\mathbb{Q}W)
and the preorder
\preceq
on

\Omega
given in (
3.1.1
), which induces a partial order
\preceq
(using the same name) defined via (
4.1.8
) by

(4.3.11)

E\preceq E^{\prime}\iff E_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}=E^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}\quad{{\text{o}r}}\quad\mathfrak{h}(E_{\mathbb{Q}(t)})<\mathfrak{h}(E^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}),\quad E,E^{\prime}\in\Lambda.

For each
E\in\Lambda
,
\Delta(E):=(S^{\natural}_{E})^{\diamond}=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(S_{E}^{\natural},T^{+\natural}),
where the
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
-module
S_{E}^{\natural}:=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}(S^{\natural}(E),{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
is defined in the display (
4.2.3
).

The following corollary continues the argument from the proof of Theorem
4.3.4
(b). We leave the counterpart for the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case in Theorem
4.3.4
(c) to the reader.

Corollary 4.3.5
.

The defining sequence
0=J_{0}^{\natural}\subseteq J_{1}^{\natural}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}^{\natural}=A^{+\natural}

in the proof may be refined to a defining sequence of length
s=|\Lambda|
:

(4.3.12)

0=I_{0}\subseteq I_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq I_{s-1}\subseteq I_{s}=A^{+\natural},

which is called an
indecomposable
defining sequence (partly our terminology).
Moreover, it may be assumed that, for every
1\leq j\leq s
,

(1)

there exists an idempotent
e
in
B^{\prime}:=A^{+\natural}/I_{j-1}
such that
J^{\prime}:=I_{j}/I_{j-1}=B^{\prime}eB^{\prime}
.

(2)

J^{\prime}\cong\Delta(E)^{\oplus l_{j}}\cong\Delta(E)\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B^{\prime}}(\Delta(E),B^{\prime})
, for some
E\in\Lambda
and
l_{j}>0
. (We will see that the
l_{j}
is equal to some
n_{i}
in the display (
4.3.9
)).

Hence,
A^{+\natural}
is also a split quasi-hereditary algebra in the sense of
[
Ro08
, 4.1.4]
.

Proof.

Continuing the argument from the proof of Theorem
4.3.4
(b), we refine each section
J^{\natural}_{h-1}\subseteq J_{h}^{\natural}
(
1\leq h\leq m
). For a fixed
h
,
let
J^{\natural}=J_{h}^{\natural}/J^{\natural}_{h-1}
and
B^{\natural}=A^{+\natural}/J^{\natural}_{h-1}
. Recall from Notation
2.1.2
that the height function
\mathfrak{h}:\Omega\to\mathbb{Z}
induces a height function
\mathfrak{h}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
. Recall also the idempotent decompsition
e_{1}+\cdots+e_{s}=1\in{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
, where
s=|\Lambda|
.
By displays (
4.3.7
), (
4.3.8
), and (
4.3.9
),

\mathcal{E}:=\text{\rm{End}}_{B^{\natural}}(J^{\natural})\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural})\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}}(N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural})\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s_{h}}\text{\rm{End}}_{e_{i}{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}}(e_{i}N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural})\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s_{h}}M_{n_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}).

Here, after relabelling, we assume
n_{i}>0
, for all
1\leq i\leq s_{h}
, and
n_{i}=0
, for
s_{h}<i\leq s
. Note that the numbers
s_{h}
are related to a partition (see (
4.3.4
))

(4.3.13)

\Lambda=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\Lambda_{j(i)},\;\text{ where }\Lambda_{h^{\prime}}:=\{E\in\Lambda\mid\mathfrak{h}(E)=h^{\prime}\}\text{ and }|\Lambda_{j(h)}|=s_{h}.

If
L:=N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
and
L_{i}:=e_{i}N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
(
1\leq i\leq s_{h}
), then
N^{\natural}=L^{\varpi^{\natural}}=\oplus_{i=1}^{s_{h}}L_{i}^{\varpi^{\natural}}
. Thus, denoting the canonical projection map
N^{\natural}\to L_{i}^{\varpi^{\natural}}\subseteq N^{\natural}
by
e_{i}
again, we have
e_{i}(N^{\natural})=L_{i}^{\varpi^{\natural}}
and the identity map
1_{N^{\natural}}=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{s_{h}}
is decomposed into a sum of orthogonal central idempotents. Since the isomorphism
\phi:\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(N^{\natural})\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{E}
is induced by the functor
(\;\,)^{*\diamond}=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(-,T^{+\natural})\circ\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}(-,{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
, the canonical projection
e_{i}
induces the canonical projection
\tilde{e}_{i}=(e_{i})^{*\diamond}:J^{\natural}\to J^{\natural}
.
Hence, the element
1_{J^{\natural}}
of
\mathcal{E}
(i.e., the identity map on
J^{\natural}
) is decomposed into a sum of orthogonal central idempotents
{\tilde{e}}_{i}\in\mathcal{E}
:
1={\tilde{e}}_{1}+{\tilde{e}}_{2}+\cdots+{\tilde{e}}_{s}
, where
{\tilde{e}}_{i}=(e_{i})^{*\diamond}
.
Putting
J^{\natural(i)}={\tilde{e}}_{i}J^{\natural}
, we have
J^{\natural}=\oplus_{i=1}^{s_{h}}J^{\natural(i)}
.

Note that
J^{\natural}
is a
B^{\natural}
-
B^{\natural}
-bimodule. Also, each
J^{\natural(i)}
is a left
B^{\natural}
-module, since the action of
\tilde{e}_{i}\in\mathcal{E}
commutes with that of
B^{\natural}
.
We claim that each
J^{\natural(i)}
(
1\leq i\leq s_{h}
) is also a right
B^{\natural}
-module. Indeed, suppose there exists
b\in B^{\natural}
such that

J^{\natural(i)}b\not\subseteq J^{\natural(i)}
. Then, the composition of the following homomorphisms

J^{\natural(i)}\longrightarrow J^{\natural(i)}b\longrightarrow(J^{\natural(i)}b+J^{\natural(i)})/J^{\natural(i)}\subseteq J^{\natural}/J^{\natural(i)}

is non-zero. This is impossible, since
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B^{\natural}}({\tilde{e}}_{i}J^{\natural},{\tilde{e}}_{j}J^{\natural})={\tilde{e}}_{j}\mathcal{E}{\tilde{e}}_{i}=0
, for all
i\neq j
. Hence, each
J^{\natural(i)}
is a
B^{\natural}
-
B^{\natural}
-bimodule.

Let
J^{\natural}_{h-1,j}=\pi^{-1}(\oplus_{i=1}^{j}J^{\natural(i)})
, where
\pi:J^{\natural}_{h}\to J_{h}^{\natural}/J^{\natural}_{h-1}
is the obvious surjective homomorphism. Each
J^{\natural}_{h-1,j}
is an ideal of
A^{+\natural}
and the section
J^{\natural}_{h-1}\subseteq J_{h}^{\natural}
is refined as

J^{\natural}_{h-1}=J^{\natural}_{h-1,0}\subseteq J^{\natural}_{h-1,1}\subseteq J^{\natural}_{h-1,2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J^{\natural}_{h-1,s_{h}}=J_{h}^{\natural}.

Note that such a refinement can be done for any order on
\Lambda_{h^{\prime}}
.
Combining these refinements for all
h
results in a candidate defining sequence which has length
\sum_{h=1}^{m}s_{h}=s=|\Lambda|
.

We now prove that it does give a defining sequence by checking (HI1)–(HI4) for each
J^{\prime}:=J^{\natural}_{h-1,i}/J^{\natural}_{h-1,i-1}\cong J^{\natural(i)}
and
B^{\prime}:=A^{+\natural}/J^{\natural}_{h-1,i-1}\cong B^{\natural}/\oplus_{j=1}^{i-1}J^{\natural(j)}
.

The proof for (HI1) and (HI2) is clear, since the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-module

B^{\prime}/J^{\prime}\cong A^{+\natural}/J^{\natural}_{h-1,i}\cong A^{+\natural}/J^{\natural}_{h}\oplus J^{\natural}_{h}/J^{\natural}_{h-1,i}
is
a direct sum of two projective
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-modules, and
{}_{B^{\prime}}J^{\prime}={}_{B^{\natural}}J^{\prime}
is a direct summand of the projective
B^{\natural}
-module
{}_{B^{\natural}}J^{\natural}
.
(HI4) may be verified as follows:

(4.3.14)

\text{\rm{End}}_{B^{\prime}}(J^{\prime})\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{B^{\prime}}(\tilde{e}_{i}J^{\natural})\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{B^{\natural}}(\tilde{e}_{i}J^{\natural})\cong\tilde{e}_{i}\mathcal{E}\cong M_{n_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}).

For (HI3), we first note from the display (
3.5.4
) that the ideal
J_{h}^{\natural}=A_{j(h)}^{+\natural}
is equal to
A^{+\natural}fA^{+\natural}
, for some idempotent
f
. Then the ideal
J^{\natural}=B^{\natural}\bar{f}B^{\natural}
, where
\bar{f}=f+J^{\natural}_{h-1}
defines an idmpotent in
J^{\natural}
. If
\tilde{f}_{i}=\tilde{e}_{i}(\bar{f})
, for each
i
(
1\leq i\leq s_{h}
), then
\tilde{f}_{i}^{2}=\tilde{e}_{i}(\bar{f})\tilde{e}_{i}(\bar{f})=\tilde{e}_{i}(\tilde{e}_{i}(\bar{f})\bar{f})=\tilde{e}_{i}(\tilde{e}_{i}(\bar{f}^{2}))=\tilde{f}_{i}
and

J^{\natural(i)}=\tilde{e}_{i}(B^{\natural}\bar{f}B^{\natural})=B^{\natural}\tilde{f}_{i}B^{\natural}
. Hence,
J^{\prime}=B^{\prime}\tilde{f}_{i}B^{\prime}
is idempotent, proving (HI3) and (1) as well.

It remains to prove (2). The assertion that
J^{\prime}
is isomorphic to a direct sum of
\Delta(E)
, for some
E\in\Lambda
, can be seen as follows.
Since, by the proof of the theorem and recalling the notation
L_{i}=e_{i}N_{\text{lift}}^{\natural}
,
f_{i}L_{i}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural^{\oplus n_{i}}}
(see the discussion above the diaplay (
4.3.9
)), it follows from the Morita equivalence (between
M_{n_{i}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
) that
L_{i}\cong E_{\natural}^{\oplus n_{i}}
, where
E=E_{h^{\prime},i}\in\Lambda_{h^{\prime}}:=\{E_{h^{\prime},i}\mid 1\leq i\leq s_{h}\}
for some height
h^{\prime}=j(h)
(see (
4.3.4
)), and the
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
-module
L_{i}^{\varpi^{\natural}}\cong S^{\natural}(E)^{\oplus n_{i}}
; see (
4.2.3
). (Here we remind the reader that
E_{\natural}

(E\in\Lambda)
is a
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
-lattice of the irreducible
{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
-module
E_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
.) Hence,
(e_{i}(N^{\natural}))^{*\diamond}=(L_{i}^{\varpi^{\natural}})^{*\diamond}\cong\Delta(E_{h^{\prime},i})^{\oplus n_{i}}
.
Finally, we have

J^{\natural(i)}=\tilde{e}_{i}(J^{\natural})=(e_{i}(N^{\natural}))^{*\diamond}\cong\Delta(E_{h^{\prime},i})^{\oplus n_{i}}.

Since
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B^{\prime}}(J^{\prime},B^{\prime}/J^{\prime})=0
(see
[
Ro08
, Lem. 4.4]
), it follows that

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B^{\prime}}(\Delta(E_{h^{\prime}i}),B^{\prime})=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B^{\prime}}(\Delta(E_{h^{\prime},i}),J^{\prime})\cong({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})^{\oplus n_{i}}.

This implies the last isomorphism in (2).

It remains to verify Rouquier’s definition
[
Ro08
, 4.1.4]
for split quasi-hereditary algebras. By the definition in display (
4.3.11
), each coideal
\Gamma
of
\Lambda
has the form
\Gamma=\big(\cup_{h^{\prime\prime}>h^{\prime}}\Lambda_{h^{\prime\prime}}\big)\cup\Phi
, where
\Phi\subseteq\Lambda_{h^{\prime}}
for some
h^{\prime}=j(h)
.
Thus, each indecomposable defining sequence of the form (
4.3.12
) defines a sequence of coideals
\{\Gamma_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq s\}
of
\Lambda
such that the set of ideals
\{I_{\Gamma_{i}}:=I_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq s\}
satisfies the two conditions in
[
Ro08
, 4.1.4]
.
This proves that
A^{+\natural}
is a split quasi-hereditary algebra in the sense of Rouquier, completing the proof of the corollary.
∎

Corollary 4.3.6
.

Maintain the notations of Corollary
4.3.5
. There exists an idempotent
e_{1}\in B^{\prime}
such that
B^{\prime}e_{1}\cong\Delta(E)
as left
B^{\prime}
-modules.

Proof.

By Corollary
4.3.5
(1) and
[
DPS18
, Prop. 2.2]
, we have

J^{\prime}=B^{\prime}eB^{\prime}\cong B^{\prime}e\otimes_{eB^{\prime}e}eB^{\prime},

\text{\rm{End}}_{B^{\prime}}(J^{\prime})
is Morita equivalent to
eB^{\prime}e
, and
eB^{\prime}
is a projective

eB^{\prime}e
-module.
By (
4.3.14
), we see that
eB^{\prime}e
is Morita equivalent to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. Since projective finite
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-modules are free, it follows from the Morita equivalence that
eB^{\prime}e\cong M_{m}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
, for some
m\geq 1
.

If
m=1
, then
eB^{\prime}e\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
and
J^{\prime}=B^{\prime}eB^{\prime}\cong B^{\prime}e\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}eB^{\prime}\cong B^{\prime}e\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B^{\prime}}(B^{\prime}e,B^{\prime})
.
Thus, the proof of
[
Ro08
, Prop. 4.7]
and Corollary
4.3.5
(2) imply
B^{\prime}e\cong\Delta(E)
. So, we take
e_{1}=e
in this case.

Suppose now
m>1
. Then, there exist idempotents
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}
(corresponding to the matrix idempotents
e_{1,1},\ldots,e_{m,m}
in
M_{m}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
) such that
e=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{m}
and
e_{i}e_{j}=\delta_{i,j}e_{i}
. Thus,
B^{\prime}e=B^{\prime}e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus B^{\prime}e_{m}
and
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B^{\prime}}(B^{\prime}e_{i},B^{\prime}e_{j})\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. (The right action of
eB^{\prime}e\cong M_{m}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})
on
B^{\prime}e
may be interpreted as matrix multiplication.) Hence,
B^{\prime}e\cong(B^{\prime}e_{1})^{\oplus m}
. Since the left
eB^{\prime}e
-module
eB^{\prime}
is projective, it follows (from the Morita equivalence between
eB^{\prime}e
and
e_{1}B^{\prime}e_{1}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
) that the
e_{1}B^{\prime}e_{1}
-module
e_{1}B^{\prime}=e_{1}(eB^{\prime})
is projective (over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
) and, hence, is free:
e_{1}B^{\prime}\cong({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})^{\oplus m^{\prime}}
for some
m^{\prime}>0
. The Morita equivalence gives
eB^{\prime}\cong({{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}^{m})^{\oplus m^{\prime}}\cong C_{1}^{\oplus m^{\prime}}
, where
C_{1}=M_{m}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})e_{1,1}
. Consequently,

J^{\prime}\cong B^{\prime}e\otimes_{eB^{\prime}e}eB^{\prime}\cong(B^{\prime}e_{1})^{\oplus m}\otimes_{M_{m}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural})}C_{1}^{\oplus m^{\prime}}\cong(B^{\prime}e_{1})^{\oplus m^{\prime}}.

In other words, we may write
J^{\prime}=P_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus P_{m^{\prime}}
, where
B^{\prime}e_{1}\cong P_{i}
. Taking isomorphism

\phi_{i}:B^{\prime}e_{1}\to P_{i}
, we have

P_{i}=\phi_{i}(B^{\prime}e_{1})=B^{\prime}e_{1}\phi_{i}(e_{1})\subseteq B^{\prime}e_{1}B^{\prime}
. Hence,
J^{\prime}\subseteq B^{\prime}e_{1}B^{\prime}
. Clearly, we have
B^{\prime}e_{1}B^{\prime}=B^{\prime}ee_{1}B^{\prime}\subseteq B^{\prime}eB^{\prime}=J^{\prime}
. This shows
J^{\prime}=B^{\prime}e_{1}B^{\prime}
. By the argument for the
m=1
case, we obtain
B^{\prime}e_{1}\cong\Delta(E)
. The corollary is proven.
∎

Corollary
4.3.5
together with
[
Ro08
, Th. 4.16]
gives the following result.

Corollary 4.3.7
.

If
\mathcal{C}:=A^{+\natural}\text{-mod}
is the category of finite
A^{+\natural}
-modules,
then
(\mathcal{C},\{\Delta(E)\}_{E\in\Lambda})
is a highest weight category in the sense of
[
Ro08
, Def. 4.11]
, where
\Lambda
is the poset with respect to the order defined in display (
4.3.11
).

Note that the projective modules
P(E)
used to form the stratifying system
\{\Delta(E),P(E)\}_{E\in\Lambda}
may be recursively constructed; see the proof of
[
Ro08
, Lem. 4.12]
.

Similarly to
A^{+\natural}
and
A^{+\natural\prime}

as defined above the statement of Theorem
4.3.4
, we define, for a poset ideal
\Theta
of
(\Omega,\leq_{L})
,
A^{\ddagger\natural}_{\Theta}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}A^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}
and
A^{\ddagger\natural\prime}_{\Theta}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}A^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}
, where
A^{\ddagger}_{\Theta}
is defined in (
2.4.6
). For later use in Sections 5.1 and 5.4, we need the quasi-hereditary structures of these algebras.

Theorem 4.3.8
.

All the statements
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
in Theorem
4.3.4
are true if
A^{+\natural}
and
A^{+\natural\prime}
are replaced by
A^{\ddagger\natural}_{\Theta}
and
A^{\ddagger\natural\prime}_{\Theta}
, respectively. Moreover, the base-changed sequence of the defining sequence (
3.5.8
) to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
(or
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
) may be refined to a defining sequence of length
s=|\Lambda_{\Theta}|
, where
\Lambda_{\Theta}:=\{E\in\Lambda\mid E_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}|S(\omega)_{\mathbb{Q}(t)},\text{ for some }\omega\in\Theta\}
,

0=I_{0}^{+}\subseteq I_{1}^{+}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq I_{s}^{+}=A^{\ddagger\natural}_{\Theta}

such that
\bar{I}_{j}:=I_{j}/I_{j-1}
is an indecomposable heredity ideal of
B_{j}:=A^{\ddagger\natural}_{\Theta}/I_{j-1}
. In particular, the highest weight category
A^{\ddagger\natural}_{\Theta}
-mod has standard objects

\Delta^{+}(E):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}}(S^{\natural}_{E},T^{\ddagger\natural}),\;\;\forall E\in\Lambda

such that, if
\Delta^{+}(E)\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{B_{j}}(\Delta^{+}(E),B_{j})\cong\bar{I}_{j}
, for some
1\leq j\leq s
, then
\Delta^{+}(E)\cong B_{j}e
for some idempotent
e\in B_{j}
with
eB_{j}e\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
.

Proof.

Note that, by Remark
3.5.4
, Theorem
1.3.5
is applicable in this case. Thus, the proof of Theorem
4.3.4
can be easily modified by simply adding a
+
to the superscripts of the various objects throughout its proof to get a proof for the first assertion. (The first assertion also follows easily from the Morita equivalence discussed in Sec 3.4.)

The remaining assertions follow from arguments similar to the proofs of Corollaries
4.3.5
and
4.3.6
.
∎

Finally, in a different direction, the following remark will be useful in the next chapter.

Remark 4.3.9
.

Let
B
be a split quasi-hereditary algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}
. (For example,
B=A^{+\natural}
as in Theorem
4.3.4
(b) above.)

If
R

is a commutative Noetherian
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra,
B_{R}:=R\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}B
is a split quasi-hereditary algebra over
R
.
This is verified by directly checking the
axioms at the start of this section. For example, if
J
is a heredity ideal in
B
, then
{}_{B}J
is a projective
B
-module. It follows easily, using this projectivity, that
the
R
-algebra

\text{\rm{End}}_{B_{R}}(_{B_{R}}J)
is isomorphic to the
R
-algebra
R\otimes_{{\mathcal{Z}}}\text{\rm{End}}_{B}(_{B}J)
and so,
is split over
R
. Thus, the operation
R\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}-
carries a defining sequence for
B
(exhibiting a splitting) into a similar defining sequence for
B_{R}
. The other steps in checking that

B_{R}
is split quasi-hereditary are straightforward.

The other parts (a) and (c) of Theorems
4.3.4
and
4.3.8
similarly behave well under base change.

Chapter 5
Some related applications

We now present some applications of our theory to several related topics. First, the split quasi-heredity of
A^{+\natural}
gives rise to a full standard basis for
A^{+\natural}
, making its centralizer subalgebras such as
A
and
{\mathcal{H}}
into standardly based algebras. Second, the quasi-heredity of
A^{+\natural}
gives a unitriangular property of several decomposition matrices, including the unipotent principal blocks of the corresponding finite groups of Lie type in cross characteristic. Third, using a recent Schur duality of Bao–Wang for certain
i
-quantum groups and Hecke algebras of type
B/C
, we relate representations of finite orthogonal/symplectic groups to those of
i
-quantum groups. Finally, we end this chapter with some open problems.

5.0.
Introduction

The usual decomposition number framework for finite groups is the classical one of Brauer, involving integral representations over DVRs (discrete valuation rings, usually complete); see, e.g.,
[
Se77
]
. However, the theory is not adequate for generic Hecke algebras
{\mathcal{H}}
(or
A
or
A^{+}
), because the generic versions of these algebras are defined over

{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]
, and some authors introduce even more variables. Geck and Rouquier provided in
[
GR97
]
a general theory which applies to
{\mathcal{Z}}
and many higher dimensional commutative rings.
The authors of this monograph introduced in
[
DPS98a
]
a decomposition number theory applicable to
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebras, which (though not applicable to commutative base rings of Krull dimension
>2
) is closer to the original Brauer framework.

We will use the
[
DPS98a
]
theory in this section. An exposition, with several new results, is provided in Appendix C. We suggest that the reader look at this section first, then read some of Appendix C. After that, both the present section and Appendix C may be read in tandem.

Regular local triples.
Consider a “local triple”
(R,Q,F)
, with a local Noetherian integral domain
R

(with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
), fraction field
Q
, and residue field
F=R/\mathfrak{m}
of
R
. Several evident variations on this notational theme are useful. For example, if
R
is just a domain, call
(R,Q,F)
a ring triple. As before,
Q
is the fraction field of
R
, and there is given an identification of
F
with
R/\mathfrak{m}
for an implicitly given maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
R
. Also, if
R
is a DVR (automatically “local”), then
(R,Q,F)
is a ‘‘DVR triple.” If
R
is a regular ring (in the sense of

[
M86
]
),
then the ring triple
(R,Q,F)
is a “regular triple.” Regular triples will play an important role in this discussion—and
R
will usually be a regular local ring of Krull dimension
\leq 2
.

In case
R
is a regular local ring of Krull dimension
d
, we call
(R,Q,F)
a
d
-regular local triple.

Let
B
be an
R
-free and finite algebra. Also, assume that
R
is a regular local ring of Krull dimension
\leq 2
. Let

B_{Q}:=B\otimes_{R}Q
be the corresponding finite-dimensional
Q
-algebra, and define
B_{F}
similarly.
A basic result given in Theorem
C.2
(and based on work of Auslander-Goldman
[
AG60a
,
AG60b
]
) states that if
\mathfrak{X}
is a finite
B_{Q}
-module, then
\mathfrak{X}\cong X_{Q}
, for some finite
R
-free
B
-submodule
X\subseteq\mathfrak{X}
.
Furthermore, assume that
X_{Q}
is irreducible. If
Y
is a second
R
-free
B
-submodule such that
\mathfrak{X}\cong Y_{Q}
, then
X_{F}
and
Y_{F}
have the same composition factors (with the same multiplicities) as
B_{F}
-modules. This means the decomposition numbers of
B
defined below depend only on
(R,Q,F)
.

Thus, under the hypotheses and notation of Theorem
C.2
, there is a natural way to relate the representation theory of

B_{F}
(often a
modular theory
, usually meaning that
F
has positive characteristic,
1
1

1

For algebras over
{\mathcal{Z}}
, if the order of
t
in
F
is finite (that is, the image of
t
is a root of unity), that order is often called the quantum characteristic. “Modular theory” can occur in this case even when
F
has characteristic zero!
) with that of
B_{Q}
(often a
characteristic zero theory
). In this regard,
we are particularly interested in the
decomposition map
in display (
C.2
). Note that the hypothesis that the Krull dimension of
R
is at most 2 is often satisfied in the present monograph, since
R
is usually closely related to the ring
{\mathcal{Z}}
of integral Laurent polynomials in a variable
t
.

Decomposition matrices.
Let
(R,Q,F)
be a regular local triple, where
R
has Krull dimension
\leq 2
. (See above.)
If
B=B_{R}
is a finite and
R
-free algebra, we can use the triple to associate decomposition maps, decomposition numbers, and decomposition matrices to
B
, imitating the classical constructions.
More explicitly, suppose that

X
is a
B
-module which is finite and projective (thus free) over
R
. Assume that

X_{Q}
is an irreducible
B_{Q}
-module. If
L
is an irreducible
B_{F}
-module, then the decomposition number
d_{L,X_{Q}}
is defined to be the multiplicity
[X_{F}:L]
of
L
as a composition factor of
X_{F}
. This number depends only on
X_{Q}
not on the choice of
X

[
DPS98a
, §1.1]
. Collectively, the decomposition numbers form the
decomposition matrix
, with rows indexed by “all non-isomorphic” irreducible
B_{F}
-modules, and columns indexed
by “all non-isomorphic” irreducible
B_{K}
-modules.
2
2

2

Many authors take the decomposition matrix to be the transpose
of our decomposition matrix. Thus, the rows of the decomposition matrix index the irreducible
B_{K}
-modules. This causes no difficulty, provided the reader is aware of the different conventions.

Notice that, as seen above, these decomposition numbers for
B
depend only on
B
and the underlying triple
(R,Q,F)
. It is, however, common also to assume (although we will not do it) that
the irreducible modules indexing the rows and columns
are absolutely irreducible. Equivalently, both
B_{F}
and
B_{Q}
are split as algebras. In many cases, this
can be achieved by enlarging

R
and replacing
B
by
B_{R}
. See Propositions
C.12
and
C.13
, as well as Proposition
C.11
regarding uniqueness.

Although we often speak of “the” decomposition matrix, it is, in fact, only determined up to a
permutation of its rows and a permutation of its columns, unless some specific (or restricted) ordering of irreducible modules is required. We will usually be somewhat informal about this and hope that it does not cause too much confusion.

Decomposition submatrices and unitriangularity.

Given two (rectangular) matrices
D=(d_{ij})
and
D^{\prime}=(d^{\prime}_{rs})
(decomposition matrices or not), we say that
D
is a submatrix of
D^{\prime}
if there are injective assignments
i\mapsto i^{\prime}
and
j\mapsto j^{\prime}
of the row (respectively, column) indices of
D
such that

d_{i,j}=d^{\prime}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}},

for each row index
i
and column index
j
of
D
.

A rectangular matrix
D=(d_{ij})
over any ring is said to be (upper)
unitriangular
if

(1)
D
has at least as many columns as rows;

(2)
d_{ij}=0
if
i>j
; and

(3)
d_{ii}=1
for each row index
i
.

5.1.
Standardly based algebras

In this section, we consider the algebras
A
and
A^{+}
from the point of view of the theory of standardly
based algebras as developed in Du-Rui
[
DR98
]
(see
[
KM20
]
for a new development). Such algebras are quite close to the Graham-Lehrer theory of cellular algebras
[
GL96
]
. For example, they have a similar family of bilinear forms (see (
5.1.2
) below), and all cellular algebras are standardly based. But, there is no requirement that a standardly based algebra have an involution as in
[
GL96
]
.

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algebra over a commutative ring
{\mathscr{K}}
.
We say that
\mathcal{A}
is a
standardly based algebra
(SBA) with respect to a poset
(\Lambda,\leq)
if the following
conditions hold:

(a) For any
\lambda\in\Lambda
, there are index sets

I(\lambda)
and
J(\lambda)
and subsets of
\mathcal{A}
,

{\mathsf{B}}^{\lambda}=\{a_{i,j}^{\lambda}\mid(i,j)\in I({\lambda})\times J({\lambda})\}

such that
{\mathsf{B}}:=\bigcup_{{\lambda}\in\Lambda}{\mathsf{B}}^{\lambda}
is a disjoint union and forms a
{\mathscr{K}}
-basis for
\mathcal{A}
.

(b) For any
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
a_{i,j}^{\lambda}\in{\mathsf{B}}
, we have

\displaystyle a\cdot a_{i,j}^{\lambda}\equiv\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I({\lambda})}f_{i^{\prime},{\lambda}}(a,i)a_{i^{\prime},j}^{\lambda}\text{\,\rm{mod}\,}(\mathcal{A}^{>{\lambda}})

\displaystyle a_{i,j}^{\lambda}\cdot a\equiv\sum_{j^{\prime}\in J({\lambda})}f^{\text{\rm o}}_{{\lambda},j^{\prime}}(a,j)a_{i,j^{\prime}}^{\lambda}\text{\,\rm{mod}\,}(\mathcal{A}^{>{\lambda}}),

where
\mathcal{A}^{>{\lambda}}
is the
{\mathscr{K}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{A}
generated by
{\mathsf{B}}^{\mu}
with
\mu>{\lambda}
, and the coefficients
f_{i^{\prime},{\lambda}}(a,i)
and
f^{\text{\rm o}}_{{\lambda},j^{\prime}}(a,j)\in{\mathscr{K}}
, defined by the display, are independent of
j

and
i
, respectively.

We also call
{\mathsf{B}}
a
standard basis
for
\mathcal{A}
.

For any coideal
\Gamma
of
\Lambda
, the submodule
A^{\Gamma}:=\text{span}(\cup_{\lambda\in\Gamma}{\mathsf{B}}^{\lambda})
is an ideal of
{\mathcal{A}}
. For a finite
\Lambda
, if we order
\Lambda=\{{\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2},\ldots,{\lambda}_{m}\}
such that
\Gamma_{i}=\{{\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2},\ldots,{\lambda}_{i}\}
is a coideal for every
1\leq i\leq m
, then we obtain a sequence of ideals

(5.1.1)

0=J_{0}\subseteq J_{1}\subseteq J_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}={\mathcal{A}},

where
J_{i}={\mathcal{A}}^{\Gamma_{i}}
. Thus, every section
A^{{\lambda}_{i}}:=J_{i}/J_{i-1}
is an
{\mathcal{A}}
-
{\mathcal{A}}
-bimodule.

Given a SBA as above, we may introduce, for each
{\lambda}\in\Lambda
, a
function
f_{\lambda}:J({\lambda})\times I({\lambda})\to{\mathscr{K}}
defined as follows:
for any
a_{ij}^{\lambda},a_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}^{\lambda}\in{\mathsf{B}}^{\lambda}
, there is a unique
element
f_{\lambda}(j,i^{\prime})\in{\mathscr{K}}
(in other words,
f_{\lambda}(j,i^{\prime}):=f_{i,{\lambda}}(a_{i,j}^{\lambda},i^{\prime})=f^{\text{\rm o}}_{{\lambda},j^{\prime}}(a_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},}^{\lambda},j)
) such that

a_{ij}^{\lambda}a_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}^{\lambda}\equiv f_{\lambda}(j,i^{\prime})a_{ij^{\prime}}^{\lambda}\text{\,\rm{mod}\,}(\mathcal{A}^{>\lambda}).

Definition 5.1.2
.

For
{\lambda}\in\Lambda
, we define
\Delta({\lambda})
(resp.

\Delta^{\!{\text{\rm o}}}({\lambda})
) to be the left (resp. right)
\mathcal{A}
-module with
{\mathscr{K}}
-basis

\{a_{i}^{\lambda}\mid i\in I({\lambda})\}
(resp.
\{b_{j}^{\lambda}\mid j\in J({\lambda})\}
)
and the module action defined by

aa_{i}^{\lambda}=\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I({\lambda})}f_{i^{\prime},{\lambda}}(a,i)a_{i^{\prime}}^{\lambda}\quad(\text{resp.},b_{j}^{\lambda}a=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in J({\lambda})}f^{\text{\rm o}}_{{\lambda},j^{\prime}}(a,j)b_{j^{\prime}}^{\lambda}.)

We shall call
the modules
\Delta({\lambda})
(respectively,

\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda})
) the
standard
modules in the category
\mathcal{A}
-mod (respectively, mod-
{\mathcal{A}}
).

One sees easily from the definition that there is an
{\mathcal{A}}
-
{\mathcal{A}}
-bimodule isomorphism

A^{\lambda}\cong\Delta({\lambda})\otimes\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda})
.

We define (compare
[
GL96
, 2.3]
) a bilinear pairing

\beta_{{\lambda}}:\Delta({\lambda})\times\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda})\rightarrow{\mathscr{K}}
by putting

(5.1.2)

\beta_{{\lambda}}(a_{i}^{\lambda},b_{j}^{\lambda})=f_{{\lambda}}(j,i).

Clearly,
\beta_{\lambda}
is “associative” in a natural sense, see
[
DR98
, Lem. 2.3.1(a)]
.
A SBA
{\mathcal{A}}
is called a
standardly full-based algebra
if
\text{im}(\beta_{\lambda})={\mathscr{K}}
for every
{\lambda}
.

When
{\mathscr{K}}
is a field, these forms can be used to determine a classification of irreducible left
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules. In this case, we first observe that
\Delta({\lambda})
is a cyclic
{\mathcal{A}}
-module if
\beta_{\lambda}\neq 0
.
Let
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{1,{\mathscr{K}}}=\{{\lambda}\in\Lambda\mid\beta_{\lambda}\neq 0\}
and, for
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{1}
, let

L({\lambda}):=\Delta({\lambda})/\text{\rm rad}\Delta({\lambda}),\;\text{ where }\text{\rm rad}\Delta({\lambda})=\{v\in\Delta({\lambda})\mid\beta_{\lambda}(v,y)=0\;\forall y\in\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda})\}

Lemma 5.1.3

(
[
DR98
, Th. 2.4.1]
)
.

Maintain the notation introduced above and assume that
{\mathcal{A}}
is a finite-dimensional standardly based algebra
over a field
{\mathscr{K}}
with respect to a given poset
\Lambda
. Then:

(a)

For each
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{1}
,
L({\lambda})
is absolutely irreducible, and
\{L({\lambda})\}_{{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{1}}
forms a complete set of all non-isomorphic irreducible
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules.

(b)

In particular,
|\Lambda_{1}|
is the number of non-isomorphic irreducible
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules.

(c)

If
L({\lambda})
is a composition factor of
\Delta(\mu)
, then
{\lambda}\leq\mu
.

(d)

If
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{1}
, then
[\Delta({\lambda}):L({\lambda})]=1
.

Corollary 5.1.4
.

Let
(K,{\mathcal{O}},k)
be a local triple in which
{\mathcal{O}}
is regular of Krull dimension
\leq 2
and suppose that
{\mathcal{A}}
is a SBA over
{\mathcal{O}}
associated with the poset
\Lambda
such that
{\mathcal{A}}_{K}
is finite dimensional and semisimple. Then the decomposition matrix
D
of
{\mathcal{A}}
is unitriangular. If, in addition,
{\mathcal{A}}
is standardly full-based, then
D
is also square.

Proof.

Clearly,
{\mathcal{A}}_{K}
is a SBA with standard modules
\Delta({\lambda})_{K}
,
\lambda\in\Lambda
, and has a filtration of ideals obtained by base changing (
5.1.1
) to
K
whose sections
I:={\mathcal{A}}_{K}^{{\lambda}_{i}}

are ideals of
{\mathcal{A}}_{K}
(since
{\mathcal{A}}_{K}
is semisimple). Thus,
I^{2}=I
and, hence, there exists an idempotent
e\in I
such that
I={\mathcal{A}}_{K}e{\mathcal{A}}_{K}
. This implies
\Lambda_{1,K}=\Lambda
. Now the first assertion for the
|\Lambda_{1,k}|\times|\Lambda|
-matirx
D
follows from the lemma above. If
{\mathcal{A}}
is standardly full-based, then
\Lambda_{1,k}=\Lambda
. Hence,
D
is a square matrix.
∎

The next proposition follows from a general result proved in
[
DR98
, (4.2.7)]
. The assumption there that the base ring
k
be a commutative local ring is not necessary here when
k={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
(or
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
if we are in the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case). This is because Swan’s result
[
Sw78
, p.111]
implies that any finite projective
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module is automatically free.
3
3

3

Note that
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}=\mathbb{Z}^{\natural}[t,t^{-1}]
, where
\mathbb{Z}^{\natural}
is a PID. Similarly,
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}={\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}=\mathbb{Z}^{\prime\natural}[t,t^{-1}]
, where
\mathbb{Z}^{\prime\natural}
is a PID. Thus, Swan’s result
[
Sw78
, p.111]
applies.
For completeness, we provide a constructive argument for the proposition.

Proposition 5.1.5
.

(a) Assume that we are not in the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case. Then, the split quasi-hereditary
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra
A^{+\natural}
is a standardly full-based algebra.

(b) In the case of
{}^{2}F_{4}
, the split quasi-hereditary
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
-algebra
A^{+\natural\prime}
is a standardly full-based algebra.

Proof.

We only give the proof in case (a).
Let
{\mathcal{A}}:=A^{+\natural}
and
let
\Lambda=\text{Irr}({\mathbb{Q}W})
. Then, by Theorem
4.3.4
(b),
{\mathcal{A}}
is a split quasi-hereditary algebra, and

\Lambda
becomes a poset with the partial order given in (
4.3.11
) which is induced from the preorder (
3.1.1
).
Consider the defining sequence of length
n=|\Lambda|
given in Corollary
4.3.5
:

0=I_{0}\subset I_{1}\subset I_{2}\subset\cdots\subset I_{n}={\mathcal{A}}.

Assume that the chain is associated with the linear order
{\lambda}^{(1)},{\lambda}^{(2)},\ldots,{\lambda}^{(n)}
on
\Lambda
, compatible with the partial order in
\Lambda
.
For each
i=1,\ldots,n
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}:={\mathcal{A}}/I_{i-1}
is quasi-hereditary and
\bar{I}_{i}=I_{i}/I_{i-1}
is a heredity ideal of
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
. The split condition then implies, by Corollary
4.3.5
(2), that there is an
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
-
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
-bimodule isomorphism

(5.1.3)

\bar{I}_{i}\overset{\phi_{i}}{\cong}\Delta(E)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}^{\natural}}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{i}}(\Delta(E),{\mathcal{A}}_{i}),

where
{\lambda}^{(i)}=E
. Since both

\Delta({\lambda}^{(i)}):=\Delta(E)
and
\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda}^{(i)}):=\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{i}}(\Delta(E),{\mathcal{A}}_{i})
are
\mathcal{Z}^{\natural}
-projective, they are free
\mathcal{Z}^{\natural}
-modules.

Now, we may construct a standard basis by induction. The case for
i=n
is clear, since bases for
\Delta({\lambda}^{(n)})
and
\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda}^{(n)})
give rise to a standard basis for
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
. Assume now, for
1<k\leq n
, that a standard basis
{\mathsf{B}}_{k}=\bigcup_{l=k}^{n}\{b_{i,j}^{{\lambda}^{(l)}}\mid i\in I({\lambda}^{(l)}),j\in J({\lambda}^{(l)})\}
, for
\mathcal{A}_{k}={\mathcal{A}}/I_{k-1}
has been constructed. Use (
5.1.3
) and the bases
\{a_{i}\}
and
\{b_{j}\}
for
\Delta({\lambda}^{(k-1)})
and
\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda}^{(k-1})
, respectively, to get a basis
\{a^{{\lambda}^{(k-1)}}_{i,j}=\phi_{k-1}^{-1}(a_{i}\otimes b_{j})\}_{i,j}
for
\bar{I}_{k-1}
. This together with a pre-image
\{a_{i,j}^{{\lambda}^{(l)}}\}_{l,i,j}
of the basis
{\mathsf{B}}_{k}

under
\pi_{k}:{\mathcal{A}}_{k-1}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
, the natural homomorphism, gives a basis

{\mathsf{B}}_{k-1}=\bigcup_{l=i-1}^{n}\{a_{i,j}^{{\lambda}^{(l)}}\mid i\in I({\lambda}^{(l)}),j\in J({\lambda}^{(l)})\},

for
{\mathcal{A}}_{k-1},
. Here, we require
{\mathsf{B}}_{k-1}
to satisfy the condition

(a_{i,j}^{{\lambda}^{(l)}}+I_{k-1})/I_{k-1}=b_{i,j}^{{\lambda}^{(l)}}
, for all
l=k,\ldots,n
. Now, by induction,
{\mathsf{B}}={\mathsf{B}}_{0}
(for
i=1
) is a desired standard basis.

Finally, the basis
{\mathsf{B}}
is a full-standard basis, since every heredity ideal
\bar{I}_{i}
has the form
\bar{I}_{i}={\mathcal{A}}_{i}e_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
with
\Delta({\lambda}^{(i)})\cong{\mathcal{A}}_{i}e_{i}
,
\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda}^{(i)})\cong e_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
(see Corollary
4.3.6
), and

e_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{i}e_{i}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
for some idempotents
e_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, where
i=1,2,\ldots,n
.
∎

We now use Theorem
4.3.8
to get a more general version of the proposition.

Corollary 5.1.6
.

For a poset ideal
\Theta
of
(\Omega,\leq_{L})
,
the statements
(a)
and
(b)
in Proposition
5.1.5
are true if
A^{+\natural}
and
A^{+\natural\prime}
are replaced by the quasi-hereditary algebras
A^{\ddagger\natural}_{\Theta}
and
A^{\ddagger\natural\prime}_{\Theta}
, respectively.

The following lemma follows from the proof of the above proposition. The base ring
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
can be replaced by any commutative Noetherian ring with the property that any finite projective module is free.

Lemma 5.1.7
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is a standardly based algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
(or

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
), and if
e\in\mathcal{A}
is an idempotent, then
e\mathcal{A}e
is standardly based.

Proof.

Suppose
{\mathsf{B}}=\bigcup_{{\lambda}\in{\Lambda}}{\mathsf{B}}^{\lambda}
is a standard basis for
{\mathcal{A}}
and assume
{\Lambda}=\{{\lambda}^{(1)},{\lambda}^{(2)},\ldots,{\lambda}^{(m)}\}
such that
i<j
whenever
{\lambda}^{(i)}>{\lambda}^{(j)}
. Then, the two-sided ideals
J_{i}=\text{span}({\mathsf{B}}^{{\lambda}^{(1)}}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathsf{B}}^{{\lambda}^{(i)}})
of
{\mathcal{A}}
gives a sequence in
{\mathcal{A}}

0=J_{0}\subseteq J_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq J_{m}={\mathcal{A}}.

This, in turn, gives a sequence of two-sided ideals in
e{\mathcal{A}}e

0=eJ_{0}e\subseteq eJ_{1}e\subseteq\cdots\subseteq eJ_{m}e=e{\mathcal{A}}e.

Since all standard modules
\Delta({\lambda})
and
\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda})
of
{\mathcal{A}}
are
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-free, it follows that
e\Delta({\lambda})
and
\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda})e
are projective
