{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-modules. Hence, they are
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-free by Swan’s result (if non-zero). Since
J_{i}/J_{i-1}\cong\Delta({\lambda}^{(i)})\otimes\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda}^{(i)})
as
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
-
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
-bimodules, where
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}={\mathcal{A}}/J_{i-1}
, it follows that

eJ_{i}e/eJ_{i-1}e\cong e\Delta({\lambda}^{(i)})\otimes\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda}^{(i)})e
for all
i=1,\ldots,m
.
By induction, we may now construct a standard basis for
e{\mathcal{A}}e
using the bases for
e\Delta({\lambda})
and
\Delta^{\text{\rm o}}({\lambda})e
.
∎

As a corollary, we obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 5.1.8
.

(a) Assume that we are not in the case of
{}^{2}F_{4}
. The
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebras
A^{\natural}
,
A^{+\natural}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
are all standardly based algebras.

(b) The analogous result holds in type
{}^{2}F_{4}
for the corresponding
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural\prime}
–algebras. (See the appendix to Section 4.1.)

This result for the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra
A^{\natural}
(and for
A^{+\natural}
, of course) is new.
Geck
[
G07
]
obtained a stronger result in the case of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\natural}
, namely, that it has a cellular basis in the sense of

[
GL96
]
. As mentioned at the beginning of this section, cellular bases require the existence of an anti-involution which interacts with the algebra’s basis and its bilinear forms.

By analyzing the structure of endomorphism algebras of tilting objects, G. Bellamy and U. Thiel proved that Hecke algebras associated to finite complex reflection groups are standardly based algebras over a field
K
. For real reflection groups, these bases are even cellular; see
[
BT22
, Th. 3.8, Ex. 3.25]
.

5.2.
Decomposition matrices, I

This and the next section present some applications to decomposition numbers for representations of finite
groups of Lie type (generally in cross-characteristic) and associated algebras, such as the generic Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
from Section 1.2 and the endomorphism algebras

A
and
A^{+}
of display (
2.4.4
).

5.2A Decomposition matrices associated with
A
,
A^{+}
, and
{\mathcal{H}}
.
In this subsection, we begin our study of decomposition numbers of the endomorphism algebras
A
and
A^{+}
, defined in display (
2.4.4
), relative to a local triple
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
. These algebras, as well as an underlying (generic) Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
, are built from a standard finite Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
arising. by the definition in Section 1.1, from a finite group
G
of Lie type. The “generic” aspect of this subsection comes from our naming of
{\mathcal{H}}
in Section 1.2 as a generic Hecke algebra.

We do not directly use
G
in this subsection, although its underlying Lie theory context is in the background, especially the defining characteristic
p
of the ambient algebraic group.

In particular, finite groups of Lie type do appear informally throughout this section, so we digress slightly to introduce some further notation regarding them. Our assumption (in Section 1.2) that the Coxeter diagram of
W
is connected implies we may take
{\mathbb{G}}
simple in the sense of algebraic groups
[
C85
, p, 17, top]
. (Alternatively, we can take
{\mathbb{G}}
to be connected reductive, provided its semisimple part is simple. See
[
C85
,
§1.9]
. Unless otherwise noted, we take
{\mathbb{G}}
to be simple.)
The group
G
may then be viewed as part of a Lie-type series associated to
{\mathbb{G}}
and a permutation
\rho
(a graph automorphism) of its Dynkin diagram.
There is also associated to
G
a power
q
of
\sqrt{p}
described explicitly on
[
C85
, pp. 39–42]
.
4
4

4

In the split Chevalley group case, the group
G
itself may be written
\mathbb{G}(q)
. Within each series, the allowable parameters
q
determine a unique group
G
.

As previously remarked, the group
G
plays a relatively minor role in the present subsection, but we will return to it in the next section. We do take this opportunity to remark that, if two groups
G
are in the same series, then they have the same standard finite Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
, as shown by the construction
[
C85
, pp. 34–36]
. We also note that the prime
p
will remain fixed throughout this section (as the defining characteristic of
{\mathbb{G}}
), and the defining parameter
q
of
G
is a positive integer power of
\sqrt{p}
. In fact, if the Ree and Suzuki groups are excluded,
q
is a positive integer power
of
p
.

We now return to the main task of studying decomposition numbers of
A
,
A^{+}
, and
{\mathcal{H}}
. The reader may recall the definition of a 2-regular local triple

({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime},K^{\prime},k)
from Section 5.0. We call such a triple
well-adapted
provided the following three conditions hold:

(1)
{\mathcal{Z}}
is a subring of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
;

(2) The field
k
has positive characteristic
r
distinct from
p
; and

(3)
K^{\prime}
is a splitting field for
{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}
.

Note that (1) implies that
\mathbb{Q}(t)
is contained in
K^{\prime}
, and so
{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}
is always semisimple, even split, by (3). The split condition holds automatically except in the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case.
See footnote
7
in Chapter 1 for the semisimplicity of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
.

Also,
the split
semisimplicity of
{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}
implies that of
A_{K^{\prime}}
and
A^{+}_{K^{\prime}}
, as endomorphism algebras of modules for a split semisimple algebra.

An example of a well-adapted 2-regular local triple is the Laurent triple associated with a DVR triple
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
,
where char
(K)=0
and char
(k)=r
different from the defining characteristic of
G
. Also assume
\sqrt{2}\in K
if
G
is of type
{}^{2}F_{4}
. See Section C3 in Appendix C.

Finally, we mention that, in the context of (1) above, condition (3) is always true when
G
is quasi-split or has rank 1.
This covers all
G
except those of type
{}^{2}F_{4}
, in which case the field
K^{\prime}
containing

\mathbb{Q}(t)
is a splitting field if and only if
\sqrt{2}\in K^{\prime}
. It is also the case that
\sqrt{2}\in K^{\prime}
if and only if
\sqrt{2}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
. This can be proved using the fact that
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
is a regular local ring, hence a UFD (Auslander-Buchsbaum), hence integrally closed
[
Jac89
, Ex. 1, p. 414]
.
In turn,
\sqrt{2}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
if and only if

(1
′
)

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
, where
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}[t,t^{-1}]
with
\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{2}].

(This condition is analogous to condition (1), and we call it condition (1
′
).)
Consequently, a 2-regular local triple
({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime},K^{\prime},k)
is well-adapted when
G
is not of type
{}^{2}F_{4}
, provided conditions (1) and (2) hold and provided (1
′
) and (2) hold when
G
is of type
{}^{2}F_{4}
.

The reader may notice that
p=2
in the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case, so that requiring
\sqrt{2}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
is the same as
requiring
\sqrt{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
. Later (such as in Lemma
5.2.7
below), we will need to consider similar requirements for other types, and even
\sqrt[4]{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
in some cases, but this will not be necessary in Theorem
5.2.2
below.

In the subsection “Laurent triples” of Appendix C, we show how to construct many 2-regular local triples
({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime},K^{\prime},k)
,
starting with a DVR triple
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
and using for
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
a localization of
{\mathcal{O}}[t,t^{-1}]
. If we take
k
to have positive characteristic
r\not=p
, as in (2) above, and
K
to have characteristic 0, then conditions (1) and (2) will be satisfied. Also, (1
′
) will be satisfied if
\sqrt{2}\in K
.

Remark 5.2.1
.

Assume that the prime
r
is good for the group
G
. Then
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
is contained in

{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
, while in type
{}^{2}F_{4}
, the ring
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime\natural}
is a subring of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
. Thus, the algebra

A^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
is a split quasi-hereditary algebra, as follows from the main result of the previous chapter, Theorem
4.3.4
, together with Remark
4.3.9
.

Theorem 5.2.2
.

Let
({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime},K^{\prime},k)
be a well-adapted 2-regular local triple with

r
(
=
the characteristic of
k
) good for

G
.

Let
D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
be the decomposition matrix of
A_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
, and let
D^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
be the decomposition matrix of
A^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
. Then, the following hold.

(a)

With respect to an ordering of the (non-isomorphic) irreducible modules for
A^{+}_{K^{\prime}}
and
A^{+}_{k}
, the decomposition
matrix
D^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
of
A^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
is unitriangular and square.

(b)

Let
D^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
be arranged as in (a). Then, a version of
D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
can be obtained by removing some (possibly none) of the rows of
D^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
. Also, this puts
D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
in row echelon form with no row of zeros.

(c)

In particular, the indices of the columns of
D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
in (b) above can be permuted so that
D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
has the form
[D_{1},D_{2}]
, where
D_{1}
is a square unitriangular matrix and
D_{2}
is a rectangular matrix with the same number of rows as
D_{1}
. In particular, this version of
D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
is itself unitriangular.

(d)

D^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
and
D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
have the same number of columns. Also,
A^{+}_{K^{\prime}}
and
A_{K^{\prime}}
have the same number
of non-isomorphic irreducible representations as
{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}
.

(e)

Parts (b) and (c) hold if
D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
is replaced by the decomposition matrix
D^{\circ}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
. Hence, there exist submatrices
D^{\prime}
and
D^{\circ\prime}
of
D^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
,
D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
, respectively, such that

D^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}=\begin{pmatrix}D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}\\
D^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}=\begin{pmatrix}D^{\circ}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}\\
D^{\circ\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

We will give arguments for the split, quasi-split, and some
{}^{2}F_{4}
cases, leaving remaining
{}^{2}F_{4}
arguments, and the rank 1 Ree and
Suzuki groups to the reader.

We first prove (a),
5
5

5

For a proof using standard basis theory, see Remark
5.2.3
(c) below.
noting that the
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebra
A^{+\natural}
in Corollary
4.3.7
gives rise to highest weight category
(\mathscr{C},\{\Delta(E)\}_{E\in\Lambda})
in the sense of Rouquier
[
Ro08
, Def. 4.11]
. Here,
\mathscr{C}=A^{+\natural}\!\!-\!\!\text{mod}
, the poset
\Lambda
is discussed in the paragraph surrounding (
4.3.11
), and the
\Delta(E)
’s (and
\Delta^{\prime}(E)
’s) are discussed in
Remark
4.2.3
. (The
\Delta^{\prime}(E)
’s are variations on the
\Delta(E)
’s. They are used in the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case.)

The
\Delta(E)
’s above, in Remark
4.2.3
, agree with those in Corollary
4.3.7
, as may be shown by tracing (backwards) through the discussion above Corollary
4.3.5
. (Here the discussion of the
{}^{2}F_{4}
case is left to the reader.) A big advantage is that all
\Delta(E)
’s in Remark
4.2.3
are shown to have
\Delta(E)_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
irreducible. Applying this, and then base changing, we may conclude that
\Delta(E)_{K^{\prime}}
is irreducible, in our
({\mathscr{Q}}^{\prime},K^{\prime},k)
context, over
A^{+}_{K^{\prime}}=(A^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}})_{K^{\prime}}
.

We begin with the highest weight category

\mathscr{C}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}:=A^{+\natural}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}\!-
mod by base changing
\mathscr{C}
above from
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
to
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
.

Using the notation in Corollary
4.3.7
, together with base-change properties such as
[
DPS98a
, Lem. 1.2.5(4)]
and
[
Ro08
, Prop. 4.14]
,
6
6

6

Compare also Remark
4.3.9
.
it is useful to define
V(\lambda)=\Delta(\lambda)_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
in
\mathscr{C}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
, modifying
E
to
\lambda\in\Lambda
by abuse of notation. Here
\Lambda=\text{Irr}(\mathbb{Q}W)
(or
\Lambda=\text{Irr}(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})W)
if
G
is of type
{}^{2}F_{4}
), and
\Delta(\lambda)=(S^{\natural}_{\lambda})^{\diamond}
with
S^{\natural}_{\lambda}
as defined in display (
4.2.3
).
Thus, passing from
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
to
k
,
A^{+}_{k}
–mod is a highest weight category in the original sense of
[
CPS88
]
for the poset
(\Lambda,\preceq)
, where
\preceq
is defined in (
4.3.11
).
In particular,
\Lambda
is in bijection with the distinct irreducible modules

\{L(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
of
A^{+}_{k}
–mod, where
L(\lambda)
is the head of
the corresponding standard modules
V(\lambda)_{k}
.

Next, we note that each
V(\lambda)_{K^{\prime}}
is irreducible and that all irreducible objects in
A^{+}_{K^{\prime}}
–mod have the form
V(\lambda)_{K^{\prime}}
. (This follows from Remark
4.2.3
.) Hence, all the decomposition numbers needed in (a) have the form
[V(\lambda)_{k}:L(\mu)]
, for any
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
. This gives a
|\Lambda|\times|\Lambda|
square(!) matrix as well as the unitriangular property, since, by
[
DS94
, Def. 2.1(i)]
, we have

[V(\lambda)_{k}:L(\mu)]=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if }\lambda=\mu;\\
0,&\text{ unless }\mu\prec\lambda.\end{cases}

This completes the proof of (a). As noted, Remark
5.2.3
(c) gives an alternate proof.

To prove Part (b) (and Part (d)),
7
7

7

This proof is inspired in part by
arguments for
[
DS00a
, Th. 6.3.2]
, but we include further details here.
we first observe from the notation in Construction
2.4.12
that
A^{+}\cong{\small\begin{pmatrix}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T,T)&\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(X,T)\\
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(T,X)&\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(X,X)\end{pmatrix}}
.
This isomorphism induces isomorphisms
eA^{+}e\cong\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\cong A
, where

e\in A^{+}
is the idempotent corresponding to
\begin{pmatrix}1_{T}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
. Clearly, by
noting that
T^{+}={T\choose X}
,

e
defines the natural projection
T^{+}\to T\subseteq T^{+}
. Thus, by combining the isomorphisms above, we may now identify
A
with
eA^{+}e
and
A_{\mathscr{K}}
with
eA^{+}_{\mathscr{K}}e
for any commutative ring
{\mathscr{K}}
.
In the rest of the proof, we will need only the cases
{\mathscr{K}}=k
and
{\mathscr{K}}=K^{\prime}
. These are fields, of course, allowing us to quote from

[
Gr80
, Section 6.2]
for algebras
S
over them such as
S=A^{+}_{k}
and idempotents
e\in S
, taking, for instance,

eSe=eA^{+}_{k}e=A_{k}
,

If
L
is an irreducible
A^{+}_{k}
-module, then
eL
is either 0 or an irreducible
A_{k}
-module (observing that
A_{k}=eA^{+}_{k}e
). Further, the irreducible
A_{k}
-modules all arise this way. Also, if
L,L^{\prime}
are irreducible
A^{+}_{k}
-modules such that
eL\not=0\not=eL^{\prime}
, then
eL\cong eL^{\prime}
if and only if
L\cong L^{\prime}
. For these assertions, see
[
Gr80
, Th. (6.2g)]
; their analogs also hold with
K^{\prime}
replacing
k
. In fact, the latter case can be used to prove Part (d).
8
8

8

See Remark
4.2.3
for an alternate proof of Part (d).
(Since the module
{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}=x_{\emptyset}{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}
, with
x_{\emptyset}
defined in display (
1.2.4
), is a direct summand
of both
T^{+}_{K^{\prime}}
and
T_{K^{\prime}}
, we have, in this semisimple case,
eV(\lambda)_{K^{\prime}}=e\Delta(\lambda)_{K^{\prime}}\neq 0
, for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
since
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{K}}((S_{\lambda}^{\natural})_{K},{\mathcal{H}}_{K})\neq 0
. Thus,
\{eV(\lambda)_{K^{\prime}}\mid\lambda\in\Lambda\}
forms a complete set of irreducible
A_{K^{\prime}}
-modules.
Note that
\Lambda
also indexes irreducible modules of
{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}
; see (
4.1.8
). Hence, Part (d) follows.)

Continuing now the proof of Part (b), note that if
L=V_{i}/V_{i-1}
is a composition factor of
V(\lambda)_{k}
, where
V_{i-1}\subseteq V_{i}
are submodules of
V(\lambda)_{k}
, and
eL\neq 0
, then
eL
is irreducible and
[
Gr80
, (6.2a)]
now implies that
eL\cong eV_{i}/eV_{i-1}
is a composition factor of
eV(\lambda)_{k}
. Also, every composition factor of
eV(\lambda)_{k}
has such a form. This shows that a composition series
0=V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq V_{m}=V(\lambda)_{k}
of the left
A^{+}_{k}
-module
V(\lambda)_{k}
gives rise to a composition series

0=eV_{0}\subseteq eV_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq eV_{m}=eV(\lambda)_{k}
of the
A_{k}
-module
eV({\lambda})_{k}
and, for every
L
with
eL\neq 0
,
[V(\lambda)_{k}:L]=[eV(\lambda)_{k}:eL]
.
Hence, Part (b) follows except for its last assertion and that follows from Part (c).

We leave the proofs of (c) and (e) to the reader. This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remarks 5.2.3
.

(a) These results appear to be new, certainly for
A
and
A^{+}
, but, because of our
“generic” focus, also new for
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Notice that
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
is a local version of the
generic
Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
, not one of its standard specializations (see Subsection 5.2B below), which typically require a replacement of the variable
t
or
t^{2}
with a power of
\sqrt{p}
. For a variation of the theorem
for such a standard specialization of
{\mathcal{H}}
, see

[
GJ11
, Th. 4.4.1]
.

(b) Another variation of the theorem is obtained in
[
DS00a
, (6.3.3)]
in Lie type
B
, where a different version of
A^{+}
is used, not involving Kazhdan-Lusztig cell modules.

(c)
Second proof of Theorem
5.2.2
(a).
The fact that
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
-algebra
A^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
is quasi-hereditary of split type (see Remark
5.2.1
) implies that
A^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
is a standardly full-based algebra associated with a poset
\Lambda
(see Proposition
5.1.5
). Thus, every bilinear form associated with a cell module for
A^{+}_{K^{\prime}}
or
A^{+}_{k}
is nonzero. Hence, we have
\Lambda_{1,K^{\prime}}=\Lambda=\Lambda_{1,k}
for both
A^{+}_{K^{\prime}}
and
A^{+}_{k}
(see Lemma
5.1.3
(b) and Corollary
5.1.4
). This proves that the decomposition matrix
D^{+}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
is square.
The unitriangular property follows from Lemma
5.1.3
(c)&(d).

5.2B. From the generic case to standard specializations.
With the exception of the more general Lemma
5.2.4
below, we continue with the notations

{\mathbb{G}}
and
G
of the previous subsection. In particular,
{\mathbb{G}}
is a simple algebraic group. We will fix
{\mathbb{G}}
and the Lie-type series to which
G
belongs. In the setup
of
[
C85
, p. 31]
, this means there is a ‘‘Frobenius endomorphism”
9
9

9

Also called a “Frobenoius map” in Section 1.1. Thus, some power of
F
is a standard Frobenius endomorphism.

F
of
{\mathbb{G}}
with
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
and with
F
inducing a fixed
permutation
\rho
of the Dynkin diagram of
{\mathbb{G}}

[
C85
, pp.31–41]
. Many of these—and other—details may
be found in Section 1.1 of this monograph—and others.

We retain the notations
F,G,\mathbb{B},B
introduced in Section 1.1, which may also be used in a more general context such as a connected reductive group equipped with a Frobenius endomorphism.

It is useful to consider connected reductive groups
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}
which are central products
10
10

10

See
[
Go80
, p.29]
for the definition of a central product.

\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{G}
of a torus
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\mathbb{G}
. We assume, in the structures we seek, that
F
extends to a Frobenius endomorphism
\widetilde{F}
of
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}
and write

\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}^{\widetilde{F}}
. We also set
\mathbb{\widetilde{B}}=\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{B}}
,

\widetilde{B}=\widetilde{{\mathbb{B}}}^{\widetilde{F}}
, and
\widetilde{Z}=\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}^{\widetilde{F}}
. Note that
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
is the connected component of the identity in the center of
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}
, and, thus, is stable under
\widetilde{F}
.
The lemma immediately below constructs functorially such a pair
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}},\widetilde{F}
in which
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
is the full center of

\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}
. (Compare
[
Lus88
, pp.161–163]
and
[
DF94
, §§1,2]
.)

Lemma 5.2.4
.

If
{\mathbb{G}}
is semisimple, then there exists a pair
\widetilde{{\mathbb{G}}},\widetilde{F}
in the setup of the above paragraph, in which
\widetilde{{\mathbb{G}}}=\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{G}}
is connected reductive and
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
is the full center of
\widetilde{{\mathbb{G}}}
.
(Equivalently, the center of
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}
is connected.) Also,
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
acts trivially on the coset space
\widetilde{{\mathbb{G}}}/\widetilde{{\mathbb{B}}}
. The
G
-sets
(\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}/\widetilde{{\mathbb{B}}})^{\widetilde{F}}
,
\widetilde{G}/\widetilde{B}
,
({\mathbb{G}}/{\mathbb{B}})^{F}
,
and
G/B
are all naturally isomorphic.

Proof.

Observe that
Z({\mathbb{G}})
is finite and contained in all maximal tori
{\mathbb{T}}
of
{\mathbb{G}}
.
Take
{\mathbb{T}}
to be
F
-stable. (See
[
C85
, p. 33]
.)
Let
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
be a copy of
{\mathbb{T}}
independent of
{\mathbb{G}}
. Let
Z({\mathbb{G}})_{1}\subseteq{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
correspond to
Z({\mathbb{G}})
. Thus, the natural action of
F
on
{\mathbb{T}}
gives rise to a corresponding action on
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
and on
Z({\mathbb{G}})_{1}\subseteq{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
. The natural maps
Z({\mathbb{G}})\cong Z({\mathbb{G}})_{1}\subseteq{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
and
Z({\mathbb{G}})\subseteq{\mathbb{G}}
give rise to a connected, reductive pushout group:
\widetilde{{\mathbb{G}}}={\mathbb{T}}_{1}{\mathbb{G}}
with (connected) center
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
, and we now write
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}={\mathbb{T}}_{1}
.
Also,

F
extends to a Frobenius endomorphism on
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}
, duplicating on
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
its action on
{\mathbb{T}}
.

Since the coset space
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}/\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}
identifies here, as a
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}
-set, with the set of
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}
-conjugates of
\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}
, the action of
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}:=Z(\widetilde{\mathbb{G}})
is trivial. The
G
-set identifications
at the end of the lemma follow from elementary arguments and the Lang-Steinberg theorem, as illustrated in
[
C85
, p. 33]
. All the identifications are the “obvious” candidates. This completes the proof. ∎

Remark 5.2.5
.

The lemma is also true if “semisimple” is replaced by “connected reductive.” We sketch a proof in this case. First, change notation so that the group in question is
{\mathbb{G}}{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
, where
{\mathbb{G}}
is semisimple and
{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
is a (central) torus. Apply the proof of the lemma for the group
{\mathbb{G}}
to show
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}{\mathbb{G}}
has connected center
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
. Then construct
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}{\mathbb{G}}{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
and show it has center
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
. The remaining details of the proof are left to the reader. (Later, we only use the lemma as stated, and only when
{\mathbb{G}}
is simple.)

In the rest of subsection 5.2B
,
let
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
be a DVR triple (in some cases, a more general local triple could be used) in which

\bullet

the field
k
has positive characteristic
r
, distinct from the defining characteristic
p>0
of
\mathbb{G}
;

\bullet

K
has characteristic 0; and

\bullet

\sqrt{2}\in{\mathcal{O}}
if
G
is of type
{}^{2}F_{4}
.

These choices are natural when concerned with the representation theory of
kG
(i.e., the
cross-characteristic theory)—see specifically
[
DPS98a
, (1), p. 170]
.

We continue the assumption of this subsection
that
G
is the group
{\mathbb{G}}^{F}
in a fixed Lie-type series with
{\mathbb{G}}
simple (as discussed above). Associated with
G
is a parameter
q
(which determines
G
in its series) and a parameter
\delta
which equals
1
,
2
, or
3
, depending on the series, with
q^{\delta}
a power of
p
(an odd power, if the series is Ree or Suzuki).

The following lemma, included for completeness, provides largely well-known “standard specialization” properties of the generic Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Recall the
\mathbb{Z}[t^{2}]
-form
{\mathcal{H}}^{o}
of
{\mathcal{H}}
considered in Lemma
1.2.2
.

Lemma 5.2.6
.

Let
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
be a DVR triple as detailed above. Suppose
\sqrt{q}\in{\mathcal{O}}
is invertible in
{\mathcal{O}}
; this gives
{\mathcal{O}}
a natural structure as a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra (via
t\mapsto\sqrt{q}
). Then, the base-changed algebras
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}
and

{\mathcal{H}}^{o}_{\mathcal{O}}
are naturally isomorphic, and
the base-changed algebras

{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
and
A_{K}
are split semisimple.

Proof.

The isomorphism
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}\cong{\mathcal{H}}^{o}_{\mathcal{O}}
follows from the hypothesis.
It remains to verify the final assertion of the lemma. Theorem
4.1.3
shows that
{\mathcal{J}}^{\natural}
is a direct product of matrix algebras over
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. Base change from
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
to
K
gives that
{\mathcal{J}}_{K}
is a similar direct product of full matrix algebras. Taking
R=K
in the discussion below display (
4.1.4
), we have
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}\cong{\mathcal{J}}_{K}
. (Note that
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}\cong{\mathcal{H}}^{o}_{K}
is semisimple, as the endomorphism ring of a
KG
-module.) The isomorphism with a direct product of matrix algebras shows that
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
is split semisimple.

Finally,
A_{K}
is the endomorphism algebra of an
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
-module by
[
DPS98a
, Th. 2.4.4(b)]
. Since
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
is split semisimple, it follows that
A_{K}
is also split semisimple.
∎

Recall the definition of a Laurent triple associated with a DVR triple in Appendix C. (See the paragraph above Example
C.9
in Appendix C.)

Lemma 5.2.7
.

Let
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
be a DVR triple as detailed above. Also, assume
\sqrt{q}\in{\mathcal{O}}
.
11
11

11

We note that the latter assumption is implied by the condition that
\sqrt[4]{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}
. If the series of
G
is not Ree or Suzuki, this may be weakened to the condition that
\sqrt{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}
.

Let
({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime},K(t),k)
be the Laurent triple associated to the DVR triple
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
and the
element
a=\sqrt{q}
.

(a) The decomposition matrix
D_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
of
A_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
(with respect to the triple
({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime},K(t),k)
)
agrees with the decomposition matrix
D_{{\mathcal{O}}}
of
A_{\mathcal{O}}
with respect to
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
.

(b) Similarly, the decomposition matrix of
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
agrees with that of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Proof.

The reader may check that the diagram in display (
C.3
) in Appendix C is interpreted as follows for
B=A
or
{\mathcal{H}}
and with the indicated prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}

(5.2.1)

\begin{CD}B@>{}>{}>B_{\mathfrak{p}}@>{}>{}>{B_{Q}}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
B/\mathfrak{p}B@>{}>{}>B(\mathfrak{p})\\
@V{}V{}V\\
B_{F}\end{CD}

where in case (a),
B=A_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
and
\mathfrak{p}=(t-a)\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
, while
B(\mathfrak{p})
identifies with

B_{K}=B_{(O^{\prime}/\mathfrak{p})_{\mathfrak{p}}}
. Now apply Theorem
C.4
and Lemma
5.2.6
.

A similar argument
applies in the Hecke algebra case (b), where

. This proves the lemma.∎

The notation
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
below continues as above. Recall the generic Hecke endo-algebra
A
defined in (
1.2.5
).

Theorem 5.2.8
.

Suppose

({\mathcal{O}},K,k)

\sqrt{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}.

12
