5.3.
Decomposition matrices, II

We have seen from Theorem
5.2.8
that the decomposition matrix
D
is an invariant relative to the standard specializations arising from finite groups
G
of Lie type in the same Lie-type series. We now prove that, for such a
G
and DVR triple
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
, this matrix
D
is part of the decomposition matrix of
{\mathcal{O}}G
. This shows that
D
is, in fact, an invariant associated with the Lie type series.

We continue with the notation in the previous section. The unipotent representations in this section are the irreducible constituents of the permutation module
\mathbb{C}G/B
,
where
B={\mathbb{B}}^{F}
for an
F
-stable Borel subgroup
{\mathbb{B}}
of
{\mathbb{G}}
.

In the following theorem, as in Theorem
5.2.8
,
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
is a DVR triple with
{\mathcal{O}}
complete,
k
is algebraically closed of characteristic
r\not=p
, and
K
has characteristic 0
(see Appendix A, Main Theorem). Also, by Hensel’s lemma,
{\mathcal{O}}
contains a primitive
p^{th}
-root of unity. Here
p

is the defining characteristic of
{\mathbb{G}}
. We also require, as in Theorem
5.2.8
, that
\sqrt{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}
and

\sqrt[4]{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}
for
G
of Ree or Suzuki type; thus, we always have
\sqrt{q}\in{\mathcal{O}}
.

The proof of part (a) involves methods invented by Dipper many years ago
(although we could not find an explicit statement in
[
DG99
]
). Part (b) seems to be a remarkable fact, not considered before in the literature. It is based on our study (in Appendix C) of Krull dimension 2 decomposition numbers.
Part (c) depends on our main theorem in Subsection 5.2B, and provides evidence for the conjecture
[
GJ11
, Conj. 4.5.2]
, which references work of Geck
[
G90
,
G06
]
and Geck–Hiss
[
GH97
, Conj. 3.4]
. Geck’s conjecture
[
G12
, Conj. 2.1]
, originating from his 1990 Ph.D. thesis
[
G90
]
, on unitriangular shape of decomposition matrices of unipotent blocks has been proved recently in
[
BDT20
]
, under the assumption that the defining characteristic for
{\mathbb{G}}
is good and the non-defining characteristic
r
is
very good
.
13
13

13

Call
r
a very good prime if
r
is good and
r
does not divide the order of
Z({\mathbb{G}})_{F}
, the largest (finite) quotient of
Z({\mathbb{G}})
on which
F
acts trivially. See
[
BDT20
, Th. A]
.
The following result suggests that, for certain unipotent blocks, these conditions could be weakened by simply assuming that
r
is good. (Compare bottom line of
[
BDT20
, p.587]
.)

Theorem 5.3.1
.

Let
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
be as above.
(Thus,
{\mathbb{G}}
is simple,
14
14

14

We continue to make this assumption for simplicity; see footnote
2
in Chapter 1. In fact, much of the argument applies if
{\mathbb{G}}
is just semisimple, or even connected reductive.
and we think of
G
as a finite group in a given Lie-type series.)

(a) Let the matrix
D
be as described above in Theorem
5.2.8
for the generic Hecke endo-algebra
A
.
Then,
D
is a submatrix of the decomposition matrix of
{\mathcal{O}}G
. The columns of
D
are indexed by the irreducible
constituents of the permutation module
\mathbb{C}G/B
.

(b) The same statements hold, with the same
matrix
D
, if
G
is replaced by any group in the same finite Lie-type series.

(c) If
r
is a good prime for
{\mathbb{G}}
, then
D
above is unitriangular.

Proof.

First, assuming that (a) holds, (b) follows from Theorem
5.2.8
and (c) follows from Theorem
5.2.2
(c) and
Theorem
5.2.8
.

It remains to prove (a). This assertion contains two parts, the second of which, counting columns, follows from the argument for Theorem
5.2.2
(d). It remains to prove the first part of (a), which asserts an inclusion of decomposition matrices. We divide the proof into two parts.

We give the proof when
{\mathbb{G}}
is simple of adjoint type in Part 1. In this case, we may directly apply Theorem
A.1
in Appendix A. We also supply the main ingredient in the argument for the general case.
In Part 2, we prove a variation on Theorem
A.1
for
{\mathbb{G}}
simple, reducing the general case to the validity of Theorem
A.1
for the group
\widetilde{{\mathbb{G}}}
in Lemma

5.2.4
(which has connected center).

Part 1. The adjoint-type case

Let
{\mathbb{G}}
be a simple algebraic group of adjoint type. Then, by the proof of
[
MT11
, Prop. 9.15]
,
{\mathbb{G}}
has a trivial center
Z({\mathbb{G}})=1
; see also the proof of
[
C85
, Prop. 8.1.2]
on page 254 for this fact. Thus, Theorem
A.1
applies in the current (adjoint) case.

Let

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}}:=\underset{J\subseteq S}{\bigoplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}),

using, on the right-hand side, the notation of the proof of Theorem
A.1
. Then, Theorem
A.1
holds for
{\mathcal{M}}_{K}
. This provides a projective cover of
{\mathcal{M}}
satisfying

[
Dip91
, 2.3]
. Equivalently, in the context of
[
Dip90
, §4]
, the cover satisfies
[
Dip90
, 2.6]
; see
[
Dip90
, Th. 4.7]
. Thus, by
[
Dip90
, Cor. 4.10]
, the decomposition matrix of
\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{O}}G}({\mathcal{M}})
is a submatrix of the decomposition matrix of
{\mathcal{O}}G
. (See the formulation of
[
GJ11
, Th. 4.1.14]
which uses a slightly different hypothesis on (
{\mathcal{O}},K,k
) but also references the same result
[
Dip90
, Cor. 4.10]
.)
Now, assertion (a) follows from the algebra isomorphism
\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{O}}G}({\mathcal{M}})\cong A_{\mathcal{O}}
, which is proved below.

For an
F
-stable maximal torus
{\mathbb{T}}\subseteq{\mathbb{B}}
, let
T={\mathbb{T}}^{F}
. Let
M_{\emptyset}:=R_{T}^{G}{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{O}}G/B
, where
{\mathcal{O}}
is the trivial
T
-module.
By remarks before the statement of the theorem,
{\mathcal{O}}
contains a primitive
p^{th}
-root of unity. Thus, the
underlying regular character
\sigma
of
\Gamma_{\sigma}
is defined over
{\mathcal{O}}
. (The reader may wish to review properties of Gelfand-Graev characters and their underlying modules in
[
C85
, §8.1]
or the helpful summary in
[
G17
, §4.3]
.)
As an
{\mathcal{O}}G
-module,
\Gamma_{\sigma}
is induced to
G
from
\sigma
on
U
, and the latter is not trivial on any fundamental root subgroup of
U
, nor are any of the conjugates of
\sigma
by elements of
T
.
We mention that
[
C85
, §2.1–§2.6]
gives an excellent treatment of root subgroups and root systems in the broad context of algebraic groups with split BN-pairs, suitable for our use here.
So, if
w\in W
is not the longest word, neither is its inverse, and there are no nontrivial
B
-homomorphisms from
\text{Ind}_{U}^{B}{\mathcal{O}}_{\sigma}
to
\text{Ind}_{{}^{w}B\cap B}^{B}{\mathcal{O}}
. (The point is that
{}^{w}B\cap B
must contain a fundamental root group
X
, on which
\sigma
and its conjugates through elements of
T
are all nontrivial.)
Mackey decomposition arguments now show that
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{O}}G}(\Gamma_{\sigma},M_{\emptyset})\cong{\mathcal{O}}
, generated by a map
\beta_{\emptyset}:\Gamma_{\sigma}\longrightarrow M_{\emptyset}
with image
{\mathcal{O}}
-pure.
By
[
G17
, 4.1]
,
\text{dim}_{K}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(K\Gamma_{\sigma},\text{St}_{K})=1
. As is well known,
\text{St}_{K}
is a submodule of
KM_{\emptyset}
. Thus, the image of
\beta_{\emptyset}
satisfies
K\text{Im}\beta_{\emptyset}=\text{St}_{K}\subseteq KM_{\emptyset}
.
So the kernel
\text{Ker}\beta_{\emptyset}
is just the intersection of
\Gamma_{\sigma}
with the sum of all irreducible
KG
-submodules of
K\Gamma_{\sigma}
not isomorphic to
\text{St}_{K}
. (Recall that
K\Gamma_{\sigma}
is multiplicity free by

[
C85
, Th. 8.1.3]
. This reference requires that
{\mathbb{G}}
be connected reductive with connected center, a condition that holds for
{\mathbb{G}}
with adjoint type and also for
\widetilde{{\mathbb{G}}}
in the more general case discussed in Part 2. Corresponding results for Levi subgroups also hold in each case. The multiplicity freeness may then be deduced for
\widetilde{G}
and its Levi subgroups by using
[
DF94
, Lem. 2.3(a),(c)]
.)
A similar kernel analysis applies to the surjective map
\Gamma_{\sigma}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}
discussed above (
A.2
). Thus, the latter map has
the same kernel as
\beta_{\emptyset}
. Consequently,

\text{Im}\beta_{\emptyset}\cong{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}.

The remaining discussion in Part 1 does not assume that
{\mathbb{G}}
is of adjoint type.

The Steinberg
KG
-submodule of
KM_{\emptyset}
can also be written as

y_{S}KM_{\emptyset}
, where

y_{S}=\sum_{w\in W}\text{{\rm sgn}}(w)t^{-2L(w)}{T}_{w}=\sum_{w\in W}\text{{\rm sgn}}(w)t^{-L(w)}\widetilde{T}_{w}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{K}.

This is just the module-theoretic realization
of the correspondence between irreducible
KG
-constituents of
KM_{\emptyset}
and irreducible characters of its

KG
-endomorphism algebra. The Steinberg character corresponds to the sign
character; see
[
G17
, Th. 2.1, Lem. 2.2]
, and Lemma
5.2.6
above. Note that
T_{s}x=-x
, for
x=y_{S}
, and,
consequently, for any
x\in y_{S}KM_{\emptyset}
. For the
x=y_{S}
case, see
[
DPS98c
, Lem. 1.1(b)]
.

There is thus a unique pure
{\mathcal{O}}G
-submodule of
M_{\emptyset}
which base changes over
K
to
\text{St}_{K}
,
namely,
M_{\emptyset}\cap y_{S}KM_{\emptyset}
. The latter module may also be written as
\sqrt{y_{S}M_{\emptyset}}
in the (now common)
terminology of
[
Dip90
, p. 251]
. We have proved
{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}\cong\sqrt{y_{S}M_{\emptyset}}
.

The argument for this isomorphism has not used the assumption that
{\mathbb{G}}
is simple of adjoint type.
There are similar characterizations of the modules
{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
introduced above Theorem
A.1
. We may write

{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}=\sqrt{y_{J}M^{L_{J}}_{\emptyset}},

where
y_{J}=\sum_{w\in W_{J}}(-1)^{\ell(w)}(t_{s}^{2})^{-\ell(w)}T_{w}
, and
\sqrt{y_{J}M_{\emptyset}}\cong R^{G}_{T}({\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})
, the
{\mathcal{O}}G
-submodule of
M_{\emptyset}
generated by
\sqrt{y_{J}M_{\emptyset}^{L_{J}}}={\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
.

Applying
[
Dip98
, Ths. 1.30&1.36]
with
R={\mathcal{O}}
, and
M_{{\mathcal{O}}}=M_{\emptyset}
in Theorem 1.30 there, we obtain
15
15

15

See also
[
Ca98
, Th. 1]
and
[
DG99
, Th. 1.10]
.

\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{O}}G}\left(\bigoplus_{J}\sqrt{y_{J}M_{\emptyset}}\right)\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}}\left(\bigoplus_{J}{y_{J}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}}\right).

However, there is a well-known identification

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}}(y_{J}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}},y_{I}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}})\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}}(x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}},x_{I}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{O}})

for each
I,J\subseteq S
. See
[
DPS98c
, Lem. 1.1(c)]
.
The left-hand side of this display sums to the right-hand side of the display above it. The lower display’s own righth-hand-side sums to the algebra
A_{\mathcal{O}}
, using the base-change result
[
DPS98a
, Th. 2.4.4]
. The left-hand side of the higher display has decomposition matrix a submatrix of that of
{\mathcal{O}}G
, as noted in our discussion of
\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{O}}G}(M)
in the opening paragraph of this proof. However, by Theorem
5.2.8
(or Lemma
5.2.7
), the decomposition matrix
D
of
A_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
agrees with that of
A_{\mathcal{O}}
.
The theorem is now completely proved for
{\mathbb{G}}
simple of adjoint type.

Part 2. The general case

Next, we consider the general case where
{\mathbb{G}}
is simple, but possibly not adjoint. (Much of the argument applies if
{\mathbb{G}}
is just semisimple, or even connected reductive.) Possibly,
{\mathbb{G}}
does not have connected center. In particular, Theorem
A.1
is not directly available, but does apply to the group
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}
as constructed in Lemma
5.2.4
. The theorem can at least be (re)stated for
G
(that is, for
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
), using a fixed regular character
\sigma:U\to K^{\times}
and drawing on the notation and discussion in Appendix A, which in turn draws on
[
G17
, §4]
. We next give the main details and then use the version of Theorem
A.1
in Appendix A to prove the modified version.

In particular, an
{\mathcal{O}}G
-lattice
\Gamma_{\sigma}
is defined as in
[
G17
, 4.1]
via an idempotent in
{\mathcal{O}}U\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}G
constructed from
\sigma
. (The homomorphism
\sigma^{-1}
applied to the idempotent is
1
.) Equivalently,
\Gamma_{\sigma}
is the
{\mathcal{O}}G
-module induced by the rank 1
{\mathcal{O}}U
-module determined by
\sigma^{-1}
(a module we call
{\mathcal{O}}_{\sigma}
by abuse of notation).
Briefly,
\Gamma_{\sigma}:=\text{Ind}_{U}^{G}{\mathcal{O}}_{\sigma}
. This definition makes sense for
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
(or for any finite group of Lie type).

Next, keeping the same level of generality, let
P_{J}=U_{J}L_{J}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
corresponding to
J\subseteq S
. A specific analog
\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
of
\Gamma_{\sigma}
is constructed “by restriction” in Appendix A. This makes sense here, since
U\cap L_{J}
is the analog for
L
of
U
, and the restriction of
\sigma
to
U\cap L_{J}
is regular. We write

\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}:=\text{Ind}^{L_{J}}_{U\cap L_{J}}\text{Res}^{U}_{U\cap L_{J}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\sigma}.

Continuing, note from
[
G17
, 4.1]
that the Steinberg module
\text{St}_{K}
appears in
K\Gamma_{\sigma}
with multiplicity

1
. It follows (see
[
G17
, Prop. 4.2]
, attributed to Hiss, and its proof) that there is a unique pure

{\mathcal{O}}G
-sublattice
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\sigma}
such that
K(\Gamma_{\sigma}/\Gamma^{\prime}_{\sigma})\cong\text{St}_{K}
. Both

[
G17
]
and Appendix A take this property to define
\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}:=\Gamma_{\sigma}/\Gamma^{\prime}_{\sigma}
, and we make the same definition here. As in Appendix A, we also make the parallel definition

\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}:=\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}/(\Gamma^{L_{J}}_{\sigma})^{\prime}.

This does not require the connected center hypothesis and makes sense in the general context
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
with
{\mathbb{G}}
simple (or even just connected reductive).

To summarize, we have now defined for
{\mathbb{G}}
all of the ingredients needed to define
\beta_{K}
for the formal statement of Theorem
A.1
.

We claim that Theorem
A.1
holds for
{\mathbb{G}}
with this notation.
We intend to prove this claim by using the fact that it holds for
\widetilde{{\mathbb{G}}}
with obvious changes in notation from
G,L_{J},\beta,\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}},\mathscr{S}^{L_{J}}_{\sigma},\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}
to
\widetilde{G},\widetilde{L}_{J},\widetilde{\beta},\Gamma_{\sigma}^{\widetilde{L}_{J}},\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{\widetilde{L}_{J}},\text{St}^{\widetilde{L}_{J}}_{K}
, for any
J\subseteq S
.

Theorem
A.1
as
currently stated requires that we prove that the kernel and image of

(5.3.1)

\beta_{K}:\bigoplus_{J\subseteq S}R^{G}_{L_{J}}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}){\longrightarrow}\bigoplus_{J\subseteq S}R^{G}_{L_{J}}(St_{K}^{L_{J}})

have no common nonzero constituents, a property that we know (and will prove) to be true in our context.

For each
J\subseteq S
, let
\gamma^{J}:K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}\to K\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}\cong\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}
, discussed
in the proof of Theorem
A.1
. We take
L_{J}
to be the standard Levi subgroup of
G
associated to
J
, and all the terms
\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}},\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{\sigma}},\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}
as discussed above. Similarly, we have a map
\widetilde{\gamma}^{J}
, replacing
G
with
\widetilde{G}
, and using

\Gamma_{\sigma}^{\widetilde{L}_{J}}
,
\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{\widetilde{L}_{J}}
, and
\text{St}_{K}^{\widetilde{L}_{J}}
in place of their counterparts for
G
.

We pause here to note that

\beta_{K}=\bigoplus_{J\subseteq S}R^{G}_{L_{J}}(\gamma^{J})\quad{\text{\rm and}}\quad\widetilde{\beta}_{K}=\bigoplus_{J\subseteq S}R^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{L}_{J}}(\widetilde{\gamma}^{J}).

Recall that
\widetilde{G}
is defined in the notation and setting of Lemma
5.2.4
as
\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}^{\widetilde{F}}
, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}={\mathbb{G}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
. Here,
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
is an isomorphic copy of the “maximally split” torus
{\mathbb{T}}
of
{\mathbb{G}}
in the terminology of
[
C85
, §1.18]
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}=Z(\widetilde{\mathbb{G}})
. The torus
{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
is maximally split in
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}
, and in
\widetilde{G}=G({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
, as may be shown using the Lang-Steinberg theorem. There is also an
F
-stable Borel subgroup
{\mathbb{B}}
of
{\mathbb{G}}
containing
{\mathbb{T}}
in the setting of Lemma
5.2.4
. A closed subgroup
{\mathbb{N}}
normalizing
{\mathbb{T}}
may then be chosen so that
{\mathbb{B}}
,
{\mathbb{N}}
give a split BN-pair structure to
{\mathbb{G}}
as per the axioms (specialized for algebraic groups) of
[
C85
, p.23]
, with
{\mathbb{B}}\cap{\mathbb{N}}={\mathbb{T}}
. (The choice in
[
C85
, p.22]
of
{\mathbb{N}}
is
{\mathbb{N}}=N_{\mathbb{G}}({\mathbb{T}})
, but this is not part of the axioms. We note that this choice of
{\mathbb{N}}
is
F
-stable, a useful property.) We now have three additional candidates for split BN-pairs: the pair
{\mathbb{B}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
with intersection
{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
, the pair
B={\mathbb{B}}^{F},N={\mathbb{N}}^{F}
with intersection
T={\mathbb{T}}^{F}
, and, finally, the pair
({\mathbb{B}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}},({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
with intersection
({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
. The first of these candidate pairs satisfies Carter’s axioms by an easy inspection, while the last two are covered by
[
C85
, pp. 33-34]
, which also discusses their respective Weyl groups.

We now have four “algebraic groups with split BN-pairs” in the sense of
[
C85
]
, namely
{\mathbb{G}}
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}={\mathbb{G}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
,
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
​, and
\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}^{\widetilde{F}}=G({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
. The first two groups are connected reductive, and the second two are the respective finite fixed-point groups under Frobenius endomorphisms. According to
[
C85
, p. 61 bottom]
these groups all “satisfy the commutator relations” of Chevalley in the form displayed there. This is needed for the full Levi decomposition theory developed in
[
C85
, §2.6]
, quoted in
[
G17
, §4]
. The standard Levi subgroup
L_{J}
of
G
is introduced above
[
C85
, Prop. 2.6.3]
. Implicit in its definitions is the split BN-pair context provided by the pair
\mathbb{B}^{F},{\mathbb{N}}^{F}
discussed above. If that pair is changed to
({\mathbb{B}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}},({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
, the analogous definition gives the Levi subgroup
\widetilde{L}_{J}
of
\widetilde{G}=({\mathbb{G}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
.

In more detail,
L_{J}
is defined as the group generated by the BN-pair intersection
B\cap N=T
and certain “root groups”
X_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\Phi_{J}
, determined by the interaction of the unipotent radical
U=({\mathbb{U}})^{F}
of (the
B
-part of) the pair with its Weyl group,
W=N/T
. See
[
C85
, p. 43, §2.2, Prop. 2.3.1, p. 57]
. We take the opportunity to observe that
W
also identifies with the Weyl group
({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}/({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
of the split BN-pair
({\mathbb{B}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}},

({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
. Briefly, using the Lang-Steinberg theorem and the observation that
{\mathbb{N}}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}\subseteq{\mathbb{G}}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}\subseteq{\mathbb{T}}
, we have

\displaystyle N/T

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbb{N}}^{F}/{\mathbb{T}}^{F}\cong({\mathbb{N}}/{\mathbb{T}})^{F}=({\mathbb{N}}/{\mathbb{T}}({\mathbb{N}}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}))^{F}=({\mathbb{N}}/{\mathbb{N}}\cap{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{F}

\displaystyle\cong({\mathbb{N}}{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}/{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}\cong({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}/({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}.

Order considerations now imply that the natural map

N/T\longrightarrow({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}/({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}

is an isomorphism. (The map is injective, since

N\cap({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}=({\mathbb{N}}\cap{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}=({\mathbb{T}}({\mathbb{N}}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}))^{\widetilde{F}}\subseteq({\mathbb{T}}({\mathbb{G}}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}))^{\widetilde{F}}={\mathbb{T}}^{\widetilde{F}}={\mathbb{T}}^{F}=T.)

Surjectivity gives the useful identity
({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}=N({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
.

Next, we note that the following statements all hold.

(1)

T
normalizes
U
.

(2)

If
V
is any subgroup of
G
or
\widetilde{G}
normalized by
T
, then
{}^{n}V:=nVn^{-1}
and
V^{n}:=n^{-1}Vn
are also normalized
by
T
, for any
n\in N
.

(3)

If
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are subgroups of
G
or
\widetilde{G}
, both normalized by
T
, then
V_{1}\cap V_{2}
and
\langle V_{1},V_{2}\rangle
, the subgroup generated by
V_{1},V_{2}
, are also normalized by
T
.

The proofs are all elementary, and the same properties (1), (2), (3) hold if
T
is replaced by
({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}.
(To see that (1) holds in this case, note that
U={\mathbb{U}}^{F}
, and
{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
normalizes
\mathbb{U}
.)

The above paragraph contains an implicit recipe for computing root groups, in the sense of
[
C85
, p. 50]
, for
G
or
\widetilde{G}
, using
[
C85
, §2.5]
. The split BN-pair context we use for
G
is
{\mathbb{B}}^{F},{\mathbb{N}}^{F}
and
({\mathbb{B}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}},({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
for
\widetilde{G}
. First, we use the first two lines of the display
[
C85
, p. 50]
to construct a “fundamental” root group
X_{i}
from
U
and a fundamental reflection
s_{i}
in
W
, represented by
n_{i}\in N
. A representative
n_{0}
for any longest word is also needed. We then write

X_{i}=U\cap U^{n_{0}n_{i}},

which is the same group whether working with
G
or
\widetilde{G}
. All the other root groups for
G
have the form
nX_{i}n^{-1}
for some fundamental reflection
s_{i}
and some element
n\in N={\mathbb{N}}^{F}

[
C85
, Prop. 2.5.15]
. A similar construction applies for
\widetilde{G}
, using
({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{{\widetilde{F}}}
in place of
{\mathbb{N}}^{F}
. However, it leads to the same root groups, since each
X_{i}
is normalized by
({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
and
({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}=N({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
.

It will now be convenient to write
nX_{i}n^{-1}
above as
wX_{i}w^{-1}(={{}^{w}X_{i}}=X_{i}^{w^{-1}}
), where
w\in W
is represented by
n\in N
. More generally, if
X,Y
are root groups and
Y=nXn^{-1}
, with
n
representing
w
(
n\in N
,
w\in W
), we write
Y=wXw^{-1}
(
={{}^{w}X}=X^{w^{-1}}
). This gives a permutation action of
W
on the set of root subgroups. This action agrees with the permutation action on the set of roots
\Phi
in a “root system” constructed from
W
as a finite Coxeter group in
[
C85
, §2.2]
. See
[
C85
, Prop. 2.5.15]
and its proof, which gives the equivariant bijection. The notation
\Phi_{J}
is introduced in
[
C85
, Prop. 2.3.1]
, using as
J
a subset of the indices of the fundamental reflections
S
of
W
. Our
J
denotes the subset of these reflections themselves. With this modification, root groups
X_{\alpha}
“with
\alpha\in\Phi_{J}
” are those of the form
w(X_{i})
with
s_{i}\in J
and

w\in W_{J}:=\langle J\rangle
. (The key to proving this is observing that the correspondence given in the proof of
[
C85
, Prop. 2.5.15]
is
W
-equivariant. It can then be applied to the definition of
\Phi_{J}=W_{J}(\Delta_{J})
in
[
C85
, Prop. 2.3.1]
, noting that
\Delta_{J}
corresponds to the fundamental reflections in
J
.)

We can now return to our discussion of
L_{J}
and
{\widetilde{L}}_{J}
earlier in the proof. Let

X_{J}=\langle w(X_{i})\,|\,s_{i}\in J\,\,\&\,\,w\in W_{J}\rangle.

We now know

L_{J}=\langle T,X_{J}\rangle
, from the definition in
[
C85
, §2.6]
in terms of
\Phi_{J}
and the reformulation above. This is in the context of
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
and its split BN-pair
{\mathbb{B}}^{F},{\mathbb{N}}^{F}
. If instead we work with
\widetilde{G}=({\mathbb{G}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
, we obtain
{\widetilde{L}}_{J}
in place of
L_{J}
. Since the root groups for
G
and
\widetilde{G}
are the same, with the same Weyl group actions on them, we have

{\widetilde{L}}_{J}=\langle({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}},X_{J}\rangle=({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}X_{J}=({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{{\widetilde{F}}}{\mathbb{T}}^{F}X_{J}=({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}L_{J},

taking into account the normalization of all the root groups by both
T
and
({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
. We may also write

{\widetilde{L}}_{J}=L_{J}({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}.

Notice that the unipotent radical of
\widetilde{B}_{J}
and the unipotent radical of
B_{J}
are the same. Hence,
\Gamma_{\sigma}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}\cong\text{Ind}_{L_{J}}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}(\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})
by definition.
This is a good place to mention that
L_{J}
(resp.,
\widetilde{L}_{J}
) inherits a split BN-pair structure from
G
(resp.,
\widetilde{G})
. See
[
C85
, Prop. 2.6.3]
. We denote the pair as
B_{J},N_{J}
(resp.,
\widetilde{B}_{J},\widetilde{N}_{J}
). We remark that
B_{J}\subseteq B
and
N_{J}\subseteq N
(resp.,
\widetilde{B}_{J}\subseteq\widetilde{B}
and
\widetilde{N}_{J}\subseteq\widetilde{N}=\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}^{\widetilde{F}}
, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}={\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}
).
Also, we find, again using
[
C85
, Prop. 2.6.3]
with our discussions above (of root subgroups and of
W
), that

B_{J}\cap N_{J}=T,\quad\widetilde{B}_{J}\cap\widetilde{N}_{J}=({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}},\quad N_{J}/T=W_{J}\subseteq W=N/T.

The group
W_{J}
in this display is the group generated by the reflections in
J
, in our notation, and by the reflections indexed by
J
in the notation of
[
C85
, Prop. 2.6(4)]
. With some abuse of notation, we also have

\widetilde{N}_{J}/({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}=W_{J}\subseteq W=({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}/({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}.

Technically, we should use groups
\widetilde{W}_{J}
,
\widetilde{W}
, naturally isomorphic to
W_{J},W
, respectively, in the display above. Here,
\widetilde{W}_{J}=\overline{\eta}(W_{J})
and
\widetilde{W}=\overline{\eta}(W)
, where

\overline{\eta}:N/T\longrightarrow({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}/({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}

is the natural isomorphism discussed above in this proof. It is induced by the natural inclusion map
\eta:N\longrightarrow({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
that sends any element of
N
to itself.

For use in the next paragraph, observe that if
w\in W=N/T
is represented by
n_{w}\in N
, then
\overline{\eta}(w)\in\widetilde{W}=({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{{\widetilde{F}}}/({\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
is represented by the same element
n_{w}
, viewed as a member of
({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}
. If
w\in W_{J}=N_{J}/T
, then
n_{w}\in N_{J}
, and
\overline{\eta}(w)\in\overline{\eta}(W_{J})=\widetilde{W}_{J}
is, as before, represented by
n_{w}
. Since the isomorphism
\overline{\eta}
takes
W_{J}
onto
\widetilde{W}_{J}
, this process can be reversed, showing that any element
{\widetilde{w}}\in{\widetilde{W}}_{J}
is representable by an element
n_{w}\in N_{J}
, where
\overline{\eta}(w)=\widetilde{w}
. We remark that the element
n_{w}
belongs to
\widetilde{N}_{J}
, since the latter group is
the preimage of
\widetilde{W}_{J}
under the natural map
\widetilde{N}=({\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})^{\widetilde{F}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{W}.

We next note, using the previous paragraph, that the Steinberg module
\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}_{K}
has
\text{St}^{L_{J}}_{K}
as its restriction to
L_{J}
, up to isomorphism: To see this, consider the description of
\text{St}^{L_{J}}_{K}
(and of
\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}_{K}
in
[
G17
, Th. 2.1]
, attributed to Steinberg):

\text{St}^{L_{J}}_{K}=KL_{J}\mathfrak{e}\subseteq KL_{J}{{\underline{\mathfrak{b}}}},

where
{\mathfrak{e}}:=\sum_{w\in W_{J}}(-1)^{\ell(w)}n_{w}\underline{\mathfrak{b}}

and
\underline{\mathfrak{b}}
is the sum of the elements in the “B”-part of a given split BN-pair structure for
L_{J}
.
We use the pair
B_{J},N_{J}

above for
L_{J}
, and
{\widetilde{B}}_{J},{\widetilde{N}}_{J}
for
{\widetilde{L}}_{J}

and an analogous description of its Steinberg module
\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}_{K}
over
K
. The elements
n_{w}
are required to be in the “N” part
N_{J}
of the pair for
L_{J}
, with
n_{w}
a preimage of
w\in W_{J}
under the natural map

N_{J}\twoheadrightarrow W_{J}
. Since
N_{J}
is the full preimage of
W_{J}
under the natural map
N\to W
, it is enough to know that
n_{w}\in N
and its image
w
lies in
W_{J}
. Passing to the formula for
\text{St}_{K}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}
, we find that the expression
\sum_{w\in W_{J}}(-1)^{\ell(w)}n_{w}
in the formula for
\text{St}^{L_{J}}_{K}
can be used in the same place for
\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}_{K}
in the formula for the analog for
\widetilde{\mathfrak{e}}
of
\mathfrak{e}
. Let
\mathfrak{c}
be a set of representatives for all the right cosets of
{\mathfrak{b}}:=B_{J}
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}:={\widetilde{B}}_{J}
, and let
\underline{\mathfrak{c}}
denote the sum of all the elements in
\mathfrak{c}
viewed as members of
K{\widetilde{L}}_{J}
. Regard also the sums
\underline{\mathfrak{b}}
and
\underline{\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}}
as taking place in
K{\widetilde{L}}_{J}
. Then

\underline{\mathfrak{b}}\,\cdot\underline{\mathfrak{c}}=\underline{\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}},\quad{\mathfrak{e}}\cdot\underline{\mathfrak{c}}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{e}}.

Right multiplication by
\underline{\mathfrak{c}}
thus provides, inside
K{\widetilde{L}}_{J}
, a nonzero map of
KL_{J}
-modules

\text{St}^{L_{J}}_{K}\longrightarrow\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}_{K}|_{L_{J}}
. The map is injective, since
\text{St}^{L_{J}}_{K}
is irreducible
[
G17
, Th. 2.1(ii)]
. It is an isomorphism, since both domain and target have the same dimension
|B_{J}|_{p}=|{\widetilde{B}}_{J}|_{p}
by
[
G17
, Th. 2.1(i)]
.
(The two given
p
-parts represent the equal cardinalities of Sylow
p
-subgroups of the underlying groups
B
and
\widetilde{B}
, and the quoted result implies these cardinalities give the dimensions of the relevant Steinberg modules.)

We now return to the task of proving that the kernel
\text{Ker}\beta_{K}
and the image
\text{Im}\beta_{K}
of
\beta_{K}
have no nonzero constituents in common. (Recall that this will prove that Theorem
A.1
holds for our
{\mathbb{G}}
here, i.e., one that may not satisfy the connected center hypothesis). Equivalently, we must show that
\text{Ker}R^{G}_{L_{J}}(\gamma^{J})
and
\text{Im}R^{G}_{L_{J^{\prime}}}(\gamma^{J^{\prime}})
have no common nonzero constituents, for each
J,J^{\prime}
contained in
S
. Recall that
\gamma^{J}
is a surjective map

K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}\twoheadrightarrow\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}
with analogous notation for
\widetilde{\gamma}^{J}
(and
\gamma^{J^{\prime}},\widetilde{\gamma}^{J^{\prime}}
).
As such, it is unique up to nonzero scalar multiples. A similar uniqueness holds for its analogous maps.

We next
claim
that
R^{\widetilde{G}}_{L_{J}}(\text{Ker}\gamma^{J})
is isomorphic to a submodule of
\text{Ker}R^{{\widetilde{G}}}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}(\widetilde{\gamma}^{J})
. To see this, first induce the
KL_{J}
exact sequences

0\longrightarrow\text{Ker}\gamma^{J}\longrightarrow K\Gamma^{L_{J}}_{\sigma}\overset{\gamma^{J}}{\longrightarrow}\text{St}^{L_{J}}_{K}\to 0

to
K{\widetilde{L}}_{J}
, obtaining

0\to\text{Ind}_{L_{J}}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}(\text{Ker}\gamma^{J})\longrightarrow K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}\longrightarrow\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}_{K}\otimes K({\widetilde{L}}_{J}/L_{J})\to 0.

Here, we view
\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
as the induction of
\sigma
to
L_{J}
from the “unipotent radical” of
B_{J}
, and
\Gamma_{\sigma}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}
as its further induction to
{\widetilde{L}}_{J}
. (We first learned this viewpoint from
[
DF94
, Lem. 2.3(a)]
.) The tensor product expression on the right is obtained from the well-known tensor identity, using the fact that
\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}_{K}|_{L_{J}}\cong\text{St}^{L_{J}}_{K}
(proved above). Since the
K{\widetilde{L}}_{J}
-module
K({\widetilde{L}}_{J}/L_{J})
has the trivial module
K
as a homomorphic image, we have an epimorphism
\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}_{K}\otimes K({\widetilde{L}}_{J}/L_{J})\twoheadrightarrow\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}_{K}
. After possibly multipling by a nonzero scalar, we can compose this map with the map
K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}\longrightarrow\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}_{K}\otimes K({\widetilde{L}}_{J}/L_{J})
above to obtain
\widetilde{\gamma}^{J}
. Consequently, there is a natural inclusion

\text{Ind}_{L_{J}}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}(\text{Ker}\gamma^{J})\subseteq\text{Ker}\widetilde{\gamma}^{J}.

Next, apply
R^{\widetilde{G}}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}
to both sides (an exact functor). The right-hand side becomes
R^{\widetilde{G}}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}(\text{Ker}\,\widetilde{\gamma}^{J})\cong\text{Ker}R^{\widetilde{G}}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}(\widetilde{\gamma}^{J})
. To understand the left-hand side, note first that the standard parabolic subgroups
P_{J}
and
{\widetilde{P}}_{J}
associated to
J
(and containing
L_{J}
and
{\widetilde{L}}_{J}
, respectively) have the same unipotent radical, called
U_{J}
in
[
C85
, §2.6]
. As a consequence, inflation commutes with induction, in the sense that
\text{Inf}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}^{{\widetilde{P}}_{J}}\circ\text{Ind}_{L_{J}}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}\cong\text{Ind}_{P_{J}}^{{\widetilde{P}}_{J}}\circ\text{Inf}_{L_{J}}^{P_{J}}
as functors on
KL_{J}
-modules. Apply this isomorphism of functors to the object
\text{Ker}\gamma^{J}
, and then apply
\text{Ind}_{{\widetilde{P}}_{J}}^{{\widetilde{G}}}
to obtain

R_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}^{\widetilde{G}}(\text{Ind}_{L_{J}}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}(\text{Ker}\gamma^{J}))\cong R^{{\widetilde{G}}}_{L_{J}}(\text{Ker}\gamma^{J}).

The
claim
which began this paragraph follows.

As a corollary to the claim, using the validity of Theorem
A.1
for
\widetilde{{\mathbb{G}}}
(and its consequences for
\widetilde{\beta}_{K}
and
the various
R_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}}^{{\widetilde{G}}}(\widetilde{\gamma}^{J})
, we now know
that each
\text{Ker}R^{{\widetilde{G}}}_{L_{J}}(\gamma^{J})=R^{{\widetilde{G}}}_{L_{J}}(\text{Ker}\gamma^{J})
has no nonzero constituents in common with each

\text{Im}R^{\widetilde{G}}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J^{\prime}}}(\widetilde{\gamma}^{J^{\prime}})\cong R^{{\widetilde{G}}}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J^{\prime}}}(\text{St}_{K}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J^{\prime}}}).

Applying Frobenius reciprocity (for ordinary induction) and writing

R^{{\widetilde{G}}}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J}^{\prime}}\cong\text{Ind}_{P_{J}}^{{\widetilde{G}}}\circ{\rm Inf}_{L_{J}}^{P_{J}}
), where Inf is inflation,
we find that
R^{G}_{L_{J}}(\text{Ker}\gamma^{J})
has no nonzero constituents in common with the restriction

\displaystyle R^{{\widetilde{G}}}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J^{\prime}}}(\text{St}_{K}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J^{\prime}}})|_{G}

\displaystyle\cong\Big(\text{Ind}^{{\widetilde{G}}}_{{\widetilde{P}}_{J^{\prime}}}({\rm Inf}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J^{\prime}}}^{{\widetilde{P}}_{J^{\prime}}}\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J^{\prime}}}_{K})\Big)\Big|_{G}

\displaystyle\cong\text{Ind}^{G}_{{\widetilde{P}}_{J^{\prime}}\cap G}({\rm Inf}^{{\widetilde{P}}_{J^{\prime}}}_{{\widetilde{L}}_{J^{\prime}}}\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J^{\prime}}}_{K})|_{{{\widetilde{P}}_{J^{\prime}}}\cap G}

\displaystyle\cong\text{Ind}^{G}_{P_{J^{\prime}}}({\rm Inf}^{P_{J^{\prime}}}_{L_{J^{\prime}}}(\text{St}^{{\widetilde{L}}_{J^{\prime}}}_{K}|_{L_{J^{\prime}}}))

\displaystyle\cong\text{Ind}^{G}_{P_{J^{\prime}}}({\rm Inf}^{P_{J^{\prime}}}_{L_{J^{\prime}}}\text{St}^{L_{J^{\prime}}}_{K})\cong R^{G}_{L_{J^{\prime}}}(\text{St}^{L_{J^{\prime}}}_{K}).

In this display, the first line holds by definition. The second line follows from the Mackey decomposition theory, using
{\widetilde{G}}={\widetilde{P}}_{J^{\prime}}G
. The third line uses
P_{J^{\prime}}\subseteq{\widetilde{P}}_{J^{\prime}}\cap G\subseteq N_{G}(P_{J^{\prime}})\subseteq P_{J^{\prime}}
; see
[
C85
, Prop. 2.1.6]
. In particular,
P_{J^{\prime}}=\widetilde{P}_{J^{\prime}}\cap G
. The agreement of
P_{J^{\prime}}
actions inside the outer parentheses, in lines two and three, can be checked on
L_{J^{\prime}}
and
U_{J^{\prime}}
. The resulting
L_{J^{\prime}}
-module in both lines is
\text{St}^{\widetilde{L}_{K}^{J^{\prime}}}|_{L_{J^{\prime}}}
, and the
U_{J^{\prime}}
-actions are both trivial. Passage from line three to four is achieved directly from the Steinberg module restriction result earlier in this proof. This concludes the proof of the
claimed variation on Theorem
A.1
.

The remaining details in the proof of the general case of Theorem
5.3.1
(
{\mathbb{G}}
is simple) parallel those given for
{\mathbb{G}}
of adjoint type and are left to the reader.
∎

Remarks 5.3.2
.

(1) If we use a result of Dipper and Fleischmann, the proof for Theorem
A.1
in Appendix A can be generalized to the general case where the center of
{\mathbb{G}}
is not necessarily connected. To show that the kernel and image of
\beta_{K}
in (
5.3.1
) have no irreducible constituents in common, it suffices to show that

\text{dim}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(P,M)=\text{dim}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(M,M),

where
P=\bigoplus_{J\subseteq S}R^{G}_{L_{J}}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})
and
M=\bigoplus_{J\subseteq S}R^{G}_{L_{J}}(St_{K}^{L_{J}})
. This can be easily reduced to prove that

(5.3.2)

\text{dim}\,\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}\big(R^{G}_{L_{I}}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),R^{G}_{L_{J}}(St_{K}^{L_{J}})\big)=\text{dim}\,\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}\big(R^{G}_{L_{I}}(St_{K}^{L_{I}}),R^{G}_{L_{J}}(St_{K}^{L_{J}})\big),

for all
I,J\subseteq S
. An application of Frobenius reciprocity and the Mackey decomposition theorem for Harish-Chandra inductions and restrictions as in the proof of Theorem
A.1
requires the fact that
{}^{*}R^{{}^{n}L_{I}}_{{}^{n}L_{I}\cap L_{J}}({}^{n}K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{I}})
is isomorphic to a Gelfand–Graev module
K\Gamma_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{{}^{n}L_{I}\cap L_{J}}
for some regular character
\sigma^{\prime}
, where
n=n_{w}\in N
with
w\in{\mathscr{D}}_{J,I}
, the set of
(W_{J},W_{I})
-double coset representatives in
W
. This result is proved in
[
DF92
, Lem. (3.6)]
via
[
DF92
, Lem. (1.8)]
. (Note that if the center of
{\mathbb{G}}
is connected, then we may replace
K\Gamma_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{{}^{n}L_{I}\cap L_{J}}
by
K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{{}^{n}L_{I}\cap L_{J}}
, the case considered in Appendix A.) Thus, by applying Frobenius reciprocity again together with the fact that
{}^{*}R^{G}_{L_{J}}(St_{K}^{G})\cong St_{K}^{L_{J}}
(see
[
CR87
, (71.6)]
) and
[\Gamma_{\sigma}:St_{K}]=1
(see
[
G17
, 4.1]
), we obtain
\text{LHS}=\#{\mathscr{D}}_{J,I}
in (
5.3.2
). A similar argument shows that
\text{RHS}=\#{\mathscr{D}}_{J,I}
as well. This proves (
5.3.2
) and, hence, the generalized version of Theorem
A.1
.

(2) We conclude this section by again calling attention to Theorem
5.3.1
(b), which shows that the decomposition matrix
D
relative to a given cross-characteristic
r
can be evaluated by working with the smallest groups (as measured by a power
q
of
p
)
in any Lie-type series as parametrized by Carter in
[
C85
, §1.1.9]
.

5.4.
i
-Quantum groups

The
q
-Schur algebras
[
DJ89
]
are certain Hecke endo-algebras of type
A
which play a central role in linking representations of quantum
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
and Hecke algebras of the symmetric groups as well as finite general linear groups (see, e.g.,
[
DD97
]
).
In this section, we will look at how certain Hecke endo-algebras of type
B/C
can be introduced to play a similar role in connecting representations of finite orthogonal/symplectic groups with those of the
i
-quantum groups introduced in
[
BW18
]
.
For this purpose, we will modify the definition of the algebra
A
to introduce
q
-Schur algebras of type
B/C
with bidegree
(n,r)
, where
n
is the rank of the
i
-quantum group and
r
is the rank of the Hecke algebra.

5.4A The
q
-Schur algebras of type
B/C
.
Let
\mathbb{F}_{q}
be the finite field of
q
elements and
r
a positive integer. (In this section,
r
is not required to be the cross-characteristic associated with
G
.) Consider the following two types of the orthogonal/symplectic groups introduced in Example
1.1.2
:

(5.4.1)

G(r_{*},q)=\begin{cases}{\text{\rm O}}_{2r+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),&\text{ if }*={\jmath}\\
{\text{\rm Sp}}_{2r}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),&\text{ if }*={\imath},\end{cases}\;\;\text{ where }\;\;r_{*}=\begin{cases}2r+1,&\text{ if }*={\jmath}\\
2r.&\text{ if }*={\imath}.\end{cases}

Then,
G(r_{*},q)
is the fixed-point subgroup of
{\text{\rm GL}}_{r_{*}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
under the graph automorphism in (
1.1.3
):

\vartheta_{r_{*}}:{\text{\rm GL}}_{r_{*}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\longrightarrow{\text{\rm GL}}_{r_{*}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),\;\;x\longmapsto J_{*}^{-1}(x^{t})^{-1}J_{*},

where
J_{{\jmath}}=J_{2r+1}
and
J_{{\imath}}=J^{-}_{2r}
as in (
1.1.4
).

The graph automorphism
\vartheta_{r_{*}}
induces an automorphism
\sigma=\sigma_{r_{*}}
on the Weyl group
\widetilde{W}^{*}
of
{\text{\rm GL}}_{r_{*}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, which is identified as the symmetric group
{\mathfrak{S}}_{r_{*}}
.
More precisely, we have an involution:

(5.4.2)

\sigma:{\mathfrak{S}}_{r_{*}}\to{\mathfrak{S}}_{r_{*}},(i,j)\mapsto(r_{*}+1-i,r_{*}+1-j),\text{ for all }i,j\in[1,r_{*}],

where
[1,r_{*}].=\{1,2,\ldots,r_{*}\}
, such that the Weyl group
W^{*}
of
G(r_{*},q)
is isomorphic to the fixed-point group
({\mathfrak{S}}_{r_{*}})^{\sigma}
.

If
S=\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{r}\}
is the set of the Coxeter generators for
W^{*}
, then we have the following identifications:

For
*=\jmath
,
W^{\jmath}=({\mathfrak{S}}_{2r+1})^{\sigma}
and

s_{i}=(i,i+1)(2r+2-i,2r+1-i)\;\;(1\leq i<r),\quad s_{r}=(r,r+1)(r+1,r+2)(r,r+1),

while, for
*=\imath
,
W^{\imath}=({\mathfrak{S}}_{2r})^{\sigma}
and

s_{i}=(i,i+1)(2r+1-i,2r-i)\;\;(1\leq i<r),\quad s_{r}=(r,r+1).

See Example
1.1.2
. Hence,
W^{\jmath}\cong W^{\imath}
and we set
W_{r}=W^{*}
.

Note that
W_{r}
is the subgroup of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{r_{*}}
consisting of permutations

(5.4.3)

\begin{pmatrix}1&2&\cdots&r&r+1&\cdots&r_{*}\\
i_{1}&i_{2}&\cdots&i_{r}&i_{r+1}&\cdots&i_{r_{*}}\end{pmatrix}

satisfying
i_{j}+i_{r_{*}+1-j}=r_{*}+1
.

Let
\widetilde{B}(r_{*},\mathbb{F}_{q})
be the subgroup of
{\text{\rm GL}}_{r_{*}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
consisting of upper triangular matrices, where
r_{*}\in\{2r,2r+1\}
and, for
*\in\{\jmath,\imath\}
, let

B(r_{*},q)=G(r_{*},q)\cap\widetilde{B}(r_{*},\mathbb{F}_{q}).

Then,
B(r_{*},q)
is a Borel subgroup of
G(r_{*},q)
and, by Lemma
1.2.2
,

\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathbb{Z}G(r_{*},q)}\Big(\text{Ind}_{B(r_{*},q)}^{G(r_{*},q)}1_{\mathbb{Z}}\Big)\cong{\mathcal{H}}^{o}(W_{r})_{\mathbb{Z}},

where
1_{\mathbb{Z}}
is the trivial representation of
B(r_{*},q)
,
{\mathcal{H}}^{o}(W_{r})_{\mathbb{Z}}
is the specialization via
t^{2}\to q
of the generic Hecke algebra of
W_{r}
with equal parameters.

Let
\Lambda(m,r)=(\mathbb{N}^{m})_{r}
be the set of sequences
(a_{1},a_{2}\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
with entries summing to
r
, and let

(5.4.4)

\Lambda_{\jmath}(n,r)=\Lambda(n+1,r),\quad\Lambda_{\imath}(n,r)=\Lambda(n,r).

Note that we often regard
\Lambda_{\imath}(n,r)
as a subset of
\Lambda_{\jmath}(n,r)
via the embedding
{\lambda}\mapsto({\lambda},0)
.
Define

(5.4.5)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\ }

\displaystyle:\Lambda_{\jmath}(n,r)\longrightarrow\Lambda(2n+1,2r+1),\;\;{\lambda}\longmapsto\widetilde{\lambda}=({\lambda}_{1},\ldots,{\lambda}_{n},2{\lambda}_{n+1}+1,{\lambda}_{n},\ldots,{\lambda}_{1})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\ }

\displaystyle:\Lambda_{\imath}(n,r)\longrightarrow\Lambda(2n,2r),\quad{\lambda}\longmapsto\widetilde{\lambda}=({\lambda}_{1},\ldots,{\lambda}_{n},{\lambda}_{n},\ldots,{\lambda}_{1}).

For
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{*}(n,r)
with
*\in\{{\jmath},{\imath}\}
, let
\widetilde{P}_{{\lambda}}(r_{*},q)
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
{\text{\rm GL}}_{r_{*}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
associated with
\widetilde{\lambda}
, consisting of upper triangular matrices with blocks of sizes
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}
on the diagonal. Let

P_{\lambda}(r_{*},q)=\widetilde{P}_{{\lambda}}(r_{*},q)\cap G(r_{*},q),

and define, for any commutative ring
R
with
q=q1_{R}\in R
,

\mathcal{E}^{*}_{R}(n,r)=\text{\rm{End}}_{RG(r_{*},q)}\bigg(\bigoplus_{{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{*}(n,r)}\text{Ind}_{P_{\lambda}(r_{*},q)}^{G(r_{*},q)}1_{R}\bigg),

where
1_{R}
denotes the trivial representation of
P_{\lambda}(r_{*},q)
.

The Weyl group of the Levi subgroup
L_{\lambda}(r_{\jmath},q)
of
P_{\lambda}(r_{{\jmath}},q)
has the form

W_{\lambda}=W^{\jmath}_{\lambda}=\langle S\backslash\{s_{{\lambda}_{1}+\cdots+{\lambda}_{i}}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}\rangle,\quad(\text{Here }{\lambda}\in{\Lambda}(n+1,r).)

while the Weyl group of the Levi subgroup
L_{\lambda}(r_{\imath},q)
of
P_{\lambda}(r_{{\imath}},q)
has the form

W_{\lambda}=W^{\imath}_{\lambda}=\langle S\backslash\{s_{{\lambda}_{1}+\cdots+{\lambda}_{i}}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}\rangle.\quad(\text{Here }{\lambda}\in{\Lambda}(n,r).)

They are both the fixed-point subgroups of the corresponding standard Young subgroups
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\widetilde{\lambda}}
under the graph automorphism
\sigma
in (
5.4.2
):
W_{\lambda}=({\mathfrak{S}}_{\widetilde{\lambda}})^{\sigma}
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}(W_{r})
be the Hecke algebra over
{\mathcal{Z}}
associated with
W_{r}
. It is generated by
T_{i}=T_{s_{i}}
, for
1\leq i\leq r
, with basis
\{T_{w}\}_{w\in W}
. The subalgebra generated by
T_{1},\ldots,T_{r-1}
is the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}({\mathfrak{S}}_{r})
associated with the symmetric group
{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}
.

For
*\in\{{\jmath},{\imath}\}
and
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{*}(n,r)
, let
x_{\lambda}=\sum_{w\in W^{*}_{\lambda}}T_{w}
(perhaps,
x^{*}_{\lambda}
is more accurate than
x_{\lambda}
),
T^{*}_{n,r}:=\bigoplus_{{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{*}(n,r)}x_{\lambda}{\mathcal{H}}(W_{r})
, and define a Hecke endomorphism
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra
of bidegree
(n,r)
:

(5.4.6)

{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(n,r)={\mathcal{S}}^{*}_{t,{\mathcal{Z}}}(n,r)=\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}(W_{r})}(T^{*}_{n,r}).

The algebra
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)
(resp.,
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)
) is called the
q
-Schur algebra of type
B
(resp,
type
C
). See the lemma below for a justification of the terminology.

Remark 5.4.1
.

(1) Note that if we replace
T
in displays (
2.4.3
) and (
2.4.4
) by
T^{\dagger}=\bigoplus_{J\subseteq S}(x_{J}{\mathcal{H}})^{m_{J}}
with all
m_{J}>0
, and define
A^{\dagger}:=\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathcal{H}}(T^{\dagger})
, then, for
n\geq r
and a suitable choice of
m_{J}
,
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)=A^{\dagger}
. However, all
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)
or
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)
for
n<r
cannot be of the form
A^{\dagger}
, since
T^{\imath}_{n,r}
does not contain
x_{J}{\mathcal{H}}
(as a direct summand) with
s_{r}\in J
and
T^{\jmath}_{n,r}
does not contain
{\mathcal{H}}
if
n<r
. But each of them has the form
eA^{\dagger}e
for some
A^{\dagger}
and some idempotent
e\in A^{\dagger}
, i.e., is a centralizer subalgebra of
A^{\dagger}
.

(2) The algebras
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)
(
n\geq r
) and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)
(
n\geq r
) are Morita equivalent to the
q
-Schur
2
algebra of parabolic and Young type, respectively (see
[
DS00a
, p.4337]
).

Proposition 5.4.2
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and assume
\sqrt{q},q^{-1}\in R
.
By specializing
t
to
\sqrt{q}
, there is an algebra isomorphism

{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(n,r)_{R}\cong\mathcal{E}^{*}_{R}(n,r)\;\;(*\in\{{\jmath},{\imath}\}).

Proof.

The isomorphism is an extension of the Iwahori isomorphism (see Lemma
1.2.2
):

{\mathcal{H}}(W_{r})|_{t^{2}=q}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\text{\rm{End}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}G^{*}}(\text{Ind}_{B^{*}}^{G^{*}}1_{\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}),\;\;T_{w}\mapsto(\underline{B^{*}}\mapsto\underline{B^{*}wB^{*}}),

where
\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{q},{\sqrt{q}}^{-1}]
,
G^{*}=G(r_{*},q)
,
B^{*}=B(r_{*},q)
, and
\underline{X}=\sum_{x\in X}x
.
Compare
[
DDPW08
, Th. 4.37]
. Now, an argument similar to the proof of
[
DDPW08
, Th. 13.15]
proves the isomorphism.
∎

Recall the ring
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
with all bad primes invertible. For
*=\jmath,\imath
, let
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(n,r)^{\natural}:={\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(n,r)
.

Proposition 5.4.3
.

There exist split quasi-hereditary
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
-algebras
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath+}(n,r)^{\natural}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath+}(n,r)^{\natural}
, which are endomorphism algebras of
{\mathcal{H}}(W_{r})
-modules with dual left-cell filtrations, such that
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)^{\natural}
(resp.,
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)^{\natural}
) is a centralizer subalgebra of

{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath+}(n,r)^{\natural}
(resp.,
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath+}(n,r)^{\natural}
).

Proof.

If
n\geq r
, then, for any
J\subseteq S
, there exists
{\lambda}\in{\Lambda}(n+1,r)
such that
W_{\lambda}=W_{J}
(e.g.,
W_{(1^{r},0)}=W_{\emptyset}
for
n=r
). Thus,

{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)^{\natural}=A^{{\dagger}\natural}
with a suitable choice of
m_{J},J\subseteq S
, as seen in Remark
5.4.1
.
Thus, in this case,
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)^{\natural}
is a centraliser subalgebra of some
A^{{\ddagger}\natural}
defined in display (
2.4.7
)) with base change to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\natural}
. Here the associated finite standard system
(W,S,L)
is
W=W_{r}
and
L=\ell
, the usual length function. In this case, we choose
{\mathcal{S}}^{{\jmath}+}(n,r)^{\natural}=A^{{\ddagger}\natural}
. Now, the split quasi-heredity of
A^{{\ddagger}\natural}
follows from Theorem
4.3.8
in our Chapter 4.

If
n<r
, let

\Theta=\Theta_{n,r}=\{\omega\in\Omega\mid\omega\leq_{L}\omega_{\lambda},\text{ for some }\lambda\in\Lambda(n+1,r)\},

where
\omega_{\lambda}
is the left cell that contains the longest word of
W_{\lambda}
. Then,
\Theta
is a poset ideal of
(\Omega,\leq_{L})
and
A^{{\ddagger}\natural}_{\Theta}
is a split quasi-hereditary algebra by Theorem
4.3.8
. For a suitable choice
\{m_{\omega}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\mid\omega\in\Theta\}
,

{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)^{\natural}
is a centralizer subalgebra of
A^{{\ddagger}\natural}_{\Theta}
. We then choose
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath+}(n,r)^{\natural}:=A^{{\ddagger}\natural}_{\Theta}
as the desired algebra.

By Remark
5.4.1
, the
\imath
case may be proved similarly as in the
n<r
case above.
∎

By Corollary
5.1.6
and Lemma
5.1.7
and noting the remark right above Lemma
5.4.2
, we immediately have:

Theorem 5.4.4
.

The algebras
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)^{\natural}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)^{\natural}
are standardly based algebras. In particular, their decomposition matrices are unitriangular.

5.4B
i
-Quantum groups and the associated Schur–Weyl duality.

We now introduce the
i
-quantum groups arising from the study of quantum symmetric pairs; see
[
Le03
]
,
[
BW18
]
and the references therein.

Symmetric pairs refer to pairs of the form
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{\theta})
or
(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}),\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}))
, where
\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie algebra with its enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
,
\theta:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
is an involution, and
\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}
is the fixed-point subalgebra. Note that
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}^{\theta})
is a Hopf subalgebra of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
.

For example, if
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
with basis consisting of matrix units
\{e_{i,j}\}_{1\leq,i,j\leq N}
, and

\theta:\mathfrak{gl}_{N}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{N},\;\;\;e_{i,j}\longmapsto e_{\tau(i),\tau(j)},

where
\tau(i)=N+1-i
, then (see, e.g.,
[
LZ
, 2.4.2, 3.5.1]
)

\mathfrak{gl}_{N}^{\theta}\cong\begin{cases}\mathfrak{gl}_{n+1}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{n},\;\text{ if }N=2n+1\\
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{n},\;\text{ if }N=2n.\end{cases}

Thus,

(5.4.7)

\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}^{\theta})\cong\begin{cases}\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n+1})\otimes\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}),\;\text{ if }N=2n+1\\
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}),\;\text{ if }N=2n.\end{cases}

Note that this involution
\theta
is different from the differentiation
\gamma
of
\vartheta
in (
1.1.3
). Here, for
*\in\{\jmath,\imath\}
,

\gamma:\mathfrak{gl}_{N}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{N},\;\;x\mapsto-J_{*}x^{t}J_{*}
,
with

J_{\jmath}=J_{2n+1},J_{\imath}=J_{2n}^{-}
being given in (
1.1.4
), is used to define
\mathfrak{o}_{2n+1}
and
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
.

We now define the quantum analogs of
(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}),\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}^{\theta}))
.

Definition 5.4.5
.

The quantum enveloping algebra
{\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})

over
\mathbb{Q}({t})
is generated by

E_{a},F_{a},K_{i}^{\pm 1},\;1\leq a,i\leq N,a\neq N.

These elements are subject to the following relations:

(QG1)

K_{i}K_{j}=K_{j}K_{i},\ K_{i}K_{i}^{-1}=K_{i}^{-1}K_{i}=1;

(QG2)

K_{i}E_{a}={t}^{\delta_{i,a}-\delta_{i,a+1}}E_{a}K_{i},\ K_{i}F_{a}={t}^{-\delta_{i,a}+\delta_{i,a+1}}F_{a}K_{i};

(QG3)

[E_{a},F_{b}]=\delta_{a,b}\frac{{\widetilde{K}}_{a}-{\widetilde{K}}_{a}^{-1}}{{t}-{t}^{-1}},
where
{\widetilde{K}}_{a}=K_{a}K_{a+1}^{-1}
;

(QG4)

E_{a}E_{b}=E_{b}E_{a},\ F_{a}F_{b}=F_{b}F_{a},
if
|a-b|>1;
and

(QG5)

E_{a}^{2}E_{b}-({t}+{t}^{-1})E_{a}E_{b}E_{a}+E_{b}E_{a}^{2}=0,F_{a}^{2}F_{b}-({t}+{t}^{-1})F_{a}F_{b}F_{a}+F_{b}F_{a}^{2}=0
, if
|a-b|=1
.

A Hopf algebra structure on
{\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
is
defined (see
[
Jan96
, p. 199]
) by:

(5.4.8)

\displaystyle\Delta(K_{i})=K_{i}\otimes K_{i},

\displaystyle\Delta(E_{a})=E_{a}\otimes{\widetilde{K}}_{a}^{-1}+1\otimes E_{a},\quad\Delta(F_{a})=F_{a}\otimes 1+{\widetilde{K}}_{a}\otimes F_{a},

\displaystyle\varepsilon(K_{i})=1,\quad\varepsilon(E_{a})=\varepsilon(F_{a})=0,

\displaystyle S(K_{i})=K_{i}^{-1},\quad S(E_{a})=-E_{a}{\widetilde{K}}_{a},\quad S(F_{a})=-{\widetilde{K}}_{a}^{-1}F_{a}.

The quantum
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
is the Hopf subalgebra of
{\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})

{\mathbf{U}}_{N}={\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})=\langle E_{i},F_{i},\widetilde{K}_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq N-1\rangle.

We now follow
[
BW18
]
to introduce two subalgebras in the cases
N=2n+1
and
N=2n
via the corresponding graph automorphism of the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
. For this purpose, we label the vertices of the Dynkin diagrams in a more symmetric fashion, i.e., simple roots are labeled symmetrically about 0. See
[
BW18
, (1.1)]
.

For integers
m<n
, let

\displaystyle[m,n]=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}\mid m\leq i\leq n\},

\displaystyle[m,n]_{\frac{1}{2}}=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\mid m\leq i\leq n\}.

Note that
[m,n]
(resp.,
[m,n]_{\frac{1}{2}}
) consists of all endpoints (resp., midpoints) of intervals
[i,i+1]
for
m\leq i<n
.

Let
V_{N}
be the natural representation of
{\mathbf{U}}_{N}={\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
. Following the notation used in
[
BW18
, Chs. 2&6]
, we now relabel the generators of
{\mathbf{U}}_{N}
by simple roots associated with
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
.

For
N=2n+1
, we label the basis for
V_{2n+1}
by

(5.4.9)

v_{-n},\ldots,v_{-2},v_{-1},v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n}

and the corresponding simple roots

\alpha_{i}=\varepsilon_{i-\frac{1}{2}}-\varepsilon_{i+\frac{1}{2}}
by all
i\in[-n,n]_{\frac{1}{2}}
. Here,
\varepsilon_{j},j\in[-n,n]
are the fundamental weights for
\mathfrak{sl}_{2n+1}
.

For
N=2n
, we label the basis for
V_{2n}
by

(5.4.10)

v_{-n+\frac{1}{2}},v_{-n+\frac{1}{2}},\ldots,v_{-\frac{1}{2}},v_{\frac{1}{2}},v_{1+\frac{1}{2}},\ldots,v_{n-\frac{1}{2}}

and the corresponding simple roots
\alpha_{i}=\varepsilon_{i-\frac{1}{2}}-\varepsilon_{i+\frac{1}{2}}
by all
i\in[-n+1,n-1]
.

Definition 5.4.6
.

(1) The
\mathbb{Q}(t)
-subalgebra
{\mathbf{U}}^{\jmath}_{n}
of
{\mathbf{U}}_{2n+1}
is generated by

{\bf k}_{i}={\widetilde{K}}_{i}{\widetilde{K}}_{-i}^{-1},\;\;{\bf e}_{i}=E_{i}+{\widetilde{K}}_{i}^{-1}F_{-i},\;\;{\bf f}_{i}=F_{i}{\widetilde{K}}_{-i}^{-1}+E_{-i},\;\;\forall i\in[0,n]_{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2) The
\mathbb{Q}(t)
-subalgebra
{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{n}
of
{\mathbf{U}}_{2n}
is generated by, for
1\leq i\leq n-1
,

{\bf k}_{i}={\widetilde{K}}_{i}{\widetilde{K}}_{-i}^{-1},\;\;{\bf e}_{i}=E_{i}+{\widetilde{K}}_{i}^{-1}F_{-i},\;\;{\bf f}_{i}=F_{i}{\widetilde{K}}_{-i}^{-1}+E_{-i},\;\;{\bf t}=E_{0}+{t}F_{0}{\widetilde{K}}_{0}^{-1}+{\widetilde{K}}_{0}^{-1}.

Remark 5.4.7
.

Note that
{\mathbf{U}}^{\jmath}_{n},{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{n}
are not Hopf subalgebras, but coideal subalgebras, and

({\mathbf{U}}_{2n+1},{\mathbf{U}}^{\jmath}_{n})
,
({\mathbf{U}}_{2n},{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{n})
form some quantum symmetric pairs,
16
16

16

{\mathbf{U}}^{\jmath}_{n}
,
{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{n}
are denoted
{\mathbf{U}}^{\jmath},{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
, respectively, in
[
BW18
]
.
which are the quantum analogs of the symmetric pairs
(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}),\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}^{\theta}))
with
N=2n+1,2n
. Thus, they are the quantum analog of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}^{\theta})
,
N\in\{2n+1,2n\}
, and will be called
i
-
quantum group
.

Note that, by (
5.4.7
), there is another quantization of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}^{\theta})
as a tensor product of two quantum
\mathfrak{gl}_{m}
which cannot be embedded into
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
.

We summarize the two cases in the following table:

{\mathbf{U}}_{N}={\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})

Basis for
V_{N}

Simple roots

i
-Quanutm groups

N=2n+1

v_{i},i\in[-n,n]

\alpha_{i},i\in[-n,n]_{\frac{1}{2}}

{\mathbf{U}}^{\jmath}_{n}\subset{\mathbf{U}}_{2n+1}

N=2n

v_{i},i\in[-n,n]_{\frac{1}{2}}

\alpha_{i},i\in[-n+1,n-1]

{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{n}\subset{\mathbf{U}}_{2n}

For
*\in\{{\jmath},{\imath}\}
, let
\epsilon_{*}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if }*={\jmath}\\
0,&\text{ if }*={\imath}\end{cases}

and let

(5.4.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{\jmath},r)

\displaystyle=\{(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r})\mid i_{j}\in[-n,n]\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{\imath},r)

\displaystyle=\{(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r})\mid i_{j}\in[-n,n]_{\frac{1}{2}}\}.

Both sets index bases for the
tensor spaces

V_{2n+1}^{\otimes r}
and
V_{2n}^{\otimes r}
, respectively.

The new symmetric notation above gives rise to an adjustment of the associated Coxeter system
(W_{0},S_{0})
with
S_{0}=\{s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{r-1}\}
. Here, we relabel the generators
s_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq r)
considered in Subsection 5.4A by
s_{r-i},1\leq i\leq r
. Thus, the Dynkin diagram of type
B_{r}
takes the form

(5.4.12)

0
1
2
3
r-1

Note that this labeling reverses the labeling in Examples
1.1.2
and
1.1.3
.

Let

(5.4.13)

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{H}}_{{t}^{-1}}(W_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}

be the Hecke algebras over
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\mathbb{Q}(t)
, respectively (relative to parameter
t^{-1}
) of the standard finite Coxeter system
(W_{0},S_{0},L)
with constant weight function
L(s_{i})=1
. Then,
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
is generated by
T_{s_{0}},T_{s_{1}},\ldots,T_{s_{r-1}}
. The subalgebra
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}({\mathfrak{S}}_{r})
generated by
T_{s_{1}},\ldots,T_{s_{r-1}}
is the Hecke algebra of the symmetric group
{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}
.

The
{\mathbf{U}}_{2n+\epsilon_{*}}
-module
V_{2n+\epsilon_{*}}^{\otimes r}
commutes with the following action by
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}({\mathfrak{S}}_{r})
on the basis

v_{\underline{i}}:=v_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{i_{r}}
, for
{\underline{i}}\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{*},r):

(5.4.14)

v_{\underline{i}}T_{s_{j}}=\begin{cases}v_{\underline{i}s_{j}},&\text{ if }i_{j}<i_{j+1};\\
{t}^{-2}v_{\underline{i}},&\text{ if }i_{j}=i_{j+1};\\
({t}^{-2}-1)v_{\underline{i}}+{t}^{-2}v_{\underline{i}s_{j}},&\text{ if }i_{j}>i_{j+1}.\end{cases}

The
{\mathbf{U}}_{2n+\epsilon_{*}}
-
{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{H}}}}^{\prime}({\mathfrak{S}}_{r})
-bimodule
V_{2n+\epsilon_{*}}^{\otimes r}
satisfies the double centralizer property—the quantum Schur–Weyl duality.

We extend the action to the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
of
W_{0}
. We first do it integrally.
Consider the free
{\mathcal{Z}}
-submodule of
V_{2n+\epsilon_{*}}^{\otimes r}
spanned by all
v_{\underline{i}}:=v_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{i_{r}}
:

(5.4.15)

T(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)=\text{span}_{\mathcal{Z}}\{v_{\underline{i}}\mid{\underline{i}}\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)\}.

This is clearly a right
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}({\mathfrak{S}}_{r})
-module with the above action in (
5.4.14
).
Extend this action to
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
by setting (cf.
[
BW18
, (6.8), (5.2)]
)

(5.4.16)

v_{\underline{i}}T_{s_{0}}=\begin{cases}v_{\underline{i}s_{0}},&\text{ if }i_{1}>0;\\
{t}^{-2}v_{\underline{i}},&\text{ if }i_{1}=0;\\
({t}^{-2}-1)v_{\underline{i}}+{t}^{-2}v_{\underline{i}s_{0}},&\text{ if }i_{1}<0.\end{cases}\quad\text{ for }N=2n+1

(5.4.17)

v_{\underline{i}}T_{s_{0}}=\begin{cases}v_{\underline{i}s_{0}},&\text{ if }i_{1}>0;\\
({t}^{-2}-1)v_{\underline{i}}+{t}^{-2}v_{\underline{i}s_{0}},&\text{ if }i_{1}<0.\end{cases}\quad\text{ for }N=2n.

For
{\lambda}\in{\Lambda}_{*}(n,r)
, we will also relabel the components as
{\lambda}=({\lambda}_{0},{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,{\lambda}_{n})
, if
{\lambda}\in{\Lambda}_{\jmath}(n,r)={\Lambda}(n+1,r)
, and the generators for the parabolic subgroup
W_{0,{\lambda}}
of
W_{0}
. See Lemma
5.4.10
below.
Let
{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda}
be the shortest representatives of the right cosets of
W_{0,{\lambda}}
in
W_{0}
. It is easily checked that, for
{\underline{i}}\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)
, if
{\underline{i}}={\underline{i}}_{\lambda}d
for some
d\in{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda}
, then

v_{\underline{i}}=v_{{\underline{i}}_{\lambda}}T_{d}
.

Note that, putting
e_{\underline{i}}=v_{\underline{i}_{\lambda}}{\widetilde{T}}_{d}
, where
{\widetilde{T}}_{d}=t^{\ell(d)}T_{d}
, then the induced action from (
5.4.14
) and
(
5.4.16
) (resp., (
5.4.17
)) coincides with the action in
[
BW18
, (6.8)]
(resp.,
[
BW18
, (5.2)]
).

For
*\in\{{\jmath},{\imath}\}
, define
i
-quantum Schur algebras
over
{\mathcal{Z}}
:

U^{*}_{n,r}:=\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\big(T(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)\big),\qquad{\mathbf{U}}_{n,r}^{*}:=\text{\rm{End}}_{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\prime}}(V_{2n+\epsilon_{*}}^{\otimes r}).

The following theorem gives a new Schur–Weyl duality between
{\mathbf{U}}^{*}_{n}
and
{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{H}}}}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{Q}(t)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
for
*\in\{\jmath,\imath\}
.

Theorem 5.4.8

(
[
BW18
, Ths. 5.4&6.27]
)
.

Maintaining the notation above and letting
*\in\{\jmath,\imath\}
, we have:

(1)

The restricted
{\mathbf{U}}^{*}_{n}
-action on
V_{2n+\epsilon_{*}}^{\otimes r}
commutes with the
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\prime}
-action.

(2)

The
{\mathbf{U}}^{*}_{n}
-
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\prime}
-bimodule
V_{2n+\epsilon_{*}}^{\otimes r}
satisfies the double centralizer property. Hence, there is a Schur–Weyl duality over
\mathbb{Q}({t})
in each of the choices of
*
.

(3)

In particular, there is an algebra epimorphism over
\mathbb{Q}({t})
:

\phi_{*}:{\mathbf{U}}^{*}_{n}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}^{*}_{n,r}:=\text{\rm{End}}_{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\prime}}(V_{2n+\epsilon_{*}}^{\otimes r}).

Remarks 5.4.9
.

(1) We will see below that
U^{\jmath}_{n,r}
and
U^{\imath}_{n,r}
are
isomorphic to
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}_{t^{-1},{\mathcal{Z}}}(n,r)
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}_{t^{-1},{\mathcal{Z}}}(n,r)
, respectively. Hence, they are
Hecke endo-algebras associated with a finite orthogonal group
{\text{\rm O}}_{2r+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
or a finite symplectic group
{\text{\rm Sp}}_{2r}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

(2) By (
5.4.7
), there is another quantum analog of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}^{\theta})
, which is a tensor product of some quantum
\mathfrak{gl}_{m}
. The quantum Schur–Weyl duality relative to these quantum analogs and Hecke algebra of type
B
is discussed in
[
SS99
]
and
[
Hu01
]
.

5.4C Place permutations and the tensor space decomposition.

We now establish isomorphism between the Hecke endo-algebras
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)
introduced in Subsection 5.4A and
U^{\jmath}_{n,r}
and
U^{\imath}_{n,r}
introduced in Subsection 5.4B. The isomorphisms are largely known;
see, e.g.,
[
LL21
,
LNX20
,
LW22
]
. We include a proof using place permutations for completion.

We may regard an element
\underline{i}\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{\jmath},r)
as a function

{\underline{i}}:[-r,r]\longrightarrow[-n,n],\;\;k\longmapsto i_{k}\text{ with }i_{-k}=\begin{cases}-i_{k},&\text{ if }i_{k}\neq 0\\
0,&\text{ if }i_{k}=0,\end{cases}

and, similarly, regard an element
\underline{i}\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{\imath},r)
as a function

{\underline{i}}:[-r,-1]\cup[1,r]\longrightarrow[-n,n]_{\frac{1}{2}},\;\;k\longmapsto i_{k}\text{ with }i_{-k}=-i_{k}.

We now introduce
W_{0}
-actions on the sets
\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)
, for
*\in\{\jmath,\imath\}
. Recall from (
5.4.12
) that
W_{0}
is the Weyl group of type
B_{r}
with Coxeter generators
S_{0}=\{s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{r-1}\}
, where
S_{0}\backslash\{s_{0}\}
generates a parabolic subgroup isomorphic to the symmetric group
{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}
.
Note that we have relabeled the Coxeter generators
s_{i},1\leq i\leq r
in Subsection 5.4A by
s_{r-i},1\leq i\leq r
. By regarding
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2r+1}
as the permutation group
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[-r,r]}
of the set
[-r,r]
,
W_{0}
is the subgroup of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[-r,r]}
(resp.,
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[-r,r]\backslash\{0\}}
, regarded as a subgroup of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[-r,r]}
) consisting of permutations

(5.4.18)

\begin{pmatrix}-r&\cdots&-2&-1&0&1&2&\cdots&r\\
i_{-r}&\cdots&i_{-2}&i_{-1}&0&i_{1}&i_{2}&\cdots&i_{r}\end{pmatrix}

satisfying
i_{-j}=-i_{j}
, for all
j\in[1,r]
(cf. (
5.4.3
)).
Hence,
W_{0}
is generated by

s_{0}=(-1,0)(0,1)(-1,0)=(-1,1),\;\;s_{i}=(i,i+1)(-i,-i-1),\;\;1\leq i\leq r-1.

Since every element
w
in
W_{0}
satisfies
w(-j)=-w(j)
, for
j\in[-r,r]\backslash\{0\}
and
w(0)=0
, the following action, defined by composition of functions, is well-defined

{\underline{i}}.w=(i_{w(1)},i_{w(2)},\ldots,i_{w(r)}),

for all
{\underline{i}}\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)
. This action generalizes the well-known
place permutation
from
{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}
to the Weyl group
W_{0}
.
So,
W_{0}
acts on
\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)
.

For
\underline{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r})\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{\jmath},r)
(here
*=\jmath
), let

\lambda_{k}=\#\{i_{j}\mid 1\leq j\leq r,k=|i_{j}|\},\text{ for all }k\in[0,n]

and define

\text{wt}(\underline{i})={\lambda}:=({\lambda}_{0},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
.

For
\underline{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r})\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{\imath},r)
(here
*=\imath
), by (
5.4.11
),
|i_{k}|+\frac{1}{2}\in[1,n]
, for all
k\in[1,r]
. Let

\lambda_{k}=\#\{i_{j}\mid 1\leq j\leq r,k=|i_{j}|+\frac{1}{2}\},\text{ for all }k\in[1,n]

and define

\text{wt}(\underline{i})={\lambda}:=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
.

Thus, for
*\in\{{\jmath},{\imath}\}
, the sets
\Lambda_{*}(n,r)
in (
5.4.4
) have the following interpretation

\Lambda_{*}(n,r)=\{\text{wt}(\underline{i})\mid\underline{i}\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)\}.

For
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{\jmath}(n,r)
, let

{\underline{i_{\lambda}}}=(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{{\lambda}_{0}},\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{{\lambda}_{1}},\ldots,\underbrace{n,\ldots,n}_{{\lambda}_{n}})

and, for
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{\imath}(n,r)
, let

{\underline{i}_{\lambda}}=(\underbrace{1_{\frac{1}{2}},\ldots,1_{\frac{1}{2}}}_{{\lambda}_{1}},\underbrace{2_{\frac{1}{2}},\ldots,2_{\frac{1}{2}}}_{{\lambda}_{2}},\ldots,\underbrace{n_{\frac{1}{2}},\ldots,n_{\frac{1}{2}}}_{{\lambda}_{n}}),

where
a_{\frac{1}{2}}=a-{\frac{1}{2}}
, for all
a\in[1,n]
.

Lemma 5.4.10
.

For
*\in\{{\jmath},{\imath}\}
and
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{*}(n,r)
, the orbit

{\underline{i_{\lambda}}}.W_{0}=\{{\underline{i}}\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{*},r),\operatorname{wt}({\underline{i}})={\lambda}\},

and
{\rm Stab}_{W_{0}}({\underline{i_{\lambda}}})=W_{0,{\lambda}}
is the subgroup generated by

(1)

S\backslash\{s_{{\lambda}_{0}+\cdots+{\lambda}_{i}}\mid 0\leq i<n\}
, if
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{\jmath}(n,r)
and

(2)

S\backslash\{s_{0},s_{{\lambda}_{1}+\cdots+{\lambda}_{i}}\mid 1\leq i<n\}
, if
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{\imath}(n,r)
.

Proof.

We first assume
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{\jmath}(n,r)
.
Consider the place permutation of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[-r,r]}
on

I(2n+1,2r+1)=\{(i_{-r},\ldots,i_{-1},i_{0},i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\mid i_{j}\in[-n,n],\;\forall j\in[-r,r]\}.

For
{\underline{i}}=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r})\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{\jmath},r)
,
let

-{\underline{i}}^{\tau}\circ{\underline{i}}:=(i_{-r},\ldots,i_{-2},i_{-1},0,i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r}),

where
i_{0}=0
.
(Recall
i_{-j}=-i_{j}
.) By regarding
W_{0}
as a subgroup of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[-r,r]}
via (
5.4.18
), it is clear that

{\rm Stab}_{W_{0}}({\underline{i}})=W_{0}\cap{\rm Stab}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{[-r,r]}}(-{\underline{i}}^{\tau}\circ{\underline{i}})
. In particular,

{\rm Stab}_{W_{0}}({\underline{i_{\lambda}}})=W_{0}\cap{\rm Stab}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{[-r,r]}}(-{\underline{i_{\lambda}}}^{\tau}\circ{\underline{i_{\lambda}}})
.
Since

{\rm Stab}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{[-r,r]}}(-{\underline{i_{\lambda}}}^{\tau}\circ{\underline{i_{\lambda}}})={\mathfrak{S}}_{\widetilde{\lambda}}\cong{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\lambda}_{n}}\times\cdots\times{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\lambda}_{1}}\times{\mathfrak{S}}_{2{\lambda}_{0}+1}\times{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\lambda}_{1}}\times\ldots\times{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\lambda}_{n}},

it follows that
{\rm Stab}_{W_{0}}({\underline{i_{\lambda}}})=W_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{S}}_{\widetilde{\lambda}}=W_{0,{\lambda}}
.

The orbit equality can be seen as follows. First, for any
{\underline{i}}=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r})\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{\jmath},r)
with
\operatorname{wt}({\underline{i}})={\lambda}
, there is
x\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}
such that
\underline{i_{\lambda}}x=(|i_{1}|,|i_{2}|,\ldots,|i_{n}|)
. Now, assume
i_{j_{1}},\ldots,i_{j_{t}}
are negative, then

(|i_{1}|,|i_{2}|,\ldots,|i_{n}|)t_{j_{1}},\ldots,t_{j_{t}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
, where
t_{i}=s_{i-1}\cdots s_{1}s_{0}s_{1}\cdots s_{i-1}
is the permutation swapping
i
and
-i
and fixing others.
Hence,
\underline{i_{\lambda}}xt_{j_{1}},\ldots,t_{j_{t}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
.

The case for
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{\imath}(n,r)
is similar.
∎

We now consider the
{\mathcal{Z}}
-submodules of
T(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)
defined in (
5.4.15
):

\displaystyle T(2n+\epsilon_{\jmath},r)_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\text{span}_{\mathcal{Z}}\{v_{\underline{i}}\mid{\underline{i}}\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{\jmath},r),\operatorname{wt}({\underline{i}})={\lambda}\}=\text{span}_{\mathcal{Z}}\{v_{\underline{i}_{\lambda}d}\mid d\in{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda}\},

\displaystyle T(2n+\epsilon_{\imath},r)_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\text{span}_{\mathcal{Z}}\{v_{\underline{i}}\mid{\underline{i}}\in\mathbb{I}(2n+\epsilon_{\imath},r),\operatorname{wt}({\underline{i}})={\lambda}\}=\text{span}_{\mathcal{Z}}\{v_{\underline{i}_{\lambda}d}\mid d\in{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda}\}.

Recall from (
5.4.13
) that
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{H}}_{{t}^{-1}}(B_{r})
.

Lemma 5.4.11
.

For
*\in\{\jmath,\imath\}
and any
{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{*}(n,r)
,
T(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)_{\lambda}
is an
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
-submodule of
T(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)
and, as right
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
-modules,

T(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)_{\lambda}\cong x_{\lambda}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\text{ where }x_{\lambda}=\sum_{w\in W_{\lambda}}T_{w}.

Thus, we have an algebra isomorphism

U^{*}_{n,r}\cong\text{\rm{End}}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\Big(\bigoplus_{{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{*}(n,r)}x_{\lambda}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\Big)={\mathcal{S}}^{*}_{t^{-1},{\mathcal{Z}}}(n,r).

Proof.

By definition,
\mathcal{Z}v_{\underline{i_{\lambda}}}
is an
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
-module isomorphic to
\mathcal{Z}x_{\lambda}
, where
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is the subalgebra of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
associated with the parabolic subgroup
W_{0,\lambda}
.
By the Frobenius reciprocity, we have an isomorphism

\text{Hom}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}(x_{\lambda}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},T(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)_{\lambda})\cong\text{Hom}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{\lambda}}(\mathcal{Z}x_{\lambda},T(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)_{\lambda}).

Thus, the
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
-module homomorphism
\mathcal{Z}x_{\lambda}\to T(2n+\epsilon_{*},r),x_{\lambda}\mapsto v_{\underline{i_{\lambda}}}
induces an
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
-module isomorphism from
x_{\lambda}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
to
T(2n+\epsilon_{*},r)_{\lambda}
.
∎

Remarks 5.4.12
.

For the
*=\imath
case, this isomorphism is also given in
[
Grn97
, Prop. 4.1.4]
. As noted in Remark
5.4.1
,
Morita equivalent versions of
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)
are considered in
[
DS00a
]
.

5.4D.
i
-quantum groups and finite orthogonal/symplectic groups.

In order to link

The following definition is taken from
[

.

Definition 5.4.13
.

(1)
The algebra
{{\mathbf{U}}}^{\jmath}(n)

\mathbb{Q}({t})
generated by
e_{i}
,
f_{i}
,
d_{a}
,

, together with the following additional relations:

(iQG1)

d_{n+1}e_{j}d_{n+1}^{-1}=v^{-2\delta_{n,j}}e_{j},

d_{n+1}f_{j}d_{n+1}^{-1}=v^{2\delta_{n,j}}f_{j}
,
j\in[1,n]
;

(iQG2)

f_{n}^{2}e_{n}+e_{n}f_{n}^{2}=[2]\big(f_{n}e_{n}f_{n}-(vd_{n}d^{-1}_{n+1}+v^{-1}d_{n}^{-1}d_{n+1})f_{n}\big)
; and

(iQG3)

e_{n}^{2}f_{n}+f_{n}e_{n}^{2}=[2]\big(e_{n}f_{n}e_{n}-e_{n}(vd_{n}d^{-1}_{n+1}+v^{-1}d_{n}^{-1}d_{n+1})\big).

(2) The algebra
{{\mathbf{U}}}^{\imath}(n)
is defined to be the associative algebra over
\mathbb{Q}({t})
generated by
e_{i}
,
f_{i}
,
d_{a}
,

{\mathfrak{t}}

,

,

replacing
E_{i}
,
F_{i}
,
K_{a}
,

and

together with the following relations involving

(iQG1
′
)

{\mathfrak{t}}d_{a}=d_{a}{\mathfrak{t}}
,

for

(iQG2
′
)

{\mathfrak{t}}^{2}e_{n-1}+e_{n-1}{\mathfrak{t}}^{2}=[2]{\mathfrak{t}}e_{n-1}{\mathfrak{t}}+e_{n-1}
,
e^{2}_{n-1}{\mathfrak{t}}+{\mathfrak{t}}e^{2}_{n-1}=[2]e_{n-1}{\mathfrak{t}}e_{n-1}
; and

(iQG3
′
)

{\mathfrak{t}}^{2}f_{n-1}+f_{n-1}{\mathfrak{t}}^{2}=[2]{\mathfrak{t}}f_{n-1}{\mathfrak{t}}+f_{n-1}
,
f^{2}_{n-1}{\mathfrak{t}}+{\mathfrak{t}}f^{2}_{n-1}=[2]f_{n-1}{\mathfrak{t}}f_{n-1}
.

Like the embedding
{\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
into
{\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
, we have the various algebra embeddings.

Proposition 5.4.14
.

The following are
\mathbb{Q}({t})
-algebra monomorphisms:

\eta^{\jmath}:{\mathbf{U}}^{\jmath}_{n}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbf{U}}^{\jmath}(n),\;\mathbf{e}_{n-i+\frac{1}{2}}{\longmapsto}e_{i},\mathbf{f}_{n-i+\frac{1}{2}}{\longmapsto}f_{i},\mathbf{k}_{n-i+\frac{1}{2}}{\longmapsto}d_{i}d_{i+1}^{-1},\mathbf{k}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\longmapsto}{t}^{-1}d_{n}d_{n+1}^{-1},

for all

(see

, Remark 4.3]
), and

\eta^{\imath}:{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{n}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}(n),\;\mathbf{e}_{n-h}{\longmapsto}e_{h},\mathbf{f}_{n-h}{\longmapsto}f_{h},\mathbf{k}_{n-h}{\longmapsto}d_{h}d_{h+1}^{-1},\mathbf{t}{\longmapsto}{\mathfrak{t}},

for all
