.

Theorem
5.4.8
continues to hold for these enlarged algebras.
17
17

17

After an index shift similar to the one in Proposition
5.4.14
and an adjustment on the coalgebra structure on
{\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
, the parameters for
{\mathbf{U}}^{*}(n)
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(n,r)_{\mathbb{Q}({t})}
can be made the same
{t}
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{\jmath}(n,r)={\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
and
{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{\imath}(n,r)={\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)_{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
.
Thus, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 5.4.15
.

The category
{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{\jmath}(n,r)
-
mod
of finite-dimensional
{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{\jmath}(n,r)
-modules is a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{U}}^{\jmath}(n)
-mod; while
the category
{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{\imath}(n,r)
-mod of finite-dimensional
{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{\imath}(n,r)
-modules is a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}(n)
-
mod
.

For certain classes of classical weight modules of
{\mathbf{U}}^{*}(n)
over
\mathbb{Q}({t})
and their crystal bases, see
[
W1
,
W2
]
.

In order to link representations of finite orthogonal/symplectic groups in cross characteristics, we need to consider
i
-quantum hyperalgebras. This is done in
[
DW22
,
DW23
]
.

Let

\left[\begin{matrix}d_{i}\\
s\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}d_{i}\\
s\end{matrix}\right]_{t}=\prod_{i=1}^{s}\frac{d_{i}{t}^{-i+1}-d_{i}^{-1}{t}^{i-1)}}{{t}^{i}-{t}^{-i}}\;(s\geq 1),\;\text{ and }\;\left[\begin{matrix}d_{i}\\
0\end{matrix}\right]=1.

Let
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\jmath}(n)
be the
{\mathcal{Z}}
-subalgebra of
{\mathbf{U}}^{\jmath}(n)
generated by

{d}_{i},\left[\begin{matrix}{d}_{i}\\
s\end{matrix}\right],\;{d}_{n+1},\left[\begin{matrix}{d}_{n+1}\\
s\end{matrix}\right]_{t^{2}},\quad{e}_{i}^{(m)},{f}_{i}^{(m)}\;(1\leq i\leq n,s,m\in\mathbb{N}).

See
[
DW22
]
. The definition of an integral form
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\imath}(n)
for
{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}(n)
is a bit tricky since the
{\mathcal{Z}}
-subalgebra generated by

{d}_{i},\left[\begin{matrix}{d}_{i}\\
s\end{matrix}\right],\;\;{e}_{h}^{(m)},{f}_{h}^{(m)},\;{\mathfrak{t}}^{(m)}\;\;(1\leq i\leq n,1\leq h\leq n-1,s,m\in\mathbb{N})

does not map onto
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)
. In fact, replacing all
{\mathfrak{t}}^{(m)}
by the image of
{}^{(m)}g:=(mE_{n,n+1}^{\theta})(\mathbf{0})
via a new realization of
{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}(n)
can fix the problem. See

[
DW23
]
for more details.

For any field
k
and a specialisation
{\mathcal{Z}}\to k
, the base-changed algebras
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\jmath}(n)_{k}
and
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\imath}(n)_{k}
are called
i
-quantum hyperalgebras
.

By Theorem
5.4.8
, Lemma
5.4.11
, and results from
[
DW22
]
, and
[
DW23
]
, we have the following.

Theorem 5.4.16
.

(1) There is an algebra epimorphism
\eta_{r}^{\jmath}:U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\jmath}(n)\to{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)
, for all
r\geq 0
. Thus, for any base change from
{\mathcal{Z}}
to any field
k
, it induces a surjective map. Hence, the category
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)_{k}
-
mod
of finite-dimensional
{\mathcal{S}}^{\jmath}(n,r)_{k}
-modules is a full subcategory of
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\jmath}(n)_{k}
-
mod
.

(2) Similarly, with
\imath
replacing
\jmath
, we obtain that
the category
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)_{k}
-
mod
of finite-dimensional
{\mathcal{S}}^{\imath}(n,r)_{k}
-modules is a full subcategory of
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\imath}(n)_{k}
-
mod
.

Since the
q
-Schur algebras of type
B
or
C
are not necessarily quasi-hereditary, it is unlikely that the representation categories of these
i
-quantum hyperalgebras are highest weight categories.
However, since the decomposition matrices of
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(n,r)
, for
*\in\{{\jmath},{\imath}\}
, are unitriangular by Theorem
5.4.4
, it
seems to indicate that the representation category of
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\jmath}(n)_{k}
or
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\imath}(n)_{k}
has a sort of connection to a highest weight category.

Remarks 5.4.17
.

(1) Lai, Nakano and Xiang considered the representation theory of
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(n,r)_{k}
over a
field

k
. They used a coordinate algebra approach, giving a natural polynomial representation theory as discussed in
[
Gr80
]
. In particular, under a certain invertibility condition, it has been shown in
[
LNX20
, Cors. 5.2.1&6.1.1)]
that these algebras are cellular and quasi-hereditary.

(2) Theorem
5.4.16
provides a method for investigating the representation theory of
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{*}(n)_{k}
(
*\in\{\jmath,\imath\}
) arising from those of
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(n,r)_{k}
. A representation of
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{*}(n)_{k}
obtained by inflating a representation of
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(n,r)_{k}
is also called a
polynomial representation
of
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{*}(n)_{k}
. The theorem establishes a link between polynomial representations of
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{*}(n)_{k}
and representations of finite orthogonal/symplectic groups. The first step toward understanding the
modular
representation theory of
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{*}(n)_{k}
would be the development of the polynomial representation theory.

5.5.
Open problems.

We formulate several open problems inspired by this monograph. We first recall some notation.

Let
G
be a finite group of Lie type with standard finite Coxeter system
(W,S,L)
. We maintain the notation of Section 5.2 (and so of Section 4.1, too). Moreover,
let
(K,{\mathcal{O}},k)
be a triple as in Section 5.0 with
{\mathcal{O}}
sufficiently large
to define the decomposition matrices

D=D_{\mathcal{O}}
and
D^{+}=D^{+}_{\mathcal{O}}
of
A_{\mathcal{O}}
and
A^{+}_{\mathcal{O}}
, respectively.
Recall that
r
is the characteristic of
k
, and we consider representations of
kG
in the cases where
r
does not equal the defining characteristic
p
of
{\mathbb{G}}
. We also assume in this section that
r
is good.
Using Theorem
5.2.2
(c), we see that the rank of
D
is the number of modular irreducible
A_{k}
-modules.

Problem 1.
What is the rank of
D
in terms of
r
and
W
?

The rank of
D
always satisfies

\text{\rm rank}(D)\leq\text{\rm rank}(D^{+})=|\text{\rm Irr}(\mathbb{Q}W)|,

by Theorem
5.2.2
(b),(d).
When equality holds, it also follows that
D=D^{+}
. In particular,
D^{+}
is a submatrix of the
decomposition matrix of
G
in this case, at least when the center of
{\mathbb{G}}
is connected, by Theorem
5.3.1
. Notice that, in any given case, the rank
can be determined as the number of nonzero bilinear forms in the standardly based algebra
A_{k}
. See Lemma
5.1.3
.

Problem 2.
Is the decomposition matrix
D^{+}=D^{+}_{\mathcal{O}}
of
A^{+}=A^{+}_{\mathcal{O}}
a submatrix of the decomposition matrix of
{\mathcal{O}}G
?

This is related to the realization problem of the modules
X_{\omega}
(
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
) constructed in Theorem
2.4.10
.
A positive answer to Problem 2 would determine many decomposition numbers of
G
associated to complex irreducible characters in the permutation module
\text{Ind}^{G}_{B(q)}{\mathbb{C}}.
These characters are all “unipotent,” and much is known about the remaining unipotent characters from Lusztig’s theory of “families” and how they are parametrized. See
[
C85
, 12.3, 12.4, 13.2, 13.8,
13.9]
.

It is a consequence of Lusztig’s theory that each irreducible complex character in the “unipotent series” lies in the same “series” as one of the irreducible complex characters in the paragraph above. A great deal is known about characters in a series and characters of the Weyl group, see

[
Lus93
, p. 191]
and
[
C85
, §§12.3-12.4]
.

Problem 3.
Assume that the center of
G
is connected and recall that
r=
characteristic of
k
is good. Is
D
a submatrix of a square unitriangular decomposition matrix
D^{++}=D^{++}_{\mathcal{O}}
(for, say, an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
A^{++}_{\mathcal{O}}
) with columns indexed by the set of
irreducible unipotent characters?

We would hope that
D^{++}
would be the decomposition matrix arising from a stratified
{\mathcal{Z}}
-algebra

A^{++}
in the spirit of this monograph, and that it is a submatrix of the decomposition matrix of
{\mathcal{O}}G
.

Related to this problem is the Geck-Hiss conjecture
[
GJ11
, Conj. 4.5.2]
(see also
[
GH97
, Conj. 3.4]
) that the decomposition
matrix associated to all the unipotent characters is square unitriangular (when
r
is good). So, an informal version of Problem 3 might be: Could the now known validity of our Conjecture
3.2.1
(Theorem
3.3.1
) above lead to progress on the Geck-Hiss conjecture? Theorem
5.3.1
may be viewed as a step in this direction.
Moreover, if the Geck-Hiss conjecture could be proved in this way, then
it would be natural to investigate if Theorem
3.3.1
might be used, in the general case (where bad primes are allowed), to formulate some kind of approximate analog (of that conjecture).

Appendix A
Projective coverings for Steinberg modules

by Veronica Shalotenko

A1. Preliminaries.

Let
p
be a prime number and let
{\mathbb{G}}
be a connected reductive algebraic group over the algebraic closure
\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
of the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. If
F
is an endomorphism of
G
such that some power of
F
is a (standard) Frobenius morphism, then the fixed-point subgroup
G={{\mathbb{G}}}^{F}
of
{\mathbb{G}}
under the action of
F
is a finite group of Lie type. (Finite groups of Lie type are discussed in detail in [1].) We will assume that the center of
{\mathbb{G}}
is connected. (We will use this assumption when describing the Gelfand-Graev module for
G
below.) The finite group of Lie type
G
is equipped with a BN-pair which is split in the sense of [1], §2.5, i.e. this BN-pair satisfies the additional axioms (a)
B=UT
, where
T=B\cap N
and
U
is the largest normal
p
-subgroup of
B
, and (b)
\cap_{n\in N}\penalty\ nBn^{-1}=T
. Let
(W,S)
be the Coxeter system corresponding to the BN-pair structure on
G
, where
W=N/T
. There is a length function
l
on
W
, where for any
w\in W
,
l(w)
is the minimal length of an expression of
w
in the generators
s\in S
.

Harish-Chandra induction and restriction.

We will briefly describe several key aspects of modular Harish-Chandra theory; for a detailed treatment, see [5], §4.2. In our description of Harish-Chandra induction and restriction, we will assume that
k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic
r\geq 0
,
r\neq p
(thus,
k
may be a field of characteristic 0).

Let
P
be a parabolic subgroup of
G
and let
L
be a Levi complement in
P
; in this case,
P=U_{P}\rtimes L
. Let
kL
-mod denote the category of (finite-dimensional) left
kL
-modules, and let
kG
-mod denote the category of (finite-dimensional) left
kG
-modules. There is a Harish-Chandra induction functor

R_{L\subseteq P}^{G}:kL\text{-mod}\to kG\text{-mod},

defined by
R_{L\subseteq P}^{G}(X)=\text{Ind}_{P}^{G}(\tilde{X})
for all
X\in kL
-mod, where
\tilde{X}
denotes the inflation of
X
from
L
to
P
via the surjective homomorphism
P\to L
with kernel
U_{P}
.

There is also a Harish-Chandra restriction functor

{}^{*}{R}^{G}_{L\subseteq P}:kG\text{-mod}\to kL\text{-mod}.

Given a
kG
-module
Y
,
{}^{*}{R}^{G}_{L\subseteq P}(Y)=Y^{U_{P}}
(which has the structure of a
kL
-module since
U_{P}
is a normal subgroup of
P
).

Howlett and Lehrer [6] and Dipper and Du [3] proved that the Harish-Chandra induction and restriction functors
R_{L\subseteq P}^{G}
and
{}^{*}{R}^{G}_{L\subseteq P}
are independent of the choice of parabolic subgroup
P
containing
L
. Thus, we will omit the parabolic subgroup
P
and write
R_{L}^{G}
and
{}^{*}{R}^{G}_{L}
for the Harish-Chandra induction and restriction functors.

We will use the following properties of Harish-Chandra induction and restriction in the proof of Theorem
A.1
.

Adjointness.

For
any Levi subgroup
L
,
R_{L}^{G}
and
{}^{*}{R}^{G}_{L}
are exact. The functors
R_{L}^{G}
and
{}^{*}{R}^{G}_{L}
are each other’s two-sided adjoints.

Mackey decomposition.

Let
L
be a Levi subgroup of
G
. Given an element
n\in N
and a (left)
kL
-module
X
, we can define a
k({{}^{n}{L}})
-module structure on
X
by setting
nln^{-1}.x=l.x
, for any
l\in L
and
x\in X
. The resulting
k({{}^{n}{L}})
-module will be denoted by
{{}^{n}{X}}
.

Now, let
L
and
M
be two Levi subgroups of
G
, and let
P
and
Q
be parabolic subgroups containing
L
and
M
, respectively. Let
X
be a
kL
-module, and let
D(Q,P)
denote a full set of
(Q,P)
-double coset representatives in
G
. The Mackey formula provides the following direct sum decomposition of the
kM
-module
{}^{*}{R}^{G}_{M}(R_{L}^{G}(X))
:

{}^{*}{R}^{G}_{M}(R_{L}^{G}(X))\cong\underset{n\in D(Q,P)}{\bigoplus}R^{M}_{{{}^{n}{L}}\cap M}({{}^{*}{R}}^{{}^{n}{L}}_{{{}^{n}{L}}\cap M}({{}^{n}{X}})).

A2. The Steinberg module and the Gelfand-Graev module.
Let
G
be a finite group of Lie type defined in characteristic
p
, and let
R
be a commutative ring (with unity).

The Steinberg module.

We define an element
\mathfrak{e}\in RG
by
\mathfrak{e}=\underset{w\in W}{\sum}(-1)^{l(w)}n_{w}\mathfrak{b}
, where
\mathfrak{b}=\underset{b\in B}{\sum}b
and
n_{w}\in N_{G}(T)
is a representative of the coset
w\in W=N/T\leq N_{G}(T)/T
. The left
RG
-module
\text{St}_{R}=RG\mathfrak{e}
is the Steinberg module for
G
over
R
[7].

An
r
-modular system.

For the remainder of this appendix,
k
will be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
r>0
,
r\neq p
. We will work with an
r
-modular system
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
, where
{\mathcal{O}}
is a complete discrete valuation ring with residue field
k
and
K
is the fraction field of
{\mathcal{O}}
. We will assume that
\text{char}(K)=0
. We will call an
{\mathcal{O}}G
-module
M
a lattice if
M
is finitely generated and free over
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Let
\text{St}_{\mathcal{O}}
=
{\mathcal{O}}G\mathfrak{e}
be the Steinberg module for
G
over
{\mathcal{O}}
(
\text{St}_{\mathcal{O}}
is finitely generated and free over
{\mathcal{O}}
, so it is a lattice). Then,
K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\text{St}_{\mathcal{O}}\cong\text{St}_{K}
is an irreducible
KG
-module [7], Theorem 3.

The Gelfand-Graev module.

We will construct the Gelfand-Graev module for
G
following [4], §4. Let
\sigma:U\to K^{\times}
be a group homomorphism, and define
\mathfrak{u}_{\sigma}:=\underset{u\in U}{\sum}\sigma(u)u\in KG
. Since
U
is a
p
-subgroup of
G
,
r\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.11111pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.11111pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.97221pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.97221pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}|U|
and
\sigma(u)\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
, for all
u\in U
, which means
\mathfrak{u}_{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{O}}G
. We have

\mathfrak{u}_{\sigma}^{2}=\underset{u,u^{\prime}\in U}{\sum}\Big(\sigma(u)u\Big)\Big(\sigma(u^{\prime})u^{\prime}\Big)=\underset{u\in U}{\sum}\underset{u^{\prime}\in U}{\sum}\sigma(uu^{\prime})uu^{\prime}=\underset{u\in U}{\sum}\mathfrak{u}_{\sigma}=|U|\mathfrak{u}_{\sigma}.

Since
|U|
is a unit in
{\mathcal{O}}
,
\frac{1}{|U|}\mathfrak{u}_{\sigma}
is an idempotent in
{\mathcal{O}}G
and the
{\mathcal{O}}G
-lattice
\Gamma_{\sigma}:={\mathcal{O}}G\mathfrak{u}_{\sigma}
is projective.

We will now assume that
\sigma:U\to K^{\times}
is a fixed regular character (defined in [4], §4.3). As above, we assume that
G={\mathbb{G}}^{F}
and that the algebraic group
{{\mathbb{G}}}
has a connected center. In this case, the projective
{\mathcal{O}}G
-lattice
\Gamma_{\sigma}
is called a Gelfand-Graev module for
G
. Since the center of
{{\mathbb{G}}}
is connected, the Gelfand-Graev module is unique up to isomorphism [1], Prop. 8.1.2. We will denote
K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\Gamma_{\sigma}
(the Gelfand-Graev module for
G
over
K
) by
K\Gamma_{\sigma}
; we will denote
k\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\Gamma_{\sigma}
(the Gelfand-Graev module for
G
over
k
) by
\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
. Since
\Gamma_{\sigma}
is a projective
{\mathcal{O}}G
-module,
\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
is a projective
kG
-module.

A3. Properties of the Steinberg and Gelfand-Graev modules.

In the proof of Theorem
A.1
, we will use several facts about the Steinberg and Gelfand-Graev modules which can be found in [4]. We begin with a summary of the relevant results.

By [4], §4.1 ,

(A.1)

\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(K\Gamma_{\sigma},\text{St}_{K})=1.\\

Since
\text{St}_{K}
is irreducible, it follows that there is a surjective
KG
-module homomorphism from
K\Gamma_{\sigma}
to
\text{St}_{K}
.

A similar result holds over
{\mathcal{O}}
. In [4], Prop. 4.2, Geck defines an
{\mathcal{O}}G
-lattice
\rho_{\sigma}(\mathscr{S}_{\sigma})
in
\text{St}_{K}
(which we will denote by
\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}
to simplify the notation) such that
K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}\cong\text{St}_{K}
. The lattice
\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}
is a homomorphic image of the Gelfand-Graev lattice
\Gamma_{\sigma}
[4], Prop. 4.2. Thus, there is a surjective homomorphism
\Gamma_{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}
, which, upon reduction to
k
, yields a surjective homomorphism

(A.2)

\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}

from the projective
kG
-module
\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
to the reduction
\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}
of the lattice
\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}
. (By [4]. Prop. 4.7, there are many groups
G
and fields
k
for which
\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}\cong\text{St}_{k}
.)

Statements analogous to (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) hold for any Levi subgroup
L
of
G
. Since a Levi subgroup
L
is itself a finite group of Lie type, there is a Steinberg module
\text{St}_{K}^{L}
for
L
over
K
. By [4], §4.3, there is also a Gelfand-Graev lattice
\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L}
for
L
(obtained using a restriction of the regular character
\sigma
). Thus, we have a Gelfand-Graev module
K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L}
for
L
over
K
. By (
A.1
),
\text{dim}_{KL}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L},\text{St}_{K}^{L})=1
; by (
A.2
), there is an
{\mathcal{O}}L
-lattice
\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L}
in
\text{St}_{K}^{L}
with
K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L}\cong\text{St}_{K}^{L}
and a surjective homomorphism
\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}^{L}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L}
.

If
L
and
M
are Levi subgroups of
G
with
L\subseteq M
, we have the following isomorphisms of
KL
-modules (these isomorphisms are recorded in [4], Lemma 4.4):

(A.3)

^{*}{R}^{M}_{L}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{M})\cong K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L},

(A.4)

^{*}{R}^{M}_{L}(\text{St}_{K}^{M})\cong\text{St}_{K}^{L},\text{ and}

(A.5)

\text{If }L={{}^{n}{L^{\prime}}},\text{ for some }n\in N,\text{ then }^{n}{K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L^{\prime}}}\cong K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L}\text{ and }^{n}{\text{St}_{K}^{L^{\prime}}}\cong\text{St}_{K}^{L}.

A4. Main result.

As above, let
G
be a finite group of Lie type defined in characteristic
p
(arising from an algebraic group with a connected center). Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
r>0
,
r\neq p
, and let
({\mathcal{O}},K,k)
be an
r
-modular system, where
{\mathcal{O}}
is a complete discrete valuation ring and
K
(the fraction field of
{\mathcal{O}}
) has characteristic 0. Let
(W,S)
be the Coxeter system associated to the
BN
-pair of
G
. Given a subset
J\subseteq S
, let
L_{J}
denote the standard Levi subgroup of
G
corresponding to
J
. By (
A.2
), for all
J\subseteq S
, there is a surjective homomorphism

\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}

from the projective
kL_{J}
-module
\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
to the
kL_{J}
-module
\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
, where
\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
is an
{\mathcal{O}}L_{J}
-lattice in
\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}
with the property that
K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}\cong\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}
. Applying the exact Harish-Chandra induction functor
R_{L_{J}}^{G}
, we obtain a surjective homomorphism

R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})\twoheadrightarrow R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}).

Thus, taking direct sums over all
J\subseteq S
, we obtain a surjective homomorphism

\beta_{k}:\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})\twoheadrightarrow\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})

from the projective
kG
-module
\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})
to the
kG
-module
\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})
.

We can construct an analogous homomorphism
\beta_{K}
over the characteristic 0 field
K
. For any
J\subseteq S
, the surjective homomorphism
\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
of (
A.2
) is induced by a surjective homomorphism
\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
from the Gelfand-Graev lattice
\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
to the lattice
\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}
of
\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}
. It follows that the surjective homomorphism
\beta_{k}
defined above is induced by a surjective homomorphism

\beta:\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})\to R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})

of
{\mathcal{O}}G
-lattices. Taking tensor products with
K
, we obtain a surjective homomorphism

\beta_{K}:\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})\twoheadrightarrow\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})

of
KG
-modules. Since Harish-Chandra induction is “compatible” with the
r
-modular system (See [4], §4), we have
K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})\cong R_{L_{J}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})
and
K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})\cong R_{L_{J}}^{G}(K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathscr{S}_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})\cong R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})
, for any
J\subseteq S
. Therefore, there is a surjective
KG
-module homomorphism

\beta_{K}:\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})\twoheadrightarrow\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}).

Theorem A.1
.

If
\beta_{K}:\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})\twoheadrightarrow\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})
is the surjective homomorphism described above, then the kernel of
\beta_{K}
has no composition factors in common with
\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})
.
1
1

1

A related open question is whether or not this result holds in positive characteristic.

Proof.

We claim that it suffices to show that

(A.6)

\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{J}),\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))=\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}),\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})).\\

For suppose that the equality in (
A.6
) holds. Since
K
is a field of characteristic 0, every short exact sequence of
KG
-modules splits and we have a direct sum decomposition

\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}})\cong\text{\rm Ker}(\beta_{K})\oplus{\rm{Im}}(\beta_{K})\cong\text{\rm Ker}(\beta_{K})\oplus\Big(\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})\Big)

(the homomorphism
\beta_{K}
is surjective, so
{\rm{Im}}(\beta_{K})\cong\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})
). It follows that
\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{J}),\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))
decomposes as a direct sum of

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(\text{\rm Ker}(\beta_{K}),\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))\text{ and }\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}),\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})).

Therefore, for equality to hold in (
A.6
), we must have

\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(\text{\rm Ker}(\beta_{K}),\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))=0.

Since
\text{char}(K)=0
and all
KG
-modules are completely reducible, this is possible if and only if the
KG
-modules
\text{\rm Ker}(\beta_{K})
and
\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})
have no composition factors in common.

We will now establish the equality

\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{J}),\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))=\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}),\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})).

In fact, since

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{J}}),\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))\cong\underset{I,J\subseteq S}{\bigoplus}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(R_{L_{I}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))\text{ and}

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}),\underset{J\subseteq S}{\oplus}R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))\cong\underset{I,J\subseteq S}{\bigoplus}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(R_{L_{I}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{I}}),R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})),

it is enough to show that

(A.7)

\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(R_{L_{I}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))=\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(R_{L_{I}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{I}}),R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})),

for all
I,J\subseteq S
.

We will now compute
\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(R_{L_{I}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))
. Since the Harish-Chandra restriction functor
{}^{*}{R}^{G}_{L_{J}}
is adjoint to the Harish-Chandra induction functor
R_{L_{J}}^{G}
, we have

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(R_{L_{I}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KL_{J}}({{}^{*}{R}}^{G}_{L_{J}}R_{L_{I}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}).

Let
P_{J}
and
P_{I}
denote the standard parabolic subgroups of
G
containing
L_{J}
and
L_{I}
, respectively, and let
D(P_{J},P_{I})\subseteq N
be a full set of
(P_{J},P_{I})
-double coset representatives in
G
. By Mackey decomposition, we have

\displaystyle\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KL_{J}}({{}^{*}{R}}^{G}_{L_{J}}R_{L_{I}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})

\displaystyle\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KL_{J}}\Big(\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\bigoplus}R_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}{{}^{*}{R}}^{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}({{}^{n}{K\Gamma}}_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}\Big)

\displaystyle\cong\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\bigoplus}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KL_{J}}(R_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}{{}^{*}{R}}^{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}({{}^{n}{K\Gamma}}_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}).

Now, by (
A.3
) and (
A.5
), the
K({{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J})
-module
{{}^{*}{R}}^{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}({{}^{n}{K\Gamma}}_{\sigma}^{L_{I}})
is the Gelfand-Graev module
K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}
, for
{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}
over
K
. Thus, continuing the computation above (and, using the adjointness of
R_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}
and
{}^{*}{R}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}
), we have

\displaystyle\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\bigoplus}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KL_{J}}(R_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}{{}^{*}{R}}^{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}({{}^{n}{K\Gamma}}_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\bigoplus}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KL_{J}}(R_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}),\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\bigoplus}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{K({{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J})}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}},{{}^{*}{R}}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})).

By (
A.4
),
{{}^{*}{R}}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})
is the Steinberg module
\text{St}_{K}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}
, for
{}^{n}{L_{I}}\cap L_{J}
over
K
. Therefore,

\text{\rm{Hom}}_{K({{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J})}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}},{{}^{*}{R}}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{K({{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J})}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}},\text{St}_{K}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}),

which has dimension 1 by (
A.1
). Thus,

\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(R_{L_{I}}^{G}(K\Gamma_{\sigma}^{L_{I}}),R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))=\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\sum}1=|D(P_{J},P_{I})|.

We can similarly compute
\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(R_{L_{I}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{I}}),R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))
. We have

\displaystyle\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(R_{L_{I}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{I}}),R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))

\displaystyle\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KL_{J}}({{}^{*}{R}}^{G}_{L_{J}}R_{L_{I}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{I}}),\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})

\displaystyle\cong\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KL_{J}}\Big(\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\bigoplus}R_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}{{}^{*}{R}}^{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}({{}^{n}{\text{St}_{K}}}^{L_{I}}),\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}\Big)

\displaystyle\cong\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\bigoplus}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KL_{J}}(R_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}{{}^{*}{R}}^{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}({{}^{n}{\text{St}_{K}}}^{L_{I}}),\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\bigoplus}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KL_{J}}(R_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}(\text{St}_{K}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}),\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\bigoplus}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{K({{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J})}(\text{St}_{K}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}},{{}^{*}{R}}_{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}^{L_{J}}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))

\displaystyle\cong\underset{n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})}{\bigoplus}\text{\rm{Hom}}_{K({{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J})}(\text{St}_{K}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}},\text{St}_{K}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}).

Since each Steinberg module
\text{St}_{K}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}}
is absolutely irreducible (see [4], Theorem 2.1), we have

\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{K({{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J})}(\text{St}_{K}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}},\text{St}_{K}^{{{}^{n}{L_{I}}}\cap L_{J}})=1,

for all
n\in D(P_{J},P_{I})
. Therefore,
\text{dim}\;\text{\rm{Hom}}_{KG}(R_{L_{I}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{I}}),R_{L_{J}}^{G}(\text{St}_{K}^{L_{J}}))=|D(P_{J},P_{I})|
, proving that the equality in (
A.7
) holds.
∎
